Evidence Practice Finals
========================

Subject: Evidence
Type: Practice Questions
Source: https://supertoadman.github.io/Wilmington-University-corner/subjects/evidence/evidence-practice-finals/

14 practice finals of 30 multiple-choice questions each (420 in all), the size of the final

---

## Final 1 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Rule 606(b): extraneous information vs. a juror’s experience
After a defense verdict in a federal products-liability suit over a collapsed extension ladder, the plaintiff moves for a new trial and submits a juror’s affidavit stating: (1) during deliberations Juror 6, a retired carpenter, told the others that in his 30 years on job sites, ladders like this one failed only when overloaded; (2) Juror 2 brought a printout of the ladder’s online customer reviews, which praised its sturdiness, into the jury room and read it aloud; and (3) after hearing the reviews, two jurors changed their votes to find for the defendant. Which parts of the affidavit may the court consider?

A. Items 1 and 2, because both brought information from outside the trial record into deliberations.
B. Items 2 and 3, because once extraneous information is shown, the court needs to know whether it actually changed the outcome.
C. Item 2 only.
D. None of them, because each describes something that happened during deliberations and the Peña-Rodriguez exception is limited to racial bias.
E. All three items, because a new-trial motion requires proof of what the jury actually relied on.

Answer: C
- A: Warger draws the line by the nature of the information, not by whether it was in the record. A juror’s own experience is something jurors bring with them, so it is internal even when it is specific and influential.
- B: The exception opens the door only to whether extraneous information reached the jury. 606(b)(1) still forbids testimony about “the effect of anything on that juror’s or another juror’s vote.” Courts judge the likely effect on a hypothetical average juror instead.
- C: Correct. Online reviews are information from outside the trial that reached the jury improperly, so 606(b)(2)(A) lets a juror testify that it happened. Item 1 is a juror drawing on his own life experience, which is internal (Warger). Item 3 describes the effect of something on jurors’ votes, which 606(b)(1) bars even when an exception applies; the court decides prejudice objectively, without asking jurors how they voted.
- D: 606(b)(2) has its own textual exceptions apart from Peña-Rodriguez. Extraneous prejudicial information, an outside influence, and a mistake on the verdict form may all be proved by juror testimony.
- E: Rule 606(b)(1) bars testimony about any effect on jurors’ votes, so item 3 is out, and Juror 6’s own experience is internal, not extraneous (Warger). The court judges prejudice objectively.
Rule: Rule 606(b) bars juror testimony about deliberations, effects on votes, and mental processes. Exceptions: extraneous prejudicial information, outside influence, a verdict-form mistake (plus Peña-Rodriguez racial bias). A juror’s own experiences are internal (Warger).

## Final 1 · Q2 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Rule 602: a shaky foundation goes to weight
In a hit-and-run prosecution, a witness testifies that she was walking her dog a block away and "thinks" she saw a gray sedan speed off after the crash. She admits she was looking at her phone part of the time and isn't completely sure. The defense moves to strike her testimony for lack of personal knowledge.

What is the best ruling?

A. Strike it, because the judge must find by a preponderance that she saw the car.
B. Strike it, because testimony that a witness "thinks" she saw something is speculation.
C. Deny the motion, because a jury could find she saw it; doubt goes to weight.
D. Deny the motion, but only if another witness corroborates her account.
E. Strike it, because a witness who was distracted is incompetent to testify.

Answer: C
- A: Personal knowledge is a Rule 104(b) question. The judge asks only whether a jury could find that she perceived the event.
- B: "I think I saw" from a witness who was in a position to see expresses imperfect certainty, not guesswork. Rule 602 is satisfied by evidence sufficient to support a finding.
- C: Correct, though her account is weak. Rule 602 requires only "evidence sufficient to support a finding" that the witness has personal knowledge. She was nearby and looking at least part of the time, so a jury could find she saw the car. Her hesitation is for cross-examination and the jury.
- D: Rule 602 doesn't require corroboration.
- E: Rule 601 presumes competence. Distraction affects the weight of her testimony, not her competence.
Rule: Rule 602 requires evidence sufficient to support a finding of personal knowledge, a Rule 104(b) standard. Uncertainty and imperfect perception go to weight, which the jury decides.

## Final 1 · Q3 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 104(b): receipt as a conditional fact
Jill Park sues Ventra Corp. after her Ventra space heater ignited. To show that Ventra had notice of the defect before her injury, she offers a letter that another customer wrote to Ventra a year earlier describing an identical fire. The customer testifies that he addressed, stamped, and mailed the letter to Ventra's headquarters and that it was never returned. Ventra's mailroom supervisor testifies that the company has no record of receiving it.

How should the court decide whether the letter is admissible?

A. The court must find that Ventra more likely than not received the letter before admitting it.
B. The court should admit it if a reasonable jury could find receipt.
C. The court should exclude the letter as hearsay, because it describes an earlier fire.
D. The court should exclude it, because receipt is disputed and only Ventra's admission would do.
E. The court should admit it only if Park first proves the customer's account of the fire was true.

Answer: B
- A: Receipt is a fact on which relevance depends, so it is a Rule 104(b) question for the jury. The judge doesn't weigh the conflicting testimony (Huddleston).
- B: Correct. The letter is relevant to notice only if Ventra received it. Under Rule 104(b), the judge asks only whether evidence could support a finding, and proper mailing could. The jury resolves the conflict with the mailroom testimony.
- C: Offered to show that Ventra was told of a fire, the letter is not offered for its truth. Notice is a non-hearsay use.
- D: Rule 104(b) requires only evidence sufficient to support a finding. A dispute doesn't defeat admission.
- E: For notice, it doesn't matter whether the fire happened as described, only that Ventra was told about it.
Rule: When relevance depends on a fact, such as receipt or knowledge, Rule 104(b) requires only evidence sufficient for a reasonable jury to find it, and the jury decides. Rule 104(a) questions, by contrast, are decided by the judge by a preponderance.

## Final 1 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 407: product defects and nonparty measures
A rider is burned when an e-bike battery overheats. She sues the manufacturer on a strict-liability design-defect theory. The manufacturer concedes that a thermal cutoff switch was technologically and economically feasible but argues the original design was reasonable. The plaintiff offers (1) evidence that after her accident the manufacturer redesigned the battery to add a thermal cutoff, and (2) evidence that after her accident a national retailer, which isn’t a party, pulled the model from its shelves and posted warnings. How should the court rule?

A. Exclude item 1, but item 2 isn’t barred by Rule 407, though the court may weigh it under Rule 403.
B. Admit item 1, because it shows that a thermal cutoff was a feasible precaution.
C. Exclude item 2 only, because a retailer’s decision reveals nothing about the manufacturer’s design.
D. Admit both, because Rule 407 doesn’t apply to claims brought on a strict-liability theory.
E. Exclude both, because Rule 407 bars every post-accident measure concerning the product.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Since 1997, 407 expressly covers measures offered to prove a defect in a product or its design, and feasibility isn’t disputed, so the redesign can’t come in that way. Most circuits hold that 407 doesn’t reach measures by non-parties (Millennium Partners), since third parties won’t be deterred from repairing. Their measures say little about this defendant, though, so some courts exclude them under 403.
- B: Feasibility is a permitted purpose only “if disputed.” The manufacturer conceded it, so feasibility can’t serve as a backdoor.
- C: That reverses the analysis. The manufacturer’s own redesign is the measure Rule 407 bars (it has applied to product claims since 1997), while a nonparty’s measure isn’t covered by Rule 407, though Rule 403 may weigh against it.
- D: Some pre-1997 decisions held that, but the amendment added “a defect in a product or its design” and “a need for a warning or instruction” to 407’s list.
- E: 407’s rationale, encouraging the defendant to repair, has little force for non-parties, and most courts don’t apply it to them.
Rule: Rule 407 applies to product-defect claims (1997 amendment). Feasibility is a permitted purpose only if disputed. Most circuits: non-party measures aren’t barred by 407, though 403 still applies.

## Final 1 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 408: no dispute, no protection
A roofer invoices a homeowner $50,000. The homeowner emails: "Your work was excellent and I owe every penny, but cash is tight. Will you take $35,000 now as payment in full?" The roofer refuses. A month later the homeowner claims for the first time that the flashing leaks and refuses to pay anything. In the roofer's suit, he offers the email to prove the debt. How should the court rule?

A. Exclude the email, because the claim later became disputed and the protection applies to all communications about it.
B. Exclude the email, because a written offer to accept less is inadmissible under Rule 408 in every case.
C. Admit it, because when the email was sent there was no dispute about validity or amount, just a discount request.
D. Admit the email, but only to show the homeowner's bias, because Rule 408 permits that purpose even for protected offers.
E. Exclude the email, because any offer to accept less than the amount demanded is an attempt to compromise the claim.

Answer: C
- A: Coverage depends on whether a dispute existed when the communication was made.
- B: Rule 408 turns on whether the claim was disputed as to validity or amount when the offer was made, not on whether the offer was written.
- C: Correct. Rule 408 applies only to claims disputed as to validity or amount. An offer to discount an admitted debt doesn't implicate the rule's policy, and a dispute that arises later does not cover the earlier email.
- D: There is no bias issue here. The email comes in as substantive proof because 408 doesn't apply at all.
- E: The Advisory Committee Note says otherwise. The rule requires an actual dispute.
Rule: Rule 408 applies only to compromise of a claim that was disputed as to validity or amount when the offer or statement was made. A request to pay an undisputed debt at a discount isn't a compromise.

## Final 1 · Q6 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 410(a)(4): agents vs. the prosecutor
Under investigation for bank fraud, a loan officer asks to meet the FBI case agent. At the meeting he asks, “If I tell you where the money went, can you get me a deal?” The agent answers that only the prosecutor can make deals but that he will pass along anything useful. The officer then describes how he routed the funds. Weeks later, in plea negotiations with the Assistant U.S. Attorney that break down, he admits forging two signatures. He later pleads guilty but is allowed to withdraw the plea. At trial, which of these may the government offer in its case-in-chief?

A. His statements to the FBI agent about routing the funds.
B. His withdrawn guilty plea.
C. His admission to the AUSA that he forged the signatures.
D. His admission to the AUSA, because a defendant who later pleads guilty waives protection for earlier plea talks.
E. None of them, because all of his statements were made in an effort to obtain leniency.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. 410(a)(4) protects statements made in plea discussions “with an attorney for the prosecuting authority.” Statements to an agent without authority to bargain aren’t covered (Greene), and the agent told him so, which defeats any claim under the Robertson test that he reasonably expected to be negotiating a plea.
- B: 410(a)(1) bars a guilty plea that was later withdrawn. Letting the jury hear it would defeat the purpose of allowing the withdrawal.
- C: That statement was made in plea discussions with the prosecutor that didn’t produce a guilty plea, so 410(a)(4) bars it against him in the case-in-chief.
- D: Rule 410(a)(4) protects statements in plea discussions with a prosecuting attorney that don’t result in a guilty plea or that result in a later-withdrawn plea, which is what happened here.
- E: Hoping for leniency doesn’t make a conversation a plea discussion. 410 protects talks with the prosecuting attorney, not unilateral overtures to police.
Rule: Rule 410 bars withdrawn guilty pleas, nolo pleas, Rule 11 statements, and statements in plea talks with a prosecuting attorney. Statements to agents without authority aren’t covered.

## Final 1 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(b)(2): motive from discovered embezzlement
Lorna Pace, a bookkeeper, is charged with murdering her employer, Hugh Brandt, who was found dead in his office. The prosecution offers evidence that Pace had embezzled $80,000 from Brandt over two years. It also offers Brandt's email to his lawyer, sent a week before his death, saying he had discovered the theft and would call the police on Monday. Pace has never been charged with embezzlement. She objects that the evidence shows only that she is a thief.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because uncharged crimes are inadmissible under Rule 404(b) without a conviction.
B. Exclude it, because a desire to avoid punishment is just another form of propensity.
C. Admit it to show motive, subject to Rule 403 and an instruction.
D. Admit it only if Pace testifies and denies having any motive to kill him.
E. Admit it, because Rule 404(b) doesn't apply to other acts against the same victim.

Answer: C
- A: Huddleston requires only evidence from which a jury could reasonably find the act occurred, not a conviction.
- B: Under Kirsch, a motive that is just a desire to commit this kind of crime is propensity. A motive to silence someone who discovered a crime runs through a specific reason to act, not through character.
- C: Correct. The chain goes from embezzlement, to Brandt's discovery and threat to call police, to a reason to stop him, to the killing. No step relies on Pace's character, so motive is a Rule 404(b)(2) purpose. The Huddleston steps follow: 104(b) sufficiency, Rule 403, and a Rule 105 instruction on request.
- D: Motive is part of the prosecution's case-in-chief. It doesn't depend on Pace's testimony.
- E: Rule 404(b) applies to "any other crime, wrong, or act," whoever the victim.
Rule: Evidence of another act comes in under Rule 404(b)(2) when it proves motive through a non-character chain, such as a reason to silence the person who discovered a crime. It is then subject to Huddleston, Rule 403, and a limiting instruction.

## Final 1 · Q8 · Character Evidence — United States v. Guardia: an ordinary Rule 403 balance
In a federal prosecution of a physician for sexually abusing two patients during examinations, the government gives timely notice that it will offer four other patients’ testimony that he abused them the same way. The district judge excludes the testimony, explaining: “Evidence of other sexual assaults is always intensely prejudicial, so in Rule 413 cases I start with a strong presumption against admission.” The government appeals. What should the appellate court conclude?

A. The judge erred, because Rule 413 evidence gets an ordinary Rule 403 balance, with no presumption against it.
B. The judge was right, because Rule 413 requires that the other assaults have resulted in convictions.
C. The judge was right, because Rule 413 evidence is presumptively inadmissible absent special need.
D. The judge was right, because Rule 413 evidence must be more probative than prejudicial before it comes in.
E. The judge erred, because Rule 403 doesn’t apply to evidence offered under Rule 413.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Guardia sets a threshold (sexual-assault charge, evidence of another sexual assault, relevance) and then requires a normal 403 weighing. Treating propensity as inherently too prejudicial would frustrate Congress’s purpose. In Guardia itself, exclusion was affirmed because of a specific risk of confusion: four more incidents with dueling experts on medical propriety. A judge may exclude for reasons like that, but not by presumption.
- B: Uncharged acts qualify. The standard of proof is presumably Huddleston’s, whether a jury could reasonably find the acts occurred.
- C: No such presumption exists. The rule says the court “may admit” the evidence “on any matter to which it is relevant,” including propensity.
- D: That reverses Rule 403’s test. Rule 413 evidence gets the ordinary balance: it is excluded only if unfair prejudice substantially outweighs probative value, with no presumption either way (United States v. Guardia).
- E: 403 applies to 413 evidence. Mound relied on that safeguard to uphold the rule against a due-process challenge.
Rule: Guardia: the 413 threshold (accused of sexual assault, evidence of another, relevance), then an ordinary 403 balance with no thumb on the scale.

## Final 1 · Q9 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(a)(2)(B) opens the door; Rule 405(a) form
Marco Bell is charged with assault. He claims the victim, Troy Quinn, swung first. In his case, Bell calls a witness who testifies that Quinn has a reputation in the neighborhood as a violent man. Bell doesn't testify. In rebuttal, the prosecution offers:

I. Testimony from Quinn's employer that, in her opinion, Quinn is a peaceful person.
II. Testimony from Bell's coworker that, in her opinion, Bell is a violent person.
III. Testimony from a bartender describing a fight Bell started at his bar last year.

Which items are admissible?

A. I only.
B. II only.
C. I and II only.
D. II and III only.
E. I, II, and III.

Answer: C
- A: Item I is proper rebuttal under Rule 404(a)(2)(B)(i), but Item II is also admissible. When the defendant attacks the victim's trait, the prosecution may offer evidence of the defendant's same trait.
- B: Item II is admissible, but so is Item I. The prosecution may rebut evidence of the victim's trait.
- C: Correct. Once Bell offers Quinn's violent character, Rule 404(a)(2)(B) lets the prosecution rebut it (Item I) and offer evidence of Bell's same trait (Item II), even though Bell never offered his own character. Item III is a specific instance. Under Rule 405(a), character is proved by reputation or opinion, and specific acts come in only on cross of a character witness.
- D: Item III fails Rule 405(a): extrinsic proof of a specific act is not a permitted way to prove character here. Item I is admissible.
- E: Item III is barred by Rule 405(a). Character isn't an essential element of the charge or the defense, so Rule 405(b) doesn't apply.
Rule: By attacking the victim's trait under Rule 404(a)(2)(B), the accused lets the prosecution rebut it and prove the accused's same trait. Under Rule 405(a), that proof must be reputation or opinion. Specific instances come in only on cross-examination.

## Final 1 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Extrinsic evidence: Rule 608(b) vs. bias
On cross-examination, the prosecutor asks the defendant’s alibi witness two questions, each with a good-faith basis: (1) “Didn’t you falsely state on a mortgage application that you had no other debts?” and (2) “Aren’t you a paid employee of the defendant’s trucking company?” The witness answers “No” to both. The prosecutor then offers the bank’s loan officer to prove the false statement and a payroll clerk to prove the employment. How should the court rule?

A. Admit the loan officer but exclude the payroll clerk.
B. Exclude the loan officer but admit the payroll clerk.
C. Admit the loan officer, but only if the prosecution first offers the mortgage application itself.
D. Exclude both, because a party must take the witness’s answer on cross-examination.
E. Admit both, because the witness denied facts the prosecution can prove.

Answer: B
- A: That reverses the rules. Bias may be proved extrinsically; specific acts offered to show untruthful character may not.
- B: Correct. The mortgage lie is a specific instance offered only to show untruthful character, so 608(b) bars extrinsic proof, and the prosecutor must take the answer. Working for the defendant shows bias, a motive to lie in this case. Bias is never collateral, so extrinsic evidence is allowed after a denial (Abel).
- C: Whether by document or witness, extrinsic proof of a specific act offered only to attack truthfulness is barred by Rule 608(b). The prosecutor must take the witness’s “No.”
- D: That is true for 608(b) acts, but not for bias, which may be proved by extrinsic evidence.
- E: A denial doesn’t open the door to extrinsic proof of a 608(b) act. As the textbook puts it, 608(b) impeachment works only when the witness is honest enough to admit the act.
Rule: Rule 608(b) bars extrinsic evidence of specific acts offered only to attack truthfulness, so the examiner takes the answer. Bias isn’t collateral and may be proved extrinsically after a denial (Abel).

## Final 1 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 608: the Bar Fight problem
In a civil assault suit, the plaintiff’s only eyewitness testifies that she saw the defendant start the fight. The defense calls her former coworker, who testifies on direct (1) that when the eyewitness sold him her car, she lied that the brakes had just been replaced, and (2) that in his opinion, based on years of dealings with her, she is a liar. On cross, plaintiff’s counsel, with a good-faith basis, asks the coworker (3) whether he was suspended from college for wrecking his dorm room in a drunken rage, and (4) whether he lied on his medical-school application about having a disciplinary record. Which items are proper?

A. Items 1, 2, and 4.
B. Item 2 only.
C. Items 1, 2, and 3.
D. Items 2 and 4.
E. Items 2, 3, and 4.

Answer: D
- A: 608(b) permits specific instances only on cross-examination, never through extrinsic evidence such as another witness’s direct testimony. The car lie could be raised only by asking the eyewitness herself on cross.
- B: Item 4 is proper too. Anyone who testifies puts his own character for truthfulness in play, and the court may allow cross about his specific acts that bear on it, such as lying on an application.
- C: Item 1 is extrinsic proof of a specific lie, which Rule 608(b) bars, and item 3, property damage in a rage, isn’t probative of truthfulness.
- D: Correct. 608(a) allows opinion testimony about a witness’s character for untruthfulness, so item 2 is fine. Item 1 proves a specific lie by extrinsic evidence, which 608(b) forbids. The coworker is a witness too, so 608(b) lets counsel ask about his own acts probative of untruthfulness: lying on an application qualifies (item 4); a drunken rampage doesn’t (item 3).
- E: 608(b) reaches only acts “probative of the character for truthfulness or untruthfulness.” Vandalism while drunk shows bad behavior, not dishonesty.
Rule: Rule 608(a): truthfulness may be attacked by reputation or opinion. 608(b): specific acts probative of truthfulness may be asked about on cross, of the witness or of a character witness, but never proved by extrinsic evidence.

## Final 1 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609(a)(1)(A): a civil party isn't the accused
In a civil assault suit, the defendant, Nick Lowe, wants to impeach the plaintiff, Trey Mason, who testified, with Mason's conviction four years ago for felony manslaughter. Mason argues that because he is a party who faces prejudice from the jury, the conviction should come in only if its probative value outweighs its prejudicial effect to him.

What standard governs?

A. It must be admitted unless its probative value is substantially outweighed by unfair prejudice.
B. It comes in only if its probative value outweighs its prejudicial effect to Mason.
C. It is inadmissible, because manslaughter doesn't involve dishonesty.
D. It must be admitted with no balancing at all, because it is a felony.
E. It comes in only if its probative value substantially outweighs its prejudice.

Answer: A
- A: Correct, even though Mason is a party who will feel the prejudice just as a defendant would. Rule 609(a)(1)(A) applies Rule 403 to "a civil case or ... a criminal case in which the witness is not a defendant." The protective balance in Rule 609(a)(1)(B) belongs only to a criminal defendant.
- B: That is Rule 609(a)(1)(B), which protects only "a defendant in a criminal case."
- C: Felonies may impeach under Rule 609(a)(1) whatever their elements. Rule 609(a)(2) is a separate, automatic route for crimen falsi.
- D: Only crimen falsi under Rule 609(a)(2) comes in with no balancing.
- E: That reverse balance applies to convictions over 10 years old under Rule 609(b).
Rule: Rule 609(a)(1)(A): a felony offered against any witness except a criminal defendant, including a civil party, is admitted subject to Rule 403. Rule 609(a)(1)(B)'s defendant-protective balance applies only to the accused.

## Final 1 · Q13 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 412(b)(1)(A) and Rule 412(c) procedure
Ty Morrow is charged with sexual abuse. He claims he never had sexual contact with the complainant. The prosecution will offer a nurse's testimony that the complainant had genital injuries consistent with forced intercourse that night. Five days before trial, Morrow moves to admit evidence that the complainant had intercourse with another man that same afternoon, to show another source of the injuries. He explains that he learned of it only the day before.

What is the correct ruling?

A. Deny it, because Rule 412 bars all evidence of a victim's sexual behavior in criminal cases.
B. Admit it to show another source of the injuries, if the court finds good cause for lateness.
C. Admit it under the exception for the victim's sexual behavior with the accused.
D. Deny it, because a Rule 412 motion filed less than 14 days before trial must be denied.
E. Admit it as evidence of the victim's pertinent character trait under Rule 404(a)(2)(B).

Answer: B
- A: Rule 412(b)(1) lists exceptions in criminal cases. One fits here.
- B: Correct. Rule 412(b)(1)(A) admits specific instances offered to prove that someone other than the defendant was the source of injury. Rule 412(c) requires a motion at least 14 days before trial unless the court, for good cause, sets a different time. It also requires notice to the victim and an in camera hearing.
- C: Rule 412(b)(1)(B) covers behavior with the accused, offered to prove consent. Morrow denies any contact.
- D: Rule 412(c)(1)(B) allows a later time "for good cause." Learning the facts the day before is a reason to find it.
- E: Rule 404(a)(2)(B) is expressly "subject to the limitations in Rule 412," and sexual behavior isn't a pertinent character trait.
Rule: In criminal cases, Rule 412(b)(1) admits a victim's sexual behavior only (A) to show another source of semen, injury, or physical evidence, (B) with the accused, or (C) when the Constitution requires. Rule 412(c) requires a motion 14 days before trial (or later for good cause), notice, and a sealed hearing.

## Final 1 · Q14 · Definition of Hearsay — Conduct intended as an assertion
(Varies question 14.13.)
Same facts, except the engineer announced to reporters that the town would prove the water was safe, then drank a cup in front of their cameras. The engineer does not testify, and the town offers a video of the event to prove the water was safe. Is the evidence hearsay?

A. Yes, because the act was meant to assert the water was safe and is offered for that truth.
B. No, because the act was recorded on video and the video speaks for itself.
C. No, because the act is an opposing party's statement offered by the town.
D. Yes, because any act performed in front of a witness counts as a statement.
E. No, because a public demonstration of this kind is a present sense impression rather than an assertion.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. The engineer meant the act to tell the public the water was safe, so it is a statement under Rule 801(a). Offered for that truth, it is hearsay under Rule 801(c).
- B: A recording preserves the out-of-court statement. It doesn't change what the statement is.
- C: Rule 801(d)(2) requires offering the statement against the opposing party. The town is offering its own employee's act.
- D: Rule 801(a) turns on intent to assert, not on whether anyone watched.
- E: A present sense impression is an exception that applies to statements. It can't show that the act wasn't an assertion.
Rule: Conduct meant to communicate is a statement under Rule 801(a). Compare a private act done for one's own assurance with a public act done to assure others.

## Final 1 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 613(b) (2024): no foundation for a party’s own statement
Dove sues Kessler for injuries from an intersection collision. Kessler testifies on direct examination that his light was green. Dove’s counsel does not cross-examine Kessler about anything he said before trial.

After Kessler leaves the stand and the defense rests, Dove offers in rebuttal an email that Kessler sent his insurer the day after the crash: “Honestly I think I might have run the red. I was looking at my GPS.” Kessler objects, citing Rule 613(b) as amended in 2024.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit the email only if Dove recalls Kessler to the stand so he can explain or deny it.
B. Admit the email, but only to impeach Kessler’s credibility, with a limiting instruction.
C. Admit the email as substantive evidence, because Rule 613(b)’s foundation requirement does not apply to an opposing party’s statement under Rule 801(d)(2).
D. Exclude the email, because extrinsic evidence of a witness’s prior inconsistent statement may not be admitted until the witness has been given an opportunity to explain or deny it.
E. Exclude the email, because it was sent to Kessler’s liability insurer, and Rule 411 bars evidence of insurance.

Answer: C
- A: No foundation is required for an opposing party’s statement, so Dove need not recall Kessler.
- B: Kessler’s email is admissible for its truth under 801(d)(2)(A), not just to impeach.
- C: Correct. A party’s own statement is substantive evidence, and Rule 613(b)’s foundation requirement does not apply to it.
- D: This is the 2024 rule for ordinary witnesses, but the rule’s last sentence exempts opposing-party statements.
- E: Rule 411 bars insurance evidence only when offered to prove negligence or wrongful conduct. The email is offered for what Kessler admitted, and any reference to insurance can be redacted.
Rule: Unless the court orders otherwise, extrinsic evidence of a witness’s prior inconsistent statement may not be admitted until after the witness is given an opportunity to explain or deny it and an adverse party is given an opportunity to examine the witness about it. This subdivision does not apply to an opposing party’s statement under Rule 801(d)(2). FRE 613(b) (as amended 2024).

## Final 1 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(2)(E) and Bourjaily
DEA Agent Pratt, posing as a buyer, records a meeting at which Quill sells her half a kilogram of cocaine. During the deal, Quill says: “You want more, I can get it. My guy is Ferris. He drives it up from Miami every other Thursday. Buy two keys and he’ll knock off ten percent.” Two weeks later, agents watch Ferris’s car arrive at Quill’s house on a Thursday night. Ferris carries a gym bag inside and leaves without it.

Quill is never charged. At Ferris’s trial for conspiracy to distribute cocaine, the prosecution offers Quill’s recorded statement. Ferris objects.

How should the court proceed?

A. Admit the statement only if evidence other than the statement itself establishes the conspiracy and Ferris’s membership in it by a preponderance of the evidence.
B. Exclude the statement, because Agent Pratt, as a government agent, was not a member of the conspiracy, so a statement to her could not further it.
C. Submit the preliminary question to the jury, instructing it to disregard the statement unless it finds beyond a reasonable doubt that Ferris and Quill were co-conspirators.
D. Exclude the statement, because Quill was never charged, and a co-conspirator’s statement is admissible only against a co-defendant charged in the same conspiracy.
E. Admit it if the judge finds by a preponderance, considering the statement and the surveillance, that it was made during and in furtherance of their conspiracy.

Answer: E
- A: This is the pre-Bourjaily independent-evidence rule. The court may consider the statement itself, though it cannot rely on it alone.
- B: The listener need not be a conspirator. Statements made to recruit, reassure, or induce a buyer, even one who turns out to be an agent, can further the conspiracy.
- C: Preliminary questions of admissibility under 801(d)(2)(E) are for the judge under Rule 104(a), decided by a preponderance, not for the jury beyond a reasonable doubt.
- D: The co-conspirator need not be charged, tried, or even identified, as long as the court finds the conspiracy and both memberships.
- E: Correct. This is the Bourjaily standard as codified: the judge decides by a preponderance, may consider the statement, and needs some corroborating evidence.
Rule: A statement by a party’s co-conspirator made during and in furtherance of the conspiracy is not hearsay when offered against that party. FRE 801(d)(2)(E). The court decides the preliminary facts under Rule 104(a) by a preponderance of the evidence and may consider the statement itself. Bourjaily v. United States. But the statement alone does not establish the existence of the conspiracy or the party’s participation in it. FRE 801(d)(2) (final sentence).

## Final 1 · Q17 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — United States v. Owens: identification despite memory loss
Correctional officer Fosse was beaten with a metal pipe and suffered a fractured skull. Three weeks later, from his hospital bed, he told FBI Agent Lund that inmate Corley attacked him and picked Corley’s photograph from an array.

At Corley’s trial, Fosse testifies that he clearly remembers identifying Corley to Agent Lund in the hospital, but that because of his head injury he now has no memory of seeing his attacker and cannot explain the basis for his identification. The prosecution then calls Agent Lund to recount Fosse’s identification. Corley objects on hearsay and Confrontation Clause grounds.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit Lund’s testimony, because Fosse testified and was subject to cross-examination about his prior identification, even though he cannot recall its basis.
B. Exclude Lund’s testimony, because Fosse’s identification was made to a law-enforcement officer and is therefore testimonial.
C. Exclude Lund’s testimony, because an identification made three weeks after the event is too remote in time.
D. Admit Lund’s testimony only to impeach Fosse’s testimony that he cannot remember his attacker.
E. Exclude Lund’s testimony, because Fosse’s memory loss makes him “unavailable,” and an unavailable declarant cannot be “subject to cross-examination.”

Answer: A
- A: Correct. This is Owens: a witness who takes the stand and answers questions is subject to cross-examination, even with memory loss.
- B: The identification is testimonial, but the Clause is satisfied because Fosse appears at trial and is subject to cross-examination.
- C: Rule 801(d)(1)(C) has no time limit. Delay goes to weight.
- D: The identification is substantive evidence under 801(d)(1)(C), not merely impeachment.
- E: Owens rejected this argument. Memory loss can be unavailability for Rule 804 purposes while the witness remains subject to cross for Rule 801(d)(1).
Rule: A testifying declarant’s prior statement identifying a person as someone the declarant perceived earlier is not hearsay if the declarant is subject to cross-examination about it. FRE 801(d)(1)(C). The Confrontation Clause and the rule are satisfied when the witness takes the stand and answers questions, even if memory loss limits the answers. United States v. Owens.

## Final 1 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(2): required case type
Bleeding from a stab wound in a store, Leah told an ER nurse, "I know I'm not going to make it. Ted did this." She died two days later. The proponent relies only on Rule 804(b)(2). Which additional fact, if true, would defeat the exception?

A. The case is a civil wrongful-death suit.
B. Leah lived for two days after she spoke.
C. Leah spoke to a nurse, not a police officer.
D. Ted is being tried only for armed robbery.
E. Ted denies that he stabbed Leah.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 804(b)(2) applies in a civil case as well as a homicide prosecution.
- B: The rule asks what she believed, not how fast she died. Compare Shepard, which turned on a lack of hopeless expectation.
- C: The rule doesn't limit who may hear the statement.
- D: Correct. Rule 804(b)(2) applies only in a homicide prosecution or a civil case. A robbery prosecution is neither.
- E: A denial goes to weight. It has no bearing on the exception's requirements.
Rule: Rule 804(b)(2) requires an unavailable declarant, a homicide prosecution or civil case, a belief that death was imminent, and a statement about its cause or circumstances (Shepard).

## Final 1 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(2): delay and questioning
In a civil suit arising from an intersection crash, a firefighter testifies that about 40 minutes after the collision she freed passenger Leah Moss from the wreck. Moss was trembling, sobbing, and bleeding from the scalp. When the firefighter asked, "What happened?", Moss cried, "The truck ran the red light. It never even slowed down!" Moss can't be located for trial.

Is the firefighter's testimony about Moss's words admissible?

A. Yes, as an excited utterance, because Moss was still under the stress of the crash.
B. Yes, as a present sense impression, because Moss described an event she herself had perceived.
C. No, because a statement made in answer to a question can't be an excited utterance.
D. No, because 40 minutes is too long for any of the spontaneous-statement exceptions.
E. Yes, but only if the plaintiff first shows Moss is unavailable under Rule 804(a).

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Rule 803(2) asks whether the declarant was still under the stress of excitement the startling event caused. Trapped, bleeding, and sobbing, Moss was. Time lapse and a simple question are factors, not bars. In this civil case, the Confrontation Clause isn't in play.
- B: Rule 803(1) requires a statement made while or immediately after the declarant perceived the event. Forty minutes is too long.
- C: A neutral question like "What happened?" doesn't by itself destroy spontaneity. The test is whether stress still dominated.
- D: Rule 803(2) has no fixed time limit. Its duration depends on how long the stress lasts.
- E: Rule 803 exceptions apply "regardless of whether the declarant is available."
Rule: A Rule 803(2) excited utterance must relate to a startling event and be made while the declarant is still under its stress. Delay and questioning count but aren't disqualifying. Rule 803(1) requires near-contemporaneity, not stress.

## Final 1 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(5): read into evidence, not an exhibit
Dock supervisor Weller saw a box truck pull away from a loading bay moments after a pallet of laptops was stolen. While the truck was still in view, she wrote its license plate number on the back of a shipping invoice.

At the thief’s trial eleven months later, Weller testifies that she wrote the number down accurately while looking at the truck, but that she cannot remember the number now, even after looking at the invoice. The prosecution moves to admit the invoice and hand it to the jury.

How should the court rule?

A. Permit the notation to be read into evidence, but do not receive the invoice itself as an exhibit unless the defense offers it.
B. Exclude the invoice, because a writing that fails to refresh a witness’s memory cannot be used for any purpose.
C. Admit the invoice as an exhibit, because it satisfies the recorded-recollection exception.
D. Exclude the invoice, because Weller remembers the theft, and the recorded-recollection exception applies only when the witness has no memory of the events.
E. Admit the invoice as a business record, because it was written on a company form during the workday.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. This is the final sentence of Rule 803(5).
- B: Failure to refresh is exactly what triggers Rule 803(5). Refreshing under Rule 612 and recorded recollection are separate tools.
- C: The rule permits the record to be read into evidence, but only the adverse party may introduce it as an exhibit.
- D: The standard is insufficient memory to testify fully and accurately about the matter recorded, here the plate number. Total amnesia about the events is not required.
- E: One supervisor’s ad hoc notation of a plate is not a record of a regularly conducted activity made as a regular practice. Rule 803(6) does not fit.
Rule: A recorded recollection is admissible if it is a record that (A) is on a matter the witness once knew about but now cannot recall well enough to testify fully and accurately; (B) was made or adopted by the witness when the matter was fresh in the witness’s memory; and (C) accurately reflects the witness’s knowledge. If admitted, the record may be read into evidence but may be received as an exhibit only if offered by an adverse party. FRE 803(5).

## Final 1 · Q21 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(8) plus Rule 805: a bystander inside a police report
In a civil suit over a highway collision, the plaintiff offers the investigating officer's report. The officer has since died. The report contains (1) the officer's measurements of 90 feet of skid marks behind the defendant's SUV, and (2) a line reading: "Witness (bystander, name unknown) stated the SUV was going 'at least 80.'" The defendant objects to the report.

What is the proper ruling?

A. Admit (1), but exclude (2) unless the bystander's statement fits its own exception.
B. Admit the whole report, because everything in a police report is a public record.
C. Exclude the whole report, because police reports are inadmissible in any case.
D. Admit (2) only, as the bystander's present sense impression recorded by the officer.
E. Exclude the whole report, because the dead officer can't be cross-examined.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Matters the officer observed under a legal duty to report are admissible under Rule 803(8)(A)(ii) in a civil case. The bystander had no duty to report, so his statement is a second layer that needs its own exception under Rule 805 (Johnson v. Lutz). Nothing shows when he spoke or under what stress.
- B: The public-records exception covers the officer's layer, not statements of outsiders recorded in the report.
- C: The criminal-case limit in Rule 803(8)(A)(ii) doesn't apply in civil cases.
- D: Nothing shows that the bystander spoke while or immediately after perceiving the event.
- E: The Confrontation Clause doesn't apply in civil cases, and Rule 803 exceptions don't depend on availability.
Rule: Rule 803(8) covers what the officer observed and recorded under a duty (in civil cases, or against the government in criminal cases). Statements by outsiders inside the record need their own exception under Rule 805 (Johnson v. Lutz).

## Final 1 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Whorton: nontestimonial statements and Roberts
Marisol Vega is found dead in her apartment. Two days before her death, she told her sister over coffee, “If anything happens to me, it was Trent. He said he'd kill me if I left.” At Trent's murder trial, the court admits the statement under the residual exception, finding sufficient guarantees of trustworthiness. Trent objects that admitting it violates the Confrontation Clause because Marisol can never be cross-examined.

How should the court rule on the constitutional objection?

A. Overrule it, because a murder victim's statements are always admissible against the person accused of killing her.
B. Overrule it, because the Clause reaches only testimonial statements, and this was a private remark to her sister.
C. Sustain it, because a statement accusing someone of a crime is testimonial whoever hears it.
D. Sustain it unless the prosecution proves that Trent killed Marisol to keep her from testifying.
E. Overrule it, because the residual exception's trustworthiness finding satisfies the reliability test of Ohio v. Roberts.

Answer: B
- A: No such rule exists. A victim's statement still needs a hearsay route, here Rule 807, and a testimonial one would face the Confrontation Clause unless Giles forfeiture applied.
- B: Correct. Since Crawford and Davis v. Washington, the Confrontation Clause reaches only testimonial statements, and Whorton v. Bockting confirmed that Ohio v. Roberts no longer governs nontestimonial ones. A private remark to a sister over coffee has no primary purpose of creating trial testimony, so only the hearsay rules apply.
- C: Crawford and Davis v. Washington tie “testimonial” to statements whose primary purpose is to create evidence for a prosecution, typically made to police or in formal settings. An informal remark to a family member isn't testimonial, even if it accuses someone.
- D: Forfeiture under Giles matters only if the statement is testimonial. Because this one isn't, the Clause doesn't apply and no forfeiture finding is needed.
- E: The result is right but the reason is outdated. Crawford abandoned Roberts' reliability test, and nontestimonial statements now face no Confrontation Clause test at all (Whorton).
Rule: The Confrontation Clause applies only to testimonial statements (Crawford; Davis v. Washington). Nontestimonial statements, such as private remarks to friends or family, are governed only by the hearsay rules; Ohio v. Roberts no longer applies (Whorton).

## Final 1 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Ohio v. Clark: a child’s statement to a teacher
J.T., a three-year-old, arrives at preschool with a bloodshot eye and red marks on his face. His teacher asks, “Who did this? What happened?” J.T. answers, “Jax did.” The teachers, who are required by state law to report suspected child abuse, call a hotline. Jax, the mother’s boyfriend, is charged with child abuse.

The court finds J.T. incompetent to testify. The prosecution offers the teacher’s testimony repeating J.T.’s statement, and Jax objects under the Confrontation Clause.

Which is the most accurate analysis?

A. The Confrontation Clause does not apply, because J.T. is incompetent and therefore could never have been a “witness” against Jax.
B. The statement is testimonial, because the teacher’s question, “Who did this?”, was directed at identifying a perpetrator.
C. The statement is admissible under the Confrontation Clause because it bears sufficient indicia of reliability.
D. It isn’t testimonial, because its primary purpose was to protect a young child in an informal exchange with teachers, not police.
E. The statement is testimonial, because mandatory-reporting laws make teachers agents of law enforcement for Confrontation Clause purposes.

Answer: D
- A: Competence to testify is not the test. The question is the primary purpose of the statement, and the analysis would be the same for an older child.
- B: Asking who caused the injuries is consistent with an emergency purpose: the teachers needed to know whether the child was safe to send home.
- C: Reliability is the overruled Ohio v. Roberts test. For nontestimonial statements, the Clause simply does not apply; for testimonial ones, reliability is not enough.
- D: Correct. This is the reasoning of Ohio v. Clark.
- E: Clark expressly rejected the argument that mandatory-reporting duties make teachers agents of law enforcement.
Rule: Statements to persons other than law enforcement officers are much less likely to be testimonial. A statement is testimonial only if, in light of all the circumstances, its primary purpose was to create an out-of-court substitute for trial testimony. Ohio v. Clark.

## Final 1 · Q24 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Bruton redaction: a unique description
Rory Tate and Eli Burke are tried jointly for a bank robbery, and neither testifies. The prosecution offers Burke's confession to police, redacted to remove Tate's name: “I drove. The other guy, the one with the scorpion tattoo on his neck, went in with the gun.” Tate has a scorpion tattoo on his neck, which the jury can see, and no other defendant has one. The court gives a limiting instruction.

Which statement is most accurate?

A. There is no violation, because the jury must link the description to Tate through evidence outside the confession.
B. There is a Bruton violation only if the prosecutor points out the tattoo in closing argument.
C. There is no violation, because Samia permits replacing a defendant's name with a neutral descriptor.
D. There is a Bruton violation, because the description identifies Tate as clearly as his name would.
E. There is no violation, because Burke's confession stops being testimonial once it has been redacted.

Answer: D
- A: Richardson's linkage exception covers confessions that become incriminating only through other trial evidence. Here the jury needs nothing but a glance at the defendant; the confession itself does the identifying.
- B: The violation lies in admitting the confession, not in how the prosecutor argues it. Stressing the tattoo in closing would make things worse, but it isn't required.
- C: Samia approved a neutral reference like “the other person,” which doesn't itself point to the defendant. A unique physical description that fits only Tate isn't neutral; it names him in all but name.
- D: Correct. Bruton bars a nontestifying codefendant's confession that facially incriminates the defendant. A description that fits only Tate, like the unique description in Harrington v. California, incriminates him as directly as his name. The error may still be harmless if the other evidence is overwhelming.
- E: Redaction doesn't change whether a statement is testimonial. A confession to police is testimonial, and that is why Bruton applies.
Rule: Bruton bars a nontestifying codefendant's confession that facially incriminates the defendant. Removing the name isn't enough if a unique description identifies him; a neutral reference may be enough (Samia), and a confession that incriminates only through linkage to other evidence is allowed (Richardson).

## Final 1 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 702(a): helpfulness and common knowledge
In which case is the proposed expert testimony most likely to be excluded as unhelpful under Rule 702(a)?

A. A trademark case, in which a survey expert will report the results of a properly designed consumer-confusion survey.
B. A drunk-driving case, in which a toxicologist will explain how blood-alcohol levels change in the hours after drinking.
C. A trademark case, in which an English professor will testify that the brand names Glo and Gloh sound confusingly alike.
D. A slip-and-fall case, in which an engineer will testify to friction tests on the wet tile where the plaintiff fell.
E. A housing-discrimination case, in which a marketing expert will testify how focus groups read all-white real estate ads.

Answer: C
- A: Properly designed consumer surveys are the standard expert evidence of likely confusion in trademark cases, because they measure how real consumers react.
- B: Alcohol absorption and elimination are scientific matters jurors don't know. Toxicology testimony plainly helps.
- C: Correct. Whether two words sound alike is something any juror can judge by listening. Rule 702(a) requires that the expertise help the jury, and courts exclude experts on matters of common knowledge, as the Ninth Circuit did with an English professor's opinion that two brand names sounded alike.
- D: Friction testing is beyond jurors' knowledge. The Eleventh Circuit reversed the exclusion of such testimony in a cruise-ship slip-and-fall case.
- E: How advertising messages are received by particular audiences is a proper subject for marketing expertise. The Seventh Circuit held its exclusion an abuse of discretion.
Rule: Rule 702(a) requires that the expert's specialized knowledge help the trier of fact. Under Ladd's test, quoted in the Advisory Committee Note, expertise is unhelpful when untrained jurors can decide the issue intelligently on their own.

## Final 1 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 703: opinion in, inadmissible basis presumptively out
At a civil-commitment hearing before a jury, the state's psychiatrist testifies that the respondent is dangerous. Her opinion rests largely on the respondent's mother's report that he punched her last month. Psychiatrists routinely rely on such family reports. The mother won't testify, and her report is inadmissible hearsay. On direct, the state asks the psychiatrist to tell the jury exactly what the mother said.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude the opinion, because it rests on inadmissible hearsay.
B. Allow the opinion and the mother's statement, because the doctor relied on it.
C. Allow the opinion, but generally not the mother's statement on direct.
D. Allow the mother's statement as substantive proof that he punched her.
E. Exclude the opinion unless the mother testifies at the hearing.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 703 lets experts rely on inadmissible facts of a kind experts in the field reasonably rely on.
- B: Reliance permits the opinion but doesn't open the door to disclosure by the proponent.
- C: Correct, though the jury will hear an opinion without its main support. Rule 703's last sentence creates a presumption against disclosure by the proponent of inadmissible bases. Melton explains why: a jury can't use the punch to evaluate the opinion without believing it happened. The opponent may bring the basis out on cross (Rule 705).
- D: Reliance doesn't make hearsay substantive.
- E: The opinion is admissible without her, based on what experts reasonably rely on.
Rule: Rule 703: an expert may rely on inadmissible facts of a kind reasonably relied on in the field. The proponent may disclose them to the jury only if their probative value in evaluating the opinion substantially outweighs their prejudicial effect (Melton). The opponent may explore them on cross.

## Final 1 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 901(b)(2)–(3): handwriting familiarity and comparison
In a fraud prosecution, the government must prove that Dana Voss signed 30 checks. A week before trial, the prosecutor gave a paralegal in her office a stack of Voss's admitted signatures to study so that she could identify his handwriting. The government calls the paralegal to testify, as a lay witness, that the signatures on the checks are Voss's. The admitted signatures are also in evidence. Voss objects.

Which ruling is best?

A. Allow the paralegal's opinion, because a lay witness may identify handwriting she has become familiar with by any means.
B. Exclude all handwriting evidence, because only a qualified document examiner may compare signatures.
C. Allow the paralegal's opinion, because familiarity with a signature is personal knowledge under Rule 602.
D. Exclude the paralegal's opinion, but let the jurors compare the checks with the authenticated signatures themselves.
E. Exclude the opinion and the comparison, because checks must be authenticated by a witness who saw Voss sign.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 901(b)(2) allows a nonexpert's opinion that handwriting is genuine only if it rests on familiarity “not acquired for the current litigation.” Studying samples to prepare for trial is exactly what the rule excludes.
- B: Rule 901(b)(3) expressly allows the trier of fact to compare the item with authenticated specimens. An expert is one option, not a requirement.
- C: Personal knowledge doesn't override Rule 901(b)(2)'s specific limit, which guards against witnesses manufactured for trial.
- D: Correct. The paralegal's familiarity was acquired for this litigation, so Rule 901(b)(2) doesn't support her opinion. But Rule 901(b)(3) allows comparison with an authenticated specimen by an expert witness or the trier of fact, so the jurors can compare the signatures themselves.
- E: An eyewitness to the signing is one way to authenticate (901(b)(1)), but Rule 901(b) lists several others, including comparison by the jury.
Rule: Rule 901(b)(2) allows lay handwriting identification only on familiarity not acquired for the current litigation. Rule 901(b)(3) allows comparison with authenticated specimens by an expert or the jury.

## Final 1 · Q28 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 1002: proving content vs. proving the event
In which situation does Rule 1002 require the original recording or a duplicate?

A. A detective who wasn't at the bank describes to the jury what the bank's surveillance video showed the robber doing.
B. A witness testifies to what the defendant told him during a phone call that the witness secretly recorded.
C. A neighbor testifies to the license plate number she read on the getaway car as it sped away.
D. A bank teller testifies from memory that the defendant robbed her, although the bank's camera recorded the robbery.
E. A payroll clerk testifies that the defendant worked at the bank last year, although payroll records exist.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. The detective knows nothing about the robbery except what the video shows, so his testimony proves the recording's content. Rule 1002 requires the original, or a duplicate under Rule 1003, unless Rule 1004 excuses it.
- B: The witness took part in the conversation and testifies to what he heard. That proves the event, not the recording's content, even though a recording exists.
- C: A license plate is at most an inscribed object, and the neighbor proves what she saw, not the content of a writing. Courts treat inscribed chattels flexibly, and a simple plate number doesn't raise the rule's concerns.
- D: The teller proves the robbery with her own memory of the event. The rule doesn't require the best evidence of an event, only an original when a party proves the content of a recording.
- E: Employment is a fact that exists independently of the payroll records. A witness with knowledge may prove it without producing them.
Rule: Rule 1002 requires an original only when a party proves the content of a writing, recording, or photograph: when the writing is itself at issue or the event is proved through it. A witness who perceived the event may testify from memory even if a recording exists.

## Final 1 · Q29 · Privileges — Lawyer-client: a lawyer acting as a business agent
Developer Ana Cruz hires attorney Ben Corwin, a family friend, to find buyers for her apartment complex, negotiate the price, and collect a broker's commission. Corwin gives no legal advice; another firm handles all the legal work. In emails to Corwin, Cruz describes mold problems in the building and estimates the repairs at $400,000. In a buyer's later fraud suit, the buyer seeks the emails, and Cruz claims the lawyer-client privilege.

How should the court rule?

A. Uphold the privilege, but only for emails sent after Cruz formally retained Corwin in writing.
B. Uphold the privilege, because communications between a client and a licensed lawyer are privileged whatever his role.
C. Uphold the privilege, because Cruz intended the emails to stay confidential.
D. Reject the privilege, because communications about the physical condition of property are facts, not communications.
E. Reject the privilege, because Corwin was acting as a business agent, not providing professional legal services.

Answer: E
- A: No formal retention is required, and prospective clients are covered. But a written engagement wouldn't help, because Corwin's role wasn't legal.
- B: The privilege protects only communications made to facilitate professional legal services. A lawyer acting as a broker, lobbyist, or business agent isn't providing legal services, so his license doesn't matter.
- C: Confidentiality is necessary but not sufficient. The communication must also be made to obtain legal services from the lawyer in that capacity.
- D: Cruz's emails are communications, and if they had been made to get legal advice, the privilege would protect them even though the mold itself could be proved another way. The problem is the purpose, not the subject.
- E: Correct. One of the privilege's common elements is that the communication be made to obtain professional legal services. Corwin was hired to sell the property and earn a commission; another firm did the legal work. As in People v. Gionis, the question is whether the lawyer was consulted in his professional capacity, and here he wasn't.
Rule: The lawyer-client privilege protects only confidential communications made to obtain professional legal services. A lawyer acting as a business agent, broker, or friend isn't acting in a professional capacity (People v. Gionis).

## Final 1 · Q30 · Privileges — Government lawyers: a circuit split
A federal grand jury investigating corruption subpoenas a state governor's former chief counsel to testify about conversations with the governor regarding state contracts. The governor's office asserts the lawyer-client privilege. The prosecutor argues that government officials can't use the privilege to withhold evidence from a federal criminal investigation.

What is the most accurate statement of the law?

A. The privilege never applies to government lawyers, who serve the public.
B. The privilege always applies, exactly as it does for private clients.
C. Every circuit holds that the privilege yields to a federal grand jury.
D. The federal circuits are divided on whether it yields to a federal grand jury subpoena.
E. The Supreme Court settled the question in Swidler & Berlin in 1998.

Answer: D
- A: No court takes this categorical view. Government entities have the privilege in many settings.
- B: Several circuits limit it in federal criminal investigations.
- C: The Second Circuit disagreed in the Rowland grand jury case.
- D: Correct, though it gives no single rule. The Second Circuit (In re Grand Jury Investigation, the Rowland case) refused to abrogate the privilege because the investigation was criminal, acknowledging conflict with the Seventh Circuit and tension with the Eighth and D.C. Circuits (the Lindsey case). The outline treats this as an unsettled syllabus topic.
- E: Swidler & Berlin held that the privilege survives the client's death. It didn't address government lawyers.
Rule: Government lawyer-client privilege in federal criminal investigations is unsettled. The Second Circuit (Rowland) upheld it, and the Seventh, Eighth, and D.C. Circuits have limited it. Swidler & Berlin concerns survival after death.

## Final 2 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Rule 606(b): misconduct reported before the verdict
On the second day of deliberations in a federal fraud trial, before any verdict, the foreperson sends the judge a note: “Juror 9 smells of alcohol and keeps falling asleep. We can’t deliberate with him.” Defense counsel argues that Rule 606(b) prevents the judge from questioning any juror about what happens in the jury room. What should the judge do?

A. Declare a mistrial at once, because an intoxicated juror denies the defendant a competent jury under the Sixth Amendment.
B. Tell the jury to keep deliberating, because Tanner held that juror intoxication is an internal matter.
C. Question only the foreperson, because Rule 606(b) lets a juror report another juror’s misconduct but not her own.
D. Wait until after the verdict, then decide whether the note shows extraneous prejudicial information.
E. Look into it now, questioning jurors as needed, because 606(b) limits only inquiries into a verdict’s validity.

Answer: E
- A: A mistrial may be unnecessary. The judge can investigate first and replace an impaired juror with an alternate. Nothing requires a mistrial on an unverified note.
- B: Tanner held that juror testimony about intoxication can’t impeach a verdict after it is returned. It pointed to pre-verdict reports like this one as a reason that rule is tolerable.
- C: Rule 606(b) draws no line between testifying about oneself and about others, and it doesn’t apply before a verdict at all. The judge may question whichever jurors she needs to resolve the report.
- D: Waiting would make things worse: after the verdict, 606(b) and Tanner would bar juror testimony about the drinking. The time to act is now.
- E: Correct. 606(b) applies “during an inquiry into the validity of a verdict or indictment.” Before the verdict, the judge may investigate and, if necessary, replace the juror with an alternate. Tanner listed exactly this safeguard: jurors can report misconduct before they return a verdict.
Rule: Rule 606(b) restricts juror testimony only in an inquiry into the validity of a verdict or indictment. Before the verdict, the court can investigate reports of misconduct, one of the safeguards Tanner relied on.

## Final 2 · Q2 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Rule 601: state competency law in diversity cases
In a federal diversity suit governed by state contract law, Earl Wynn sues the estate of his late business partner for money he says the partner orally promised him. The state has a Dead Man's statute that bars an interested party from testifying about transactions with a decedent in a suit against the decedent's estate. The estate objects to Wynn testifying about the conversation.

What is the most likely ruling?

A. He may testify, because Rule 601 declares every person competent.
B. He may testify, because Dead Man's statutes don't bind federal courts.
C. He may testify, but the jury must be told that he is interested.
D. He may not, because state competency law governs this state-law claim.
E. He may not, because parties are never competent in suits against estates.

Answer: D
- A: This is Rule 601's first sentence, which makes it tempting. But the second sentence adds: "in a civil case, state law governs the witness's competency regarding a claim or defense for which state law supplies the rule of decision."
- B: Rule 601 expressly incorporates state competency law in these cases.
- C: Interest goes to credibility under federal law generally, but here state competency law controls.
- D: Correct, though modern evidence law disfavors such rules. A Dead Man's statute is a state competency rule, and Rule 601 applies it to a state-law claim in federal court.
- E: Too broad. Only state law, applied through Rule 601, bars him here, and only as to transactions with the decedent.
Rule: Rule 601: every person is competent unless a rule provides otherwise, but in civil cases state law governs competency on claims or defenses governed by state law (for example, Dead Man's statutes). Rule 501 does the same for privilege.

## Final 2 · Q3 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 104(a) vs. 104(b) vs. 1008: who decides
In a will contest, the contestant says the decedent signed a later will revoking the earlier one, but no copy of the later will can be found. The contestant offers a paralegal's testimony describing its terms. The proponent of the earlier will raises several preliminary questions.

Which of these questions does the jury, rather than the judge, ultimately decide?

A. Whether the contestant has shown the original was lost without bad faith.
B. Whether the paralegal is qualified to identify the decedent's handwriting.
C. Whether the decedent's statements to the paralegal are privileged.
D. Whether the decedent ever signed a later will at all.
E. Whether the paralegal's testimony fits a hearsay exception.

Answer: D
- A: Under Rule 1008, the court decides whether the conditions for admitting other evidence of content (such as loss without bad faith under Rule 1004) are met.
- B: Qualification of a witness is a Rule 104(a) question for the judge.
- C: Privilege is decided by the judge under Rule 104(a), and the judge is bound by privilege law even in making that decision.
- D: Correct, though this may feel like the most important question to leave to laypeople. Rule 1008(a) gives the jury the question whether an asserted writing ever existed. The judge only screens the Rule 1004 conditions. Because the existence of the later will decides the case, letting the judge resolve it would take the case from the jury.
- E: Hearsay foundations are Rule 104(a) questions decided by the judge by a preponderance (Bourjaily).
Rule: The judge decides admissibility questions under Rule 104(a): qualification, privilege, hearsay foundations, and the Rule 1004 conditions. Under Rule 1008, the jury decides whether a writing ever existed, which version is the original, and whether other evidence of content is accurate.

## Final 2 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 407: “subsequent” means after the injury
A tenant is hurt when her apartment building’s elevator drops two floors. She sues the landlord for negligence. Which of the following may she offer to prove the landlord was negligent?

A. An inspector’s report, delivered to the landlord three months before the accident, recommending new elevator brakes.
B. A new building policy, adopted after the accident, requiring monthly elevator inspections.
C. A warning sign the landlord posted in the elevator after the accident, offered to show that a warning was needed.
D. Testimony that the landlord fired the elevator-maintenance company the day after the accident.
E. The landlord’s replacement of the brake assembly the week after the accident.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. 407 covers measures taken after the injury that “would have made an earlier injury or harm less likely to occur.” A report the landlord received before the accident isn’t a subsequent measure at all. It shows notice of the danger, and 407 has nothing to say about it.
- B: Policy changes count as measures too. 407 isn’t limited to physical repairs.
- C: 407 expressly bars subsequent measures offered to prove “a need for a warning or instruction.”
- D: Firing the contractor after the accident is a measure that would have made the injury less likely, so Rule 407 bars it to prove negligence.
- E: This is the classic subsequent remedial measure. It can’t prove negligence, though it might come in to impeach or to prove disputed ownership, control, or feasibility.
Rule: Rule 407 applies only to measures taken after the injury. Pre-accident reports, inspections, and recommendations aren’t covered and may show notice.

## Final 2 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 408: no impeachment by inconsistency
Corvin Foods sued Delta Freight for spoiled produce, claiming the shipment arrived on March 20, three weeks late. At a settlement meeting, Delta's operations chief said, "Our logs show the trucks reached your dock March 2. We'll offer $10,000 just to end this." At trial the same operations chief testifies that the trucks arrived "around March 18." Corvin offers testimony about the settlement-meeting remark to impeach him.

Is the remark admissible?

A. No, because Rule 408 bars negotiation statements offered to impeach by prior inconsistent statement.
B. Yes, because Rule 408 bars such evidence only when it is offered to prove liability, not to impeach.
C. Yes, because the remark is a statement of fact, and Rule 408 covers only offers of money.
D. Yes, because it is a statement of Delta's agent made within the scope of his employment.
E. Yes, under Rule 408(b), because it shows that the operations chief is biased in Delta's favor.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Rule 408(a) bars statements made during compromise negotiations offered to prove or disprove a disputed claim, "or to impeach by a prior inconsistent statement or a contradiction." The 2006 amendment closed the impeachment route.
- B: This was the pre-2006 view in some courts. The text now expressly bars impeachment by inconsistency or contradiction.
- C: Rule 408(a)(2) covers "conduct or a statement made during compromise negotiations," not just the offer itself.
- D: Rule 801(d)(2)(D) only answers the hearsay objection. Rule 408 is a separate bar.
- E: Proving that a witness changed his story is impeachment by inconsistency, not bias. Bias would be something like a settlement that gives a witness a stake in the outcome.
Rule: Rule 408(a) bars compromise offers and statements made in negotiations, on behalf of any party, to prove or disprove the validity or amount of a disputed claim or to impeach by inconsistency or contradiction. Rule 408(b) permits other purposes, such as bias.

## Final 2 · Q6 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 410: plea talks that ended in a guilty plea
During plea negotiations with an Assistant U.S. Attorney, Mark Dale said, "I knew the brake parts failed testing when I sold them." The negotiations produced a guilty plea to wire fraud, which the court accepted. Dale was sentenced and didn't appeal. A trucking company that bought the parts now sues Dale for fraud and offers his statement from the plea negotiations.

Is the statement admissible?

A. No, because Rule 410 bars plea-discussion statements made to a prosecutor in any later case.
B. No, because Rule 408 bars statements made while negotiating to resolve a disputed claim.
C. Yes, because Rule 410 doesn't protect plea talks that ended in a guilty plea that still stands.
D. No, because only the judgment of conviction, not the statements behind it, may be used later.
E. Yes, but only to impeach Dale if he testifies that he didn't know about the test failures.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 410(a)(4) is limited to discussions that "did not result in a guilty plea or ... resulted in a later-withdrawn guilty plea."
- B: Plea bargaining is governed by Rule 410, and Rule 408(a)(2)'s criminal-case clause concerns regulatory claims. Neither protects these statements.
- C: Correct. Rule 410(a)(4) protects statements in plea discussions with a prosecutor only if they didn't result in a guilty plea or resulted in a plea later withdrawn. Dale's plea stands, so the rule's policy of protecting failed negotiations doesn't apply. The statement comes in against him under Rule 801(d)(2)(A).
- D: Nothing in Rule 410 limits a later case to the judgment.
- E: Because Rule 410 doesn't apply at all, the statement is admissible substantively, not merely to impeach.
Rule: Rule 410 bars, against the defendant, withdrawn guilty pleas, nolo pleas, and statements in plea proceedings or in plea discussions with a prosecutor that didn't end in a guilty plea or ended in one later withdrawn. A plea that stands isn't protected.

## Final 2 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Other acts to prove an employer's notice
A delivery driver for Route One Logistics rammed Grace Toll's car in a road-rage incident. Toll sues Route One for negligent retention, alleging that it kept the driver on despite knowing he was dangerous. She offers three customer complaints, received by Route One's safety manager during the prior year, each describing the driver tailgating and threatening other motorists.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude them, because Rule 404(b) bars other acts offered to show the driver's propensity.
B. Exclude them, because character may be proved only by reputation or opinion testimony.
C. Admit them, but only if the driver testifies and denies the earlier incidents.
D. Admit them, because they show Route One knew of the risk, which is not a propensity use.
E. Exclude them as hearsay, because they are customers' out-of-court statements.

Answer: D
- A: The complaints aren't offered to show the driver acted in character on the day of the crash. They show what Route One knew.
- B: In negligent retention, the employee's dangerousness is an essential element, so Rule 405(b) allows specific instances. And the employer's notice isn't a character use at all.
- C: Admissibility doesn't depend on the driver's testimony.
- D: Correct. Negligent retention requires proof that the employer knew or should have known the employee was dangerous. Complaints received by the employer prove notice, a non-character purpose. To the extent the driver's dangerousness is an element, Rule 405(b) permits specific instances. Rule 403 still applies.
- E: Offered to show that Route One received complaints, the statements aren't offered for their truth. That is notice, a non-hearsay use.
Rule: Other acts offered to show what a party knew (notice) are a non-character use. When character is an essential element, as in negligent retention or entrustment, Rule 405(b) permits specific instances.

## Final 2 · Q8 · Character Evidence — State v. Kirsch: no Rule 413 analog
In a state whose evidence rules mirror the federal rules except that it has no counterparts to Rules 413–415, a youth swim coach is charged with sexually assaulting a 14-year-old team member. The prosecution offers testimony from two former team members that he groomed and assaulted them in the same way years earlier. It argues the evidence shows his (1) motive (sexual attraction to teenage swimmers), (2) intent, and (3) common plan. Under the reasoning of State v. Kirsch, how should the court rule?

A. Admit it as evidence of a common plan, because he groomed and assaulted each victim in the same way.
B. Admit it under Rule 413, because state courts must follow the federal rules on sexual-assault propensity evidence.
C. Admit it as evidence of motive, because sexual attraction explains why he committed the charged assault.
D. Admit it to show intent, because a defendant who denies the assault puts his intent in issue.
E. Exclude it, because each theory rests on propensity, and similar methods show a pattern, not a plan.

Answer: E
- A: Similar methods show a pattern, which Kirsch held insufficient. A true plan requires each act to be a constituent part of a larger scheme.
- B: The Federal Rules of Evidence bind federal courts. States choose their own rules, and this state has no 413 counterpart.
- C: Kirsch saw through this label: the “motive” is just the propensity itself, described differently.
- D: When the defense is that the act never happened, intent isn’t genuinely disputed. Kirsch held that intent, like motive and plan, was propensity under another name.
- E: Correct. Kirsch rejected all three around-the-box theories. A desire to have sex with young girls “is proof of propensity, not motive.” The intent theory was “indistinguishable” from propensity. And “a pattern or systematic course of conduct is insufficient to establish a plan.” In federal court, by contrast, Rule 413 or 414 would let the evidence in for propensity, subject to 403.
Rule: Without 413–415, motive, intent, and plan labels can’t disguise sexual propensity (Kirsch). A plan requires an overall scheme, not a pattern.

## Final 2 · Q9 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(a)(2)(A): the trait must be pertinent
