Oscar Keene is charged with federal income-tax evasion. In his defense, he offers: (1) a neighbor's testimony that Keene has a reputation as a peaceful, gentle man; and (2) a business associate's opinion that Keene is honest and law-abiding. The government objects to both.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit both, because a criminal defendant may offer evidence of any good character trait.
B. Exclude both, because character evidence is inadmissible to prove conduct on a particular occasion.
C. Admit (2) only, because honesty is pertinent to tax evasion and peacefulness is not.
D. Admit (1) only, because reputation is a more reliable form of proof than opinion.
E. Exclude both unless Keene first takes the stand and testifies in his own defense.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 404(a)(2)(A) admits only a "pertinent" trait, one relevant to the crime charged.
- B: Rule 404(a)(2)(A) is an exception: the accused may offer a pertinent trait to show action in conformity.
- C: Correct. Under Rule 404(a)(2)(A), the accused may offer a pertinent trait. Honesty and law-abidingness bear on tax evasion, which is a crime of deceit. Peacefulness doesn't, so it is irrelevant. Both items use proper forms under Rule 405(a).
- D: Rule 405(a) allows reputation or opinion equally. And peacefulness isn't pertinent to tax evasion.
- E: The accused may offer character witnesses without testifying.
Rule: Under Rule 404(a)(2)(A), an accused may offer evidence of a pertinent trait, one relevant to the charged crime, in reputation or opinion form (Rule 405(a)). Doing so lets the prosecution rebut it.

## Final 2 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — United States v. Abel: bias is never collateral
In an armed-robbery trial, a prosecution eyewitness identifies the defendant. On cross, defense counsel asks, “Isn’t it true you’ve been dating the store owner’s daughter for the past year?” The witness answers, “No.” The defense later calls the daughter to testify that she and the witness have been dating for a year. The prosecution objects. How should the court rule?

A. Sustain the objection, because Rule 608(b) bars extrinsic evidence of a witness’s specific conduct.
B. Overrule the objection, but only if the daughter testifies in the form of opinion or reputation.
C. Overrule the objection, because bias may be proved by extrinsic evidence after the witness denies it.
D. Sustain the objection, because the defense needed the court’s permission before calling a bias witness.
E. Sustain the objection, because the defense must take the witness’s answer on a collateral matter.

Answer: C
- A: 608(b) applies only to conduct offered to show character for truthfulness. Bias works like 404(b) motive, around the box, so 608(b) is irrelevant.
- B: Bias isn’t a character trait, so 608(a)’s opinion-or-reputation limit doesn’t apply. The daughter may testify to the specific fact of the relationship.
- C: Correct. A relationship giving the witness a reason to shade testimony is bias, a non-character mode of impeachment. Abel holds that bias may be proved by extrinsic evidence, and 608(b)’s extrinsic-evidence bar doesn’t apply because the defense isn’t attacking character for truthfulness (Problem 4.8(2)).
- D: No such rule exists. Once the witness denies the relationship on cross, bias may be proved by extrinsic evidence (United States v. Abel).
- E: The common-law collateral-matter rule never reached bias. A motive to favor the prosecution’s side bears directly on how much weight to give the identification.
Rule: Bias impeachment is non-character and never collateral, so extrinsic evidence is allowed after a denial (Abel).

## Final 2 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 608(b)(2): cross of a character witness; no extrinsic proof
In a bank-fraud trial, the defense attacks the character for truthfulness of the government's cooperating witness, Ivy Stone. The government then calls Stone's pastor, who testifies that in his opinion Stone is a truthful person. On cross, defense counsel asks the pastor, "Did you know that last year Ivy Stone lied about her income on a mortgage application?" The pastor says he did not. The defense then calls the loan officer to testify that the application was false.

Which ruling is correct?

A. The question is improper, because specific instances may be raised only with the witness herself.
B. The question is proper, and so is the loan officer's testimony, because the pastor opened the door.
C. The question is improper, because Stone's truthfulness can't be supported until she is convicted.
D. The question is proper, but the loan officer's testimony is barred extrinsic evidence.
E. Both are improper, because a mortgage application has nothing to do with a person's truthfulness.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 608(b)(2) expressly allows inquiry into specific instances of another witness's conduct when cross-examining a witness who has testified about that witness's character for truthfulness.
- B: Rule 608(b) bars extrinsic evidence of specific instances offered to attack or support character for truthfulness, no matter who opened the door. The examiner takes the answer.
- C: Rule 608(a) permits rehabilitation once character for truthfulness has been attacked, and the defense attacked it.
- D: Correct. The question is allowed under Rule 608(b)(2): it tests the basis of the pastor's opinion, and lying on a loan application is probative of untruthfulness. Rule 608(b) bars extrinsic proof of the act, so the defense must accept the pastor's answer.
- E: A false statement on a financial application is a lie, which is the core of what bears on character for truthfulness.
Rule: Under Rule 608(b), the court may allow cross-examination about specific instances probative of truthfulness. That includes those of a witness whose character the cross-examined witness has vouched for. Extrinsic evidence of those instances is barred.

## Final 2 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609(a)(2): deception element, no balancing
Lana Pierce is on trial for assault and testifies that the victim attacked her. The prosecution offers to impeach her with her conviction three years ago for "theft by deception," a misdemeanor whose elements require obtaining property by creating a false impression. Pierce argues that the conviction is so prejudicial it should be excluded, and that theft isn't usually a crime of dishonesty.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because theft offenses are not crimes of dishonesty.
B. Exclude it, because a misdemeanor can't impeach a criminal defendant.
C. Admit it only if its probative value outweighs the prejudice to Pierce.
D. Admit it without balancing, because its elements required deception.
E. Admit it, but only if Pierce first denies the conviction on cross.

Answer: D
- A: Usually true for simple theft, which is why this is tempting. But this offense's elements require deception, which is what Rule 609(a)(2) asks about.
- B: Rule 609(a)(2) applies "for any crime regardless of the punishment."
- C: That is Rule 609(a)(1)(B)'s balance for felonies. Crimen falsi bypasses it.
- D: Correct, though it is prejudicial and unrelated to assault. Under Rule 609(a)(2), the court looks to the elements (2006 ACN). Because establishing them required proving a dishonest act or false statement, the conviction "must be admitted." Rule 403 doesn't apply.
- E: Convictions are proved by the record or the witness's admission. They aren't limited to cases where the witness denies them.
Rule: Rule 609(a)(2) requires admission, with no Rule 403 balancing, of any conviction whose elements required proof of a dishonest act or false statement. The label "theft" doesn't control; the elements do. Rule 609(b) still applies to convictions more than 10 years old.

## Final 2 · Q13 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 412(b)(2): reputation the plaintiff placed in controversy
In her sexual-harassment suit, Lena Brooks testifies on direct that she is "a very private, modest person" who was "deeply humiliated" by her supervisor's crude remarks. Fifteen days before trial, the employer had moved to offer coworkers' testimony that Brooks has a reputation at work for openly discussing her sex life, conditioned on her putting her modesty at issue.

What is the most accurate statement?

A. It is inadmissible, because Rule 412 never permits reputation evidence about a victim.
B. It is admissible under Rule 403 alone, because Brooks opened the door.
C. It may come in, but only if it passes Rule 412's reverse balancing test.
D. It is inadmissible, because the motion had to be filed 30 days before trial.
E. It is admissible without limit, because Rule 412 doesn't protect a plaintiff.

Answer: C
- A: In civil cases, Rule 412(b)(2) admits reputation evidence when "the alleged victim has placed it in controversy."
- B: Opening the door satisfies the reputation condition but doesn't replace Rule 412(b)(2)'s balancing test, which tilts against admission.
- C: Correct, though it means her own testimony may expose her to this evidence. By claiming modesty, Brooks may have placed her reputation in controversy. Even so, the evidence must clear Rule 412(b)(2)'s reverse balance. Rule 412(c) requires a motion at least 14 days before trial and a sealed hearing, and 15 days met that.
- D: Rule 412(c)(1) requires 14 days, not 30.
- E: Rule 412 applies in civil proceedings involving alleged sexual misconduct, whoever is the plaintiff.
Rule: In civil cases under Rule 412(b)(2), evidence of a victim's sexual behavior or predisposition requires that probative value substantially outweigh harm to the victim and unfair prejudice. Reputation is admissible only if the victim placed it in controversy. Rule 412(c) procedures apply.

## Final 2 · Q14 · Definition of Hearsay — Bridges: circumstantial proof of knowledge
In a civil false-imprisonment suit against a ranch owner, the plaintiff's sister testifies that the day the plaintiff returned home, the plaintiff described the inside of the ranch's locked tack room: a green stove, a stuffed owl, and a cracked mirror. Other evidence shows the room looks exactly like that. The plaintiff denies having any other way to know. The statement is offered to show she had been in the room. Is it hearsay?

A. Yes, because it is an out-of-court statement describing facts about the room.
B. No, because it is offered to show what she knew, not to prove how the room looks.
C. Yes, because a party can't offer her own out-of-court statement.
D. No, because a description of a room is not an assertion under Rule 801(a).
E. Yes, unless the sister personally saw the inside of the tack room herself.

Answer: B
- A: It is an out-of-court statement, but its value doesn't depend on the truth of what it asserts. Other evidence proves what the room looks like.
- B: Correct. As in Bridges, the words are circumstantial evidence of knowledge she could only have gained by being there. They aren't offered for their truth under Rule 801(c)(2).
- C: That limit applies to Rule 801(d)(2). It doesn't affect whether a statement is offered for its truth.
- D: A description is an assertion. The reason it isn't hearsay is the purpose for offering it.
- E: The sister needs personal knowledge only of what was said. She need not have seen the room.
Rule: Words offered as circumstantial evidence of what the speaker knew aren't offered for their truth, so they aren't hearsay (Bridges).

## Final 2 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — A party can't offer its own statement under 801(d)(2)
Ray Ostrom is charged with arson. The prosecution's case doesn't mention anything Ostrom said. In the defense case, Ostrom doesn't testify. Instead he calls the arresting detective to repeat what Ostrom told him at the arrest: "I was at my mother's house all night. I never went near that building." The prosecution objects that this is hearsay.

How should the court rule?

A. Sustain it, because a party's own statement is admissible only when offered against him.
B. Overrule it, because it is a statement of a party, which is not hearsay.
C. Overrule it, because the rule of completeness entitles the jury to hear his side.
D. Overrule it, because Ostrom is available to testify and could be cross-examined.
E. Overrule it, because it reveals Ostrom's then-existing state of mind about the fire.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Rule 801(d)(2) applies only to a statement "offered against an opposing party." A defendant offering his own exculpatory statement is offering hearsay, and none of the exceptions fits.
- B: The exclusion applies only when a statement is used against its maker, on the theory that he can't complain he couldn't cross-examine himself.
- C: Rule 106 applies only when an adverse party has introduced part of a statement. The prosecution introduced nothing.
- D: Availability doesn't make hearsay admissible. Ostrom can testify himself, subject to cross.
- E: "I was at my mother's house" describes a past fact, not a then-existing state of mind.
Rule: Rule 801(d)(2) admits a party's statement only when the opponent offers it. A party's self-serving out-of-court statement is hearsay unless an exception or Rule 106 (once the opponent introduces part) applies.

## Final 2 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 806: impeaching a co-conspirator declarant
At Veltri’s trial for securities fraud, the court admits, as a co-conspirator statement, an email from Pask to another conspirator: “Veltri signed off on the fake revenue numbers.” Pask does not testify.

Veltri then offers (1) a certified record of Pask’s wire-fraud conviction from four years ago and (2) testimony from Pask’s former assistant that, a month after the email, Pask told her, “Veltri never knew about the revenue numbers.” Pask was never asked about this later statement because he never testified. The prosecution objects to both.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit the conviction, but exclude the later statement, because Pask was never given an opportunity to explain or deny it.
B. Admit the later statement as substantive evidence that Veltri lacked knowledge, but exclude the conviction as unfairly prejudicial.
C. Admit both to attack Pask’s credibility, even though Pask had no opportunity to explain or deny the inconsistent statement.
D. Exclude the conviction, because a non-testifying declarant’s credibility may be attacked only with evidence of inconsistent statements.
E. Exclude both, because Rule 806 applies only to hearsay, and a co-conspirator statement is by definition not hearsay.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 806 dispenses with the opportunity to explain or deny, because the declarant is not on the stand.
- B: The later statement is impeachment, not substantive evidence, unless an exception applies. The wire-fraud conviction is automatically admissible under Rule 609(a)(2), with no balancing.
- C: Correct. Rule 806 allows both forms of impeachment and waives the foundation requirement.
- D: Any impeachment that would be proper against a live witness is proper against the declarant, including convictions under Rule 609.
- E: Rule 806 expressly applies to statements under 801(d)(2)(C), (D), and (E).
Rule: When a hearsay statement, or a statement described in Rule 801(d)(2)(C), (D), or (E), has been admitted, the declarant’s credibility may be attacked by any evidence that would be admissible if the declarant had testified. The court may admit evidence of the declarant’s inconsistent statement or conduct regardless of when it occurred or whether the declarant had an opportunity to explain or deny it. FRE 806.

## Final 2 · Q17 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(1)(C): nod, memory loss, and Owens
After a beating that left him unable to speak, Ray Choi was shown a six-photo array in the hospital. When the detective pointed to Kyle Dorn's photo and asked, "Is this the man?", Choi nodded firmly. At Dorn's trial, Choi testifies that he remembers nodding at a photo, but he no longer remembers his attacker's face or why he picked that photo. The prosecution calls the detective to describe the nod. Dorn objects on hearsay and Confrontation Clause grounds.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit it, because a nod is nonverbal conduct and so is never a statement under Rule 801(a).
B. Exclude it, because Choi's memory loss makes him unavailable for cross-examination about it.
C. Exclude it, because an identification to a detective is testimonial and Choi can't be cross-examined.
D. Admit it as a prior identification by a witness who testifies.
E. Admit it only as a recorded recollection, read to the jury but not received as an exhibit.

Answer: D
- A: Nonverbal conduct is a statement if the person intended it as an assertion, and a nod answering "Is this the man?" is assertive.
- B: In United States v. Owens, the Court held that a witness who testifies and answers questions is "subject to cross-examination" even if he can't remember the basis for the identification.
- C: When the declarant testifies at trial and submits to cross, the Confrontation Clause is satisfied (California v. Green; Owens). It guarantees an opportunity for effective cross, not cross that succeeds.
- D: Correct. The nod is a statement because Choi intended it as an assertion. Rule 801(d)(1)(C) excludes from hearsay a testifying declarant's prior identification of a person he perceived earlier. Under Owens, memory loss doesn't defeat "subject to cross-examination," and the Confrontation Clause is satisfied.
- E: Rule 803(5) requires a record the witness made or adopted when fresh and vouches for. A nod isn't a record.
Rule: Rule 801(d)(1)(C) admits a testifying witness's earlier identification of a person, even if the witness now can't remember the basis (United States v. Owens). Conduct intended as an assertion is a statement under Rule 801(a).

## Final 2 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(3) (2024): corroboration and the totality
Sutter is charged with a bank robbery committed by a lone gunman with a sawed-off shotgun. Sutter calls Rhee, who shared a jail cell last year with Kirby, now deceased. Rhee will testify that Kirby told him: “I’m the one who did the Elm Street bank job. Used the sawed-off I keep under my mattress.”

A sawed-off shotgun was found under Kirby’s mattress after his death. But Kirby was a notorious jailhouse braggart who regularly claimed credit for high-profile crimes to build status, and a bank surveillance image shows that the robber was several inches taller than Kirby. The government objects.

Under the current version of Rule 804(b)(3), how should the court evaluate Kirby’s statement?

A. Exclude it, because statements to cellmates are never against interest, since inmates commonly boast to each other without fear of prosecution.
B. Consider only the circumstances in which Kirby made the statement, such as his bragging habit, and disregard the shotgun and surveillance evidence, which bear on the statement’s truth rather than its trustworthiness.
C. Admit it, because Kirby is unavailable and his statement exposed him to criminal liability; corroboration goes only to weight.
D. Admit it, because the corroboration requirement applies only to statements offered by the prosecution against the accused.
E. Admit it only if corroborating circumstances clearly indicate its trustworthiness, considering both how it was made and the shotgun and surveillance evidence.

Answer: E
- A: There is no categorical rule. Whether a reasonable person would make the statement only if true is a fact-specific question; a boasting context bears on it but does not settle it.
- B: This was one side of the pre-2024 split. The amended rule requires considering independent evidence that supports or undermines the statement.
- C: Corroboration is an admissibility requirement decided by the judge, not merely a matter of weight.
- D: Since 2010, corroboration is required for statements against penal interest offered by either side in a criminal case.
- E: Correct. This tracks the 2024 text of Rule 804(b)(3)(B).
Rule: A statement against penal interest offered in a criminal case must be “supported by corroborating circumstances that clearly indicate its trustworthiness, after considering the totality of circumstances under which it was made and any evidence that supports or undermines it.” FRE 804(b)(3)(B) (as amended 2024).

## Final 2 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Shepard: state of mind or accusation of a past act
Dr. Halloway is charged with murdering his wife, Corinne, by poisoning her with a compound found in his laboratory. The defense contends that Corinne committed suicide. Two weeks before her death, while bedridden but improving, Corinne asked her nurse to bring a bottle of whiskey from Dr. Halloway’s closet. After sipping it, she said it tasted strange, then said: “Dr. Halloway has poisoned me.”

The prosecution concedes that Corinne did not believe she was about to die. It offers the statement only to show her state of mind: that she had the will to live and was not suicidal.

What is the best ruling?

A. Admit the statement under Rule 803(3) with a limiting instruction, because it is offered only to show Corinne’s then-existing state of mind.
B. Admit the statement under Rule 803(4), because it was made to a nurse and identified the cause of Corinne’s symptoms.
C. Admit the statement as a dying declaration, because Corinne died within two weeks of making it.
D. Exclude the statement, because a homicide victim’s statements are admissible only under the dying-declaration exception.
E. Exclude it, because its real force is as an accusation of a past act, which Rule 803(3) excludes and an instruction can’t cure.

Answer: E
- A: This is the argument Shepard rejected. The forbidden inference from a backward-looking accusation overwhelms the permitted state-of-mind use.
- B: The statement was not made for diagnosis or treatment, and who caused the poisoning is not reasonably pertinent to treating it.
- C: A dying declaration requires that the declarant believe death is imminent, and the prosecution concedes she did not.
- D: Overbroad. A homicide victim’s statements can come in under many exceptions, such as 803(1), 803(2), and 803(3) for forward-looking intent.
- E: Correct. This is the Shepard reasoning, reflected in Rule 803(3)’s exclusion and Rule 403.
Rule: Rule 803(3) admits a statement of the declarant’s then-existing state of mind but not “a statement of memory or belief to prove the fact remembered or believed.” When the legitimate state-of-mind use is slight and the forbidden use is overwhelming, the court should exclude under Rule 403. Shepard v. United States.

## Final 2 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(5): the witness must vouch
At a November 2026 trial, the prosecution calls Dwayne Hollis, who signed a written statement for police the night of a shooting naming the shooter. On the stand, Hollis says he doesn't remember the events. Asked about the statement, he says: "That's my signature, but I'd been drinking all day. I'd have signed anything, and I can't say it's right." The detective who wrote it down testifies that he recorded Hollis's words accurately.

Is the statement admissible as a recorded recollection?

A. Yes, because it was made while the events were fresh and Hollis now lacks memory.
B. Yes, because the detective's testimony about accuracy satisfies the foundation.
C. Yes, and the prosecution may also have the written statement received as an exhibit.
D. No, because Hollis doesn't vouch that the statement was accurate when he made it.
E. No, because a recorded recollection must be written in the witness's own handwriting.

Answer: D
- A: Freshness and lack of memory are two of the four requirements. The record must also reflect the witness's knowledge accurately, and here no one can vouch for that.
- B: In a two-person record, the recorder can vouch that he transcribed accurately. But the witness must still vouch that what he said was true, and Hollis disowns it.
- C: Even a proper recorded recollection may be read into evidence but received as an exhibit only if offered by an adverse party.
- D: Correct. The Johnson v. State foundation has four parts: firsthand knowledge, made when fresh, insufficient present memory, and the witness's vouching for accuracy. Hollis refuses to vouch, so Rule 803(5) fails.
- E: A record the witness "made or adopted" qualifies. Adopting a statement someone else wrote is enough.
Rule: A Rule 803(5) recorded recollection requires firsthand knowledge, a record made or adopted when fresh, insufficient present memory, and the witness's vouching for accuracy (Johnson v. State). It is read in, not received as an exhibit unless the adverse party offers it.

## Final 2 · Q21 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(8)(A)(iii): the defendant may use it against the government
At his federal bribery trial, the defendant, a city official, offers a report by the city's inspector general. After a formal investigation, the inspector general concluded that the payments at issue were "legitimate consulting fees with no evidence of any improper influence." The government objects that investigative findings are inadmissible in a criminal case.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because Rule 803(8) bars investigative findings in criminal cases.
B. Exclude it, because the inspector general's conclusions are only opinions.
C. Exclude it, because it would violate the government's confrontation right.
D. Admit it, because investigative findings may be used against the government.
E. Admit it, but only if the inspector general testifies in person.

Answer: D
- A: The limit is one-sided: findings may be used "in a civil case or against the government in a criminal case."
- B: Beech Aircraft held that "factual findings" include opinions and conclusions based on a factual investigation.
- C: The Confrontation Clause protects only the accused.
- D: Correct, though it seems lopsided that the government can't use such findings against the defendant. Rule 803(8)(A)(iii) is deliberately asymmetrical. The government may try to show untrustworthiness under Rule 803(8)(B).
- E: Rule 803 exceptions apply regardless of availability.
Rule: Rule 803(8)(A)(iii): factual findings (including conclusions, under Beech Aircraft) from legally authorized investigations are admissible in civil cases and against the government in criminal cases, but not against the accused.

## Final 2 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Green: a declarant who testifies
Lena Cho testified before a grand jury, under oath, that she saw the defendant with a gun at the scene. At trial, she takes the stand and testifies that she never saw the defendant with a gun and that the police pressured her. She answers all questions on cross. The prosecution offers her grand jury testimony as substantive evidence, and the defense objects on hearsay and Confrontation Clause grounds.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit it, because Cho testifies subject to cross and the statement was sworn.
B. Exclude it, because grand jury testimony is testimonial and wasn't subject to cross.
C. Admit it only to impeach Cho, because she now recants the earlier account.
D. Exclude it unless the prosecution shows Cho is unavailable to testify.
E. Admit it only under the residual exception, after reasonable written notice.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. When the declarant testifies at trial and submits to cross, the Confrontation Clause places no limit on her prior statements (California v. Green; Crawford). Her sworn grand jury testimony, inconsistent with her trial testimony, is not hearsay under Rule 801(d)(1)(A), under either the old or the amended version.
- B: Prior cross isn't needed when the declarant is cross-examined at trial (Green).
- C: Rule 801(d)(1)(A) makes it substantive, and it was given under penalty of perjury at a proceeding.
- D: Rule 801(d)(1) assumes the declarant testifies. Unavailability belongs to Rule 804.
- E: Rule 807 applies only to statements not admissible under Rules 803 or 804. This statement isn't hearsay at all.
Rule: A declarant who testifies and submits to cross at trial satisfies the Confrontation Clause for her prior statements (California v. Green). Inconsistent grand jury testimony is substantive under Rule 801(d)(1)(A).

## Final 2 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Ohio v. Clark: child and teacher
A four-year-old with bruises tells her preschool aide, who must by law report suspected abuse, "Uncle Ray hit me." The child is found incompetent to testify. At Ray's trial, the prosecution offers the statement through the aide under a state child-hearsay rule. How does the Confrontation Clause apply?

A. It bars the statement, because a mandatory reporter acts as an agent of the police.
B. It bars the statement, because the child was never cross-examined by Ray.
C. It doesn't bar the statement, because the primary purpose wasn't to assist a prosecution.
D. It doesn't bar the statement, because all statements by children are reliable.
E. It doesn't bar the statement, because the aide can be cross-examined at trial.

Answer: C
- A: Ohio v. Clark held that reporting duties don't turn teachers into police agents.
- B: Prior cross-examination matters only for testimonial statements.
- C: Correct. Under Ohio v. Clark, statements to non-police are much less likely to be testimonial, and very young children's rarely are. With no primary purpose of assisting a prosecution, only the hearsay rules apply.
- D: Reliability isn't the test after Crawford. Nontestimonial statements simply fall outside the Clause.
- E: Cross-examining the person who heard the statement doesn't address the declarant. The reason here is that the statement is nontestimonial.
Rule: Statements to non-police, especially by very young children, are rarely testimonial. Reporting duties don't make teachers agents of the police (Ohio v. Clark).

## Final 2 · Q24 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Richardson v. Marsh: other evidence that links the defendant
At a joint murder trial, the prosecution introduces codefendant Ben Tate's confession. It was redacted to remove every reference to the defendant, Cara Mott, and to anyone besides Tate. As redacted, it says Tate and "his cousin" planned the killing while driving to the victim's house. Mott then testifies that she was in the car with Tate and his cousin but heard nothing about a killing. The court instructs the jury to consider the confession only against Tate.

Was Mott's confrontation right violated?

A. Yes, because once Mott testified, the jury could link the confession to her.
B. Yes, because any confession by a nontestifying codefendant violates Bruton.
C. No, because Mott waived her confrontation rights by choosing to testify.
D. No, because the confession as redacted didn't refer to Mott at all.
E. No, because the confession was admissible against Mott as a coconspirator's.

Answer: D
- A: This is the intuitive objection. But Richardson v. Marsh held that a confession is outside Bruton if it doesn't refer to the defendant on its face, even if other evidence, including the defendant's own testimony, links her to it.
- B: Bruton covers confessions that facially incriminate the defendant. A properly redacted confession doesn't.
- C: Testifying doesn't waive confrontation rights.
- D: Correct, though a juror may well connect the dots. Under Richardson v. Marsh, the confession is admissible with a limiting instruction because it doesn't name or refer to Mott. Whether the jury links it to her through other evidence doesn't matter. This differs from Gray, where an obvious redaction pointed at the defendant.
- E: A custodial confession isn't in furtherance of a conspiracy.
Rule: Under Bruton, a nontestifying codefendant's confession that facially incriminates the defendant is barred. Under Richardson v. Marsh, one redacted to remove any reference to the defendant is permitted with an instruction, even if other evidence links her. Gray bars obvious redactions, and Samia permits neutral phrases.

## Final 2 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 704(b): intent to manufacture
Wade Hollis is charged with possessing pseudoephedrine with intent to manufacture methamphetamine. Police watched him buy cold tablets, matches, acetone, and hydrogen peroxide at three stores within an hour. The government calls a forensic chemist.

Which part of her proposed testimony is most likely to be excluded?

A. That buying such products at several stores in a short time is a pattern often seen in methamphetamine cases.
B. That the pseudoephedrine in the tablets could yield a significant quantity of the drug.
C. That the four products together contain the main ingredients of a common recipe for methamphetamine.
D. That in her opinion Hollis bought the products intending to use them to manufacture methamphetamine.
E. That red phosphorus from matchbooks is used in the most common method of cooking methamphetamine.

Answer: D
- A: Testimony about general patterns, without an opinion about this defendant's mental state, is allowed. Diaz v. United States held that Rule 704(b) bars only opinions about whether the particular defendant had the required mental state.
- B: The yield of the tablets is a technical fact about chemistry, not an opinion about what Hollis intended.
- C: Explaining what the ingredients are and how they combine is classic expert testimony that helps the jury. It leaves the inference about intent to the jurors.
- D: Correct. Rule 704(b) provides that in a criminal case an expert must not state an opinion about whether the defendant had a mental state that is an element of the crime. Intent to manufacture is an element. The permissible version explains the recipe and leaves intent to the jury.
- E: Explaining how one ingredient is used in the process is background the jury needs. It says nothing about Hollis's mental state.
Rule: Rule 704(b) bars an expert in a criminal case from stating an opinion about whether the defendant had a mental state that is an element of the crime or a defense. Under Diaz, the expert may still give general testimony, such as patterns typical of a crime, and leave the inference to the jury.

## Final 2 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 703: reverse-403 on disclosure
In a commitment hearing before a jury, a psychiatrist's opinion rests partly on a report from the patient's brother, who won't testify. Psychiatrists reasonably rely on such reports. The proponent wants the psychiatrist to tell the jury what the brother said. Which statement is correct?

A. The opinion is barred, because an expert's bases must themselves be admissible.
B. The opinion may come in, and the brother's report becomes substantive evidence.
C. The opinion may come in, but disclosure must pass a reverse-403 balance.
D. The opinion may come in, and disclosure is automatic under Rule 705.
E. The opinion is barred, because Rule 602 requires personal knowledge.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 703 says that if experts in the field would reasonably rely on such facts, they need not be admissible.
- B: Reliance doesn't make the facts admissible. Only the opinion comes in.
- C: Correct. Rule 703 allows the opinion, but the proponent may disclose otherwise-inadmissible facts only if their value in helping the jury evaluate the opinion substantially outweighs their prejudicial effect.
- D: Rule 705 concerns the order of testimony and disclosure on cross. It doesn't override the Rule 703 test.
- E: Rule 602 doesn't apply to expert testimony under Rule 703.
Rule: Under Rule 703 an expert may rely on inadmissible data of a kind reasonably relied on in the field, but the proponent's disclosure to the jury faces a reverse-403 test.

## Final 2 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 901(a): the purpose defines the foundation
Surveillance video shows a masked warehouse burglar wearing a black hooded sweatshirt with stylized white letters “SP” across the chest. A Facebook photo shows the defendant in a similar sweatshirt. To show the jury what that brand's sweatshirt looks like, the prosecution offers images of a “SouthPole” hooded sweatshirt that a detective found through an online search; he can't identify the websites. The defense concedes the brand exists but objects that the images can't be authenticated without testimony from the manufacturer or the website owners.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude the images, because internet evidence requires a heightened showing of who created and posted it.
B. Exclude the images, because the detective lacks personal knowledge of how the sweatshirt was made.
C. Exclude the images unless the manufacturer certifies them as business records under Rule 902(11).
D. Admit the images, because anything printed from a public website is self-authenticating under Rule 902.
E. Admit the images, because offered only to show what the sweatshirt looks like, they need little more foundation.

Answer: E
- A: Courts apply no heightened standard to internet evidence. The question is always whether the evidence supports a finding that the item is what the proponent claims.
- B: The detective needs only enough knowledge to say the images fairly show the branded sweatshirt the defense concedes exists. How it was made is beside the point.
- C: Rule 902(11) authenticates certified business records. The images aren't offered as anyone's business records, and no certification is needed for this limited use.
- D: Rule 902 lists specific self-authenticating items, such as newspapers and official publications. Ordinary web pages aren't among them.
- E: Correct. In United States v. Oreckinto, the court admitted such images because the kind and amount of authentication depend on what the evidence is offered to show. Offered only to show what a commercially available sweatshirt looks like, like a generic picture of a soda can, they needed no testimony from the manufacturer. The answer could differ if they were offered to prove who made the shirt or were suspected of being altered.
Rule: Rule 901(a) asks whether the item is what the proponent claims, and the evidence needed depends on that claim (United States v. Oreckinto). Internet evidence gets no heightened standard.

## Final 2 · Q28 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 1008: the jury decides existence
In a breach-of-contract suit, the plaintiff claims the parties signed a second page adding a $200,000 bonus term, and that the defendant has since destroyed it. She offers her own testimony describing the page's terms. The defendant testifies that no second page ever existed. The judge finds that if the page existed, the defendant destroyed it, and that the plaintiff didn't lose it in bad faith.

Who decides whether the second page ever existed?

A. The jury, because Rule 1008 leaves to the trier of fact whether an asserted writing ever existed.
B. Neither, because the plaintiff's testimony is barred until she produces a copy of the page.
C. The judge, because the plaintiff can't satisfy Rule 1004 without first proving that the page existed.
D. The judge, by a preponderance, because every admissibility question under Rules 1001–1008 arises under Rule 104(a).
E. The jury, but only after the judge finds by clear and convincing evidence that the page existed.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under Rule 1008(a), in a jury trial the jury determines whether an asserted writing, recording, or photograph ever existed. Those questions go to the heart of the case, so they are handled like conditional relevance under Rule 104(b).
- B: Rule 1004(a) excuses the original when it was lost or destroyed without the proponent's bad faith, and then other evidence of content, including the plaintiff's testimony, is admissible. No copy is needed.
- C: The judge has already found the Rule 1004 condition on the assumption that the page existed. Whether it existed at all is the jury's question under Rule 1008.
- D: Rule 1008 gives the judge the factual conditions for Rules 1004 and 1005, such as loss without bad faith, but sends three questions to the jury: whether the asserted writing ever existed, whether another one produced at trial is the original, and whether other evidence of content accurately reflects it.
- E: No heightened finding by the judge is required. The jury decides existence; the judge asks only whether the evidence would support a finding.
Rule: Rule 1008: the court decides the factual conditions for admitting other evidence of content under Rules 1004–1005, but the jury decides whether an asserted writing ever existed, whether another writing is the original, and whether other evidence of content is accurate.

## Final 2 · Q29 · Privileges — Rule 502(b): the holder's burden
In a federal employment suit, the city produces 140 pages of personnel files. Page 9 is a privileged memo from the city attorney. Two weeks later the city sends a letter asking that the memo be returned. The plaintiff doesn't respond, and the city does nothing more for two years. When the plaintiff lists the memo as a trial exhibit, the city moves to exclude it. Its only evidence of its review process is its current lawyer's statement that “a paralegal reviewed the documents.”

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude the memo, because an inadvertent disclosure never waives the lawyer-client privilege.
B. Exclude the memo, because the city's prompt written request for its return was enough to preserve the privilege.
C. Admit the memo, because producing a privileged document in discovery always waives the privilege.
D. Admit the memo, because the city didn't show reasonable steps to prevent disclosure or prompt steps to fix it.
E. Exclude the memo, because the plaintiff's failure to answer the letter violated the discovery rules.

Answer: D
- A: Inadvertence is only the first of Rule 502(b)'s three elements. The holder must also show reasonable steps to prevent disclosure and prompt, reasonable steps to rectify the error.
- B: A single request followed by two years of silence isn't a prompt, reasonable effort to rectify. The privilege must be jealously guarded by its holder (Williams v. District of Columbia).
- C: Rule 502(b) exists to prevent automatic waiver. An inadvertent disclosure doesn't waive if the holder meets the rule's requirements.
- D: Correct. Under Rule 502(b), the holder bears the burden of showing that the disclosure was inadvertent, that it took reasonable steps to prevent it, and that it promptly took reasonable steps to rectify it. A bare assertion about a paralegal's review of a small production and one unpursued letter fall short, as in Williams v. District of Columbia.
- E: Whatever the plaintiff's duties under Rule 26(b)(5)(B), the question is whether the city met its own burden under Rule 502(b). Two years of inaction defeat that claim.
Rule: Rule 502(b): an inadvertent disclosure in a federal proceeding doesn't waive the privilege if the holder took reasonable steps to prevent it and promptly took reasonable steps to rectify it. The holder bears the burden on each element.

## Final 2 · Q30 · Privileges — Crime-fraud and Zolin
A creditor has a judgment against Dunn. Dunn asks a lawyer how to move assets so the creditor can't reach them. Lawfully obtained emails suggest the plan. The creditor asks the judge to review the lawyer's file in camera. Which statement is correct?

A. The judge may never review allegedly privileged material in deciding whether the exception applies here.
B. The privilege is lost as soon as the creditor alleges fraud.
C. The judge may review on a good-faith factual showing, below what defeats the privilege.
D. The crime-fraud exception reaches a client's confession of any past crime.
E. The exception applies only if the lawyer knew of and joined the fraud.

Answer: C
- A: United States v. Zolin held that in camera review is permitted.
- B: An allegation isn't enough. United States v. Zolin requires a factual basis.
- C: Correct. United States v. Zolin requires a factual basis adequate to support a good-faith belief by a reasonable person that review may reveal evidence of the exception, a lower showing than defeating the privilege.
- D: The exception reaches advice sought for future wrongdoing. Past crimes stay protected.
- E: This is too narrow. The exception turns on the client's purpose in seeking advice, so the lawyer need not join the fraud.
Rule: The crime-fraud exception reaches advice sought for future wrongdoing, not past crimes. Before in camera review, a judge needs a good-faith factual basis, a lower showing than needed to defeat the privilege (United States v. Zolin).

## Final 3 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Tanner: juror intoxication is internal
After a federal fraud conviction, the defendant moves for a new trial. He offers (1) a juror’s affidavit that several jurors drank vodka at lunch throughout the trial and dozed through afternoon testimony, and (2) testimony from a deputy marshal who saw one juror asleep during afternoon testimony and smelled alcohol in the jury box. Which evidence may the court consider?

A. The juror’s affidavit only, because court officers may not testify about what happened in the jury box during trial.
B. The deputy marshal’s testimony, but not the juror’s affidavit.
C. Both, because drinking at lunch happened outside deliberations, so Rule 606(b) doesn’t apply.
D. Both, because drugs and alcohol are an “outside influence” under Rule 606(b)(2)(B).
E. Neither, because both concern the jury’s conduct during the trial.

Answer: B
- A: Tanner reverses this. Nonjuror evidence, such as a deputy marshal’s observations, is exactly what remains available, while juror testimony about intoxication is barred because intoxication is an internal matter.
- B: Correct. Tanner held that juror intoxication is internal, no more an “outside influence” than a virus, poor food, or lack of sleep, so 606(b) bars juror testimony about it. But 606(b) limits only jurors. Nonjuror evidence, such as observations by courtroom personnel, remains one of the safeguards Tanner pointed to, along with voir dire, observation during trial, and jurors’ reports before the verdict.
- C: That was the Tanner dissent’s view. The majority, relying on the legislative history (the Senate version was enacted), held the juror testimony barred.
- D: The Tanner majority rejected that reading. An outside influence means things like a bribe, a threat, or outside contact, not what jurors consume.
- E: 606(b) restricts juror testimony and affidavits. It doesn’t bar a deputy’s own observations, which the district court in Tanner invited.
Rule: Tanner: juror intoxication is internal, so juror testimony about it is barred. Nonjuror evidence (court personnel) remains available.

## Final 3 · Q2 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Rule 602: personal knowledge
A witness testifies she saw the defendant leave the bank. The defense argues she couldn't have seen him, pointing to a parked van, but she says she was standing where she had a clear view. What should the judge do?

A. Require corroboration of her position from a second witness.
B. Decide the issue in a separate hearing with no rules of evidence.
C. Find by a preponderance whether she actually saw the defendant.
D. Admit it, if the evidence could support a finding that she saw him.
E. Exclude the testimony, because personal knowledge has been disputed.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 602 has no corroboration requirement.
- B: Rule 104(a) hearings apply to preliminary questions for the judge. This isn't one.
- C: That is the Rule 104(a) approach. Personal knowledge is a Rule 104(b) question.
- D: Correct. Rule 602 requires evidence sufficient to support a finding of personal knowledge, and her own testimony may supply it. The jury decides the rest.
- E: A dispute goes to the jury. It doesn't require exclusion.
Rule: Under Rule 602 a witness needs personal knowledge, shown by evidence sufficient to support a finding. It is a Rule 104(b) question for the jury.

## Final 3 · Q3 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 403: gruesome photos after a concession (State v. Bocharski)
At a murder trial, the defense concedes the victim’s death, its cause, and the number of stab wounds; the only issue is identity. The prosecution offers (1) a crime-scene photograph of the wounds, which the medical examiner uses to explain that their angle shows a left-handed attacker (the defendant is left-handed), and (2) three color autopsy photographs of the victim’s internal organs, which no witness refers to. The defense objects to both under Rule 403. Which ruling is most defensible?

A. Admit photograph 1 and exclude the autopsy photographs.
B. Exclude photograph 1 as cumulative of the medical examiner’s testimony and admit the autopsy photographs.
C. Exclude all of them, because the defense conceded the death, its cause, and the wounds.
D. Admit all of them, because photographs of the victim are always relevant to the fact and cause of death in a murder case.
E. Admit all of them, but instruct the jury not to let the photographs inflame its passions.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. This tracks Bocharski. Concessions lower a photo’s probative value on the conceded points but don’t eliminate its value on contested ones. Photo 1 bears directly on identity, the disputed issue. The autopsy photos, tied to no testimony, serve mainly to inflame, so their slight value is substantially outweighed by unfair prejudice.
- B: Photo 1 illustrates and supports the examiner’s key inference. If anything is needlessly cumulative or inflammatory here, it’s the autopsy photos.
- C: A concession doesn’t make evidence irrelevant (ACN to 401). Photo 1 is probative of the one disputed issue, the attacker’s identity, through the angle of the wounds.
- D: Relevance isn’t the end of the analysis. Where those facts are conceded and the photos are unexplained, 403 exclusion is appropriate (Bocharski Exhibits 46–47).
- E: An instruction can’t supply probative value the autopsy photographs lack. With the death and wounds conceded and no witness using them, their only effect is emotional, so Rule 403 supports excluding them (State v. Bocharski).
Rule: Rule 403: weigh probative value on contested issues against unfair prejudice. Concessions lower probative value (Bocharski: the angle-and-depth photos came in; the unexplained autopsy photos didn’t).

## Final 3 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 407: impeachment and disputed feasibility
A guest slips on a polished marble lobby floor on a rainy evening and sues the hotel for negligence. A week after the fall, the hotel began putting out non-slip runner mats whenever it rains. At trial the hotel’s general manager testifies, “That lobby was as safe as it could possibly be on a rainy night. There was nothing more we could have done.” The plaintiff then offers evidence of the runner mats. How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because Rule 407 bars evidence of subsequent remedial measures for every purpose.
B. Exclude it, because the mats were a change in policy rather than a physical repair of the floor itself.
C. Admit it for feasibility only, because a manager’s opinion that premises were safe can’t be impeached with later measures.
D. Admit it to impeach the manager and to show feasibility, which he put in dispute, but not as proof of negligence.
E. Admit it to prove negligence, because the manager opened the door by vouching for the hotel’s safety, so the hotel can’t invoke Rule 407.

Answer: D
- A: 407 is a limited exclusion. Its second sentence lists permitted purposes, including impeachment and disputed feasibility. Rule 410 is the specialized rule that bars evidence for nearly all purposes.
- B: 407 covers any “measures” that would have made the harm less likely, including new policies, warnings, and procedures, not just physical repairs.
- C: Feasibility is one proper purpose, but so is impeachment: “nothing more we could have done” is directly contradicted by the mats, so both permitted purposes apply.
- D: Correct. 407 bars subsequent remedial measures only when offered to prove negligence or culpable conduct. The court may admit them for impeachment or, if disputed, to prove feasibility. “Nothing more we could have done” does both, much like the class hypo where the restaurant’s claim of a $40,000 repair let in its $55.26 receipt.
- E: Opening the door doesn’t change the purpose for which the jury may use the evidence. It comes in for impeachment and feasibility only, and the hotel is entitled to an instruction saying so.
Rule: Rule 407: post-accident measures can’t prove negligence, culpable conduct, a defect, or the need for a warning, but may be admitted to impeach or, if disputed, to prove ownership, control, or feasibility.

## Final 3 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 408: no dispute, no protection
Garnet Supply delivered $50,000 of lumber to Lomax Builders. Before any lawsuit, Lomax's owner emailed Garnet: "I know we owe the full $50,000 and the lumber was fine, but cash is tight. Will you take $30,000 to close this out?" Garnet refused and sued. At trial, Lomax claims for the first time that the lumber was defective. Garnet offers the email.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because Rule 408 bars any offer to accept less than the amount claimed.
B. Admit it, because there was no dispute over the debt's validity or amount to compromise.
C. Admit it, but only to impeach Lomax's owner if he testifies the lumber was defective.
D. Exclude it, because statements made before any lawsuit is filed are protected.
E. Admit it, but only to show that Lomax's owner is biased against Garnet.

Answer: B
- A: Rule 408 protects compromise of a disputed claim, not a request to pay less on an admitted debt.
- B: Correct. Rule 408(a) applies to compromise of "a claim that was disputed" as to validity or amount. Lomax conceded both the debt and the quality, so there was nothing to compromise. A plea to pay less on an undisputed debt isn't protected, and the email comes in as Lomax's own statement.
- C: Because Rule 408 doesn't apply, the email is admissible substantively. And even where Rule 408 applies, it bars impeachment by inconsistency.
- D: Rule 408 can protect negotiations before suit if there is a dispute. Here there was none.
- E: Bias is a Rule 408(b) permitted purpose for evidence the rule otherwise covers. This email isn't covered.
Rule: Rule 408 requires a claim disputed as to validity or amount when the offer or statement is made. Asking for a discount on an admitted debt isn't a compromise and gets no protection.

## Final 3 · Q6 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Biaggi: rejecting immunity as consciousness of innocence
Paul Mariner, a company CEO, is on trial for bribery. Before indictment, prosecutors offered him immunity in exchange for information about other executives' payments. He replied that he knew of no payments by anyone, and the offer was withdrawn. At trial, the government proved consciousness of guilt by showing that Mariner moved large sums into gold after colleagues began cooperating. Mariner offers evidence of his rejection of immunity. The government objects that it is irrelevant and is plea-negotiation material.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because Rule 410 bars any evidence of plea or immunity discussions.
B. Exclude it, because declining an offer has no tendency to show innocence.
C. Admit it only if Mariner testifies and is subject to cross-examination.
D. Exclude it, because his own rejection of the offer is self-serving hearsay.
E. Admit it, because rejecting immunity tends to show consciousness of innocence.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 410 bars use "against the defendant." It doesn't stop the defendant from offering his own conduct.
- B: Biaggi distinguished rejecting a plea to a lesser charge, which may be a gamble, from rejecting immunity, which strongly suggests a lack of guilty knowledge.
- C: The evidence can be proved through others. Biaggi imposed no such condition.
- D: His rejection is offered as conduct showing consciousness of innocence, not to prove the truth of an assertion.
- E: Correct. United States v. Biaggi reversed the exclusion of this evidence. Rejecting immunity is probative of innocence, and fairness required admitting it once the government proved consciousness of guilt. Rule 410 protects the defendant who negotiated.
Rule: Rule 410 shields the defendant, not the government. Under Biaggi, a defendant's rejection of immunity is relevant to consciousness of innocence, especially when the government offers consciousness-of-guilt evidence.

## Final 3 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(b)(2): name the purpose actually in dispute
Tess Grady is charged with knowingly passing counterfeit $100 bills at a mall. She admits she spent the bills but testifies that she got them as change at a bank and had no idea they were fake. The prosecution offers evidence that six months earlier, a casino cashier refused one of Grady's $100 bills, told her it was counterfeit, and handed it back.

On what basis is the evidence most likely admitted?

A. To prove identity, because the two incidents share a distinctive method.
B. To prove a common plan, because both involved counterfeit $100 bills.
C. To prove her character for dishonesty, because she put it in issue by testifying.
D. To prove motive, because people pass counterfeit money to obtain goods.
E. To prove knowledge, since she'd been told a bill was fake.

Answer: E
- A: Grady admits she passed the bills, so identity isn't disputed. Identity also requires an idiosyncratic signature (Trenkler).
- B: A plan requires that the acts be parts of one overall scheme, not a pattern of similar acts (Kirsch). Two separate incidents aren't one plan.
- C: Testifying doesn't make her character for honesty admissible to prove conduct. A witness's credibility goes to Rules 608–609, not Rule 404(a).
- D: This "motive" is just the general desire to commit the crime, which Kirsch treats as propensity.
- E: Correct. Several labels sound plausible, but the only real issue is whether Grady knew. Being told a bill was counterfeit tends to show she could recognize fakes and knew what she was spending, without inferring that she's a dishonest person. The Huddleston steps and Rule 403 follow, with a limiting instruction on request.
Rule: Rule 404(b)(2) purposes must be tied to a disputed issue by a non-character chain of inference. Choose the purpose the defense actually contests: here knowledge, not identity or plan. Then apply Huddleston, Rule 403, and Rule 105.

## Final 3 · Q8 · Character Evidence — Rule 413’s threshold: a sexual-assault charge
A defendant is charged in federal court with kidnapping a woman from a parking garage and holding her for ransom. The indictment alleges no sexual offense. The government gives timely notice that it will offer evidence, under Rule 413, that six years ago he abducted a different woman from a garage and raped her. How should the court rule?

A. Rule 413 doesn’t apply, because he isn’t accused of a sexual assault, so only Rule 404(b) could admit it.
B. Admit it under Rule 413, because the earlier crime was a sexual assault.
C. Admit it under Rule 413, but only if the government proves the earlier rape beyond a reasonable doubt.
D. Exclude it, because evidence of an uncharged rape is inadmissible in any federal prosecution.
E. Admit it under Rule 413, because kidnapping a woman is treated as a crime of sexual violence.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. 413(a) applies only “in a criminal case in which a defendant is accused of a sexual assault,” the first step of Guardia’s threshold test. Outside that setting, the propensity ban governs, and the earlier crime comes in only for a non-propensity purpose, such as identity shown by a distinctive method, subject to 403.
- B: That the earlier act was a sexual assault is the second threshold requirement. The first is that the charged crime be one, and kidnapping for ransom isn’t.
- C: The threshold problem comes first. And even where 413 applies, the standard is presumably Huddleston’s: whether a jury could reasonably find the act by a preponderance.
- D: If it fits a non-propensity purpose, such as a signature method proving identity, and passes 403, it could still come in under 404(b).
- E: Rule 413(d) defines sexual assault by the listed sexual conduct. Kidnapping for ransom involves none of it, so the defendant isn’t “accused of a sexual assault,” and only Rule 404(b) could admit the earlier rape.
Rule: Guardia’s threshold: 413 applies only if (1) the defendant is accused of sexual assault, (2) the evidence shows another sexual assault, and (3) it is relevant. Then ordinary 403 applies.

## Final 3 · Q9 · Character Evidence — Self-defense: the victim's violence the defendant knew of
Nadia Fox is charged with murdering Ray Doyle, and she claims self-defense. She offers:

I. Her own testimony that two weeks before the killing, Doyle told her he had stabbed a man outside a bar.
II. Testimony from Doyle's former coworker, whom Fox never met, that Doyle once broke the coworker's jaw.
III. A witness's opinion that Doyle was a violent person.

Which items are admissible?

A. I only.
B. II only.
C. I and III only.
D. II and III only.
E. I, II, and III.

Answer: C
- A: Item III is also admissible. Rule 404(a)(2)(B) lets an accused offer the victim's pertinent trait in opinion form.
- B: Item II is a specific act Fox didn't know about. It can only show Doyle's character, and specific instances aren't a permitted form here.
- C: Correct. Item I goes to Fox's state of mind: what she heard bears on whether her fear was reasonable, which is a non-character, non-hearsay use (compare United States v. James). Item III is the victim's pertinent trait in opinion form under Rules 404(a)(2)(B) and 405(a). Item II is a specific act offered only to prove character. Character isn't an essential element of self-defense, so Rule 405(b) doesn't admit it.
- D: Item I is admissible to show Fox's reasonable fear. Item II isn't, because Rule 405(a) limits proof of the victim's character to reputation or opinion.
- E: Item II fails. Specific instances prove character only on cross or when character is an essential element (Rule 405(b)).
Rule: Violent acts of the victim that the defendant knew of are admissible to show the defendant's reasonable fear. To prove the victim's violent character, the accused may offer reputation or opinion (Rules 404(a)(2)(B), 405(a)). Character isn't an element of self-defense, so Rule 405(b) doesn't apply.

## Final 3 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Contradiction on a collateral matter
In a convenience-store robbery trial, eyewitness Omar Haddad testifies that he saw the robbery as he was walking past the store, and adds that he was wearing his red raincoat that night. The defense calls Haddad's roommate, who would testify that Haddad's only raincoat is blue and that he wore it that night. Nothing else turns on the coat's color.

What is the proper ruling on the roommate's testimony?

A. Admit it, because a witness may always be contradicted by extrinsic evidence on any point.
B. Exclude it, because extrinsic proof on a collateral matter is generally barred.
C. Admit it under Rule 608(b), because a false detail shows Haddad is a liar.
D. Exclude it, because contradiction isn't a permitted method of impeachment.
E. Admit it, because matters affecting a witness's credibility are never collateral.

Answer: B
- A: Contradiction on a collateral point may be explored on cross, but extrinsic proof usually isn't allowed.
- B: Correct. The coat's color is relevant only to contradict Haddad. Under the collateral-matter principle, extrinsic proof on such a point is generally excluded, and Rule 403 supports that. The defense may cross-examine about it but must take his answer.
- C: Rule 608(b) bars extrinsic evidence of specific acts offered to attack character for truthfulness, so it points against admission, not toward it.
- D: Contradiction is one of the "lying now" modes of impeachment. It is limited, not forbidden.
- E: Only bias is never collateral (Abel). A mistaken detail is.
Rule: Under the collateral-matter principle, extrinsic evidence offered only to contradict a witness on a point with no independent relevance is generally barred. Bias and non-collateral contradictions may be proved extrinsically.

## Final 3 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 608(a): opinion of character, not of the testimony
The defense in a fraud trial attacks the government's witness, Rena Holt, with a reputation witness who says she is known as a liar. The government then calls Holt's longtime supervisor, who would testify: (1) "In my opinion, Rena is a truthful person"; and (2) "I've heard her testimony in this case, and I believe she told the truth today."

What is the best ruling?

A. Admit both, because Holt's character for truthfulness was attacked first.
B. Exclude both, because a witness can't be rehabilitated with opinion testimony.
C. Admit (1), but exclude (2), which vouches for her testimony in this case.
D. Admit (2) only, because it is more specific and more helpful to the jury.
E. Exclude (1), because rehabilitation must use the same form as the attack.

Answer: C
- A: Partly right: the attack opened the door to Rule 608(a) support. But Rule 608(a) admits only opinion or reputation about character for truthfulness, not an opinion that particular testimony was true.
- B: Rule 608(a) allows either reputation or opinion.
- C: Correct. The supervisor's general opinion of Holt's character for truthfulness is proper rehabilitation under Rule 608(a). Saying she told the truth today vouches for specific testimony. That invades the jury's role in judging credibility, the same line Batangan draws for experts.
- D: The specificity is the problem. Opinions on whether a witness testified truthfully aren't helpful to the jury, which decides credibility.
- E: Rule 608(a) doesn't require matching forms. Opinion may answer reputation.
Rule: Under Rule 608(a), once character for truthfulness is attacked, it may be supported by reputation or opinion about character for truthfulness. No witness, lay or expert, may vouch that particular testimony is true.

## Final 3 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609(a)(1)(B): testifying defendant
Cole is on trial for armed robbery and plans to testify; his testimony is the core of his defense. Six years ago he was convicted of armed robbery and served two years. The government moves to impeach him with that conviction. Which analysis is correct?

A. It must be admitted unless its value is substantially outweighed by unfair prejudice.
B. It must be admitted with no balancing, because robbery is a crime of dishonesty.
C. It comes in only if its value outweighs the prejudice to Cole, and similarity cuts against it.
D. It comes in only if its value substantially outweighs prejudice, because it is over five years old.
E. It is excluded automatically, because the conviction is for the same crime as the charge.

Answer: C
- A: That is the Rule 403 test of Rule 609(a)(1)(A), used for witnesses other than a criminal defendant.
- B: Robbery generally isn't a crime of dishonesty, so Rule 609(a)(2) doesn't apply.
- C: Correct. Rule 609(a)(1)(B) requires that probative value outweigh prejudicial effect to the defendant. Under the Gordon factors in United States v. Brewer, similarity to the charge and the importance of his testimony weigh against admission.
- D: The reverse balance belongs to Rule 609(b), which starts after 10 years, not five.
- E: Similarity weighs against admission but doesn't exclude automatically. The rule calls for a balance.
Rule: A testifying criminal defendant's felony comes in only if probative value outweighs prejudicial effect, under Rule 609(a)(1)(B). The Gordon factors treat similarity to the charge and the importance of the defendant's testimony as weighing against admission.

## Final 3 · Q13 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 412 applies only where sexual misconduct is alleged
Paul Vance is charged with burglarizing the home of Mia Holt, who identified him from a lineup. He wants to cross-examine Holt about her secret affair with Vance's business rival. He argues she identified Vance falsely to help her lover's business by putting Vance in prison. The prosecution objects under Rule 412, arguing that evidence of a victim's sexual behavior is barred.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because Rule 412 bars evidence of any victim's sexual behavior.
B. Exclude it unless Vance files a Rule 412 motion 14 days before trial.
C. Admit it only if excluding it would violate Vance's constitutional rights.
D. Exclude it, because the affair is a collateral matter in a burglary case.
E. Rule 412 doesn't apply here; the affair is admissible to show bias.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 412(a) applies only "in a civil or criminal proceeding involving alleged sexual misconduct."
- B: Rule 412(c)'s procedure applies only when Rule 412 does.
- C: The constitutional exception in Rule 412(b)(1)(C) isn't needed because the rule doesn't apply. (Olden would support admission even if it did.)
- D: Bias is never collateral (Abel).
- E: Correct, though the evidence is about a sexual relationship. Rule 412 is limited to proceedings involving alleged sexual misconduct. Here the affair is offered to show Holt's motive to lie, which is bias. Bias is admissible subject to Rule 403, and Rule 611(a) protects her from harassment.
Rule: Rule 412 applies only in proceedings involving alleged sexual misconduct. In other cases, a witness's relationships may be explored to show bias, subject to Rules 403 and 611(a).

## Final 3 · Q14 · Definition of Hearsay — Implied assertions: Zenni
While searching an apartment for drugs, agents answer the phone. Unknown callers ask, "Is Mark selling today? I need two bags." At Mark's trial, the government offers the calls to prove the apartment was used to sell drugs. Under Rule 801, what is the most likely ruling?

A. Hearsay, because the calls imply the callers' belief that drugs were sold there.
B. Hearsay, because the callers aren't available for cross-examination.
C. Not hearsay, because questions and requests can never be statements.
D. Not hearsay, because the callers didn't intend to assert that drugs were sold there.
E. Not hearsay, because they are statements of Mark's coconspirators.

Answer: D
- A: That is the Wright v. Doe d. Tatham approach. Rule 801(a) rejects it unless the speaker intended to communicate the belief.
- B: Lack of cross-examination is the reason for the hearsay rule, not the definition of hearsay.
- C: This overstates the rule. A question or request can be a statement if the speaker intended to assert something by it.
- D: Correct. Under Rule 801(a) and United States v. Zenni, the callers placed orders; they didn't intend to assert that the apartment was a drug operation.
- E: Buyers calling to buy aren't shown to be coconspirators, and the evidence doesn't need Rule 801(d)(2)(E) anyway.
Rule: Rule 801(a) defines a statement by the speaker's intent to assert. A belief implied by words not meant to assert it isn't hearsay (United States v. Zenni).

## Final 3 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(2)(B): adoption by silence
At a crowded bar the night after a jewelry-store robbery, Pruitt’s longtime friend Gage said to Pruitt, loudly enough for three others at the table to hear: “You need to lie low, man. The cops are all over town looking for whoever hit Kessel’s Jewelers, and that was some sloppy work you did.” Pruitt shrugged, laughed, and ordered another round. No police were present, and Pruitt had not been arrested or questioned.

At Pruitt’s robbery trial, the prosecution calls one of the others at the table to recount the exchange.

Which statement is most accurate?

A. The testimony is inadmissible, because silence can amount to adoption only when the accusation is made by a police officer.
B. The testimony is inadmissible, because using a defendant’s silence as evidence of guilt violates the Fifth Amendment and due process.
C. Gage’s statement is admissible as a co-conspirator statement, because Gage was helping Pruitt avoid detection.
D. The testimony is admissible only to show Pruitt’s state of mind, and the jury must be instructed not to consider it as evidence that he committed the robbery.
E. It is admissible as an adoptive admission if Pruitt heard and understood it and would naturally have denied it if untrue.

Answer: E
- A: Adoption by silence most often arises with private accusers. Police accusations, especially after arrest, are actually more problematic.
- B: Doyle bars the use of post-Miranda silence. Pre-arrest silence in response to a private person’s accusation raises no Fifth Amendment or due process problem.
- C: There is no evidence that Gage was a co-conspirator in the robbery, and after-the-fact advice to lie low is not in furtherance of a conspiracy shown here.
- D: An adoptive admission is substantive evidence. Once adopted, the statement is treated as the party’s own.
- E: Correct. This states the adoptive-admission test for silence.
Rule: A statement is not hearsay if offered against a party who manifested that it adopted or believed it to be true. FRE 801(d)(2)(B). Silence can be an adoption when the party heard and understood the statement, was able to respond, and, under the circumstances, would naturally have denied it if it were untrue.

## Final 3 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(2)(E): bootstrapping alone isn't enough
In a cocaine-conspiracy trial against Dale Ruiz, the government offers a recorded call. In it, Rick Vance, an admitted dealer, tells a person he thinks is a new buyer (actually an informant): "Don't worry about supply. My guy Dale runs the tire shop on Elm. He fronts me a kilo every week." Ruiz does run a tire shop on Elm. The government offers no other evidence connecting Ruiz to Vance or to drug trafficking.

How should the court rule on Ruiz's hearsay objection?

A. Overrule it, because under Bourjaily the court may consider the statement itself in finding participation.
B. Overrule it, because the jury decides whether a conspiracy existed, and a jury could believe the call.
C. Sustain it, because a statement to a government informant can never be in furtherance of a conspiracy.
D. Sustain it, because the statement by itself can't establish that Ruiz participated in a drug conspiracy with Vance.
E. Overrule it, because Ruiz need not be charged with conspiracy for the coconspirator rule to apply.

Answer: D
- A: Bourjaily lets the court consider the statement, but the 1997 amendment says it "does not by itself establish" the conspiracy or the defendant's participation. Some independent evidence is required.
- B: Bourjaily holds that the foundation is a Rule 104(a) question for the judge, decided by a preponderance, not a Rule 104(b) question for the jury.
- C: A statement to someone the speaker believes is a buyer can further the conspiracy, for example by reassuring a customer. The flaw here is the lack of independent evidence.
- D: Correct. Under Rule 801(d)(2), the statement "does not by itself establish" the existence of the conspiracy or the party's participation in it. Ruiz's running a tire shop on Elm is only an identifying detail, not evidence that he joined a drug conspiracy. Without independent evidence, the judge can't find his participation by a preponderance.
- E: That's true, since no conspiracy charge is needed, but it doesn't solve the missing independent evidence of participation.
Rule: For Rule 801(d)(2)(E), the judge finds the conspiracy, the party's participation, and that the statement was "during and in furtherance" by a preponderance under Rule 104(a) (Bourjaily). The statement counts but can't carry that burden alone.

## Final 3 · Q17 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(1)(C): a composite sketch
Right after a carjacking, the victim worked with a police sketch artist to produce a composite drawing of the carjacker. At trial, the victim testifies and is cross-examined, but she can't identify the defendant in the courtroom. The prosecution offers the sketch, which closely resembles the defendant, and the artist's testimony about how it was made.

What is the best basis for admitting the sketch?

A. As a prior identification by a witness who testifies at trial.
B. As not hearsay, because a drawing isn't a statement at all.
C. As a recorded recollection, adopted by the victim when fresh.
D. As a public record of the police department's investigation.
E. It is inadmissible, because she can't identify him in court.

Answer: A
- A: Correct, though she failed to identify the defendant in court. A composite produced from the victim's description is her assertion of what the carjacker looked like, a statement of identification. Rule 801(d)(1)(C) doesn't require an in-court identification. Owens holds that memory problems don't defeat it.
- B: A drawing made to communicate what the perpetrator looked like is an intended assertion under Rule 801(a).
- C: Rule 803(5) requires the witness to vouch for the record, and it is read rather than received. Rule 801(d)(1)(C) is the cleaner, substantive route.
- D: A sketch drawn from a witness's description isn't a matter the officer observed or a factual finding, and Rule 803(8) limits apply in criminal cases anyway.
- E: The exclusion exists precisely for earlier identifications, which are often more reliable than courtroom ones.
Rule: Rule 801(d)(1)(C) admits a testifying witness's earlier identification of a person, including through a lineup pick, photo array, or composite sketch, even if she can't identify the defendant at trial (Owens).

## Final 3 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Williamson and Crawford: a blame-shifting confession
Police arrest Eddie Hart with stolen jewelry. In a recorded stationhouse interview after Miranda warnings, Hart says: "Yeah, I carried the bag out. But Lou Pike planned the whole thing, picked the house, and kept most of the take." At Lou Pike's federal trial for the burglary, Hart invokes the Fifth Amendment. The prosecution offers Hart's whole statement against Pike.

What is the correct ruling?

A. All of it is admissible, because the whole confession was against Hart's own penal interest.
B. The parts about Pike are admissible, because they were corroborated by the stolen jewelry.
C. The parts about Pike fail Rule 804(b)(3), and the Confrontation Clause bars them anyway.
D. All of it is admissible against Pike as a statement of his coconspirator in the burglary.
E. It is admissible only if Pike gets a chance to cross-examine Hart at this trial.

Answer: C
- A: Williamson holds that Rule 804(b)(3) admits only the individual remarks that are self-inculpatory, not the collateral parts of a broader confession.
- B: Blame-shifting statements to police get "special suspicion" (Williamson), and corroboration doesn't make them testimonially safe.
- C: Correct. Under Williamson, only remarks that are themselves against Hart's interest qualify, and naming Pike as the planner who took most of the take shifts blame. Independently, a Mirandized stationhouse statement is testimonial. Hart is unavailable and Pike never had a chance to cross-examine him, so Crawford bars it.
- D: A confession to police after arrest isn't "during and in furtherance" of a conspiracy.
- E: Hart invoked the privilege and didn't testify, so Pike had no opportunity to cross-examine.
Rule: Rule 804(b)(3) admits only the specific remarks that are self-inculpatory (Williamson). Custodial statements to police are testimonial, so against a defendant they need unavailability plus a prior opportunity to cross (Crawford).
