## Final 3 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Hillmon: intent to meet another person
Nineteen-year-old Lark disappears. At 6:30 p.m. on the night she vanished, she told a friend: “I’m meeting Brody at the Taco Hut lot at nine to pick up some weed. He says he’s got a good deal.” Lark was never seen again.

At Brody’s federal kidnapping trial, the prosecution offers the friend’s testimony to prove (1) that Lark went to the Taco Hut lot and (2) that Brody met her there. Brody objects.

Which statement most accurately describes the admissibility of Lark’s statement?

A. It is admissible to show Lark went to the lot; whether it may show Brody’s conduct is disputed, and many courts require corroboration.
B. It is inadmissible for any purpose, because Lark’s reference to what Brody “says” is a statement of memory about a past conversation.
C. It is admissible for both purposes without limitation, because Rule 803(3) admits any statement of a declarant’s then-existing intent.
D. It is admissible to prove that Brody met Lark, but not to prove Lark’s own conduct, because a declarant’s stated intentions do not reliably predict her own behavior.
E. It is inadmissible, because the statement-of-intent exception applies only when the declarant is unavailable, and the prosecution has not proven that Lark is dead.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. This states the Hillmon rule and the current split over the Pheaster use.
- B: The embedded statement is Brody’s own, admissible against him under 801(d)(2)(A). Lark’s main assertion looks forward, not backward.
- C: This overstates the rule. Using one person’s stated intent to prove another person’s conduct is the contested part, and the legislative history cuts against it.
- D: This reverses the doctrine. The declarant’s own future conduct is the uncontroversial use; the third party’s conduct is the contested one.
- E: Rule 803 exceptions apply regardless of the declarant’s availability.
Rule: A statement of the declarant’s then-existing state of mind, such as intent or plan, is admissible. FRE 803(3). Under Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Hillmon, a statement of intent is admissible to show that the declarant likely acted on it. Whether it may also prove the conduct of a third person named in the statement is disputed.

## Final 3 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(5): read, not received
A gas-station clerk wrote a getaway car's license plate in a notebook as it drove off. At trial, looking at the notebook doesn't refresh her memory. She testifies that she wrote the number while looking at the plate and that she was careful to get it right. The plaintiff offers the notebook page as an exhibit. What is the correct ruling?

A. The page comes in as an exhibit, because the foundation has been laid.
B. The page stays out entirely, because a witness who can't remember is unavailable.
C. The page may be read into evidence, but the plaintiff can't make it an exhibit.
D. The page stays out, because Rule 612 is the only use for a witness's writing.
E. The page may be read only if the clerk is unavailable under Rule 804(a).

Answer: C
- A: Under Rule 803(5), the record may be received as an exhibit only if offered by an adverse party.
- B: Memory loss doesn't keep Rule 803(5) from applying. The rule is built for that witness.
- C: Correct. The foundation is met: firsthand knowledge, made when fresh, no present memory, and vouching (Johnson v. State). Rule 803(5) lets it be read, and only an adverse party may make it an exhibit.
- D: Rule 612 covers refreshing memory. When refreshing fails, Rule 803(5) is a separate route.
- E: Rule 803 applies regardless of availability.
Rule: Rule 803(5) requires firsthand knowledge, a record made when fresh, insufficient present memory, and the witness's vouching. The record may be read in, but received as an exhibit only if offered by an adverse party.

## Final 3 · Q21 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(8): law enforcement limit
In a criminal trial, the government offers a patrol officer's written report of what she saw at the scene. The officer has retired, and the government says the report is a public record or, failing that, a business record. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admitted under Rule 803(8), because police reports are records of a public office.
B. Admitted under Rule 803(6), because writing up reports is a regular practice of every police department.
C. Excluded, because Rule 803(8) bars such observations here and Rule 803(6) can't evade that.
D. Admitted, because a retired officer is unavailable under Rule 804(a).
E. Excluded, because a public record is never admissible in a criminal trial.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 803(8)(A)(ii) excludes matters observed by law-enforcement personnel in a criminal case.
- B: Police reports offered against a defendant can't evade the limits of Rule 803(8) through Rule 803(6).
- C: Correct. Rule 803(8)(A)(ii) doesn't reach matters observed by law-enforcement personnel in a criminal case, and the business-records route can't be used to evade that limit.
- D: Retirement alone doesn't show unavailability, and unavailability isn't itself an exception.
- E: This is too broad. Rule 803(8) allows some records in criminal cases, such as the office's activities.
Rule: In a criminal case, Rule 803(8)(A)(ii) doesn't cover matters observed by law-enforcement personnel. Offered against a defendant, such reports can't come in through Rule 803(6) instead.

## Final 3 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Mattox: former testimony at a retrial
At Dale Hart's first murder trial, eyewitness Ivy Lane testified for the prosecution and was thoroughly cross-examined by Hart's lawyer. The conviction was reversed on appeal for an instructional error. Before the retrial, Lane died. The prosecution offers the transcript of her testimony. Hart objects that the jury at his new trial will never see Lane tested by cross-examination.

What is the best ruling?

A. Admit it, because Lane is unavailable and Hart had a prior chance to cross.
B. Exclude it, because confrontation must occur before the jury deciding the case.
C. Exclude it, because the reversal voided the record of the first trial.
D. Admit it only if the prosecution shows the testimony is reliable.
E. Exclude it, because testimony from a prior trial is testimonial.

Answer: A
- A: Correct, though the new jury can't watch her face cross-examination. Mattox v. United States allowed exactly this, and Crawford built on it: testimonial statements are admissible when the declarant is unavailable and the defendant had a prior opportunity to cross-examine. Rule 804(b)(1) also admits the testimony on the hearsay side.
- B: Mattox held that the Clause gives way to "public policy and the necessities of the case" when the witness was cross-examined and has since died.
- C: Reversal for instructional error doesn't erase the testimony or the opportunity to cross-examine.
- D: Crawford rejected reliability as the test. The test is unavailability plus prior opportunity.
- E: It is testimonial, but testimonial statements are admissible with unavailability and a prior opportunity to cross.
Rule: Under Mattox and Crawford, testimonial former testimony of an unavailable witness is admissible if the defendant had a prior opportunity to cross-examine. Rule 804(b)(1) is the hearsay counterpart.

## Final 3 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Giles: forfeiture requires intent
Dwayne is charged with murdering his former girlfriend, Brenda, by shooting her during an argument in her driveway. Three weeks earlier, Brenda gave a recorded interview to a detective at the police station in which she said that Dwayne had choked her and threatened to kill her if she “ever went to the cops again.”

The prosecution offers the recorded interview, arguing that Dwayne forfeited his confrontation right by killing Brenda. Apart from the earlier threat, there is no evidence of why Dwayne shot her other than the argument itself.

What must the court find before admitting the interview over a Confrontation Clause objection?

A. That Dwayne killed Brenda, beyond a reasonable doubt, because the forfeiture finding overlaps with the ultimate issue the jury must decide.
B. That Brenda’s statement bears adequate indicia of reliability, because forfeiture is an equitable doctrine concerned with the trustworthiness of the evidence.
C. That Dwayne’s wrongdoing was designed, at least in part, to keep Brenda from testifying, which his earlier threat may help show.
D. Only that Dwayne killed Brenda, by a preponderance of the evidence, because a defendant whose wrongdoing makes a witness unavailable forfeits the right to confront her.
E. Nothing further, because statements describing threats of violence are not testimonial.

Answer: C
- A: Federal courts use a preponderance standard for the forfeiture finding, as under Rule 104(a). The Supreme Court in Davis noted that practice.
- B: Reliability-based confrontation analysis is the Ohio v. Roberts approach that Crawford rejected. Forfeiture turns on the defendant’s wrongdoing and intent, not on the statement’s reliability.
- C: Correct. Giles requires intent to prevent testimony, and the threat about going to the police is strong evidence of it.
- D: Giles rejected forfeiture based on the killing alone. The defendant must have intended to prevent testimony.
- E: A recorded station-house interview narrating past abuse is testimonial. Its subject matter does not change that.
Rule: Forfeiture by wrongdoing applies only when the defendant engaged in conduct designed to prevent the witness from testifying. Giles v. California. Rule 804(b)(6) likewise requires that the party wrongfully caused or acquiesced in causing the declarant’s unavailability “and did so intending that result.” Federal courts apply a preponderance standard.

## Final 3 · Q24 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Bruton redaction: Gray and Samia
Ruiz and Beck are tried together. Beck, who won't testify, confessed to police. The confession will be read to the jury with a limiting instruction. Which version may be admitted against Beck without violating Ruiz's rights?

A. "Ruiz and I did the robbery."
B. "Deleted and I did the robbery."
C. "[Blank] and I did the robbery."
D. "The man at the defense table and I did the robbery."
E. "The other person I was with and I did the robbery."

Answer: E
- A: This is Bruton itself. A limiting instruction doesn't cure a facially incriminating confession.
- B: Gray holds that obvious redactions like "deleted" fall within Bruton as a class.
- C: A blank works the same way as "deleted" under Gray: it points directly at someone.
- D: This is directly accusatory. It identifies Ruiz as plainly as his name would.
- E: Correct. Samia allows a natural neutral descriptor with a limiting instruction, since it isn't an obvious redaction that directly accuses the defendant.
Rule: Bruton bars a nontestifying codefendant's facially incriminating confession at a joint trial. Removing all reference is fine (Richardson). Obvious blanks aren't (Gray). A natural neutral phrase is (Samia).

## Final 3 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 702 (2023): sufficiency of basis isn't just weight
In a trucking-accident case, the plaintiff's accident reconstructionist estimates the truck's speed at 68 mph. The defense shows that he measured skid marks from a photograph taken after the road was resurfaced and never examined the truck's data recorder, which shows 52 mph. The judge denies the defense's motion to exclude, explaining: “Whether he had enough data goes to weight, not admissibility. Cross-examination will sort it out.” The trial takes place in 2026.

What is the best assessment of the ruling?

A. Correct, because Daubert's gatekeeping applies only to an expert's methods, not to the data he uses.
B. Incorrect, because the judge must find by a preponderance that the opinion rests on sufficient facts or data.
C. Correct, because the proponent need only offer evidence sufficient to support a finding that the basis is adequate.
D. Incorrect, because an expert's opinion must be excluded whenever it conflicts with other evidence in the case.
E. Correct, because once an expert is qualified, the adequacy of his basis is for the jury.

Answer: B
- A: Rule 702(b) expressly requires sufficient facts or data for every expert, and Kumho Tire extended gatekeeping to all expert testimony.
- B: Correct. Rule 702 as amended December 1, 2023 requires the proponent to demonstrate to the court that it is more likely than not that the testimony is based on sufficient facts or data. The Committee Note calls rulings that treat sufficiency of basis as a question of weight “an incorrect application of Rules 702 and 104(a).”
- C: That is the Rule 104(b) conditional-relevance standard. Rule 702's requirements are Rule 104(a) questions decided by the judge by a preponderance, as the 2023 text makes explicit.
- D: Conflicting evidence alone isn't a ground for exclusion; experts on contested facts may both testify, and the jury resolves the facts. The problem here is an inadequate basis, which the judge must evaluate.
- E: The 2023 amendment rejected this approach. Qualification is only the first requirement; the basis must also be sufficient, and the court decides that under Rule 104(a).
Rule: Under Rule 702 as amended in 2023, the proponent must show the court that it is more likely than not that each requirement is met, including sufficient facts or data (702(b)) and a reliable application of the method (702(d)). These are Rule 104(a) questions, not matters of weight.

## Final 3 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 705: sequence of testimony
A valuation expert states an opinion on direct without first describing the data behind it. The opponent objects. What is the correct ruling?

A. Sustained, because the data must always come first.
B. Overruled, because an expert's data may never be disclosed.
C. Sustained, because hearsay bases must be admitted before any opinion.
D. Sustained, unless the expert personally gathered all the data.
E. Overruled, because the data may wait for cross-examination.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 705 lets the expert state the opinion first, unless the court orders otherwise.
- B: The opponent may bring out the data on cross.
- C: Under Rule 703, the bases need not be admissible at all.
- D: Rule 703 allows reliance on data made known to the expert.
- E: Correct. Under Rule 705, unless the court orders otherwise, an expert may state an opinion and reasons without first testifying to the underlying data, but may have to disclose them on cross.
Rule: Rule 705 lets an expert give an opinion first. The opponent may require disclosure of the underlying facts or data on cross.

## Final 3 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 901(b)(6): an outgoing call and self-identification
Investigating a fraud, Detective Moreno calls the number the phone company lists for Hal Price. A man answers, “Price speaking.” When Moreno says he is calling about a car Price sold, the man says, “That Honda's odometer was fine when I sold it,” and hangs up. Moreno has never heard Price's voice. At Price's trial for odometer fraud, the prosecution offers Moreno's testimony about the call. Price objects that the speaker wasn't identified.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit the testimony only if phone-company records are introduced to prove the call was completed.
B. Exclude the testimony, because Moreno can identify the speaker only by recognizing his voice.
C. Exclude the testimony, because the speaker denied wrongdoing, so the call is irrelevant whoever he was.
D. Exclude the testimony, because a speaker's self-identification is never enough to authenticate a phone call.
E. Admit the testimony, because the call went to Price's listed number and the speaker identified himself.

Answer: E
- A: Moreno can testify from his own knowledge that he dialed the listed number. Phone records might corroborate the call but aren't required.
- B: Voice identification under Rule 901(b)(5) is one route. Rule 901(b)(6) provides another, based on the number called and the circumstances.
- C: A denial can be highly relevant: if the government shows the odometer was rolled back, a false statement that it was fine helps prove knowledge. Relevance doesn't depend on the statement being true.
- D: Self-identification alone doesn't authenticate an incoming call, since anyone can claim a name. But Rule 901(b)(6)(A) treats self-identification on an outgoing call to the number assigned to that person as enough.
- E: Correct. Rule 901(b)(6)(A) authenticates a telephone conversation by evidence that a call was made to the number assigned at the time to a particular person, if circumstances, including self-identification, show that the person answering was the one called. The speaker's knowledge of the car sale adds to it (901(b)(4)).
Rule: Rule 901(b)(6)(A): a call to the number assigned to a particular person is authenticated if circumstances, including self-identification, show that the person answering was the one called. Voice identification (901(b)(5)) and distinctive contents (901(b)(4)) are other routes.

## Final 3 · Q28 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 1001(d): a printout of electronic data is an original
In a suit over a software license, the plaintiff offers a printout of an email in which the defendant's sales manager promised free upgrades for five years. A paralegal testifies that she printed it from the plaintiff's email account and that the printout accurately shows the email as stored. The defendant objects that the “original” is the electronic file on the server, so the printout violates the best evidence rule.

How should the court rule?

A. Sustain the objection unless the plaintiff shows the electronic file is lost or destroyed under Rule 1004.
B. Overrule the objection, because an accurate printout of electronically stored information is an original.
C. Overrule the objection, because the best evidence rule doesn't apply to emails, which aren't writings.
D. Overrule the objection, but only if the defendant was shown the electronic file before trial.
E. Sustain the objection, because the paralegal didn't write the email and can't testify to its contents.

Answer: B
- A: No excuse is needed, because the printout is itself an original. Rule 1004 matters only when the proponent offers other evidence of content instead of an original or a duplicate.
- B: Correct. Rule 1001(d) provides that for electronically stored information, “original” means any printout or other output readable by sight if it accurately reflects the information. The paralegal's testimony supports that, so Rule 1002 is satisfied.
- C: Rule 1001(a) defines writings to include letters, words, or numbers set down in any form, which covers emails. The rule applies, and the printout satisfies it.
- D: There's no such condition. A printout that accurately reflects the stored data is an original; a dispute about accuracy goes to authentication and to the jury under Rule 1008.
- E: The paralegal isn't testifying to the email's content from memory; she authenticates the printout under Rule 901(b)(1). The printout proves the content, and it is an original.
Rule: Rule 1001(d): an original of electronically stored information includes any printout or other readable output that accurately reflects it. Rule 1003 admits duplicates unless there is a genuine question about authenticity or admitting the duplicate would be unfair.

## Final 3 · Q29 · Privileges — Rule 502(e): a clawback agreement without an order
In a federal patent case, the parties sign a clawback agreement providing that producing a privileged document won't waive the privilege, but they never ask the court to enter it as an order. The plaintiff produces a privileged engineering memo without reviewing it. In a separate state-court suit, a different company subpoenas the memo, arguing that the privilege was waived.

Which statement is most accurate?

A. The memo stays privileged everywhere, because a disclosure in a federal case can never waive a privilege in state court.
B. The agreement protects the memo in every proceeding, because Rule 502 makes clawback agreements binding on all courts.
C. The memo is protected in state court only if that state has adopted its own version of Rule 502.
D. The agreement is void, because parties can't contract around the federal rules on privilege.
E. The agreement binds only the parties to it, so it doesn't stop the other company from arguing waiver.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 502(b) prevents waiver only for an inadvertent disclosure by a holder who took reasonable precautions. Producing the memo without any review likely fails that test, so waiver is a real risk.
- B: That describes a court order under Rule 502(d). Under Rule 502(e), an agreement on the effect of disclosure binds only the parties unless it is incorporated into a court order.
- C: Rule 502 applies in state proceedings by its own terms (502(f)), so state adoption isn't the issue. The issue is that the agreement binds only the parties.
- D: Rule 502(e) expressly recognizes such agreements, and they are enforceable between the parties. They just don't bind anyone else.
- E: Correct. Rule 502(e) provides that an agreement on the effect of disclosure in a federal proceeding binds only the parties to it, unless it is incorporated into a court order. A Rule 502(d) order would have bound every other federal and state proceeding, regardless of the care taken.
Rule: Rule 502(d): a federal court's order that disclosure is not a waiver binds every other federal and state proceeding. Rule 502(e): a party agreement binds only the parties unless it is incorporated into a court order.

## Final 3 · Q30 · Privileges — Swidler: privilege survives death
A company executive consults a lawyer about a federal investigation and dies a week later. A grand jury subpoenas the lawyer's notes, arguing the need is great and the client has no further interest. No crime-fraud claim is made. What is the most likely result?

A. The notes stay protected only if the client's family claims the privilege.
B. The notes are produced, because the privilege doesn't apply in criminal cases.
C. The notes stay protected, because the privilege survives the client's death.
D. The notes are produced, because the privilege ends when the client dies.
E. The judge balances the need for the notes against the client's interests.

Answer: C
- A: The rule doesn't depend on a family member making the claim.
- B: The privilege applies the same way in criminal and civil cases.
- C: Correct. Under Swidler & Berlin, clients care about reputation and harm to family after death, so the privilege survives, with no case-by-case balancing.
- D: Swidler & Berlin holds that the privilege survives death.
- E: Swidler & Berlin rejected posthumous balancing, which would add the uncertainty Jaffee rejected.
Rule: The lawyer-client privilege survives the client's death under federal common law, with no balancing (Swidler & Berlin).

## Final 4 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Rule 606(b)(2)(B): outside influence, not its effect
During deliberations in a federal arson trial, the court security officer escorting the jurors to lunch remarks, “Don’t waste much time on this one. Everybody at the courthouse knows he torched his last place too.” After a guilty verdict, a juror tells defense counsel what the officer said and adds that the remark persuaded her and two other holdouts to vote guilty. The defendant moves for a new trial. What juror testimony may the court receive?

A. Only testimony from the officer himself, because jurors may never testify about events during deliberations.
B. Testimony about the remark, but only if the officer confirms it, because juror testimony needs corroboration.
C. Testimony that the officer made the remark to the jurors, but not testimony about how it affected anyone’s vote.
D. Testimony about both the remark and its effect, because the defendant can’t show prejudice without proof that it changed votes.
E. None, because the remark came during deliberations, and Tanner treats everything that happens during deliberations as internal.

Answer: C
- A: Nonjuror evidence, such as the officer’s own account, is always available. But 606(b)(2) also lets jurors testify that extraneous information or an outside influence reached the jury.
- B: Rule 606(b)(2)(B) has no corroboration requirement. A juror may testify that an outside influence was improperly brought to bear on any juror; the officer’s account would help on the facts but isn’t a precondition.
- C: Correct. A court officer’s comment about the defendant’s supposed past is both extraneous prejudicial information and an outside influence, so 606(b)(2)(A)–(B) let a juror testify that it reached the jury. But 606(b)(1) still bars testimony about “the effect of anything on that juror’s or another juror’s vote.” The court judges prejudice objectively, asking how the remark would likely affect a typical juror.
- D: The exceptions let a juror testify only about whether the outside matter reached the jury. Proof of its actual effect on votes stays barred, and courts don’t need it: they ask whether the information was likely to prejudice an average juror.
- E: Tanner turned on the nature of the matter, not its timing. Drugs and alcohol were internal, like fatigue or illness. A court officer’s comment about the defendant is the classic outside influence, the kind the common law always let jurors report.
Rule: Rule 606(b)(2)(A)–(B): a juror may testify that extraneous prejudicial information or an outside influence reached the jury, but never about its effect on any juror’s vote or mental processes. The court judges prejudice objectively.

## Final 4 · Q2 · Relevance and Rule 403 — People v. Collins: invented, dependent probabilities
In a robbery trial, witnesses saw the robber flee in a green pickup with a ladder rack, driven by a man with a red beard and a neck tattoo. The prosecution calls a statistics professor who, using estimates he says are “reasonable” (one in 10 trucks is green, one in 20 has a ladder rack, one in 15 men has a red beard, one in 25 has a neck tattoo), multiplies the figures and testifies that there is only a 1-in-75,000 chance that a random man would match. The defendant matches. What is the defense’s best objection?

A. The professor isn’t qualified, because only a forensic scientist may testify about how often physical traits occur.
B. The testimony is irrelevant because the defendant has no burden to prove someone else committed the crime.
C. None, because experts may rely on reasonable estimates, and any weakness goes to weight, not admissibility.
D. The figures are invented and may not be independent, and they invite the jury to equate a rare description with guilt.
E. Statistical evidence offered to prove identity is inadmissible as a matter of law after People v. Collins.

Answer: D
- A: A statistician may well be qualified to do the arithmetic. The defects are in the inputs, which are invented and not independent, and in the leap from a rare description to guilt (People v. Collins).
- B: That confuses burdens with relevance. The real issues are foundation and the misleading leap from a description’s rarity to the probability of guilt.
- C: When the numbers have no evidentiary basis and the method assumes an independence it can’t show, the testimony lacks an adequate foundation. Its danger of misleading the jury also warrants 403 exclusion.
- D: Correct. That was People v. Collins: no foundation (made-up probabilities and non-independent factors, as a moustache is a subset of a beard) and distraction. A 1-in-75,000 description says nothing about whether this matching man, rather than another, was the robber, and an impressive expert can overwhelm the jury’s own weighing.
- E: Collins doesn’t bar probabilistic evidence. DNA match statistics are routinely admitted (People v. Turner), and market-share liability rests on probabilities. The problem is foundation and misuse.
Rule: Collins: probability evidence needs a foundation (real data, independent factors) and can’t turn a description’s rarity into a probability of guilt. Probabilistic evidence isn’t barred per se (DNA).

## Final 4 · Q3 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Old Chief: status stipulations
A defendant charged as a felon in possession of a firearm offers to stipulate that he has a prior felony. His prior is attempted murder with a firearm. The government refuses and offers the conviction record naming the crime. What should the court do?

A. Admit the record, because the government may always prove its case its own way.
B. Exclude the record as irrelevant, because a fact the defendant stipulates to has no remaining probative value.
C. Accept the stipulation, because the crime's name adds little and risks a propensity inference.
D. Force the government to accept any stipulation a defendant offers on any element.
E. Admit the record, because a limiting instruction would always cure the prejudice.

Answer: C
- A: That is the general rule, but Old Chief creates an exception when the only issue is legal status.
- B: The record stays relevant under Rule 401. The problem arises under Rule 403.
- C: Correct. Under Old Chief, when the element is only legal status, the name of the offense adds nothing and risks a propensity inference, so Rule 403 requires accepting the concession.
- D: Old Chief is confined to status elements. Ordinarily the prosecution need not accept a stipulation.
- E: Old Chief found an abuse of discretion despite the availability of instructions. Rule 403 weighs evidentiary alternatives.
Rule: Under Rule 403 a court weighs evidentiary alternatives. Old Chief requires accepting a stipulation to prior-conviction status, but it is confined to status elements; otherwise the prosecution may prove its case its own way.

## Final 4 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 407: the narrow impeachment exception
A shopper trips over a hose stretched across a garden-center aisle and sues the store. After the accident, the store installed retractable hose reels at the end of every aisle. At trial the store manager testifies on direct, “When I walked that aisle an hour before she fell, I thought it looked reasonably safe.” The plaintiff offers the hose reels to impeach him. How should the court rule?

A. Admit the evidence, because any defense testimony that the premises were safe opens the door to impeachment with later repairs.
B. Admit the evidence to show that the store controlled the aisle, with a limiting instruction.
C. Exclude the evidence, because Rule 407 bars evidence of later repairs for every purpose.
D. Admit the evidence to prove feasibility, because the store didn’t stipulate that hose reels were feasible.
E. Exclude it, because a later improvement doesn’t contradict his belief that the aisle was reasonably safe then.

Answer: E
- A: That reading would let the exception swallow the rule. It reaches testimony the later measure actually contradicts, like the claim in the class hypothetical that a $55.26 carpet fix would have cost $40,000.
- B: Control is a permitted purpose only if disputed, and nothing suggests the store denied controlling its own aisle.
- C: 407 bars only the listed uses: negligence, culpable conduct, a defect, or a need for a warning. Impeachment and disputed ownership, control, or feasibility remain open. The evidence fails here because it doesn’t actually impeach.
- D: Feasibility is a permitted purpose only “if disputed.” The store never claimed hose reels were impractical, and it doesn’t have to stipulate to keep feasibility out of dispute.
- E: Correct. Courts read the impeachment exception narrowly, or it would swallow the rule, since nearly every defense witness denies negligence. A later improvement doesn’t contradict a belief that conditions were reasonably safe before the accident: wiser after isn’t foolish before. A sweeping claim, such as that no safer arrangement was possible, would be different.
Rule: Rule 407’s impeachment exception is narrow: the later measure must contradict the testimony, not merely a denial of negligence. Feasibility, ownership, and control are permitted purposes only if disputed.

## Final 4 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 408(b): settlement to show a witness's bias
Carla Ruiz sued two drivers, Ames and Burke, for injuries from a three-car collision. Before trial, Ruiz settled with Ames. Ames paid nothing, and Ruiz agreed to release him if he testified "fully and truthfully" at trial. Ames now testifies for Ruiz that Burke ran the red light. Burke offers the settlement agreement on cross-examination of Ames. Ruiz objects under Rule 408.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because Rule 408 bars evidence of any compromise on behalf of any party.
B. Exclude it, because the settlement was with Ames, not between Ruiz and Burke.
C. Admit it as substantive evidence that Ames, not Burke, was at fault.
D. Admit it to show Ames's bias, with an instruction limiting its use.
E. Admit it, but only if Ames first denies on cross that he settled.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 408(a) does reach compromises on behalf of any party, which is why this answer is tempting. But it bars only the use to prove or disprove the validity or amount of a claim, or to impeach by inconsistency or contradiction. Rule 408(b) expressly allows proof of a witness's bias.
- B: Courts may apply Rule 408 to settlements with third parties. That doesn't help Ruiz, because the bias purpose remains.
- C: Using the settlement to show that Ames was liable is exactly what Rule 408(a) bars.
- D: Correct, even though it puts the settlement in front of the jury. A deal that frees Ames from liability only if he testifies gives him a reason to favor Ruiz. Rule 408(b) permits evidence of a witness's "bias or prejudice," and Rule 105 limits its use.
- E: Bias is never collateral and may be shown extrinsically. Admission doesn't depend on Ames denying it.
Rule: Rule 408 bars compromise evidence only to prove or disprove the validity or amount of a disputed claim, or to impeach by inconsistency or contradiction. Rule 408(b) permits other purposes, such as a witness's bias, negating undue delay, or proving obstruction.

## Final 4 · Q6 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Mezzanatto: waiving Rule 410 for impeachment
To get a proffer session with federal prosecutors, Tony Vega signed an agreement stating that if he testified at trial inconsistently with his proffer statements, those statements could be used to impeach him. In the session, Vega admitted being at the warehouse during a drug sale. No plea agreement resulted. At trial, Vega testifies that he was never at the warehouse, and the prosecutor offers his proffer statement on cross.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because Rule 410's protections can't be waived by the defendant.
B. Admit it, because Rule 410 always permits plea statements to be used for impeachment.
C. Admit it to impeach, because Vega knowingly and voluntarily waived Rule 410.
D. Exclude it, because plea statements may be used only in a later perjury prosecution.
E. Exclude it, because statements to prosecutors are protected only if a guilty plea results.

Answer: C
- A: Mezzanatto held that Rule 410's protections are waivable, like many constitutional rights.
- B: Congress removed the impeachment use from Rule 410 in 1975. Absent a waiver, the statement would be barred.
- C: Correct. United States v. Mezzanatto upheld a knowing and voluntary agreement allowing plea-discussion statements to impeach a defendant who testifies inconsistently. The statements otherwise fall within Rule 410(a)(4).
- D: Rule 410(b)(2) is one exception (perjury, made under oath, on the record, with counsel), but a valid waiver is another route.
- E: Rule 410(a)(4) protects discussions that didn't result in a guilty plea, which is the situation here.
Rule: Under Rule 410(a)(4), statements in plea discussions with a prosecutor that don't produce a guilty plea are inadmissible against the defendant, even to impeach. Under Mezzanatto, a defendant may knowingly and voluntarily waive that protection.

## Final 4 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Rule 413 beats a strained Rule 404(b) theory
Owen Pratt is charged in federal court with sexually assaulting a woman he met through a dating app, and he claims consent. Twenty days before trial, the prosecution disclosed testimony from another woman, who says Pratt assaulted her two years ago after a date arranged through the same app. The prosecution argues the evidence shows "a common plan to use the app to find victims."

What is the best basis for admitting the evidence?

A. Rule 404(b)(2), as evidence of a common plan to find victims on the app.
B. Rule 404(b)(2), as evidence of intent, because consent is disputed.
C. Rule 413, which lets the jury consider it for any purpose, including propensity.
D. Rule 406, because his repeated use of the same dating app shows a habit.
E. Rule 404(a)(2)(A), because Pratt put his character in issue by claiming consent.

Answer: C
- A: The prosecution's own theory, but a weak one. Two separate assaults through the same app are a pattern, not parts of one overall scheme (Kirsch).
- B: Intent is closer, but it still requires a non-propensity chain. Here the reasoning is essentially "he did it before." Rule 413 makes that reasoning lawful.
- C: Correct. The answer that sounds least careful is right: Rule 413 openly permits the propensity inference that Rule 404(b) forbids. Pratt is accused of sexual assault, the other act is a sexual assault, and the evidence was disclosed at least 15 days before trial. The court still applies Rule 403 with its normal balance (United States v. Guardia).
- D: Using an app on two occasions isn't a semi-automatic response to a recurring situation.
- E: Claiming consent isn't offering evidence of his own good character.
Rule: In sexual-assault cases, Rule 413 (and Rules 414–415) admits other sexual assaults for any relevant purpose, including propensity, subject to disclosure and ordinary Rule 403 balancing. There is no need to fit the evidence into a Rule 404(b)(2) purpose.

## Final 4 · Q8 · Character Evidence — Rule 414: uncharged, decades-old act
Wade Lyle is charged in federal court with molesting his nine-year-old niece. Seventeen days before trial, the prosecution disclosed a statement from Lyle's nephew, now 28, who would testify that Lyle molested him when he was eight. The nephew never reported it, and Lyle was never charged. Lyle objects that the act is uncharged, 20 years old, and offered only to show he is the kind of person who molests children.

What is the most likely ruling?

A. Exclude it, because Rule 414 admits only other acts of molestation that resulted in a conviction.
B. Admit it only for a Rule 404(b)(2) purpose such as plan or intent, with a limiting instruction.
C. Admit it without balancing, because Rule 414 makes other child molestation admissible as of right.
D. Exclude it unless the prosecution proves the earlier act by clear and convincing evidence.
E. Admit it, including for propensity, if a jury could find it occurred and Rule 403 allows.

Answer: E
- A: Rules 413–415 require no conviction. Any other act of child molestation may qualify.
- B: Rule 414(a) is a true exception to the propensity ban. The evidence "may be considered on any matter to which it is relevant," including propensity, so it needs no Rule 404(b) purpose.
- C: Rule 414 says the court "may" admit. Rule 403 still applies with its normal balance (United States v. Guardia).
- D: Courts use the Huddleston standard, under which a jury could reasonably find the act by a preponderance. Rule 414 has no clear-and-convincing requirement.
- E: Correct. Rule 414(a) admits other acts of child molestation for any relevant purpose, including propensity. The act need only be provable under the Huddleston standard, and ordinary Rule 403 balancing still applies (Guardia). Remoteness counts in that balance but isn't a bar. Disclosure 17 days before trial met the 15-day requirement.
Rule: Rules 413–415 let other sexual-assault or child-molestation acts be used for propensity. No conviction is needed, the Huddleston sufficiency standard applies, the proponent must disclose them 15 days before trial, and Rule 403 balancing applies as usual.

## Final 4 · Q9 · Character Evidence — Rule 405(a) cross: Rule 609's time limit doesn't apply
Lyle Banner is charged with assault. He calls a neighbor, who testifies that Banner has a reputation in the community as a peaceful man. On cross, the prosecutor, who has a certified record in hand, asks the neighbor: "Have you heard that Lyle Banner was convicted of aggravated assault fifteen years ago?" The defense objects.

What is the best ruling?

A. Sustain it, because Rule 609(b) bars convictions more than 10 years old.
B. Sustain it, because specific instances can't be raised after reputation evidence.
C. Sustain it, because only an arrest, not a conviction, may be asked about.
D. Overrule it, but only if the prosecution first proves the conviction by record.
E. Overrule it, subject to Rule 403, because cross may test what the character witness has heard.

Answer: E
- A: The trap. Rule 609 governs impeachment of a witness's character for truthfulness by conviction. Here the prosecutor is testing a Rule 405(a) character witness's knowledge of the defendant's reputation for peacefulness. Remoteness is weighed under Rule 403, but Rule 609(b) isn't the rule.
- B: Rule 405(a) expressly allows inquiry into specific instances on cross of a character witness.
- C: Convictions and arrests may both be the subject of such questions if there is a good-faith basis (Michelson).
- D: On cross under Rule 405(a), the prosecutor asks but doesn't prove the act extrinsically. The question tests the witness. It doesn't introduce the conviction as substantive evidence.
- E: Correct, though it means a 15-year-old conviction reaches the jury. The question tests whether the neighbor really knows Banner's reputation. The prosecutor has a good-faith basis, and the court weighs remoteness under Rule 403 and gives a limiting instruction.
Rule: Once an accused offers a character witness, Rule 405(a) allows cross into relevant specific instances, including old convictions, to test the witness's basis, with a good-faith basis and Rule 403 control (Michelson). Rule 609's time limit doesn't apply to this use.

## Final 4 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 610: religion and bias
A patient's estate sues St. Brendan's Hospital, which is owned and operated by a church, for wrongful death. On cross-examination of a defense nurse, the plaintiff's counsel asks two questions: (1) "You're a member of the church that runs St. Brendan's, aren't you?" and (2) "And your faith teaches that lying is a grave sin, so you'd never shade the truth for anyone, would you?"

Which questions are proper?

A. Neither, because Rule 610 bars any reference at all to a witness's religion.
B. Both, because a witness's religious beliefs bear on whether she tells the truth.
C. (1) only, because membership in the church that runs the defendant hospital may show the nurse's bias.
D. (2) only, because it concerns her truthfulness rather than her church affiliation.
E. (1) only, and only if the nurse first testifies about her religion on direct.

Answer: C
- A: The ACN to Rule 610 says inquiry to show interest or bias because of religious affiliation, as with a church that is a party, isn't within the prohibition.
- B: Rule 610 bars religious beliefs or opinions offered to attack or support credibility.
- C: Correct. Rule 610 bars evidence of religious beliefs to show that a witness's credibility is impaired or enhanced. Question (2) does that. Membership in the church that operates the defendant hospital shows potential bias toward a party, which the ACN permits. That makes (1) proper.
- D: Question (2) uses the nurse's religious beliefs to assess her truthfulness, which is exactly what Rule 610 forbids.
- E: Bias may be explored on cross whether or not the witness raised her religion on direct.
Rule: Rule 610 bars evidence of a witness's religious beliefs to attack or support credibility. Religious affiliation offered to show bias or interest, as with a church that is a party, isn't barred.

## Final 4 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 608(b): ask about the act, not the arrest
In a contract suit, the defense wants to cross-examine the plaintiff's key witness about the fact that two years ago she was arrested for insurance fraud, for falsely reporting her car stolen. The charges were dropped. Defense counsel has the police report and an insurance investigator's file showing the car was found in her cousin's garage.

Which question may the court permit?

A. "You were arrested for insurance fraud two years ago, weren't you?"
B. "You falsely reported your car stolen two years ago, didn't you?"
C. Both questions, because a witness may be asked about any arrest.
D. Neither question, because the charges against her were dropped.
E. Neither question, unless the investigator first proves the act.

Answer: B
- A: An arrest isn't conduct of the witness. It is the police's suspicion, and it isn't probative of her character for truthfulness. Rule 609 requires a conviction.
- B: Correct. Rule 608(b) allows inquiry on cross into specific instances of the witness's own conduct that are probative of untruthfulness, with a good-faith basis (Whitmore). The defense has one in the police and insurance files. Making a false report is the act, and the arrest is irrelevant to it.
- C: Arrests aren't proper Rule 608(b) subjects for this purpose. (Arrests may be asked about on cross of a Rule 405(a) character witness, which is a different situation.)
- D: Dropped charges don't bar inquiry into the underlying conduct, which a conviction isn't needed to show.
- E: Rule 608(b) bars extrinsic evidence. Counsel may ask with a good-faith basis but must take the answer.
Rule: Under Rule 608(b), cross may ask about the witness's own acts that are probative of untruthfulness, with a good-faith basis and no extrinsic proof. An arrest or charge isn't the act, and only convictions come in under Rule 609.

## Final 4 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609(b): the clock and crimen falsi
In a 2026 civil trial, a non-party witness has two convictions: (1) misdemeanor perjury in 2014, fine only; and (2) felony burglary in 2012, released from prison in 2019. The opposing party seeks to impeach with both. Which analysis is correct?

A. Both must be admitted with no balancing, because each is under 15 years old.
B. (1) must be admitted with no balancing; (2) requires the reverse balance of Rule 609(b).
C. (1) requires the Rule 609(b) reverse balance and notice; (2) is governed by Rule 403.
D. (1) is excluded, because a misdemeanor can't impeach; (2) is governed by Rule 403.
E. Both require the reverse balance, because each conviction is over 10 years old.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 609 has no 15-year line. The 10-year limit of Rule 609(b) is the relevant one.
- B: Rule 609(b) applies to crimen falsi too, and the burglary clock runs from release in 2019.
- C: Correct. The perjury is 12 years old with no confinement, so Rule 609(b) applies even to crimen falsi. The burglary clock runs from the 2019 release, leaving it under Rule 609(a)(1)(A) and Rule 403.
- D: Rule 609(a)(2) covers any crime involving a dishonest act or false statement, whatever the punishment.
- E: The 10 years run from the later of conviction or release, so the burglary is inside the window.
Rule: Rule 609(b) runs from the later of conviction or release and applies even to crimen falsi. Past 10 years, probative value must substantially outweigh prejudice, with written notice.

## Final 4 · Q13 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 412(b)(1)(B): behavior with the accused
At a sexual-assault trial, the defendant claims the encounter was consensual. Which evidence is most likely admissible, assuming a timely Rule 412(c) motion?

A. The complainant's reputation in her neighborhood for promiscuity, offered to show consent.
B. Her earlier consensual relationship with another man, offered to show she likely consented.
C. Her earlier consensual encounters with the defendant, offered by the defendant to show consent.
D. A former partner's opinion that she is sexually adventurous, offered to show consent.
E. Her style of dress and dating habits, offered to show sexual predisposition.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 412(a) bars this, and criminal cases have no reputation route in Rule 412(b)(1).
- B: Past consent with someone else is the inference Rule 412 exists to prevent.
- C: Correct. Rule 412(b)(1)(B) allows specific instances of sexual behavior with the accused, offered by the defendant to prove consent.
- D: Rule 412(b)(1) allows only specific instances, and this opinion goes to predisposition.
- E: Rule 412(a)(2) bars evidence offered to prove a victim's sexual predisposition.
Rule: In criminal cases, Rule 412(b)(1) allows specific instances to prove another source of physical evidence, instances with the accused to prove consent, and evidence whose exclusion would violate the Constitution.

## Final 4 · Q14 · Definition of Hearsay — Machine output
To prove a delivery truck was at a loading dock at 2:14 a.m., the plaintiff offers a printout automatically generated by the truck's GPS unit. No person entered the data. The defendant objects on hearsay grounds. What is the best ruling?

A. Overruled, because the printout is self-authenticating under Rule 902.
B. Sustained, because the printout is an out-of-court assertion offered for its truth.
C. Sustained, unless a technician testifies to the unit's records under Rule 803(6).
D. Overruled, because the printout is not a statement made by a person.
E. Overruled, because any computer record is a business record.

Answer: D
- A: Authentication is a separate question from hearsay, and the objection here is hearsay.
- B: Rule 801(a) requires a person's assertion. A machine's automatic output isn't one.
- C: No exception is needed if the output isn't hearsay.
- D: Correct. Hearsay requires a statement by a person under Rule 801(a)–(b). Machine output isn't hearsay, though it must still be authenticated, as under Rule 901(b)(9).
- E: Rule 803(6) has requirements of its own, and data aren't business records just because they are on a computer.
Rule: Machine output isn't a person's statement, so it isn't hearsay. The proponent still authenticates it, typically by showing the process produces an accurate result under Rule 901(b)(9).

## Final 4 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Pre-arrest silence to impeach
Kurt Rowe shot Leon Ivers on May 3. Rowe didn't contact police, and he was arrested on May 17 and given Miranda warnings, after which he stayed silent. At trial Rowe testifies for the first time that Ivers attacked him with a knife. On cross, the prosecutor asks: (1) "In the two weeks before your arrest, you never went to police to report that you'd been attacked, did you?" and (2) "And after your arrest and warnings, you never told the detectives about any knife, did you?"

Which questions are proper?

A. Both, because a defendant who testifies waives any objection to questions about his silence.
B. Neither, because using a defendant's silence against him always violates the Fifth Amendment.
C. (2) only, because silence after Miranda warnings is the most probative kind of silence.
D. (1) only, because Doyle protects only a defendant's silence after Miranda warnings from impeachment use.
E. Neither, because silence can't be an adoptive admission unless he was accused to his face.

Answer: D
- A: Testifying doesn't open post-Miranda silence to impeachment (Doyle).
- B: Pre-arrest silence may be used to impeach a testifying defendant (Jenkins). The constitutional bar of Doyle covers silence after Miranda warnings.
- C: Doyle forbids exactly that question. Post-Miranda silence is "insolubly ambiguous," and the warnings implicitly promise it won't be penalized.
- D: Correct. Doyle bars impeachment with silence after Miranda warnings. Pre-arrest silence carries no such implied promise and may be used to impeach a testifying defendant whose story, if true, one would have expected him to report.
- E: Question (1) isn't offered as an adoptive admission but to impeach Rowe's trial testimony. That use doesn't require an accusation.
Rule: Under Doyle, post-Miranda silence can't be used to impeach. Pre-arrest and pre-Miranda silence may be used to impeach a defendant who testifies. Adoption by silence requires an accusation that would naturally call for a denial.

## Final 4 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(2)(E) in a civil case
Grove Hospital sues two medical-equipment suppliers under state antitrust law, alleging they secretly agreed to fix prices on hospital beds. No one has been criminally charged. Grove offers an email from a sales manager at Supplier A to a sales manager at Supplier B, sent during the alleged agreement: "Hold the line at $9,800 per bed this quarter—Supplier B's boss is on board." Grove offers it against Supplier B. Other evidence shows matching price increases and secret meetings.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because the coconspirator rule applies only in criminal cases.
B. Admit it against Supplier B as a coconspirator's statement in furtherance.
C. Exclude it, because the sales manager works for Supplier A, not B.
D. Admit it only against Supplier A, as its own employee's statement.
E. Exclude it, because no conspiracy has been proved by a conviction.

Answer: B
- A: Rule 801(d)(2)(E) applies in civil and criminal cases. No conspiracy charge is required, and a joint venture suffices.
- B: Correct, though Supplier B had no control over another company's employee. If the judge finds by a preponderance under Rule 104(a), using the email plus the independent evidence of meetings and pricing, that the two suppliers conspired and the email furthered it, it is admissible against B (Bourjaily).
- C: Rule 801(d)(2)(E) is about coconspirators, not employees.
- D: It is also admissible against Supplier A under Rule 801(d)(2)(D), but Rule 801(d)(2)(E) reaches Supplier B too.
- E: The foundation is a preponderance finding by the judge, not a conviction.
Rule: Rule 801(d)(2)(E) applies in civil cases. A statement by a coconspirator during and in furtherance of a conspiracy or joint venture is admissible against each member, once the judge finds the foundation under Rule 104(a) with some independent evidence.

## Final 4 · Q17 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(1)(C): identification
A mugging victim picked Lowe from a lineup two days after the crime. At trial she testifies and answers questions, but says she can no longer remember what her attacker looked like. The officer who ran the lineup testifies that she picked Lowe. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Excluded, because an out-of-court identification must be repeated in court.
B. Admitted, because she testified and identified a person she perceived earlier.
C. Excluded, because her memory loss means she is not subject to cross-examination.
D. Admitted, but only to impeach her testimony about her present memory.
E. Excluded, because the officer has no personal knowledge of the mugging.

Answer: B
- A: The rule admits the identification even if the witness now can't or doesn't identify in court.
- B: Correct. Rule 801(d)(1)(C) makes the identification not hearsay when the declarant testifies and is subject to cross. Under United States v. Owens, memory loss doesn't change that.
- C: United States v. Owens rejected this. Memory loss doesn't make a testifying witness unavailable for cross.
- D: The identification is substantive evidence under Rule 801(d)(1)(C), not just impeachment.
- E: The officer has personal knowledge of what happened at the lineup, which is what he reports.
Rule: Rule 801(d)(1)(C) admits a prior identification if the declarant testifies and is subject to cross, even with memory loss or a misidentification in court (United States v. Owens).

## Final 4 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(3): civil liability, no corroboration needed
A fence company sues Jake Moss for damage done when Moss's car crashed through its fence at 2 a.m. Moss says his friend Rob Tully borrowed the car and was driving. Tully died last month. Moss offers testimony from Tully's brother that a week after the crash Tully told him, privately, "I'm the one who wrecked Jake's car into that fence. Don't tell anyone." The company objects that the statement is uncorroborated hearsay.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because a statement against interest needs corroboration of its trustworthiness.
B. Admit it as against Tully's interest; corroboration isn't required in a civil case.
C. Exclude it, because a statement made in private to a relative isn't against interest.
D. Admit it, because Tully's death makes all of his relevant statements admissible.
E. Exclude it, because Moss, who benefits from the statement, can't be the one to offer it.

Answer: B
- A: The corroboration requirement in Rule 804(b)(3)(B) applies only to a statement offered in a criminal case as one that tends to expose the declarant to criminal liability.
- B: Correct. Tully is unavailable because he died (Rule 804(a)(4)). A reasonable person wouldn't admit wrecking another's car into a fence unless it were true, because the admission exposes him to civil liability. In a civil case, Rule 804(b)(3) requires no corroboration.
- C: The test is objective, about whether a reasonable person would make the statement only if true. A private admission to family still exposes the speaker to liability.
- D: Unavailability is only a prerequisite. The statement must still fit an exception in Rule 804(b).
- E: Any party may offer a statement against interest. It isn't limited to use against the declarant.
Rule: Rule 804(b)(3) admits an unavailable declarant's statement that a reasonable person would make only if true because it was so contrary to pecuniary, proprietary, or penal interest, or to civil liability. Corroboration is required only for penal-interest statements in criminal cases.

## Final 4 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(3) vs. 803(4): present and past pain
Tina Marr sues for back injuries from a fall in a store. Her coworker would testify to three statements Marr made to her:

I. Two days after the fall: "My back is killing me right now. I can barely sit."
II. A month after the fall: "Last week my back hurt so badly I couldn't sleep."
III. Two days after the fall: "This all started when I slipped on that grease."

The coworker has no medical role. Which statements are admissible to prove Marr's pain or its cause?

A. I only.
B. I and II only.
C. I and III only.
D. II and III only.
E. I, II, and III.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Item I describes then-existing pain and is admissible under Rule 803(3) whoever hears it. Item II describes past pain, which Rule 803(3) excludes as memory, and Rule 803(4) doesn't apply because the coworker wasn't consulted for diagnosis or treatment. Item III describes cause, which Rule 803(4) covers only when made for medical purposes.
- B: Item II looks backward. Rule 803(3) covers only a then-existing condition, and statements of memory are excluded.
- C: Item III states past events and cause to a layperson. Rule 803(3) doesn't cover it, and Rule 803(4) requires a medical purpose.
- D: Items II and III both fail, for the reasons above, and Item I qualifies.
- E: Only the statement of present pain qualifies.
Rule: Rule 803(3) covers then-existing physical condition (pain) said to anyone, but not past pain or its cause. Rule 803(4) reaches past symptoms and general cause only when the statement is made for medical diagnosis or treatment.

## Final 4 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(7): absence of a business record
A patient's estate sues Bayview Hospital, claiming nurses never gave the patient the antibiotic his doctor ordered for the night of March 3. The estate offers the hospital's electronic medication record, which shows no entry of any antibiotic dose that night. Hospital policy requires nurses to scan every dose into the record when they give it. The estate authenticates the record with the custodian's written certification that it meets Rule 803(6)(A)–(C), served on the hospital a month before trial. The hospital offers no evidence that the system was unreliable but objects.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit it to prove no dose was given, because such an entry would regularly have been made.
B. Exclude the evidence, because the absence of an entry is not itself a record of anything.
C. Admit the evidence only if the estate first proves the system was reliable, because the proponent bears that burden.
D. Admit the evidence, but only on the hospital's recordkeeping, not on whether the dose was given.
E. Exclude the evidence unless the custodian testifies in person, because certified records aren't self-authenticating.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under Rule 803(7), the absence of an entry in a regularly kept record is admissible to prove that the event didn't occur, if such an entry would regularly have been made and the opponent doesn't show that the possible source or circumstances indicate a lack of trustworthiness. The foundation may come by certification under Rule 902(11) with advance written notice.
- B: Rule 803(7) covers exactly this: evidence that a matter is not included in a record kept under Rule 803(6), offered to prove that the matter didn't occur, if a record was regularly kept for a matter of that kind.
- C: Since 2014 the opponent bears the burden of showing a lack of trustworthiness under Rules 803(6)(E) and 803(7)(C). The hospital offered nothing.
- D: Rule 803(7) admits the absence of an entry for its natural use: to prove that the matter didn't occur.
- E: Rule 902(11) makes a certified domestic business record self-authenticating if the proponent gives the adverse party reasonable written notice and access before trial. The estate did.
Rule: Rule 803(7) admits the absence of an entry in a regularly kept business record to prove that the matter didn't occur, unless the opponent shows a lack of trustworthiness. Rule 902(11) lets a custodian's certification, with advance written notice, supply the foundation.

## Final 4 · Q21 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(8)(A)(iii): findings and opinions
In a civil suit after a helicopter crash, the plaintiff offers a federal agency's report from a legally authorized investigation. It concludes that a maintenance failure was the most probable cause. The defendant objects that this is an opinion, not a fact. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admitted, unless the defendant shows that the report is untrustworthy.
B. Admitted, because a public report may not be challenged at all.
C. Excluded, because Rule 803(8) covers only facts, not conclusions.
D. Excluded, because the investigators didn't personally see the crash.
E. Excluded, because investigative findings can't be used in civil cases.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under Beech Aircraft and Rule 803(8)(A)(iii), conclusions from an authorized investigation qualify in a civil case. Rule 803(8)(B) leaves the opponent to show untrustworthiness.
- B: Rule 803(8)(B) allows an opponent to show untrustworthiness.
- C: Beech Aircraft held that factual findings include opinions and conclusions based on an investigation.
- D: The rule covers findings from a legally authorized investigation, not observation.
- E: Rule 803(8)(A)(iii) applies in civil cases and against the government in criminal cases.
Rule: Rule 803(8)(A)(iii) covers factual findings from a legally authorized investigation, in civil cases and against the government in criminal cases. These include opinions and conclusions, subject to Rule 803(8)(B) (Beech Aircraft).

## Final 4 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Bruton: a nontestimonial coconspirator statement
Ana Ruiz and Ben Holt are tried together for a kickback scheme. The government offers an email Holt sent during the scheme to Cal Reyes, another participant: "Ana and I split the payment as soon as the invoices clear—keep the paperwork clean." Holt doesn't testify. Ruiz moves to exclude the email or sever her trial, citing Bruton.

How should the court rule?

A. Grant severance, because Bruton bars any nontestifying codefendant's statement naming the defendant.
B. Admit it against Holt only, with an instruction that the jury may not consider it against Ruiz.
C. Admit it only if Ruiz's name is replaced with a neutral phrase such as "my partner."
D. Admit it against both, because a nontestimonial coconspirator statement raises no Bruton issue.
E. Exclude it unless Holt takes the stand and submits to cross-examination about the email.

Answer: D
- A: Bruton applies only to statements inadmissible against the defendant, and after Crawford only to testimonial ones. This email is admissible against Ruiz.
- B: No limiting instruction is needed, because the email is admissible against both defendants.
- C: Redaction cures Bruton problems, but there's no problem to cure.
- D: Correct. An email sent during and in furtherance of the scheme is admissible against Ruiz under Rule 801(d)(2)(E), and statements to a coconspirator are nontestimonial. Bruton reaches only statements inadmissible against the defendant, and the Confrontation Clause reaches only testimonial ones. The Problem 8.8 letter is the classroom example.
- E: The Confrontation Clause doesn't apply to nontestimonial statements (Whorton), so Holt need not testify.
Rule: Bruton bars a nontestifying codefendant's facially incriminating confession that is inadmissible against the defendant. After Crawford, the statement must also be testimonial. A nontestimonial coconspirator statement admissible against both defendants raises no Bruton issue.

## Final 4 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Smith v. Arizona: basis evidence offered for its truth
In Jason’s drug-possession trial, the state crime-lab analyst who tested the seized substance, Rast, no longer works at the lab. Rast’s notes and report, prepared after police requested testing for Jason’s case, record the tests she ran and that the results indicated methamphetamine.

The prosecution calls a substitute expert, Longoni, who never saw the substance. Longoni tells the jury what Rast’s notes say about the tests performed and their results, then gives her “independent opinion” that the substance was methamphetamine. The prosecution argues that Rast’s notes were offered not for their truth but only to show the basis of Longoni’s opinion under Rule 703. Jason objects under the Confrontation Clause.

Under current Supreme Court precedent, which statement is most accurate?

A. There is no Confrontation problem, because basis evidence disclosed under Rule 703 is not offered for its truth.
B. There is no Confrontation problem, because Longoni offered her own independent opinion and was available for cross-examination.
C. There is a Confrontation violation only if Longoni failed to review Rast’s raw data independently before testifying.
D. Rast’s statements were offered for their truth, since they supported the opinion only if true; the question is whether they were testimonial.
E. There is a Confrontation violation whether or not Rast’s notes are testimonial, because any out-of-court statement an expert relays to the jury is subject to the Clause.

Answer: D
- A: Smith rejected this theory. Basis testimony that supports the opinion only if true is offered for its truth.
- B: An expert’s independent opinion does not launder the absent analyst’s statements she relays to the jury. Cross-examining Longoni cannot test what Rast did.
- C: The problem is what Longoni told the jury Rast said, not how carefully Longoni prepared.
- D: Correct. This is the holding of Smith v. Arizona and the question it left for remand.
- E: The Clause applies only to testimonial statements. Smith left the testimonial question open on remand.
Rule: When an expert conveys an absent analyst’s statements in support of the expert’s opinion, and the statements provide that support only if they are true, the statements come into evidence for their truth. Smith v. Arizona (2024). The Confrontation Clause then bars them if they are testimonial.

## Final 4 · Q24 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Bruton: prosecutors' options
A prosecutor plans a joint trial of Kerr and Lang. Kerr gave police a confession naming Lang. Which option does NOT avoid the Bruton problem?

A. Redacting all reference to Lang's existence from the text of the confession.
B. Severing the trials so that Lang is tried separately.
C. Using separate juries for the two defendants.
D. Having Kerr testify and submit to cross-examination.
E. Reading the confession with a clear limiting instruction.

Answer: E
- A: Richardson approves removing the defendant's name and any reference to his existence.
- B: Severance removes the confession from Lang's trial.
- C: Separate juries work the same way as severance.
- D: If the confessing codefendant testifies and can be crossed, the problem goes away.
- E: Correct. Bruton holds that a clear limiting instruction doesn't cure the problem, because the risk that jurors can't follow it is too great.
Rule: A limiting instruction doesn't cure a facially incriminating confession by a nontestifying codefendant (Bruton). Prosecutors may sever, use separate juries, have the codefendant testify, or redact (Richardson, Gray, Samia).

## Final 4 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 702(b) and (d) as amended in 2023
In a truck-collision case, the plaintiff's accident reconstructionist uses a momentum analysis that is widely accepted in the field. On voir dire he admits that he guessed the truck's weight at 18,000 pounds. The truck's registration shows 31,000 pounds, and his speed estimate changes substantially if the true weight is used. The defense moves to exclude. The plaintiff argues that the error goes to weight, not admissibility.

What should the court do?

A. Admit it, because once the method is generally accepted, errors in the inputs go only to weight.
B. Admit it, because Daubert tells courts to focus solely on methodology, not on conclusions.
C. Exclude it, because accident reconstruction is not a science, so it can't meet Daubert.
D. Admit it, because the credibility of an expert witness is always a question for the jury.
E. Exclude it unless the plaintiff shows, more likely than not, that it rests on sufficient data and a reliable application.

Answer: E
- A: The 2023 ACN calls decisions treating sufficiency of basis and application as weight questions "an incorrect application of Rules 702 and 104(a)."
- B: General Electric Co. v. Joiner held that "conclusions and methodology are not entirely distinct." An opinion separated from the data by too great an analytical gap may be excluded.
- C: Kumho Tire holds that gatekeeping applies to all expert testimony. That means reliability screening, not automatic exclusion.
- D: Rule 702 makes reliability a Rule 104(a) question for the court before the jury hears the opinion.
- E: Correct. Since 2023, the proponent must show the court by a preponderance that the testimony is based on sufficient facts or data (702(b)) and that the opinion reflects a reliable application of the method to the facts (702(d)). A guessed input that contradicts the record and drives the result fails both. That is not merely a question of weight.
Rule: Under Rule 702 as amended in 2023, the court must find each requirement by a preponderance (Rule 104(a)), including sufficient facts or data and a reliable application of the method. Only attacks that remain after that finding go to weight.

## Final 4 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Daubert binds federal courts only; Frye states
In a state-court murder trial, the defense offers expert testimony based on a new method of analyzing gunshot residue. The state's highest court has always applied the Frye general-acceptance test, and the method is not yet generally accepted. The defense argues that Daubert abolished Frye, so the state court must instead ask whether the method is testable, has been peer reviewed, and has a known error rate.

How should the court rule on the argument?

A. Accept it, because Daubert announced a constitutional standard for scientific evidence that binds every court.
B. Reject it, because Daubert reaffirmed general acceptance as the controlling test in every court.
C. Accept it, because a state court must follow every federal decision construing rules like its own.
D. Accept it, because Frye applies only to the physical sciences, and residue analysis is a technical field.
E. Reject it, because Daubert interpreted Federal Rule 702, which doesn't govern a state's own evidence law.

Answer: E
- A: Daubert is a statutory decision interpreting Federal Rule 702. It sets no constitutional floor, and states may keep Frye.
- B: Daubert rejected Frye's “austere” general-acceptance test as the exclusive standard under the Federal Rules. General acceptance survives there only as one factor.
- C: A state court construing its own code may look to federal decisions, but they don't bind it. Several states with codes modeled on the Federal Rules have kept Frye.
- D: Frye was never limited to the physical sciences, and Kumho Tire's extension of gatekeeping to technical evidence is a reading of Federal Rule 702, not a rule for state courts.
- E: Correct. Daubert held that Federal Rule 702 superseded Frye in federal courts. It binds no state. By Fisher's 2022 count, 31 states follow Daubert, six still follow Frye, and the rest use other standards.
Rule: Daubert interprets Federal Rule 702 and replaced Frye only in federal court. States may keep Frye's general-acceptance test, and several do.

## Final 4 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 901(a): the judge screens, the jury decides
In an extortion trial, the prosecution offers a typed, unsigned letter demanding $50,000, which the victim received. The victim testifies that the letter uses a private nickname only she and the defendant used, refers to a loan she made the defendant two weeks earlier, and arrived the day after the two argued about that loan. The defendant testifies that he didn't write it. The judge says, “I'm not persuaded he more likely than not wrote it,” and excludes the letter.

Was the ruling correct?

A. Yes, because an unsigned typed letter can be authenticated only by handwriting or expert analysis.
B. No, because the defendant's denial goes to weight, so the letter must be admitted without any foundation.
C. No, because the evidence would let a reasonable jury find that the defendant wrote the letter.
D. Yes, because the judge decides authenticity herself under Rule 104(a), by a preponderance of the evidence.
E. Yes, because the victim's testimony is self-serving and can't alone authenticate a letter she received.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 901(b)(4) allows authentication by distinctive characteristics such as contents and substance, taken together with all the circumstances. Handwriting is only one route, and a typed letter has none to compare.
- B: The proponent must still lay a foundation under Rule 901(a). The point is that the threshold is low and was met here, not that no foundation is needed.
- C: Correct. Rule 901(a) requires only evidence sufficient to support a finding that the letter is what the prosecution claims. The private nickname, the reference to the loan, and the timing let a reasonable jury find that the defendant wrote it (901(b)(4)). He may still contest authorship before the jury.
- D: Authentication is a conditional-relevance question under Rules 104(b) and 901(a). The judge asks only whether there is evidence sufficient to support a finding that the item is what the proponent claims; she doesn't decide authenticity herself.
- E: A witness with knowledge may supply the foundation under Rule 901(b)(1), and the jury weighs her credibility. The circumstantial details also authenticate under 901(b)(4).
Rule: Rule 901(a) requires evidence sufficient to support a finding that the item is what the proponent claims, a conditional-relevance standard (Rule 104(b)). The judge screens and the jury decides. Distinctive contents and circumstances can authenticate a document (901(b)(4)).

## Final 4 · Q28 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 1007: the opponent's testimony about content
Rida Corp. sues Talon Inc. for late delivery under a written supply contract. Neither side can find the signed original, and there is no evidence of how it was lost. In his deposition, Talon's president testified: "The contract called for delivery by June 1." Rida offers that deposition testimony at trial to prove the contract's delivery term. Talon objects under the best evidence rule.

How should the court rule?

A. Sustain it, unless Rida first proves the original was lost without bad faith.
B. Overrule it, because the opponent's own testimony may prove a writing's content.
C. Sustain it, because only a duplicate may substitute for a lost original contract.
D. Overrule it, but only if Talon's president repeats the testimony at trial.
E. Sustain it, because the president's deposition testimony is hearsay at trial.

Answer: B
- A: Rule 1004(a) is one route, but Rule 1007 is an independent route that doesn't depend on why the original is missing.
- B: Correct. Rule 1007 allows proof of a writing's content by the testimony, deposition, or written statement of the party against whom it is offered, without accounting for the original. Talon's president testifying for Talon qualifies.
- C: Duplicates are one route (Rule 1003). Rules 1004 through 1007 offer others.
- D: Rule 1007 expressly includes "deposition" testimony. No repetition at trial is needed.
- E: Offered against Talon, its president's statement on a matter within the scope of his position is an opposing party's statement (Rule 801(d)(2)(D)).
Rule: Rule 1002 requires an original to prove content unless an exception applies: duplicates (1003), lost or unobtainable originals (1004), certified public records (1005), summaries (1006), or the opponent's own testimony or written statement (1007).

## Final 4 · Q29 · Privileges — Lawyer-client: prospective client vs. Gionis
Maya Lowe met with attorney Carl Day about a dispute with her business partner. Early in the meeting she told him that two months earlier she had moved $60,000 of company funds into her personal account "to protect it." At the end of the meeting, Day said he couldn't take the case because of a conflict. A federal grand jury investigating embezzlement subpoenas Day to testify about what Lowe told him.

Must Day disclose the statement?

A. No, because a prospective client's confidences are privileged even when declined.
B. Yes, because no lawyer-client relationship was ever formed between Lowe and Day.
C. Yes, because the crime-fraud exception applies to a client's statements about a crime.
D. Yes, because the privilege protects communications, not underlying facts such as the transfer.
E. No, because the privilege belongs to the lawyer, so Day may simply decline to testify.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. The privilege covers prospective clients consulting a lawyer in a professional capacity, even if the lawyer later declines. Unlike in People v. Gionis, the lawyer hadn't made clear before the statement that he wouldn't act. Lowe was seeking legal help in confidence.
