- B: Prospective clients are covered. The question is whether Lowe consulted Day in his professional capacity, and she did.
- C: The crime-fraud exception reaches advice sought to further a future or ongoing crime or fraud. A disclosure of past conduct while seeking advice is protected.
- D: That's true but doesn't help the government here. The transfer itself can be proved by other means, and Lowe can't refuse to state it if asked. But Day can't be compelled to reveal what Lowe told him.
- E: The privilege belongs to the client. Day asserts it on Lowe's behalf.
Rule: The lawyer-client privilege protects confidential communications made to obtain professional legal services, including those of a prospective client whose case is declined. It protects communications, not underlying facts, and the client holds it.

## Final 4 · Q30 · Privileges — Trammel and marital confidences together
Marcus Reed is charged with smuggling. His wife, Maya, agrees to testify for the government in exchange for immunity. She plans to describe (1) seeing Marcus unload packages from a boat at night, (2) his remark to her and his business partner at dinner that the shipments “pay for the house,” and (3) his whispered statement to her alone in their bedroom that the packages held heroin. Marcus invokes both spousal privileges.

What may Maya testify to?

A. Items 1 and 3, because a confession of a crime is never a protected marital communication.
B. Nothing, because a defendant may bar his spouse's adverse testimony in a criminal case.
C. Items 1 and 2, but not item 3.
D. Item 1 only, because both of Marcus's statements were made during the marriage.
E. All three, because Maya, as the witness-spouse, holds every marital privilege and has chosen to waive them.

Answer: C
- A: A confession of a crime made in confidence is a protected marital communication. The joint-crime exception would apply only if Maya were taking part in the crime with him, which the facts don't show.
- B: That was the rule of Hawkins v. United States. Trammel modified it: in federal court the witness-spouse alone holds the testimonial privilege, so Marcus can't stop a willing Maya from testifying.
- C: Correct. Under Trammel, Maya may choose to testify against Marcus, so the testimonial privilege doesn't bar items 1 and 2. But the separate marital confidences privilege protects confidential communications made during the marriage, and Marcus may claim it. The remark at dinner, made in front of a third party, isn't confidential; the whispered bedroom statement is.
- D: Being made during the marriage is necessary but not sufficient. A remark made in front of a business partner isn't confidential, so item 2 isn't protected.
- E: Trammel gave the witness-spouse control of the testimonial privilege only. The communicating spouse may claim the marital confidences privilege, so Marcus can bar item 3.
Rule: Spousal testimonial privilege (Trammel): the witness-spouse decides whether to testify against the defendant-spouse in a criminal case. Marital confidences privilege: a spouse may bar disclosure of confidential communications made during the marriage; acts observed and statements made in front of others aren't covered.

## Final 5 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Peña-Rodriguez: stereotypes about defense witnesses
After a federal jury convicts a defendant of armed robbery, a juror swears in an affidavit that during deliberations another juror said the defendant’s two alibi witnesses, both Mexican-American, “can’t be believed, because Mexicans always lie to protect their own,” and urged the others to disregard the alibi for that reason. The government argues that Peña-Rodriguez covers only statements about the defendant himself. How should the court proceed?

A. Consider it only if the juror who made the statement admits he was biased.
B. Consider it only if the defendant first shows that the juror lied during voir dire about racial bias.
C. Refuse to consider it, because the Peña-Rodriguez exception applies only in state prosecutions.
D. Refuse to consider it, because the statement was about witnesses, not the defendant.
E. Consider it, because relying on ethnic stereotypes to reject the alibi falls within Peña-Rodriguez.

Answer: E
- A: The exception turns on the statement itself, which other jurors may prove. The juror who made it needn’t concede anything.
- B: Peña-Rodriguez doesn’t depend on a voir dire lie. Proving voir dire dishonesty by other evidence is a separate route (Warger); the constitutional exception applies to a clear statement of reliance on racial or ethnic stereotypes whether or not voir dire uncovered it.
- C: Peña rests on the Sixth Amendment right to an impartial jury, which applies in federal court directly and in state court through the Fourteenth Amendment.
- D: Peña’s exception isn’t limited to remarks about the defendant. Dismissing his witnesses because of their ethnicity infects the verdict just as directly, and Peña’s own facts included such a remark.
- E: Correct. Peña-Rodriguez requires the no-impeachment rule to give way when a juror makes a clear statement showing reliance on racial or ethnic stereotypes or animus to convict. In Peña itself, the juror also discounted the alibi witness as “an illegal.” Rejecting the defense case on that basis is reliance on bias to convict. The court then decides whether the bias was a significant motivating factor.
Rule: Peña-Rodriguez: a clear statement that a juror relied on racial or ethnic stereotypes or animus to convict, including to discredit defense witnesses, must be considered despite 606(b). The animus must be a significant motivating factor.

## Final 5 · Q2 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Flight: the weak link in the chain
A defendant is charged with burglarizing a jewelry store on a Monday night. On Thursday, two officers responding to a neighbor’s noise complaint knock on the door of the defendant’s apartment building. When they ask the defendant, who is on the front steps, whether he lives there, he runs. The prosecution offers his flight as evidence of consciousness of guilt of the burglary. Which is the defense’s strongest argument for excluding it?

A. Flight is an assertion of guilt by conduct, so it is hearsay that no exception covers.
B. Commonwealth v. Warren makes evidence of flight from police inadmissible as a matter of law.
C. Nothing shows he knew he was a burglary suspect when he ran, so the flight says little about guilt of this crime.
D. The prosecution must first prove beyond a reasonable doubt that he was fleeing rather than simply leaving.
E. Flight is character evidence showing he is the kind of person who runs from police, so Rule 404(a)(1) bars it.

Answer: C
- A: Running isn’t intended as an assertion, so it isn’t a “statement” under Rule 801(a). The problem with this flight is the weak link from running to guilt of this burglary, not hearsay.
- B: Warren, a Massachusetts case, held that flight may carry little weight and that a qualified community witness may explain other reasons for it. It didn’t create a rule excluding flight evidence.
- C: Correct. Flight evidence depends on a chain of inferences: (1) the behavior was flight; (2) flight shows consciousness of guilt; (3) of this crime; (4) so actual guilt. Here link 3 is weakest. The flight came three days later, from officers asking about a noise complaint, and he had no reason to think they were there about the burglary (Thelma & Louise clip). Commonwealth v. Warren adds other reasons people run from police.
- D: Preliminary facts of this kind are at most 104(b) questions, which require evidence sufficient to support a finding. No beyond-a-reasonable-doubt threshold applies.
- E: Flight isn’t offered to show a character trait. It’s conduct offered to show his state of mind about a particular crime. The proper attack is on the inferential chain and under 403.
Rule: Flight rests on four links: the behavior was flight, flight shows consciousness of guilt, of this crime, and so actual guilt. Attack the weak link and argue 403 (Warren: there are other reasons to flee).

## Final 5 · Q3 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 403: undisputed fact
In a murder trial, the only disputed issue is who committed the killing. The defense concedes the cause and manner of death. The State offers graphic autopsy photographs of the opened skull. The defense objects. What is the best analysis?

A. The photos are relevant, but their value is low here and may be substantially outweighed.
B. The photos are irrelevant, because evidence on an undisputed fact fails Rule 401.
C. The photos must be admitted, because the State may always prove each element its own way.
D. The photos must be excluded, because gruesome images are unfairly prejudicial as a matter of law.
E. The photos come in, because a limiting instruction cures any emotional reaction to them.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under State v. Bocharski, an undisputed fact shrinks probative value on the Rule 403 scale without making the evidence irrelevant. The two autopsy photos there served mainly to inflame.
- B: The fact need not be disputed under Rule 401 (ACN). A brick is not a wall, and the problem arises under Rule 403.
- C: That is the general rule of Old Chief, but it doesn't remove the Rule 403 balance.
- D: Rule 403 has no per se rule. Some gruesome photos in State v. Bocharski were properly admitted.
- E: The likely effectiveness of an instruction is one factor. It doesn't automatically resolve the balance.
Rule: An undisputed fact can still be relevant, but Rule 403 discounts its probative value. Exclusion requires that the value be substantially outweighed by unfair prejudice or another listed danger (State v. Bocharski).

## Final 5 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 407: a measure taken between two accidents
On June 3, a child breaks an ankle landing in the foam pit at a trampoline park. On July 1, the park installs a padded border around the pit. On August 12, a second child is injured in the same pit despite the new border, and the second child’s family claims the pit itself was too shallow. Both families sue the park for negligence, and each wants to prove the July 1 installation to show that the park knew the pit was dangerous. Rule 407 bars the evidence in which suit?

A. Only the second child’s suit, because the border failed to prevent the second injury.
B. Neither suit, because the park’s knowledge is a permitted purpose under Rule 407.
C. Neither suit, because a padded border is a new design feature, not a repair, so Rule 407 doesn’t reach it.
D. Both suits, because the border was installed in response to an accident at the same pit.
E. Only the first child’s suit.

Answer: E
- A: That reverses the timing. The measure followed the first injury, which is the suit in which 407 applies.
- B: Knowledge of danger is just another way of saying culpable conduct or negligence, which 407 bars. The permitted purposes are impeachment and, if disputed, ownership, control, or feasibility.
- C: Rule 407 covers any measure that would have made the earlier injury less likely, including new equipment and design changes, not only repairs.
- D: 407 measures time against the plaintiff’s own injury. A measure taken before that plaintiff was hurt isn’t “subsequent” as to that plaintiff.
- E: Correct. 407 applies to measures taken after the injury that would have made “an earlier injury” less likely. The July border came after the first child’s injury but before the second child’s, so as to the second child it isn’t a subsequent remedial measure (Problem 2.1, Wolf Attack: the chaining was barred in the beagle owner’s suit but not in Daniel’s). In the second suit the court applies 401 and 403.
Rule: Rule 407 applies only to measures taken after the plaintiff’s own injury. A measure taken between two accidents is barred in the first plaintiff’s suit only (Wolf Attack).

## Final 5 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 408(b): settlement talk to prove obstruction
During settlement negotiations in a wrongful-termination suit, the employer's vice president told the plaintiff: "We'll add $25,000 to our offer if your coworker Jana 'forgets' what she saw at the meeting and skips her deposition." The plaintiff rejected the offer. At trial, the plaintiff offers the statement to show the employer tried to obstruct the proceedings by keeping a witness away. The employer objects under Rule 408.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because negotiation statements are inadmissible to prove liability.
B. Admit it to prove that the employer's termination decision was unlawful.
C. Admit it to prove an attempt to obstruct, with a limiting instruction.
D. Exclude it, because the plaintiff rejected the offer when it was made.
E. Admit it, but only to impeach the vice president if he takes the stand.

Answer: C
- A: True as far as it goes, but the plaintiff doesn't offer it to prove the validity of the claim.
- B: That would be a barred use: proving the validity of the claim.
- C: Correct, though the jury will learn of settlement talks. Rule 408(b) permits compromise evidence for another purpose, including "proving an effort to obstruct a criminal investigation or prosecution." Courts read the obstruction purpose to cover efforts to tamper with witnesses in the litigation. Rule 105 limits its use.
- D: Rule 408 doesn't depend on whether the offer was accepted, and rejection doesn't make it admissible either.
- E: Rule 408(a) bars impeachment by inconsistency or contradiction. The obstruction purpose is the proper route.
Rule: Rule 408(b) lists permitted purposes for compromise evidence: bias, negating undue delay, and proving obstruction. A statement in negotiations offering to pay for a witness's silence fits the obstruction purpose.

## Final 5 · Q6 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 410(b)(1): the defendant opens the door
In plea discussions with the prosecutor, which ended without a deal, robbery defendant Hal Ruiz said: "I'll never plead to something I didn't do. I was in the alley that night, but I never touched the clerk." At trial, Ruiz calls the prosecutor's former paralegal to repeat only the first sentence, offering it to show consciousness of innocence. The court admits it. The prosecution then offers the rest of the statement.

What is the best ruling on the prosecution's offer?

A. Exclude it, because Rule 410 bars plea-discussion statements against the defendant.
B. Exclude it, because Rule 410 can be waived only by a written agreement.
C. Admit it, because Ruiz introduced part of the same plea discussion.
D. Admit it as Ruiz's own statement, because Rule 410 covers only guilty pleas.
E. Admit it, but only in a later prosecution of Ruiz for perjury.

Answer: C
- A: This is the rule's default, and it's tempting because Rule 410 exists to protect the defendant. But Rule 410(b)(1) lets the other side respond when "another statement made during the same plea or plea discussions has been introduced."
- B: Mezzanatto waivers are one route. Rule 410(b)(1)'s completeness exception is separate and requires no agreement.
- C: Correct. Ruiz can't use the favorable half of a plea conversation while hiding the rest. Rule 410(b)(1) admits a statement from the same discussions when one has been introduced and "in fairness the statements ought to be considered together." This is the class's Biaggi retrial example (Problem 2.9).
- D: Rule 410(a)(4) expressly covers statements in plea discussions with a prosecutor that didn't result in a guilty plea.
- E: Rule 410(b)(2) covers perjury and false-statement prosecutions. Rule 410(b)(1) applies here.
Rule: Rule 410 bars plea material against the defendant, with two exceptions: (b)(1) completeness, when another statement from the same plea or discussions has been introduced, and (b)(2) perjury prosecutions. Rule 410 protects the defendant, not the government (Biaggi).

## Final 5 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(b) reaches later acts
Mara Quinn is charged with knowingly filing a false Medicare claim in March for a wheelchair that was never delivered. She claims the March claim was a billing clerk's mistake. The prosecution offers evidence that in July, four months later, Quinn personally submitted six more claims for equipment never delivered, after an auditor had warned her about the March claim.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because Rule 404(b) permits only evidence of prior acts.
B. Admit it to show intent and the absence of mistake in March.
C. Exclude it, because later acts can't show her state of mind earlier.
D. Admit it to show Quinn's propensity to commit Medicare fraud.
E. Exclude it, because the July claims were never charged.

Answer: B
- A: The rule says "any other crime, wrong, or act," without a time limit. "Prior acts" is a common but inaccurate shorthand.
- B: Correct, though later acts feel less probative of an earlier mental state. Repeating the conduct after a specific warning makes an innocent billing mistake in March less likely, a non-propensity chain. The July acts are subject to Huddleston, Rule 403, and a limiting instruction.
- C: Later acts can be probative of earlier intent, especially when the explanation offered is accident or mistake. Timing goes to weight under Rule 403.
- D: Propensity is the barred inference.
- E: No charge or conviction is required (Huddleston).
Rule: Rule 404(b) covers "any other crime, wrong, or act," including later acts. They may show intent, knowledge, or absence of mistake through a non-character chain, subject to Huddleston and Rule 403.

## Final 5 · Q8 · Character Evidence — Rule 413: the 15-day disclosure requirement
Neil Crane is charged with aggravated sexual abuse. Six days before trial, the prosecution first disclosed a witness who would testify that Crane tried to force himself on her four years ago, before she broke free. The prosecution offers no explanation for the timing, and Crane objects that he has no time to investigate.

What is the best ruling?

A. Admit it, because Rule 413 evidence is admissible regardless of when it is disclosed.
B. Admit it, because Rule 404(b)(3) requires only reasonable notice, and six days is reasonable.
C. Exclude it, because an attempted assault isn't a sexual assault under Rule 413.
D. Exclude it, because Rule 413 applies only to sexual assaults that led to a conviction.
E. Don't admit it absent good cause, because it wasn't disclosed 15 days before trial.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 413(b) imposes a specific disclosure requirement.
- B: Rule 413(b) sets its own 15-day minimum for propensity evidence offered under that rule.
- C: Rule 413(d) defines sexual assault to include an attempt.
- D: No conviction is required, and the Huddleston standard applies.
- E: Correct. The attempt qualifies under Rule 413(d)'s definition. But Rule 413(b) requires disclosure, including witnesses' statements, at least 15 days before trial "or at a later time that the court allows for good cause." With no good cause shown, the court shouldn't admit it as offered (it may consider a continuance).
Rule: Rules 413–415 require disclosure at least 15 days before trial or at a later time the court allows for good cause. Their definitions include attempts. Once admitted, the evidence may be used for propensity, subject to Rule 403.

## Final 5 · Q9 · Character Evidence — Rule 405(b): entrapment makes predisposition an element
Charged with selling heroin to an undercover agent, Cole Ramsey claims entrapment, arguing the agent badgered him for weeks. The prosecution offers testimony from two witnesses that Ramsey sold them heroin the year before, and a third witness's opinion that Ramsey is a drug dealer.

What is the most accurate statement?

A. All of it is inadmissible character evidence under Rule 404(a)(1).
B. Only the opinion testimony, because character must be shown by opinion or reputation.
C. The prior sales are admissible only to show knowledge, with an instruction.
D. All of it, because entrapment puts predisposition in issue.
E. None of it, unless Ramsey himself takes the stand and testifies.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 404(a)(1) bars character to prove conduct on a particular occasion. Here predisposition is itself an element of the defense.
- B: That would be right under Rule 405(a). But Rule 405(b) allows specific instances when character is an essential element of a charge, claim, or defense.
- C: Knowledge is a possible purpose, but the stronger and more direct ground is predisposition under Rule 405(b), which permits the propensity use.
- D: Correct, though it means the jury hears that he dealt drugs before. Entrapment puts the defendant's predisposition directly in issue. Under Rule 405(b), when character is an essential element, it may be proved by specific instances as well as reputation or opinion. Rule 403 still applies.
- E: The defense raised the issue by claiming entrapment, whether or not Ramsey testifies.
Rule: Rule 405(b): when character is an essential element of a charge, claim, or defense (entrapment predisposition, defamation truth, negligent entrustment, custody), it may be proved by specific instances. Self-defense isn't such a case.

## Final 5 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Capacity: extrinsic proof isn't barred by Rule 608(b)
In a bar-fight battery trial, the plaintiff's key eyewitness testifies that the defendant threw the first punch. On cross, she denies having had anything to drink that night. The defendant then calls the bartender, who would testify that the eyewitness drank six shots of tequila in the hour before the fight. The plaintiff objects that extrinsic evidence of a witness's conduct is barred.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it under Rule 608(b), because the defendant must take her answer.
B. Exclude it as collateral, because drinking has nothing to do with who started the fight.
C. Admit it, but only if the witness has a conviction involving alcohol.
D. Admit it, because it shows she may not have perceived the fight clearly.
E. Exclude it as improper character evidence of the witness's intemperance.

Answer: D
- A: The tempting answer. But Rule 608(b)'s bar applies only when the sole purpose is to attack character for truthfulness (ACN; Abel). Drinking isn't offered to show she is a liar. It shows she may not have seen clearly.
- B: A witness's capacity to perceive the very event she describes isn't collateral. It bears directly on how much weight her account deserves.
- C: Rule 609 concerns convictions offered to show untruthful character. It doesn't limit capacity evidence.
- D: Correct. Capacity is one of the "lying now" (or "mistaken now") modes of impeachment. Like bias, it may be proved extrinsically, because it bears on the accuracy of this testimony rather than on the witness's character. Rule 403 still applies.
- E: The evidence isn't offered to show she acted in conformity with a trait. It shows her perception was impaired.
Rule: Bias and capacity (perception and memory) may be proved extrinsically. Rule 608(b)'s extrinsic-evidence bar applies only when the sole purpose is to attack character for truthfulness (Abel).

## Final 5 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 608(b): take the answer
In a contract trial, the plaintiff's sales manager testifies. Defense counsel has an insurance claim the manager filed swearing a laptop was stolen, and a repair invoice showing she still had it a month later. Counsel asks, "You lied on that insurance claim, didn't you?" She says no. Counsel then offers the claim and invoice, both authenticated. She was never charged. What is the correct ruling?

A. The question is improper, because counsel must have admissible proof of the lie before asking.
B. The question is proper, and the documents come in because they have been authenticated.
C. The question is improper, because Rule 609 is the only way to prove a witness's lies.
D. The question is proper, and the documents come in as contradiction of her denial.
E. The question is proper, but the documents are excluded and counsel must take her answer.

Answer: E
- A: The questioner needs only a good-faith basis, not admissible proof (United States v. Whitmore).
- B: Authentication clears only one hurdle. Rule 608(b) still bars extrinsic evidence offered to prove the lie.
- C: Rule 609 covers convictions. Rule 608(b) covers untruthful acts that didn't result in one.
- D: Contradicting a denial on a matter relevant only to truthfulness would swallow Rule 608(b). The sole purpose here is to attack character for truthfulness.
- E: Correct. Rule 608(b) allows inquiry on cross into acts probative of truthfulness but bars extrinsic evidence to prove them. If the witness denies the act, counsel is stuck with the answer.
Rule: Rule 608(b) permits cross-examination about specific untruthful acts, with a good-faith basis, but bars extrinsic proof when the only purpose is to attack character for truthfulness (United States v. Whitmore).

## Final 5 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609(a)(1): maximum punishment controls
A witness in a civil trial was convicted of felony theft eight years ago. The crime was punishable by up to three years, but the witness received only six months of probation. Theft of this kind has no element of dishonesty or false statement. Which is correct?

A. It can't be used, because theft isn't a crime of false statement.
B. It must be admitted with no balancing, because it is a felony.
C. It can't be used, because the sentence the court actually imposed was under one year.
D. It comes in unless Rule 403 requires exclusion, because the maximum exceeds a year.
E. It comes in only if probative value substantially outweighs prejudice.

Answer: D
- A: That defeats only Rule 609(a)(2). Rule 609(a)(1) covers any crime punishable by more than one year.
- B: No balancing applies only to crimen falsi under Rule 609(a)(2). This conviction is subject to Rule 403.
- C: What counts is the maximum punishment, not the sentence imposed.
- D: Correct. Rule 609(a)(1)(A) applies because the crime was punishable by more than one year. For a civil witness, the conviction must be admitted, subject to Rule 403.
- E: The reverse balance belongs to Rule 609(b). This conviction is under 10 years old.
Rule: Rule 609(a)(1) looks at the maximum punishment in the convicting jurisdiction, not the sentence actually imposed.

## Final 5 · Q13 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 412(b)(1)(A): another source
In a sexual-assault trial, the prosecution offers medical evidence of semen. The defendant says he wasn't the source and offers evidence that the complainant had sex with another man the night before. The defense filed a timely Rule 412(c) motion. What is the most likely result?

A. Barred, because only the prosecutor may offer specific instances of behavior.
B. Barred, because Rule 412(a) excludes all of a victim's other sexual behavior.
C. Admissible to show someone other than the defendant was the source.
D. Admissible only if the probative value substantially outweighs harm to the victim.
E. Admissible, because any sexual history is relevant to consent.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 412(b)(1)(A) has no such limit. The defendant may offer this evidence.
- B: Rule 412(b)(1) lists exceptions, and this is one of them.
- C: Correct. Rule 412(b)(1)(A) allows specific instances to prove that someone other than the defendant was the source of semen, injury, or other physical evidence.
- D: That balance is the civil test in Rule 412(b)(2).
- E: Past behavior with others isn't admissible to show consent. This is offered for a different purpose.
Rule: In criminal cases, Rule 412(b)(1)(A) allows specific instances to prove another source of semen, injury, or other physical evidence. The civil reverse balance is Rule 412(b)(2).

## Final 5 · Q14 · Definition of Hearsay — Verbal acts: words that characterize a transfer
Fenwick died testate. His executor sues Fenwick’s nephew, Gil, to recover $60,000, alleging that Fenwick lent Gil the money. Gil contends it was a gift. The executor calls Fenwick’s housekeeper, who will testify that she watched Fenwick hand Gil a cashier’s check and say: “This is a loan, not a present. You’ll pay it back when the restaurant turns a profit.” Gil objects on hearsay grounds.

What is the strongest basis for admitting the housekeeper’s testimony?

A. It is a statement against interest, because Fenwick is unavailable and characterizing the transfer as a loan exposed him to tax liability on the interest.
B. It is an opposing-party statement, because Fenwick’s estate is a party to the action and Fenwick’s statements are attributable to it.
C. It is admissible under the residual exception, because no specific exception applies and the statement was made at the moment of the transfer.
D. It is admissible under Rule 803(3) as a statement of Fenwick’s memory of the terms on which he had agreed to transfer the money.
E. It is not hearsay, because Fenwick’s words accompanying the transfer are legally operative, whether or not they are true.

Answer: E
- A: Calling the transfer a loan created a $60,000 asset for Fenwick, which is in his pecuniary favor. Speculative tax consequences do not make it a statement a reasonable person would make only if true.
- B: Rule 801(d)(2) admits statements only when offered against the party. Here the executor, standing in Fenwick’s shoes, is offering Fenwick’s statement for itself.
- C: Rule 807 is a last resort that requires notice and special findings. There is no need to reach it when the words are not hearsay.
- D: Rule 803(3) expressly excludes statements of memory or belief offered to prove the fact remembered. Fenwick was also not describing a memory; he was characterizing the transfer as it happened.
- E: Correct. Words that accompany and characterize a transaction are legally operative and are not offered for their truth.
Rule: Words that have independent legal significance, sometimes called verbal acts or legally operative words, are not hearsay. Examples include the words of an offer or acceptance, a defamatory statement in a defamation case, and words that accompany and characterize a transfer of property. They are offered because they were said, not to prove the truth of anything asserted.

## Final 5 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Adoption by silence: what the circumstances call for
At a crowded backyard party, Rosa Diaz walked up to Kyle Penn in front of several guests and said loudly, "You're the one who smashed my car's windows last night—I saw you." Penn laughed, shrugged, and walked away without a word. In Diaz's civil suit against Penn for the damage, she offers the exchange.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because only silence, not laughter or a shrug, can be an adoption.
B. Exclude it, because silence is too ambiguous ever to be an admission.
C. Admit it, if an innocent person in Penn's place would naturally have denied it.
D. Admit it, because any accusation made in a party's presence is admitted if not denied.
E. Exclude it, because using a party's silence against him violates the Fifth Amendment.

Answer: C
- A: Adoption may be shown by any conduct manifesting belief, including laughing it off and walking away.
- B: Silence can be ambiguous, which is why the doctrine has conditions. It isn't categorically excluded.
- C: Correct. Under Rule 801(d)(2)(B), the elements are that he heard and understood, was free to respond, the circumstances naturally called for a denial, and he didn't deny. A direct public accusation by the victim among friends is the kind of situation that ordinarily calls for a denial. His reaction may be found to adopt it.
- D: Not every accusation qualifies. The circumstances must naturally call for a denial, and the party must have heard and understood it.
- E: This is a private encounter in a civil case. Doyle concerns silence after Miranda warnings.
Rule: Adoption by silence under Rule 801(d)(2)(B) requires that the party heard and understood, was free to respond, the circumstances naturally called for a denial, and the party didn't deny. Post-Miranda silence is protected (Doyle).

## Final 5 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Bourjaily and Rule 104(a)
The government offers a recorded call in which Lonnie tells a buyer that his "partner Greer" will deliver the drugs. Greer objects that no conspiracy has been shown. Which statement about the Rule 801(d)(2)(E) foundation is correct?

A. The judge, not the jury, decides whether the foundation is met.
B. The standard for the foundation is a preponderance of the evidence.
C. The judge must consider the statement itself, but it can't establish the conspiracy alone.
D. All of the above.
E. None of the above.

Answer: D
- A: This is true, but it isn't the only correct statement. Under Bourjaily, the foundation is a Rule 104(a) question.
- B: This is true, but it isn't the only correct statement. Bourjaily sets the preponderance standard.
- C: This is true, but it isn't the only correct statement. The last paragraph of Rule 801(d)(2) adds it.
- D: Correct. Under Rule 104(a) and Bourjaily, the judge finds the conspiracy and "during and in furtherance" by a preponderance, must consider the statement, and needs some independent evidence under Rule 801(d)(2).
- E: All three statements are correct, so "All of the above" is the answer.
Rule: The Rule 801(d)(2)(E) foundation is a Rule 104(a) question decided by a preponderance. The judge must consider the statement, but it doesn't by itself establish the conspiracy or participation (Bourjaily).

## Final 5 · Q17 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(2) (2024): a decedent’s statement against his estate
Six days before a fatal collision, Ostrander told his mechanic: “The brakes have been grabbing and fading for a month, but I’m not paying for new rotors until after the holidays.” Ostrander was killed in the collision, which also injured Pell.

Pell sues Ostrander’s estate for negligence and calls the mechanic to repeat Ostrander’s statement. The estate objects. The trial takes place in 2026.

What is the best ruling?

A. Admit it under Rule 803(3), because it reflects Ostrander’s then-existing intent to postpone the repair.
B. Exclude it, because the only potentially applicable exception is the statement-against-interest exception, and the statement was not against Ostrander’s interest when he made it.
C. Admit it as an opposing-party statement, because the estate’s liability derives directly from Ostrander and the statement would be admissible against him.
D. Exclude it, because a decedent’s statements are not attributable to the decedent’s estate.
E. Admit it as a statement against interest, because Ostrander is unavailable and the statement exposed him to civil liability.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 803(3) reaches the forward-looking intent to delay repairs, but not the backward-looking assertion that the brakes had been failing for a month, which is the important part.
- B: This ignores the 2024 amendment to Rule 801(d)(2), which supplies a route that does not depend on the statement being against interest.
- C: Correct. The 2024 amendment makes the decedent’s statement admissible against the estate whose liability derives from him.
- D: This was the minority view before 2024. The amended rule now attributes the statement to a party whose liability derives from the declarant.
- E: Against-interest status is measured when the statement is made. Before any accident, describing worn brakes did not expose Ostrander to any realistic liability.
Rule: As amended effective December 1, 2024, Rule 801(d)(2) provides: “If a party’s claim, defense, or potential liability is directly derived from a declarant or the declarant’s principal, a statement that would be admissible against the declarant or the principal under this rule is also admissible against the party.”

## Final 5 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(3)(B): corroboration binds the prosecution too
At a federal robbery trial, the prosecution offers the statement of an accomplice, now dead, who told his girlfriend, "Me and Dex hit the pawnshop Tuesday. I drove." The statement is offered against Dex. The defense argues that a statement against penal interest offered in a criminal case needs corroboration. The prosecution responds that corroboration is required only when the defendant offers a statement to exculpate himself.

Who is right?

A. The prosecution, because corroboration guards only against defense fabrication.
B. The prosecution, because a statement to a girlfriend is nontestimonial.
C. The defense, because corroboration is required in a criminal case whoever offers the statement.
D. Neither, because Rule 804(b)(3) never requires corroboration.
E. The defense, because statements implicating others are never against interest.

Answer: C
- A: That was the original concern and the pre-2010 text, which makes it tempting. Since 2010, the requirement applies to statements offered by either side.
- B: Whether a statement is testimonial is a confrontation question. The hearsay exception has its own corroboration requirement.
- C: Correct. The rule now requires corroboration whenever the statement "is offered in a criminal case as one that tends to expose the declarant to criminal liability," whoever offers it. Since 2024, the court considers the totality of circumstances and any evidence that supports or undermines it. The words naming Dex must also be self-inculpatory under Williamson. A statement to a girlfriend sharing blame, "me and Dex," can be.
- D: Rule 804(b)(3)(B) requires corroboration in criminal cases.
- E: Under Williamson, a statement that inculpates others can also be against the declarant's interest, depending on context. A private admission of joint participation to a girlfriend isn't blame-shifting.
Rule: Rule 804(b)(3)(B): in a criminal case, any statement against penal interest, offered by either side, needs corroborating circumstances clearly indicating trustworthiness, judged on the totality and on evidence for and against it (2024). Williamson limits it to the self-inculpatory parts.

## Final 5 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Shepard: no settled hopeless expectation; looking backward
In a federal murder prosecution, the government offers testimony from the victim's nurse. Two days after the victim fell ill, she told the nurse, "My husband poisoned my coffee. I'm sure of it." At the time, the victim asked whether she would be well enough for a trip next month, and the doctors had told her she was improving. She relapsed and died three weeks later.

Is the statement admissible?

A. Yes, as a dying declaration, because the victim later died from the poisoning.
B. Yes, as a statement of the victim's then-existing belief and state of mind.
C. No, because she didn't expect to die and was describing another person's past act.
D. Yes, as a statement for medical diagnosis, because it identifies the cause of her illness.
E. No, because a dying declaration is admissible only in a civil case.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 804(b)(2) looks to the declarant's belief when she spoke. Death that happens later doesn't satisfy it without "a settled hopeless expectation" of imminent death.
- B: Rule 803(3) excludes "a statement of memory or belief to prove the fact remembered or believed." Shepard rejected this route for a backward-looking accusation.
- C: Correct. Shepard v. United States held that the statement wasn't a dying declaration: she planned a trip and was told she was improving, so she lacked a settled hopeless expectation of death. It also wasn't admissible as state of mind, because it looked backward to her husband's act.
- D: Rule 803(4) reaches general cause pertinent to treatment, not the identity of a person at fault.
- E: Rule 804(b)(2) applies in homicide prosecutions and civil cases.
Rule: A Rule 804(b)(2) dying declaration requires a belief that death is imminent when the statement is made (Shepard). Rule 803(3) excludes memory or belief offered to prove the fact remembered, especially accusations about another person's past acts.

## Final 5 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Palmer v. Hoffman and Rule 803(6)(E)
Two days after a grade-crossing collision, the locomotive engineer, at his railroad employer’s direction, gave a statement describing the crash to the railroad’s claims agent. The railroad takes such statements after every accident, files them in its claims department, and uses them in settlement negotiations and litigation. The engineer has since died.

In the injured motorist’s suit, the railroad offers the statement as a business record, supported by a qualified custodian’s testimony satisfying Rule 803(6)(A) through (C). The motorist objects.

Which statement best describes how the court should proceed?

A. The court must admit the statement, because the railroad established every foundational element of Rule 803(6), and trustworthiness is not part of the rule.
B. The court should admit the statement, but only against the railroad, because it is the railroad’s own employee’s statement.
C. The court may exclude it if the motorist shows that its source or the circumstances of its preparation indicate a lack of trustworthiness.
D. The court should admit the statement only if the railroad proves affirmatively that it is trustworthy.
E. The court must exclude the statement, because an employee’s statement about an accident can never be a business record.

Answer: C
- A: Trustworthiness is expressly part of the rule in subsection (E).
- B: The railroad is offering its own employee’s statement. Rule 801(d)(2) applies only to statements offered against a party, and “only against the railroad” makes no sense when the railroad is the proponent.
- C: Correct. This states the 2014 allocation of the burden and the concern from Palmer v. Hoffman.
- D: Since 2014, the burden is on the opponent to show untrustworthiness, not on the proponent to prove trustworthiness.
- E: There is no categorical bar. Many accident reports qualify. The problem is records prepared in anticipation of litigation, which the court evaluates case by case.
Rule: A record of a regularly conducted activity is admissible if the foundational requirements are met and “the opponent does not show that the source of information or the method or circumstances of preparation indicate a lack of trustworthiness.” FRE 803(6)(E). The 2014 amendment placed this burden on the opponent.

## Final 5 · Q21 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(18): learned treatises
In a malpractice suit over a delayed diagnosis of appendicitis, the defense expert testifies that waiting twelve hours for an ultrasound met the standard of care. On cross-examination, the expert concedes that Harmon's Textbook of Emergency Medicine is a reliable authority in the field. Plaintiff's counsel then reads aloud a passage stating that suspected appendicitis in a child “requires imaging within four hours,” and moves to admit the textbook as an exhibit for the jury to take into deliberations.

How should the court rule?

A. The passage is inadmissible hearsay, because the expert didn't rely on the textbook in forming her opinion.
B. The passage is admissible only if its author testifies, because a treatise can't be cross-examined.
C. The passage is substantive evidence of the standard of care, but the textbook may not be received as an exhibit.
D. The textbook is admissible as an exhibit, because the expert conceded that it is a reliable authority.
E. The passage may be considered only to impeach the expert, not as proof of the standard of care.

Answer: C
- A: Reliance on direct is one route. The other is calling the passage to the expert's attention on cross-examination, which plaintiff's counsel did.
- B: No author testimony is needed. Reliability is established by the expert's admission, another expert's testimony, or judicial notice.
- C: Correct. Rule 803(18) admits a statement in a treatise that is called to an expert's attention on cross-examination (or relied on by the expert on direct) and established as a reliable authority by testimony, admission, or judicial notice. If admitted, the statement may be read into evidence but not received as an exhibit, so the jury won't give a book undue weight.
- D: Rule 803(18) says the statement “may be read into evidence but not received as an exhibit.” The expert's concession establishes reliability, not exhibit status.
- E: That was the common-law limit. Rule 803(18) makes a qualifying passage substantive evidence, admissible for its truth.
Rule: Rule 803(18): a statement in a treatise, periodical, or pamphlet is admissible for its truth if it is called to an expert's attention on cross or relied on by the expert on direct and is established as a reliable authority. It may be read into evidence but not received as an exhibit.

## Final 5 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Davis and Hammon: when a 911 call becomes testimonial
Rita Vance calls 911 and screams, "He's hitting me—my husband, Carl Vance!" The dispatcher asks whether he has a weapon. Rita says, "No, his fists. He just ran out the back door." The dispatcher then asks a series of questions, and Rita, now calm, answers: Carl had been drinking since noon, he hit her twice last month, and he once broke her wrist. Rita refuses to testify at Carl's assault trial, and the prosecution offers the whole recording.

Which parts are admissible over Carl's Confrontation Clause objection?

A. All of it, because an emergency call is made to get help and so can never be testimonial.
B. None of it, because Rita's statements accuse Carl and she won't submit to cross-examination.
C. Only what she said during the attack and about the weapon, not her calm account of past abuse.
D. All of it, because a victim's statements about her own attacker are nontestimonial.
E. None of it, because Rita's refusal to testify forfeits the prosecution's use of her statements.

Answer: C
- A: Davis v. Washington held that a 911 call can become testimonial once the emergency ends and the questions turn to proving past events.
- B: Statements whose primary purpose is to meet an ongoing emergency are nontestimonial and fall outside the Clause.
- C: Correct. Under Davis v. Washington, Rita's statements during the attack and about the weapon were made to meet an ongoing emergency, so they are nontestimonial. Once Carl fled on foot, unarmed, and Rita calmly recounted past abuse, the primary purpose shifted to establishing past events. That made those statements testimonial, as in Hammon. Unlike in Michigan v. Bryant, no armed assailant posed a continuing threat to the public.
- D: The victim's status doesn't decide the issue. In Hammon, the victim's statements were testimonial.
- E: Forfeiture applies to a defendant who wrongfully procures the witness's absence (Giles), not to a witness's own refusal.
Rule: Statements to police are nontestimonial when their primary purpose is to meet an ongoing emergency, and testimonial once the purpose becomes establishing past events (Davis v. Washington). A conversation can shift from one to the other.

## Final 5 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Melendez-Diaz and Bullcoming: a surrogate analyst
Dunn is prosecuted for aggravated driving while intoxicated. Analyst Dorrance ran Dunn’s blood sample through a gas chromatograph and signed a “Certificate of Analyst” reporting a blood-alcohol concentration of 0.21 and affirming that the sample’s seal was intact, that he followed the lab’s protocol, and that no circumstance affected the sample’s integrity. Before trial, Dorrance is placed on unpaid leave for undisclosed reasons.

The prosecution calls analyst Petrakis, who works in the same lab and knows its procedures but did not observe or review Dorrance’s test, to introduce the certificate. Dunn objects.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude the certificate, because it is testimonial and Dunn has the right to confront the analyst who made the certification, not a surrogate.
B. Admit the certificate, because Dorrance merely transcribed a machine’s output, and machine output is not a statement by a person.
C. Admit the certificate, because Dunn could have subpoenaed Dorrance himself if he wanted to cross-examine him.
D. Admit the certificate, because Petrakis is subject to full cross-examination about the lab’s procedures and the reliability of gas chromatography.
E. Admit the certificate as a business record, because the lab regularly prepares such certificates and business records are nontestimonial.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. This is Bullcoming: the certifying analyst must be made available, absent unavailability and a prior opportunity for cross.
- B: The certificate conveyed the analyst’s own representations about chain of custody and protocol, not raw machine output.
- C: Melendez-Diaz held that the prosecution bears the burden of presenting its witnesses. The defendant’s subpoena power does not substitute for confrontation.
- D: Bullcoming rejected surrogate testimony. Cross about general procedures does not test what the certifying analyst did and saw.
- E: Records made for the purpose of proving a fact at trial are testimonial even if routinely made. Melendez-Diaz rejected the business-record argument for exactly this kind of document.
Rule: Forensic reports created to serve as evidence in a criminal prosecution are testimonial. Melendez-Diaz v. Massachusetts. The Confrontation Clause is not satisfied by surrogate testimony from an analyst who did not perform or observe the test reported in the certification. Bullcoming v. New Mexico.

## Final 5 · Q24 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Arbitrariness: Rock, Scheffer, Egelhoff, and Holmes
Each rule below was applied to exclude evidence offered by a criminal defendant. Which application is most likely to violate the defendant's constitutional right to present a defense?

A. A per se rule barring hypnotically refreshed testimony, applied to keep the defendant from describing what she now remembers.
B. A rule excluding third-party-guilt evidence that raises only a bare suspicion, applied to a vague rumor about another man.
C. A statute making voluntary intoxication irrelevant to mental state, applied to exclude evidence that he was drunk.
D. A per se rule barring polygraph evidence, applied to exclude a favorable polygraph result the defendant offered.
E. Rule 403, applied to exclude the defendant's fourth alibi witness as cumulative of the other three.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under the arbitrariness test of Washington v. Texas, Rock, and Holmes, a rule is unconstitutional if it infringes a weighty interest of the accused and is arbitrary or disproportionate to its purposes. Rock v. Arkansas held that a per se bar on a defendant's own hypnotically refreshed testimony is arbitrary, because it excludes all of it without asking whether this testimony is reliable.
- B: Holmes v. South Carolina approved ordinary third-party-guilt rules that exclude evidence raising only a bare suspicion. What it struck down was judging the defense evidence solely by the strength of the prosecution's case.
- C: Montana v. Egelhoff upheld such a statute. A state may decide that voluntary intoxication is irrelevant to mental state, which makes the evidence immaterial rather than arbitrarily excluded.
- D: United States v. Scheffer upheld a per se ban on polygraph evidence: given the scientific disagreement about its reliability, the ban is neither arbitrary nor disproportionate to its purposes.
- E: Holmes reaffirmed that rules like Rule 403, which exclude evidence whose probative value is outweighed by dangers such as needless cumulation, are well within constitutional limits.
Rule: A rule violates the right to present a defense if it infringes a weighty interest of the accused and is arbitrary or disproportionate to its purposes (Washington v. Texas; Rock v. Arkansas; Holmes). Per se polygraph bans (Scheffer), bare-suspicion rules, and Egelhoff-type statutes survive.

## Final 5 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 702(a): helpfulness and common knowledge
In a trademark case, the plaintiff's sauce is sold as "Brisko." It claims that the defendant's "Crisko" sauce is confusingly similar. The plaintiff calls a linguistics professor, who would testify that, in her opinion, "Brisko" and "Crisko" sound alike to ordinary American listeners. The jurors will hear both names spoken. The defendant moves to exclude her.

What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admit it, because the professor is well qualified to testify about linguistics.
B. Admit it, because Rule 704(a) allows opinions on ultimate issues such as confusion.
C. Exclude it, because linguistics isn't a science that can satisfy Daubert.
D. Exclude it, because the opinion is a legal conclusion telling the jury what result to reach.
E. Exclude it, because jurors can judge for themselves whether two words sound alike.

Answer: E
- A: Qualification is necessary but not sufficient. The testimony must also help the trier of fact.
- B: Rule 704(a) removes the ultimate-issue objection, but the opinion must still satisfy Rule 702(a)'s helpfulness requirement.
- C: Kumho Tire applies gatekeeping to all expert testimony. The issue here is helpfulness, not whether linguistics is a science.
- D: Saying two words sound alike isn't a statement of law. The problem is that jurors can judge it for themselves.
- E: Correct. Rule 702(a) requires that the specialized knowledge help the trier of fact. The outline's example is whether "Match" and "Macho" sound alike. Jurors who hear the words can decide that themselves, so the testimony doesn't help.
Rule: Rule 702(a) requires that expert knowledge help the trier of fact. Opinions on matters within jurors' common knowledge, like how two words sound, aren't helpful. Technical matters (slip resistance of wet tile) are.

## Final 5 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Kumho Tire: hedonic damages
In a wrongful-death suit, the plaintiff offers an economist to value the decedent's lost “pleasure of life” at $2.4 million. His method extrapolates from what consumers pay for small reductions in risk, such as smoke detectors and airbags. The defense moves to exclude. The plaintiff argues that because economics isn't a laboratory science, the Daubert factors don't apply, and the method's soundness should be left to cross-examination.

How should the court respond?

A. Agree, because Daubert's gatekeeping applies only to scientific testimony, and economics isn't a laboratory science.
B. Agree, because general acceptance among economists ends the court's inquiry under Rule 702.
C. Disagree, because gatekeeping covers all experts, and the court may ask if the method measures what it claims.
D. Agree in part, because the court may exclude the opinion under Rule 403 but not under Rule 702.
E. Disagree, because the court must apply all five Daubert factors to every expert, whatever the field.

Answer: C
- A: Kumho Tire held that the gatekeeping duty extends to all expert testimony, because Rule 702 makes no distinction among scientific, technical, and other specialized knowledge.
- B: General acceptance is one Daubert factor, not the end of the inquiry, and acceptance of a method for valuing risk doesn't show it reliably measures damages in a courtroom.
- C: Correct. Under Kumho Tire, the judge must ensure that all expert testimony rests on a reliable foundation and may use the Daubert factors where they are reasonable measures of reliability. Courts have excluded hedonic-damages valuations because extrapolating from small-risk purchases doesn't reliably measure the value of one particular life.
- D: Rule 403 remains available, but Rule 702 is the primary screen. A method that doesn't reliably measure what it claims fails Rule 702(c)–(d).
- E: Kumho Tire made the factors flexible: the judge may use them where they fit, but they are neither required nor ruled out for any category of expert.
Rule: Kumho Tire: the gatekeeping duty applies to all expert testimony, and the Daubert factors may be used flexibly where they are reasonable measures of reliability. Rule 702(c)–(d) require reliable methods, reliably applied.

## Final 5 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 902: what is self-authenticating
Which of the following items requires extrinsic evidence of authenticity before it may be admitted?

A. A printout of a company's web page, offered to prove what the company said about its product.
B. A copy of a deed, certified as correct by the county recorder who keeps the original.
C. A can of soup bearing the manufacturer's label, offered to prove who made the soup.
D. A page from a printed newspaper, offered to prove that a particular advertisement appeared in it.
E. A loan agreement accompanied by a notary's certificate of acknowledgment.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Rule 902 lists specific self-authenticating items, and ordinary web pages aren't among them. The proponent needs evidence, such as testimony from someone who viewed the site or a certification under Rule 902(13) or (14), that the printout accurately reflects the company's page.
- B: Rule 902(4) makes a copy of a public record self-authenticating if it is certified as correct by the custodian or another authorized person.
- C: Rule 902(7) makes self-authenticating an inscription, sign, tag, or label purporting to have been affixed in the course of business and indicating origin, ownership, or control.
- D: Printed material purporting to be a newspaper or periodical is self-authenticating under Rule 902(6). Forging an entire newspaper is unlikely.
- E: Rule 902(8) covers a document accompanied by a certificate of acknowledgment lawfully executed by a notary public.
Rule: Rule 902 lists items that need no extrinsic evidence of authenticity, including certified copies of public records (902(4)), newspapers (902(6)), trade labels (902(7)), acknowledged documents (902(8)), and certified records under 902(11)–(14). The opponent may still dispute authenticity, and self-authentication isn't a hearsay exception.

## Final 5 · Q28 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Best evidence: testimony about stored GPS data
A Coast Guard officer boarded Sal Rizzo's fishing boat 20 miles offshore and found 300 kilos of cocaine. At Rizzo's federal trial for importation, the officer testifies that the boat's GPS unit "showed that the boat had come from Mexican waters." The government offers no printout, photograph, or data from the GPS unit and gives no reason why it can't. Rizzo objects.

How should the court rule?

A. Overrule it, because the officer testifies from personal knowledge of what he saw.
B. Overrule it, because the best evidence rule applies only to writings, not electronic data.
C. Overrule it, because the GPS unit is an object, like a knife, and objects aren't covered.
D. Sustain it, because the testimony proves the content of a recording without the original.
E. Sustain it, because GPS evidence must be authenticated by a qualified expert witness.

Answer: D
- A: The officer didn't see the boat in Mexican waters. He is relaying what the device recorded, which is its content.
- B: Rule 1001 defines writings and recordings to include electronically stored information, and an accurate printout of it is an "original."
- C: The stored data is a recording. The device isn't being offered as an object. Its contents are being used as proof.
- D: Correct. Under Rule 1002, proving the content of a recording requires an original or duplicate unless an exception applies. In the Bennett case, testimony about what a GPS device's stored data showed was proof of content. With no original and no Rule 1004 excuse, the testimony is inadmissible.
- E: Authentication may be done by a witness with knowledge or by describing the process (Rule 901(b)(9)). The problem here is the best evidence rule.
Rule: Rule 1002 requires the original (or a duplicate under Rule 1003) to prove the content of a writing, recording, or photograph, including electronically stored data, unless Rules 1004–1007 excuse it. Testimony about an event that was also recorded is not proof of content.

## Final 5 · Q29 · Privileges — Rule 502(d): a federal non-waiver order binds state courts
In a federal antitrust case, the court entered an order under Rule 502(d) providing that production of privileged material in discovery "shall not constitute a waiver in this or any other proceeding." Kellan Corp. produced a privileged memo from its general counsel. Later, in a related state-court suit, the plaintiff argues that Kellan waived the privilege by producing the memo in the federal case.

What is the correct result?

A. Waiver, because state law alone governs privilege in a state-court suit.
B. Waiver, unless Kellan shows the production was inadvertent and it took reasonable steps.
C. No waiver, because a Rule 502(d) order binds the parties but not other courts.
D. No waiver, because a Rule 502(d) order binds all other federal and state proceedings.
E. Waiver of the memo and of every communication on the same subject matter.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 502(f) makes the rule apply in state proceedings, and Rule 502(d) orders bind state courts.
- B: Rule 502(b)'s inadvertence test applies when no order governs. A Rule 502(d) order replaces it.
- C: That describes a party agreement under Rule 502(e). A court order binds everyone.
- D: Correct. Under Rule 502(d), a federal court may order that a disclosure connected with its litigation isn't a waiver. The order binds "any other Federal or State proceeding," and Rule 502(f) confirms the rule applies in state proceedings.
- E: Subject-matter waiver under Rule 502(a) requires an intentional waiver. The order means there is none.
Rule: Rule 502(d) orders bind all federal and state proceedings. Rule 502(b) protects inadvertent disclosures with reasonable precautions and prompt rectification. Rule 502(a) limits subject-matter waiver to intentional disclosures, where fairness requires it. Rule 502(e) agreements bind only the parties.

## Final 5 · Q30 · Privileges — Marital confidences: a monitored work email
State trooper Lyle Brandt is charged in federal court with extortion. While on duty, he emailed his wife from his state police computer: “I took the cash. Don't say anything to anyone.” Every time he logged on, a banner warned that users have no expectation of privacy and consent to monitoring, and he clicked “accept.” Investigators retrieve the email from the department's server. Brandt invokes the marital confidences privilege.

How should the court rule?

A. Uphold the privilege, because marital communications are presumed confidential and he meant it for his wife alone.
B. Uphold the privilege, because only his wife, as the recipient, could waive it, and she hasn't.
C. Uphold the privilege, because he never actually read the banner and so expected privacy.
D. Reject the privilege, because the marital confidences privilege covers only spoken communications.
E. Reject the privilege, because he had no reasonable expectation of privacy on a monitored work system.

Answer: E
- A: The presumption of confidentiality can be overcome. Sending the message through a system he had been warned was monitored defeats the reasonable expectation of confidentiality the privilege requires.
- B: Most courts let either spouse claim the privilege. But it never attached here, because the email wasn't confidential.
- C: Confidentiality turns on a reasonable expectation, not only his subjective belief. Clicking through the banner every day gave him notice whether or not he read it.
- D: The privilege covers communications in any form, including letters and emails. The problem is confidentiality, not the medium.
- E: Correct. The marital confidences privilege covers only communications made in confidence. Using a work computer after accepting a banner that warned of monitoring and no expectation of privacy defeats confidentiality, as a federal court held on similar facts.
Rule: Federal courts recognize the marital confidences privilege as federal common law under Rule 501. It protects communications made in confidence during the marriage. Marital communications are presumed confidential, but the presumption fails when the spouse has no reasonable expectation of privacy, as on a monitored workplace system.

## Final 6 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Rule 606(b)(2)(A): what the juror may say
A jury convicts Dorian Pratt of reckless homicide after a trial that turned on whether the victim could have seen Pratt's truck from a crosswalk 300 feet away. Two days later, juror Ellen Marsh tells defense counsel that during deliberations another juror pulled up a satellite image of the intersection on her phone, measured the sight line, and announced, "There's no way he couldn't see her." Marsh adds that the image "changed three votes, including mine." Pratt moves for a new trial and offers Marsh's affidavit.

Which part of the affidavit may the court consider?

A. None of it, because Rule 606(b) bars any juror testimony about anything that happened during deliberations.
B. All of it, because a juror's own research is an outside influence that Rule 606(b) does not reach at all.
C. Only her account that the image was brought into deliberations, not her account of its effect on any juror's vote.
D. All of it, because the Sixth Amendment overrides Rule 606(b) whenever a criminal jury was compromised.
E. Only her statement that it changed her own vote, because a juror may always describe her own reasoning.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 606(b)(2)(A) is an exception for extraneous prejudicial information. A juror may testify that such information was brought to the jury's attention, even if that happened during deliberations.
- B: The internet image is extraneous information under Rule 606(b)(2)(A), so testimony that it was brought in is allowed. But the exception doesn't open up the jurors' votes. Rule 606(b)(1) still bars testimony about the effect of anything on a juror's vote.
- C: Correct. Rule 606(b)(2)(A) lets Marsh testify that extraneous prejudicial information was brought to the jury's attention. Rule 606(b)(1) still bars her testimony about the effect of anything on any juror's vote or mental processes. The court decides prejudice objectively, without asking jurors how they were swayed.
- D: The only constitutional exception is Peña-Rodriguez, for clear racial bias relied on to convict. Tanner held that Rule 606(b) generally doesn't violate the Sixth Amendment.
- E: A juror's own mental processes about the verdict are the core of what Rule 606(b)(1) bars.
Rule: Rule 606(b)(2)(A)–(C) allows juror testimony only about extraneous prejudicial information, outside influence, or a mistake on the verdict form. Even then, Rule 606(b)(1) bars testimony about the effect on any juror's vote or mental processes.

## Final 6 · Q2 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Old Chief: stipulations outside legal status
A defendant is charged with possessing cocaine with intent to distribute. His defense is that the drugs were for his personal use. He offers to stipulate that police found 360 individually wrapped packets of cocaine in his car and asks the court to bar the prosecution from showing the jury the packets or photographs of them. The prosecution refuses the stipulation. How should the court rule?

A. Bar the evidence, because the stipulation removes the issue from the case and makes the physical evidence irrelevant.
B. Let the prosecution use the packets, because Old Chief’s stipulation rule is limited to proof of the defendant’s legal status.
C. Bar the photographs but allow the packets, because Rule 403 requires the least prejudicial form of proof.
D. Admit the evidence, because Rule 403 doesn’t apply to physical evidence seized from the defendant.
E. Bar the evidence, because Old Chief requires the prosecution to accept any stipulation conceding those facts.

Answer: B
- A: A fact need not be disputed for evidence of it to be relevant (ACN to Rule 401; Old Chief). The question is whether 403 calls for exclusion, and it doesn’t here.
- B: Correct. Old Chief reaffirmed that a party ordinarily chooses its own proof, citing descriptive richness, a morally reasonable verdict, and jurors’ expectations. Its holding covers felon status, where the name of the crime adds nothing but prejudice. Here the quantity and packaging bear directly on intent to distribute, the disputed issue.
- C: Rule 403 lets a court consider alternatives, but it doesn’t require the least prejudicial proof, and photographs of drug packets carry little unfair prejudice. Outside legal status, the prosecution may tell its story with its own evidence (Old Chief).
- D: 403 applies to all relevant evidence. The point is that the danger of unfair prejudice here doesn’t substantially outweigh strong probative value.
- E: That overreads Old Chief, which limited its holding to proof of legal status. Outside that setting, the prosecution may tell its story with its own evidence.
Rule: Rule 403: a stipulation doesn’t make evidence irrelevant. Old Chief requires accepting a stipulation only when the issue is the defendant’s legal status; otherwise parties choose their own proof.

## Final 6 · Q3 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Spark-of-life evidence vs. the victim’s character
In a federal murder trial where the only issue is whether the defendant was the shooter, the prosecution calls the victim’s sister. It seeks to have her (1) show a photograph of the victim with his two young children at a birthday party and say briefly that he was a father of two who worked as a mechanic, and (2) testify that he “was the gentlest man alive and never got in a fight in his life.” The defense objects to both. Which ruling is most consistent with the rules as discussed in class?

A. Item 2 is admissible, but only as opinion or reputation testimony rather than as the sister’s own description.
B. Both are admissible under the “spark-of-life” doctrine, which lets the prosecution humanize the victim.
C. Both must be excluded as irrelevant, because the victim’s identity and death are undisputed.
D. Item 2 is admissible under Rule 404(a)(2)(C), because in any homicide case the prosecution may prove the victim’s peaceful character.
E. Item 1 is within the court’s Rule 403 discretion, but item 2 is improper character evidence.

Answer: E
- A: Form isn’t the problem. A victim’s peacefulness is character evidence the prosecution may offer only to rebut evidence that the victim was the first aggressor (Rule 404(a)(2)(C)), and identity, not self-defense, is the issue.
- B: Spark-of-life is a Minnesota doctrine (State v. Graham), and even there it doesn’t override the character rules. Item 2 is offered to show the victim’s peaceful nature.
- C: The defense’s 401/403 objection to spark-of-life evidence is a real argument, but courts have discretion to allow brief humanizing background. Item 2 fails for a different reason: it’s character evidence.
- D: 404(a)(2)(C) applies only to rebut evidence that the victim was the first aggressor, which no one has offered here.
- E: Correct. Brief background that humanizes a victim, like the “spark-of-life” photo in the Chauvin trial, is left to the judge’s 401/403 discretion and is supported by Old Chief’s point about a morally reasonable verdict. But as Judge Cahill put it, propensity for peacefulness gets into character evidence. The prosecution may offer a homicide victim’s peacefulness only to rebut evidence that the victim struck first (404(a)(2)(C)), and identity is the only issue here.
Rule: Spark-of-life background is within 401/403 discretion. A victim’s peacefulness is character evidence, admissible only to rebut first-aggressor evidence (404(a)(2)(C)).

## Final 6 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 407: remedial measures by a nonparty
Hal Ortiz fell from a stepladder bought at Brightway Hardware and sued only the manufacturer, Apex Ladders, for failure to warn. A month after his fall, Brightway put its own stickers on every Apex ladder in stock: "Do not stand on top two steps." Apex had nothing to do with the stickers. Ortiz offers them to prove a warning was needed, and Apex objects under Rule 407.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude the stickers, because Rule 407 bars any post-accident measure offered to prove a need for a warning.
B. Admit the stickers, because Rule 407 doesn't apply in a products-liability case like this one.
C. Exclude the stickers, because they are relevant only if Apex itself adopted or approved them.
D. Admit the stickers, but only to impeach an Apex witness who says no warning was needed.
E. Rule 407 doesn't bar them, because Brightway isn't a party, though Rule 403 still applies.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 407's policy is to avoid discouraging a defendant from making repairs. A nonparty's measure doesn't implicate that policy, so courts generally don't apply the rule to it.
- B: Since 1997, Rule 407 expressly covers product and design defects and the need for a warning. It does apply in products cases.
- C: The stickers have some tendency to show a warning was needed, whoever posted them. Weakness goes to Rule 403, not to admissibility.
- D: Impeachment is one permitted purpose, but it isn't the only route. Because Rule 407 doesn't apply to a nonparty's measure, the stickers may also be offered substantively.
- E: Correct. Third-party remedial measures are generally admissible over a Rule 407 objection, because the rule's policy of encouraging defendants to make repairs isn't at stake. They are often weak, so Rule 403 may still exclude them.
Rule: Rule 407 bars a party's own post-injury measures offered to prove negligence, defect, or the need for a warning. Measures taken by nonparties fall outside its policy but remain subject to Rule 403.

## Final 6 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rules 408 and 409: apology vs. negotiation
After a parking-lot collision, Dana jumps out and tells Lee, "This was my fault. I'll pay your hospital bill." Three weeks later, Lee's lawyer demands $50,000 and Dana's lawyer disputes the amount. At a settlement meeting, Dana says, "I know I ran the stop sign." Lee sues Dana. Which statement is admissible to prove Dana's liability?

A. Only "I know I ran the stop sign."
B. Only "This was my fault."
C. "This was my fault" and "I'll pay your hospital bill."
D. All three, because each is an opposing party's statement.
E. None of the three, because Rules 408 and 409 cover them all.

Answer: B
- A: This one was made during compromise negotiations over a claim disputed in amount, so Rule 408(a)(2) bars it.
- B: Correct. Rule 409 bars only the offer to pay expenses, not statements that go with it. The fault statement is Dana's own under Rule 801(d)(2)(A), and no claim was yet in dispute for Rule 408.
- C: The fault statement comes in, but Rule 409 bars the offer to pay the hospital bill when offered to prove liability.
- D: Rule 801(d)(2) answers only a hearsay objection. Rules 408 and 409 are separate bars that still apply.
- E: Rule 409 is narrower than Rule 408. It doesn't protect an accident-scene admission of fault.
Rule: Rule 409 protects only the offer to pay medical expenses. Rule 408 protects conduct and statements in negotiations over a disputed claim as well. An apology at the scene isn't covered; the same words at a settlement meeting are.

## Final 6 · Q6 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(b): preparation and plan
Two masked men robbed a credit union and escaped in a gray minivan, which was found abandoned an hour later. Leo Grant is charged with the robbery. The prosecution offers evidence that two days before the robbery, Grant stole that same minivan from a mall parking lot, where cameras recorded him. Grant objects that the car theft is an uncharged crime offered to show he is a thief.

What is the best basis for the ruling?

A. Admit it to show preparation and plan, and through them identity.
B. Exclude it, because car theft and bank robbery are different crimes.
C. Exclude it, because Rule 404(b) bars evidence of uncharged crimes.
D. Admit it to show Grant's character for theft, since both crimes involve stealing.
E. Admit it, but only to show Grant's motive for the robbery.

Answer: A
- A: Correct, even though it proves Grant committed a second crime. Stealing the minivan later used in the robbery was preparation for it, part of one overall plan rather than a mere pattern (contrast Kirsch). That links Grant to the robbery without any inference about his character, subject to Rule 403.
- B: Similarity of crimes matters for signature identity, not here. The link is that the minivan was used in this robbery.
- C: Rule 404(b)(2) permits uncharged acts for non-character purposes (Huddleston).
- D: That is propensity reasoning, which Rule 404(b)(1) bars.
- E: Motive is a weak fit. Stealing a car doesn't explain why he would rob a bank. Preparation and plan describe the link.
Rule: Acts that are steps toward the charged crime, such as obtaining the vehicle, weapon, or tools, show preparation and plan under Rule 404(b)(2). They can identify the defendant without propensity reasoning.

## Final 6 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Guardia: Rule 403 still limits Rule 413
A physical therapist is charged with sexually assaulting two patients during treatment, and he claims his touching was medically appropriate. The prosecution offers testimony from five other former patients who describe touching during therapy that they considered improper. Experts for both sides disagree about whether each technique described was medically legitimate. The trial court excludes the five patients under Rule 403, finding that the trial would become seven mini-trials on medical propriety.

How will an appellate court most likely rule?

A. Affirm, because Rule 413 evidence remains subject to Rule 403.
B. Reverse, because Rule 413 evidence is admissible as of right.
C. Reverse, because Rule 413 makes propensity evidence presumptively fair.
D. Reverse, because the evidence was also admissible as a common plan.
E. Affirm, because Rule 413 applies only to prior convictions.

Answer: A
- A: Correct, though Rule 413 was enacted to let such evidence in. United States v. Guardia affirmed on nearly these facts. Rule 413 does not displace Rule 403, which applies with its normal balance, and disputes over medical propriety would confuse the jury.
- B: Rule 413(a) says "may admit," and Rule 403 still applies.
- C: Guardia rejected a "presumption in favor of admission" but also held courts may not treat propensity as inherently too prejudicial. The balance is ordinary.
- D: Whatever rule admits the evidence, Rule 403 applies, and the confusion problem is the same.
- E: No conviction is required.
Rule: Rules 413–415 admit propensity evidence in sex cases but remain subject to ordinary Rule 403 balancing (Guardia). Mini-trials and confusion can justify exclusion, reviewed for abuse of discretion.

## Final 6 · Q8 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(a)(2)(B): victim's trait opens the door
Owen is charged with assaulting Reese and claims self-defense. Owen calls a witness who testifies, "In my opinion, Reese is a violent person." Which response by the prosecution is proper?

A. Calling witnesses to give opinions that Reese is peaceful and that Owen is violent.
B. Calling an officer to testify that Owen broke a man's jaw in a bar fight in 2021.
C. Offering nothing on Owen's character, because Owen never offered his own character.
D. Offering Reese's peacefulness only, because rebuttal is limited to the alleged victim.
E. Offering reputation evidence only, because Rule 405(a) excludes opinion on rebuttal.

Answer: A
