- A: Correct. Once a defendant offers the alleged victim's pertinent trait, Rule 404(a)(2)(B) lets the prosecutor rebut it and offer the defendant's same trait. Rule 405(a) allows reputation or opinion.
- B: Rule 405(a) limits this proof to reputation or opinion. Violence isn't an essential element here, so Rule 405(b) doesn't allow specific acts.
- C: Attacking the victim's trait exposes the defendant's same trait, even if he never offered his own character.
- D: Rule 404(a)(2)(B) reaches further: the prosecutor may also offer the defendant's same trait.
- E: Rule 405(a) treats reputation and opinion alike, including on rebuttal.
Rule: Under Rule 404(a)(2)(B), a defendant who offers the victim's pertinent trait lets the prosecutor rebut it and offer the defendant's same trait. Under Rule 405(a), the form is reputation or opinion.

## Final 6 · Q9 · Character Evidence — A defendant who vouches for her own character
A woman charged with arson testifies on direct, “I would never set a fire. I’m just not that kind of person.” On cross-examination, the prosecutor, who has a fire-department report, asks, “Isn’t it true you set fire to your ex-boyfriend’s car two years ago?” Defense counsel objects. How should the court rule?

A. Sustain the objection, because only her reputation, not her specific acts, may be raised on cross.
B. Overrule the objection, and admit the fire-department report if she denies the car fire.
C. Sustain the objection, because Rule 608(b) limits cross-examination about specific acts to conduct probative of truthfulness.
D. Sustain the objection, because she didn’t call a character witness, so her character isn’t in issue.
E. Overrule the objection, because by vouching for her own character she opened herself to cross on specific instances.

Answer: E
- A: 405(a) expressly allows inquiry into relevant specific instances of conduct on cross-examination of a character witness, which she became.
- B: Cross on the specific instance is proper because she made herself a character witness, but Rule 405(a) doesn’t allow extrinsic proof of the act. The prosecutor must take her answer.
- C: 608(b) governs attacks on character for truthfulness. This cross tests her own testimony about a different trait (not setting fires) under 405(a), and it also contradicts her.
- D: A defendant who testifies to her own good character has offered character evidence just as if she’d called a witness. She can’t vouch for herself and avoid cross on the same trait.
- E: Correct. This is Problem 3.16 (“I Wouldn’t Shoot Anybody”). Her statement offered her own pertinent trait under 404(a)(2)(A), and 405(a) allows cross about relevant specific instances with a good-faith basis. The question also contradicts her testimony. The defense’s best move is a 105 instruction limiting how the jury uses her answer.
Rule: A defendant who vouches for her own trait becomes her own character witness, so cross about relevant specific instances is allowed (405(a); Problem 3.16).

## Final 6 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Impeachment by contradiction: "I've never been in trouble"
Before trial, the court ruled under Rule 609(a)(1)(B) that the prosecution could not use defendant Sam Hyde's seven-year-old felony drug conviction to impeach him. On direct examination, Hyde volunteers: "I've never been in trouble with the law in my life. I'd never do something like this." The prosecutor asks to cross-examine Hyde about the drug conviction.

What is the best ruling?

A. Deny it, because the court already excluded the conviction under Rule 609.
B. Deny it, because statements on direct can't open the door to excluded evidence.
C. Allow it, because Hyde's claim of a clean record opened the door.
D. Allow it, but only through extrinsic proof of the conviction.
E. Deny it, because only crimen falsi may impeach a testifying defendant.

Answer: C
- A: The ruling barred using the conviction to show character for truthfulness under Rule 609. It doesn't license a false impression on a point Hyde chose to raise.
- B: A defendant who makes a sweeping false claim on direct invites contradiction.
- C: Correct, though it undoes his pretrial victory. Saying he's "never been in trouble" is both a false factual claim the prosecution may contradict and an assertion of law-abiding character. Under Rule 404(a)(2)(A), that lets the prosecution rebut, and Rule 405(a) permits inquiry into specific instances on cross. The court still applies Rule 403 and gives a limiting instruction.
- D: Cross-examination is the natural route. The point is that the door is open, not that extrinsic proof is required.
- E: Felonies may impeach a testifying defendant under Rule 609(a)(1)(B), and here the door was opened anyway.
Rule: A defendant who makes broad claims of a clean record or good character opens the door to contradiction and to rebuttal of character (Rules 404(a)(2)(A), 405(a)), even if the conviction was excluded under Rule 609. Rule 403 and a limiting instruction remain.

## Final 6 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 608(a): form of proof
After a plaintiff testifies, the defense calls her former coworker. Which testimony is proper under Rule 608(a)?

A. "In my opinion, she is an untruthful person."
B. "She lied to the jury when she testified this morning."
C. "She lied to our boss last year about being sick."
D. "She has a bad temper and I wouldn't trust her with money."
E. "She has been arrested for shoplifting twice."

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Rule 608(a) allows opinion or reputation testimony about a witness's character for untruthfulness.
- B: This is an opinion on specific testimony, not on general character for truthfulness, and it invades the jury's role.
- C: This is a specific instance, which Rule 608(b) bars as extrinsic evidence.
- D: Temper isn't truthfulness. Rule 608(a) is limited to character for truthfulness.
- E: An arrest isn't a conviction, so Rule 609 doesn't apply. Rule 608(b) bars extrinsic proof.
Rule: Rule 608(a) permits only opinion or reputation about character for truthfulness. Specific instances go to Rule 608(b), on cross and without extrinsic proof.

## Final 6 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Luce: preserving a Rule 609 ruling
Before trial, the judge rules that the government may impeach Boyd with a prior conviction if he testifies. Boyd decides not to testify and is convicted. On appeal, he challenges the in limine ruling. What is the most likely result?

A. The claim isn't preserved, because Boyd never testified.
B. Reversal, because the ruling effectively kept him off the stand.
C. Review on the merits, because a definitive ruling preserves the claim.
D. Remand, so the trial judge can decide what Boyd would have said.
E. Reversal only if he proves he would have testified otherwise.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under Luce, a defendant must testify to challenge an in limine Rule 609 ruling.
- B: Under Luce the harm is wholly speculative when the defendant doesn't testify.
- C: A definitive ruling doesn't preserve this claim. Luce requires the defendant to testify.
- D: Luce doesn't call for a remand. The challenge simply isn't preserved.
- E: No offer of proof substitutes for testifying under Luce.
Rule: A defendant must testify to preserve a challenge to an in limine Rule 609 ruling (Luce).

## Final 6 · Q13 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 613(a): showing the statement
In a shooting trial, prosecution eyewitness Tess Ames testifies that she clearly saw the defendant's face. On cross, defense counsel asks: "Didn't you text your cousin the next morning, 'I never saw the shooter's face'?" The prosecutor objects that counsel must first show Ames the text. Defense counsel refuses, and the prosecutor then asks to see the text.

How should the court rule?

A. Sustain it, because a witness must be shown a prior written statement before being asked about it.
B. Overrule it, because the text isn't hearsay, so counsel never has to show it to anyone.
C. Sustain it, because Rule 613(b) requires a foundation before any questions about the text.
D. Overrule it, but have defense counsel show the text to the prosecutor on request.
E. Overrule it, but forbid any later extrinsic proof of the text if Ames denies sending it.

Answer: D
- A: That was the common-law rule of the Queen's Case. Rule 613(a) abolishes it.
- B: The non-hearsay point is right, but Rule 613(a) requires disclosure to opposing counsel on request.
- C: Rule 613(b) governs extrinsic evidence of a prior inconsistent statement, not questioning the witness about it.
- D: Correct. Under Rule 613(a), a party examining a witness about a prior statement need not show it or disclose its contents to the witness. But it must, on request, show or disclose it to an adverse party's attorney.
- E: If Ames denies sending the text, extrinsic proof is allowed on this non-collateral point, after she has had a chance to explain or deny it (Rule 613(b), as amended in 2024).
Rule: Under Rule 613(a), the examiner needn't show a prior statement to the witness but must disclose it to opposing counsel on request. Under Rule 613(b), extrinsic proof requires an opportunity to explain or deny, ordinarily given first since 2024.

## Final 6 · Q14 · Definition of Hearsay — Effect on the listener: notice, with a Rule 105 instruction
Lorna slipped on spilled soda in aisle 7 of a grocery store and sued the store for negligence. The store contends there was no spill at all and that Lorna tripped over her own feet.

At trial, Lorna calls a cashier, who testifies that about 40 minutes before the fall, a shopper she did not know walked up to the store manager and said: “Somebody dropped a big bottle of soda in aisle 7. It’s all over the floor.” The shopper had passed through aisle 7 roughly twenty minutes before she said it. The store objects.

What is the proper ruling?

A. Exclude the statement, because the shopper is unidentified and her personal knowledge cannot be shown.
B. Admit the statement for all purposes as an opposing-party statement, because the store’s manager heard it.
C. Exclude the statement entirely, because notice matters only if there was a spill, so the statement’s relevance depends on its truth.
D. Admit the statement for all purposes as a present sense impression.
E. Admit it to show the store had notice of a possible spill, with an instruction on request that it doesn’t prove a spill existed.

Answer: E
- A: When a statement is offered only for its effect on the listener, the declarant’s knowledge is irrelevant.
- B: The shopper was not the store’s agent, and nothing shows the manager adopted the statement.
- C: Notice is a separate element from the existence of the hazard. A warning is relevant to what the store knew regardless of its truth; Lorna must prove the spill with other evidence.
- D: A twenty-minute gap is too long for “while or immediately after perceiving.”
- E: Correct. The statement is admissible for notice, with a limiting instruction on request.
Rule: A statement offered to show its effect on the listener, such as notice, knowledge, or a reason for later action, is not offered for its truth and is not hearsay for that purpose. The court must, on timely request, restrict the evidence to its proper purpose and instruct the jury accordingly. FRE 105.

## Final 6 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Post-arrest, pre-Miranda silence
Police arrested Nico Ray at the scene of a stabbing and drove him to the station. No Miranda warnings were given during the ride, and Ray said nothing. At trial, Ray testifies for the first time that the victim pulled the knife on him. On cross, the prosecutor asks, "When the officers arrested you and drove you to the station, you didn't say a word about the victim attacking you, did you?" Ray objects under Doyle.

What is the most likely ruling?

A. Sustain it, because using any post-arrest silence violates due process.
B. Sustain it, because the Fifth Amendment bars any reference to silence.
C. Overrule it, because Ray hadn't yet received Miranda warnings.
D. Overrule it, because Ray's silence was an adoptive admission.
E. Sustain it, because silence may be used only in the case-in-chief.

Answer: C
- A: Doyle rests on the implicit promise in Miranda warnings. Without warnings, there is no such promise.
- B: Impeaching a testifying defendant with silence before warnings is permitted (Jenkins; the Fletcher case).
- C: Correct, though it feels like penalizing an arrested person's silence. The outline notes that pre-arrest and pre-Miranda silence may impeach (the Fletcher case). Doyle protects only silence after warnings, because the warnings implicitly promise that silence carries no penalty.
- D: The question is offered to impeach Ray's trial story, not as an admission adopting an accusation. No one accused him.
- E: Using post-arrest silence substantively in the case-in-chief raises more serious problems than impeachment.
Rule: Doyle bars impeaching a defendant with silence after Miranda warnings. Pre-arrest silence (Jenkins) and post-arrest silence before warnings (the Fletcher case) may be used to impeach a defendant who testifies.

## Final 6 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(2)(E): in furtherance
Which statement by a coconspirator is most likely admissible under Rule 801(d)(2)(E)?

A. A member says to the crew, "Bring the cash Friday so we can pay the supplier."
B. An arrested member tells police, "Lena runs the whole operation."
C. Years after the scheme ended, a member tells his barber all about how the crew operated.
D. A cooperating member testifies at a grand jury about Lena's role.
E. A member tells his girlfriend after the last sale, "We sure fooled them."

Answer: A
- A: Correct. It was made during the conspiracy and advances its goal, so it satisfies Rule 801(d)(2)(E).
- B: Confessions to police almost never qualify. It doesn't advance the conspiracy.
- C: The conspiracy has ended, so the statement isn't made during it.
- D: Testimony for the government doesn't further the conspiracy.
- E: A boast after the scheme ended is neither during nor in furtherance.
Rule: Rule 801(d)(2)(E) requires that the statement be made during and in furtherance of the conspiracy. Confessions to police and idle narrative usually fail.

## Final 6 · Q17 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(3): self-inculpatory parts only
Arrested with stolen copper wire, Lasker tells the arresting agent, "Yes, I took the copper, but Nolan planned it and rented the truck." At Nolan's trial, Lasker invokes the Fifth Amendment. The government offers the whole statement against Nolan. Considering only the hearsay rules, what is the most likely result?

A. The whole statement comes in, because it is against Lasker's penal interest as a whole.
B. None of it comes in, because a statement naming another person loses the exception entirely.
C. None of it comes in, because a person who invokes the Fifth Amendment isn't unavailable.
D. The whole statement comes in as a coconspirator's statement made in furtherance of the conspiracy to steal.
E. Only the remarks that are themselves self-inculpatory qualify, so the blame on Nolan stays out.

Answer: E
- A: Williamson rejects this approach. The exception is applied remark by remark, not to a confession as a whole.
- B: The rule doesn't drop the whole statement. The self-inculpatory remarks can still qualify.
- C: A privilege ruling is unavailability under Rule 804(a)(1).
- D: A confession to an agent after arrest doesn't further the conspiracy, so Rule 801(d)(2)(E) doesn't apply.
- E: Correct. Under Williamson, Rule 804(b)(3) covers only those remarks that are themselves self-inculpatory. A remark shifting blame to Nolan isn't against Lasker's interest, and the corroboration requirement also applies.
Rule: Rule 804(b)(3) is applied statement by statement: only remarks that are themselves against the declarant's interest qualify, and accomplices' blame-shifting gets special suspicion (Williamson). In a criminal case, corroborating circumstances are also required, and a custodial statement to an agent separately raises Crawford.

## Final 6 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(3): memory about the declarant's will
Ruth Gale's 2019 will left everything to her nephew. A 2024 will leaving everything to a charity was found torn in half in her desk after her death. The nephew claims the charity's will was revoked. He offers a friend's testimony that a month before her death, Ruth said, "Last week I tore up that charity will. I changed my mind." The charity objects that this is a statement of memory offered to prove the fact remembered.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because Rule 803(3) excludes memory offered to prove the fact remembered.
B. Admit it, because the memory bar doesn't apply to statements about one's own will.
C. Admit it as a dying declaration, because she died a month later.
D. Exclude it, because only the will itself can show revocation under Rule 1002.
E. Admit it only to show Ruth's state of mind, not that she tore the will.

Answer: B
- A: This is the general rule and the charity's argument. But the exception has its own exception for statements relating to the declarant's will.
- B: Correct, though it seems to violate the memory rule. Rule 803(3) excludes memory or belief offered to prove the fact remembered "unless it relates to the validity or terms of the declarant's will." Ruth's statement that she revoked her will is within that carve-out and may be used to prove revocation.
- C: Rule 804(b)(2) requires a belief that death was imminent.
- D: The torn will is in evidence. The statement doesn't prove its content but proves Ruth's act of revocation.
- E: The will carve-out allows use for the truth: that she revoked it.
Rule: Rule 803(3) excludes statements of memory or belief offered to prove the fact remembered, except those relating to the validity or terms of the declarant's own will.

## Final 6 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 805: business record carrying a party's own statement
Seth Varga sues Dana Ruiz for injuries suffered when Varga's motorcycle collided with Ruiz's car. Ruiz claims Varga was drunk. Ruiz offers a hospital emergency-room record, authenticated by the records custodian, that reads: "Pt states he had 'six or seven beers' before riding." Varga objects that the record is double hearsay.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because Varga had no business duty to the hospital, so his words can't be in its record.
B. Exclude it, because how much Varga drank isn't pertinent to his medical diagnosis or treatment.
C. Admit it, because the record and Varga's own statement each fit a hearsay route.
D. Admit it, because a hospital record comes in whole once the custodian lays the foundation.
E. Exclude it, because Rule 805 bars hearsay within hearsay in a business record.

Answer: C
- A: That is the Johnson v. Lutz problem. It means Varga's statement needs its own route, not that it is excluded. Here it has one.
- B: This might defeat Rule 803(4), but that exception isn't needed. Varga's statement is offered against him.
- C: Correct. Rule 805 requires an exception for each layer. The nurse's entry is a record of regularly conducted activity under Rule 803(6). Varga's own words, offered by his opponent, are not hearsay under Rule 801(d)(2)(A).
- D: Business-record status covers only the entrant's layer. Each statement within it needs its own exception (Rule 805).
- E: Rule 805 admits hearsay within hearsay if each part conforms with an exception.
Rule: Under Rule 805, each layer of a multi-level statement needs its own route. A business record covers the recorder. An outsider's statement inside it needs a separate basis, such as Rule 801(d)(2) when offered against that person.

## Final 6 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(16): ancient documents after 2017
In a 2026 boundary dispute, Elm Farms offers a 1987 county newspaper article reporting that a stone wall marked "the long-accepted line between the Elm and Birch properties." The article came from the paper's bound archive at the county library. Birch Orchards objects that the article is hearsay.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because newspaper articles are hearsay and no exception applies to them.
B. Admit it only if the reporter who wrote it is shown to be unavailable.
C. Admit it only under the residual exception, after reasonable written notice.
D. Exclude it, because the 2017 amendment abolished the ancient-documents exception.
E. Admit it under Rule 803(16), since it is authentic and was written before 1998.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 803(16) still covers statements in documents prepared before January 1, 1998, whose authenticity is established.
- B: Rule 803(16) is an 803 exception, so availability doesn't matter.
- C: Rule 807 applies only to statements not admissible under Rules 803 or 804. This one is admissible under Rule 803(16).
- D: The 2017 amendment didn't abolish the exception. It limited it to documents prepared before 1998.
- E: Correct. Since 2017, Rule 803(16) covers statements in a document prepared before January 1, 1998, whose authenticity is established. The 1987 article qualifies. A newspaper is self-authenticating under Rule 902(6), and Rule 901(b)(8)'s 20-year test also fits.
Rule: Rule 803(16) now admits statements in authenticated documents prepared before January 1, 1998. Authentication (Rule 901(b)(8): 20 years old, unsuspicious, found where it would likely be, or Rule 902) is a separate hurdle from hearsay.

## Final 6 · Q21 · Hearsay Exceptions — Spontaneous statements: the declarant's personal knowledge
In a civil suit arising from a hit-and-run that injured a cyclist, the plaintiff calls a police officer. He testifies that within a minute of the crash an unidentified woman ran up to him, breathless and pointing down the street. She shouted, "That blue van ran the light, hit the cyclist, and took off that way!" The woman left before giving her name. The defendant, who owns a blue van, objects.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because an unidentified declarant's statement can never be an excited utterance.
B. Admit it even with no sign she saw the crash, since Rule 602 covers only witnesses.
C. Exclude it unless the plaintiff first shows the woman is unavailable to testify.
D. Admit it only if the woman's identity is established before the end of trial.
E. Admit it as an excited utterance, if the circumstances show she likely saw the crash.

Answer: E
- A: Anonymity makes it harder to show that she perceived the event, but it doesn't bar the statement.
- B: The ACN applies personal knowledge to hearsay declarants. The exception is justified only if she perceived what she described.
- C: Rule 803 exceptions apply regardless of availability.
- D: Identity isn't required. The question is whether she perceived the event and spoke under its stress.
- E: Correct. Under Rule 803(2), a startling event and a statement made under its stress qualify. The declarant must have perceived the event. Timing, her breathless pointing, and the detail of her statement can show that she did. An unidentified bystander's statement may qualify (it could also be a Rule 803(1) present sense impression).
Rule: Spontaneous-statement exceptions (Rules 803(1) and (2)) require that the declarant perceived the event. That may be inferred from the circumstances and the statement itself, so an unidentified declarant's statement can qualify.

## Final 6 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Crawford: an excited utterance can still be testimonial
Two hours after a mugging, the victim, Joy Park, came to the police station. She was still shaking and crying. In a recorded interview, a detective asked her to describe the attack step by step, and she identified the mugger as her neighbor, Sam Ortiz. Park died in an unrelated accident before trial, and Ortiz never had a chance to cross-examine her. At Ortiz's trial, the prosecution offers the recording as an excited utterance.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit it, because a statement within a firmly rooted hearsay exception satisfies confrontation.
B. Exclude it, because it is testimonial and Ortiz never had a chance to cross Park.
C. Admit it, because Park's obvious stress makes the statement especially reliable.
D. Exclude it, because Park's death in an unrelated accident means she isn't unavailable.
E. Admit it, because the Confrontation Clause doesn't apply once the declarant is dead.

Answer: B
- A: That was the Ohio v. Roberts test, which Crawford overruled for testimonial statements.
- B: Correct. Hearsay and confrontation are separate hurdles. A structured stationhouse interview about a completed crime, with no ongoing emergency, is testimonial (Crawford; Hammon). Testimonial statements require unavailability and a prior opportunity to cross. Death supplies the first but not the second.
- C: Under Crawford, "Dispensing with confrontation because testimony is obviously reliable is akin to dispensing with jury trial because a defendant is obviously guilty."
- D: Death makes a declarant unavailable whatever the cause, but unavailability alone isn't enough.
- E: Death supplies unavailability, but Crawford also requires a prior opportunity to cross. Forfeiture would apply only if Ortiz caused her absence intending to prevent testimony (Giles).
Rule: Evidence against a criminal defendant must satisfy both the hearsay rules and the Confrontation Clause. A testimonial statement of an absent declarant requires unavailability plus a prior opportunity to cross (Crawford), however reliable or excited the statement.

## Final 6 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Bullcoming: a surrogate supervisor
In a federal DUI-manslaughter trial, the prosecution offers a signed laboratory report stating that the defendant's blood-alcohol content was 0.17. The analyst who ran the test is on extended leave. Instead, the lab supervisor testifies. She didn't perform or observe the test, but she reviewed the paperwork and certifies that the lab's procedures were followed. The defense objects under the Confrontation Clause.

How should the court rule?

A. Overrule it, because the supervisor is available for cross-examination about lab procedures.
B. Overrule it, because lab reports are business records and so are nontestimonial.
C. Sustain it, because a surrogate can't stand in for the certifying analyst.
D. Overrule it, because the report is offered only as the basis for the supervisor's opinion.
E. Sustain it, but only if the analyst's report was sworn before a notary.

Answer: C
- A: In Bullcoming, the Court held that cross-examining a surrogate who didn't perform or observe the test doesn't satisfy the Clause.
- B: A forensic report made to prove a fact at trial is testimonial (Melendez-Diaz). Calling it a business record doesn't change that.
- C: Correct. Under Melendez-Diaz and Bullcoming, a forensic report certifying a test result is testimonial. The analyst who certified it must be available for cross unless he is unavailable and the defendant had a prior opportunity to cross. A supervisor who neither ran nor watched the test is a surrogate.
- D: Smith v. Arizona rejected the "not for its truth" basis theory. A report that supports an opinion only if true is offered for its truth.
- E: Bullcoming's report wasn't sworn. Formality is one factor, not a requirement.
Rule: Forensic certifications made for trial are testimonial (Melendez-Diaz). A surrogate witness who didn't perform or observe the test can't satisfy the Clause (Bullcoming), and basis testimony conveys the analyst's statements for their truth (Smith v. Arizona).

## Final 6 · Q24 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Chambers today: Rule 607 and amended Rule 801(d)(1)(A)
Cal Brooks is prosecuted in federal court, in a case filed in 2027, for shooting a park ranger. The defense calls Lyle Garner, who on three occasions told different friends, spontaneously and in detail, that he shot the ranger himself. On the stand Garner denies the shooting and denies ever saying he did it. Brooks then offers the three friends to repeat Garner's confessions. The government objects that Brooks can't impeach his own witness and that the confessions are hearsay that fits no exception, because Garner is available.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit it as statements against interest, because Chambers overrides the unavailability requirement.
B. Exclude the testimony, because Rule 804(b)(3) requires that Garner be unavailable.
C. Exclude the testimony, because a party may not impeach a witness it chose to call.
D. Admit the testimony only to impeach Garner, because his confessions weren't made under penalty of perjury at a proceeding.
E. Admit it as substantive evidence, because Garner testified and is subject to cross about the inconsistent confessions.

Answer: E
- A: Chambers didn't rewrite the hearsay rules; it held that applying them mechanistically to exclude critical, trustworthy defense evidence can violate due process. Here the Federal Rules already admit the confessions.
- B: Rule 804(b)(3) does require unavailability, and Garner is available. But the confessions need no exception, because they are excluded from hearsay as a testifying witness's inconsistent statements.
- C: That is the common-law voucher rule Chambers called “archaic.” Rule 607 provides that any party, including the party that called the witness, may attack the witness's credibility.
- D: That was the law before December 1, 2026, when Rule 801(d)(1)(A) required a statement given under penalty of perjury at a proceeding. The amendment governs a case filed in 2027, so the confessions are substantive.
- E: Correct. Rule 607 lets any party, including the one that called him, attack a witness's credibility, so the voucher rule Chambers condemned is gone. And under Rule 801(d)(1)(A) as amended December 1, 2026, a testifying declarant's prior inconsistent statement is not hearsay, sworn or not, if he is subject to cross-examination about it. Rule 613(b)'s foundation is met because Garner is on the stand and has denied the statements.
Rule: Rule 607 abolishes the voucher rule: any party may impeach any witness. Under Rule 801(d)(1)(A) as amended December 1, 2026, a testifying witness's prior inconsistent statement is substantive evidence whether or not it was sworn, if he is subject to cross-examination about it. Chambers remains a due process backstop when evidence rules are applied mechanistically.

## Final 6 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 704(a) vs. Hygh legal conclusions
In a federal civil-rights suit alleging excessive force, the plaintiff's police-practices expert would testify: (1) "Officer Vance's use of a chokehold on a handcuffed, compliant man departed from nationally accepted police training standards"; and (2) "The force he used was unreasonable under the Fourth Amendment and constituted excessive force." The officer moves to exclude both.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit both, because Rule 704(a) permits opinions that embrace an ultimate issue.
B. Exclude both, because expert testimony on police practices isn't helpful to jurors.
C. Admit (2) only, because it addresses the exact question the jury must decide.
D. Admit (1), but exclude (2) because it states a legal conclusion about the force.
E. Exclude both, because police practices aren't a scientific field under Daubert.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 704(a) removes the ultimate-issue objection but doesn't admit opinions that instruct the jury on law or tell it what result to reach.
- B: How officers are trained is specialized knowledge beyond common experience.
- C: Statement (2) is the problem: it applies a legal standard and announces the verdict.
- D: Correct. Testimony about professional standards helps the jury evaluate the conduct. Calling the force "unreasonable under the Fourth Amendment" and "excessive" states a legal conclusion that tells the jury how to decide. Hygh v. Jacobs held such testimony must be excluded.
- E: Kumho Tire applies Rule 702 to "technical, or other specialized knowledge," not just science.
Rule: Under Rule 704(a), an opinion embracing an ultimate issue is allowed, but experts may not state legal conclusions or tell the jury what result to reach (Hygh). In criminal cases, Rule 704(b) also bars opinions on the defendant's mental state.

## Final 6 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — General Electric v. Joiner: the analytical gap
A painter who used Solvex paint thinner for twenty years develops a rare liver cancer and sues the maker. His expert relies on two studies: one in which infant mice injected with massive doses of Solvex's main chemical developed a different kind of tumor, and one finding a slightly higher cancer rate among refinery workers exposed to many chemicals, whose authors said it showed no link to that chemical. The district court excludes the expert's opinion that Solvex caused the cancer and grants summary judgment. The painter appeals.

What is the appellate court most likely to hold?

A. Affirmance, because animal studies are inadmissible as a matter of law to prove causation in humans.
B. Reversal, because a court reviews only an expert's methodology, not his conclusions, and both studies used accepted methods.
C. Reversal, because an exclusion that leads to summary judgment gets de novo review.
D. Reversal, because conflicts between the studies and the opinion go to weight and are for the jury.
E. Affirmance, because the court could find too great an analytical gap between the data and the opinion.

Answer: E
- A: Animal studies aren't barred as a category. The question is whether these studies, given the dose, the species, and the tumor type, support this opinion.
- B: General Electric Co. v. Joiner rejected a clean split: conclusions and methodology are not entirely distinct, and a court needn't admit an opinion connected to the data only by the expert's say-so.
- C: Joiner held that abuse-of-discretion review applies to rulings admitting or excluding expert testimony, even when the ruling decides the case.
- D: Under Rule 702 as amended in 2023, whether the opinion stays within what the basis and method can support is for the judge to decide by a preponderance, not a question of weight for the jury.
- E: Correct. On facts much like these (infant mice, massive doses, a different tumor, and epidemiology whose authors disclaimed a link), General Electric Co. v. Joiner upheld exclusion because of “too great an analytical gap between the data and the opinion proffered,” and held that such rulings are reviewed for abuse of discretion.
Rule: Under General Electric Co. v. Joiner, a court may exclude an opinion when there is too great an analytical gap between the data and the conclusion, and appellate review of expert rulings is for abuse of discretion. Rule 702(d) now requires each opinion to reflect a reliable application of the method to the facts.

## Final 6 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 901(a): an anonymous note can't vouch for itself
Minutes after a bank robbery, police find a note on the seat of the abandoned getaway car: “Light green Buick ZPJ-254. They changed cars. This is the other car.” The plate belongs to the Buick owned by the defendant's fiancée, which he was seen driving that afternoon. No one knows who wrote the note; handwriting, fingerprint, and ink analyses all failed. The prosecution claims a bystander who saw the switch wrote it. Setting hearsay aside, the defense objects that the note hasn't been authenticated.

What is the defense's strongest argument?

A. A note found at a crime scene must be authenticated by a complete chain of custody from the moment it was written.
B. Authentication requires proof by clear and convincing evidence when an exhibit is anonymous.
C. The only thing tying the note to an eyewitness is its own content, which assumes what it is offered to prove.
D. Only the author can authenticate a handwritten note, and the author is unknown.
E. A handwritten exhibit is likely to impress the jury more than live testimony, so Rule 403 requires its exclusion.

Answer: C
- A: Chain of custody matters for fungible items and for showing an item's condition, not for proving who wrote a document. The note's custody after police found it isn't disputed.
- B: The standard is the same for every exhibit: evidence sufficient to support a finding by a preponderance. There is no heightened standard for anonymous documents.
- C: Correct. Rule 901(a) asks whether there is evidence sufficient to support a finding that the note is what the prosecution claims: a note written by someone who saw the switch. Nothing but the note's own content suggests that, and using its accuracy to prove its author's knowledge is circular. The Third Circuit found a note like this inadequately authenticated.
- D: Authorship can be proved circumstantially under Rule 901(b)(4). The difficulty isn't the method but the absence of any evidence tying the note to an eyewitness.
- E: Rule 403 is available for any evidence, but a jury's possible overvaluing of a writing isn't the problem here. The missing link is any evidence that an eyewitness wrote the note.
Rule: Rule 901(a): the proponent must offer evidence sufficient to support a finding that the item is what it claims. When the claim is that a document was written by someone with knowledge, the document's own assertions can't bootstrap that finding.

## Final 6 · Q28 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 1004(d): a collateral writing
A witness in a car-accident case testifies that she remembers the accident happened on March 3 because she had just picked up that day's newspaper, which had a front-page story about a local election. The defense objects under the best evidence rule that the plaintiff must produce the newspaper to prove what it said.

What is the best ruling?

A. Sustain it, because the witness is testifying to a writing's content.
B. Sustain it unless the plaintiff shows the paper was lost in good faith.
C. Overrule it, because a newspaper is self-authenticating under Rule 902.
D. Overrule it, because a daily newspaper is a kind of public record.
E. Overrule it, because the newspaper's content is only collateral here.

Answer: E
- A: Technically she does describe a writing's content, which is what makes the objection tempting. But Rule 1004(d) excuses the original when its content is collateral.
- B: Loss is one excuse (Rule 1004(a)). Rule 1004(d) is a separate one that needs no showing about the original.
- C: Authentication is separate from the best evidence rule.
- D: A newspaper isn't a public record, and Rule 1005 would require a certified copy anyway.
- E: Correct. The election story only helps the witness fix the date. Under Rule 1004(d), other evidence of content is admissible when the writing "is not closely related to a controlling issue."
Rule: Rule 1004 excuses the original when (a) it was lost or destroyed without bad faith, (b) it can't be obtained by process, (c) the opponent has it and was on notice, or (d) it is not closely related to a controlling issue.

## Final 6 · Q29 · Privileges — Rule 502(a): subject-matter waiver
In a federal age-discrimination suit, Norvane Corp. defends by claiming it relied in good faith on its lawyer's advice that the layoffs were lawful. To support that defense, Norvane intentionally produces one memo from outside counsel approving the layoff plan. The plaintiff then demands all of Norvane's other communications with counsel about the layoffs, including emails in which counsel raised concerns. Norvane says it waived privilege only for the one memo.

Which ruling is correct?

A. Waiver extends to other communications on the same subject that fairness requires.
B. Only the memo is disclosed, because Rule 502 limits waiver to the document produced.
C. All of Norvane's privileged communications on any subject at all are waived.
D. Nothing is waived, because the production was a reasonable litigation choice.
E. The waiver extends beyond the memo only if the disclosure was inadvertent.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under Rule 502(a), an intentional disclosure in a federal proceeding extends to undisclosed communications if they concern the same subject matter and in fairness ought to be considered together. A party can't use favorable advice as a sword while shielding contrary advice on the same layoffs.
- B: Rule 502(a) limits subject-matter waiver to fairness situations, and this is the classic one.
- C: Waiver doesn't reach unrelated subjects. Rule 502(a) is limited to the same subject matter.
- D: An intentional disclosure is a waiver. The question is only its scope.
- E: It is the opposite: an inadvertent disclosure never triggers subject-matter waiver under Rule 502(a), and may be no waiver at all under Rule 502(b).
Rule: Rule 502(a): an intentional waiver extends to undisclosed material only if it concerns the same subject and in fairness ought to be considered together. Inadvertent disclosures are governed by Rule 502(b).

## Final 6 · Q30 · Privileges — Marital confidences: relayed through a friend
Phil Carver is charged with murder. His wife, Dee, is willing to testify, but Phil invokes the marital confidences privilege to bar her from repeating what he told her privately the night of the killing. The prosecution then calls Dee's friend, who will testify that the next morning Dee burst into tears and blurted, “He did it! Phil told me he killed that woman!” Phil never consented to Dee's disclosure.

How should the court rule on the friend's testimony?

A. Admit it, because Dee waived the privilege by voluntarily telling her friend.
B. Admit it, because Dee's outburst is an excited utterance, and a hearsay exception overrides the privilege.
C. Exclude it, because a spouse's statements about the other spouse are always inadmissible hearsay.
D. Exclude it, because it would disclose Phil's confidential communication, and he never waived the privilege.
E. Admit it, because the marital confidences privilege bars only testimony by a spouse, not by others.

Answer: D
- A: Under the federal view, either spouse may prevent the other from disclosing the confidential communication. A disclosure the communicating spouse never consented to doesn't waive his privilege; the proposed waiver rule, which Congress never enacted, likewise required the holder's own voluntary disclosure.
- B: Even if the outburst qualifies under Rule 803(2), a hearsay exception only removes the hearsay objection. It doesn't overcome a privilege.
- C: There is no such categorical rule. The friend's account might fit a hearsay exception such as Rule 803(2); the obstacle is the privilege.
- D: Correct. Phil holds the privilege and can bar disclosure of his confidential communication. Dee's unauthorized disclosure to a friend didn't waive it, and letting the friend repeat it would let the prosecution do indirectly what it can't do through Dee. The privilege, not the hearsay rule, decides the question.
- E: The privilege protects the communication, not just a spouse's testimony. Letting a third party relay it would gut the protection.
Rule: The marital confidences privilege protects the communication itself. A disclosure by the other spouse without the communicating spouse's consent doesn't waive it, and a hearsay exception doesn't overcome a privilege.

## Final 7 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Peña-Rodriguez: civil cases
After a federal jury returns a defense verdict in Luis Ortega's breach-of-contract suit, a juror signs an affidavit. It says the foreperson told the other jurors during deliberations, "You can't trust anything a Mexican says on the stand, and Ortega's whole case is his own word." Ortega moves for a new trial, relying on the affidavit.

May the court consider the affidavit?

A. Yes, because a juror's racial bias is extraneous prejudicial information under Rule 606(b)(2)(A).
B. Yes, because racial animus that motivated the verdict always overrides Rule 606(b).
C. No, because the constitutional exception for racial bias protects only criminal defendants.
D. Yes, because a juror's statement of bias is an outside influence improperly brought to bear.
E. No, because jurors may testify about racial bias only if the biased juror lied in voir dire.

Answer: C
- A: A juror's own beliefs and biases are internal matters, not extraneous information (Warger; Tanner).
- B: Peña-Rodriguez rests on the Sixth Amendment right to an impartial jury in criminal cases. The outline lists three requirements: clear racial bias, a criminal case, and reliance on it to convict.
- C: Correct. Rule 606(b)(1) bars juror testimony about statements made during deliberations. Peña-Rodriguez's exception rests on the Sixth Amendment and requires a criminal defendant convicted with clear racial animus as a significant motivating factor. A civil litigant can't invoke it.
- D: An outside influence is pressure from outside the jury, such as a bribe or a threat. A juror's own prejudice isn't one.
- E: Even a voir-dire challenge must be proved without testimony about deliberations (Warger). In any event, the exception doesn't depend on voir dire.
Rule: Rule 606(b) bars juror testimony about deliberations except for extraneous information, outside influence, or a form error. The Peña-Rodriguez exception for clear racial animus is constitutional and applies only to criminal convictions.

## Final 7 · Q2 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 403: “substantially outweighed”
In a civil fraud trial, the plaintiff offers a recorded phone call in which the defendant brags about “taking that sucker for everything he had.” The judge excludes it, explaining: “This recording is moderately probative, but the risk of unfair prejudice is somewhat greater, so it stays out.” The plaintiff loses and appeals. What is the plaintiff’s strongest argument?

A. Rule 403 doesn’t apply to a party’s own recorded words, which are always admissible against that party.
B. The judge applied the wrong standard: Rule 403 requires that unfair prejudice substantially outweigh probative value.
C. Rule 403 allows exclusion only for unfair prejudice, not for confusion, delay, or the other dangers it lists.
D. A party’s own admission can never be unfairly prejudicial.
E. The judge should have given a limiting instruction instead, because Rule 105 always displaces Rule 403.

Answer: B
- A: 403 applies to all relevant evidence, including a party’s own statements.
- B: Correct. 403 tilts toward admission: “The court may exclude relevant evidence if its probative value is substantially outweighed by a danger of” unfair prejudice or another listed danger. Finding prejudice “somewhat greater” than probative value doesn’t meet that standard. Rulings are reviewed for abuse of discretion, but applying the wrong legal test is an abuse of discretion.
- C: 403 lists unfair prejudice, confusing the issues, misleading the jury, undue delay, wasting time, and needlessly cumulative evidence. The error here is the weighing standard, not the kind of danger.
- D: Admissions can carry unfair prejudice, through crude or inflammatory language, for example. The point is that the judge had to find that danger substantially outweighed probative value, and he didn’t.
- E: A limiting instruction is one alternative a court weighs under Rule 403, but Rule 105 doesn’t displace Rule 403. The error is the standard: the judge excluded on a “somewhat greater” balance instead of requiring that unfair prejudice substantially outweigh probative value.
Rule: Rule 403 allows exclusion only when probative value is substantially outweighed by unfair prejudice or another listed danger. The test favors admission.

## Final 7 · Q3 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 105: no request, no instruction
At a joint trial of two defendants for fraud, the prosecution introduced Defendant A's own written admission. It was admissible against A but not against Defendant B, though it didn't name B and raised no Bruton issue. B's lawyer neither objected nor asked for a limiting instruction. B is convicted and argues on appeal that the court erred by not instructing the jury, on its own, to consider the admission only against A.

What is the most likely result?

A. Reversal, because the court must always instruct on the limited use of evidence.
B. Reversal, because evidence inadmissible against one defendant requires severance.
C. Reversal, because the admission violated B's confrontation rights.
D. Affirmance, because evidence admissible against one defendant is admissible against all.
E. Affirmance, because a limiting instruction is required only on timely request.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 105 says the court "on timely request, must restrict the evidence to its proper scope and instruct the jury accordingly." Absent a request, it isn't required.
- B: Severance is one tool for Bruton problems. Ordinary limited admissibility is handled by Rule 105.
- C: A's admission doesn't name or incriminate B on its face, so Bruton isn't triggered.
- D: The admission was admissible against A only, so B was entitled to an instruction if requested.
- E: Correct, though B suffered from the missing instruction. Rule 105 puts the burden on the party to make a timely request. At most, B gets plain-error review, which rarely succeeds for an omitted limiting instruction.
Rule: Rule 105: when evidence is admissible against one party or for one purpose, the court must restrict it and instruct the jury on timely request. Failing to request it forfeits the ordinary claim of error.

## Final 7 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 407: a change made before the injury
In January 2026, after internal testing, Halo Heaters redesigned the tip-over switch on its space heaters. Ken Ward had bought an older unit in 2024. In March 2026 it tipped over without shutting off and burned him. Ward sues Halo for a design defect and offers evidence of the January redesign. Halo objects under Rule 407.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because the redesign came after Ward's heater was manufactured and sold.
B. Rule 407 doesn't bar it, because the redesign came before Ward's injury, though Rule 403 still applies.
C. Exclude it, because Rule 407 applies to strict-liability design-defect claims.
D. Admit it only to prove feasibility, and only if Halo disputes feasibility.
E. Exclude it, because Rule 407 bars any design change offered to prove a defect.

Answer: B
- A: Rule 407 measures timing from the injury or harm sued on, not from manufacture or sale.
- B: Correct. Rule 407 covers measures taken after the injury that would have made it less likely. A change made before Ward's injury falls outside the rule, as in the Wolf Attack timing example. Its admissibility turns on Rules 401–403.
- C: That's true, but irrelevant. The rule doesn't reach measures taken before the injury.
- D: The "if disputed" purposes are what the rule permits for a measure it otherwise bars. A pre-injury measure needs no such purpose.
- E: Only measures taken after the injury are barred.
Rule: Rule 407 bars only measures taken after the injury or harm in suit. Pre-injury changes fall outside it (subject to Rule 403). For measures within the rule, the "if disputed" purposes (ownership, control, feasibility) and impeachment remain.

## Final 7 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 408: criminal case and public office
During a civil enforcement negotiation with a federal agency over disputed fines, Yates says, "I knew the reports were false." Yates is later prosecuted for making false reports. The government offers the statement. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Excluded, because Rule 410 covers all discussions with a government agency.
B. Excluded, because Rule 408 bars statements in negotiations in every kind of case.
C. Admitted, because Rule 408 has an exception for negotiations with a public office.
D. Admitted, because Rule 408 applies only when a claim is settled.
E. Excluded, because a claim by the government is never a disputed claim.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 410 covers plea discussions with a prosecutor, not civil negotiations with an agency.
- B: Rule 408(a)(2) has an exception for criminal cases when negotiations related to a claim by a public office.
- C: Correct. Rule 408(a)(2) statements are admissible in a criminal case when the negotiations related to a claim by a public office exercising regulatory, investigative, or enforcement authority.
- D: Rule 408 protects negotiations whether or not they succeed.
- E: A fine disputed in amount or validity is a disputed claim.
Rule: Statements in compromise negotiations are barred by Rule 408, except in a criminal case when the negotiations related to a claim by a public office exercising regulatory, investigative, or enforcement authority.

## Final 7 · Q6 · Character Evidence — Doctrine of chances; acquittal; Huddleston
Ruth is charged with burning her warehouse to collect insurance. She says old wiring started the fire by accident. The government offers evidence that two other buildings she owned burned in the past six years and that she collected insurance on both. Ruth was tried and acquitted for the second fire. No one was charged for the first. How should the court analyze this evidence?

A. It is barred as to the second fire, because an acquittal establishes that the act did not occur.
B. It may come in only if the judge herself finds by a preponderance that Ruth set both earlier fires.
C. It is barred, because Rule 404(b) requires a conviction or at least a charge for each other act.
D. It may show lack of accident if a jury could reasonably find the acts occurred, subject to Rule 403.
E. It may come in to show Ruth is the kind of person who burns buildings for money, subject to Rule 403.

Answer: D
- A: An acquittal shows only that guilt wasn't proved beyond a reasonable doubt. Under Huddleston, a jury need only be able to find the act occurred, so acquittal doesn't bar it.
- B: This is a Rule 104(a) standard. Huddleston treats other acts as a Rule 104(b) question: could a jury reasonably find the act occurred?
- C: Rule 404(b) speaks of any other crime, wrong, or act. No conviction or charge is required.
- D: Correct. Lack of accident is a listed purpose under Rule 404(b)(2). Repeated accidents are improbable (Rex v. Smith), and Huddleston sets the Rule 104(b) standard, followed by Rule 403 and a limiting instruction on request.
- E: That is the propensity use barred by Rule 404(b)(1). Rule 403 doesn't make a barred purpose acceptable.
Rule: Rule 404(b)(2) allows other acts for a non-character purpose. Under Huddleston: a proper purpose, Rule 104(b) sufficiency, Rule 403, and a Rule 105 instruction on request. An acquittal or the lack of a charge doesn't bar the evidence.

## Final 7 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Rule 415: civil cases
In a civil suit alleging sexual assault, the plaintiff offers testimony from another woman that the defendant sexually assaulted her. The plaintiff disclosed it 20 days before trial. The defendant was never convicted. Which is correct?

A. It is barred, because the notice must be given at least 30 days before trial.
B. It is admissible under Rule 415 for any relevant matter, subject to Rule 403.
C. It is barred, because Rule 404(b) governs every other act in a civil case.
D. It is barred, because Rules 413 and 414 apply only in criminal cases.
E. It must be admitted, because Rule 415 removes the court's discretion.

Answer: B
- A: The period is 15 days, and 20 days meets it.
- B: Correct. Rule 415(a) applies the Rule 413 approach in civil cases, with disclosure at least 15 days before trial. No conviction is required, and Rule 403 still applies.
- C: Rule 415 is a true exception, so no Rule 404(b) purpose is required.
- D: Rule 415 extends the same treatment to civil cases based on sexual assault or child molestation.
- E: Rule 415 says the court may admit the evidence, and Rule 403 applies.
Rule: Rule 415 applies Rules 413–414 to civil cases based on sexual assault or child molestation, on 15 days' disclosure and subject to Rule 403.

## Final 7 · Q8 · Character Evidence — Rule 405(a): cross of a character witness
Perry, charged with embezzlement, calls Rosa, who testifies that Perry has an excellent reputation for honesty. With a good-faith basis, the prosecutor asks Rosa, "Have you heard that Perry was arrested for forging a check in 2022?" Rosa says no. The prosecutor then offers the arrest record. What is the correct ruling?

A. The question and the record are both improper, because an arrest isn't a conviction.
B. The question is proper, but the record is excluded.
C. The question is proper, and the record comes in to contradict Rosa.
D. The question is improper, but the record comes in as proof of Perry's character.
E. Both are proper, because Perry opened the door to all proof of his honesty.

Answer: B
- A: Michelson approved a question about an arrest. It tests what the witness has heard, not whether the act occurred.
- B: Correct. Rule 405(a) allows inquiry into specific instances on cross of a character witness, with a good-faith basis (Michelson). The examiner is stuck with the answer, so no extrinsic proof comes in.
- C: Specific acts may be asked about but not proved. The examiner takes the answer.
- D: This reverses the rule. Rule 405(a) allows the question and doesn't allow proof of the act.
- E: Opening the door lets the prosecutor rebut under Rule 404(a)(2)(A), but the form is still limited by Rule 405(a).
Rule: On cross of a character witness, Rule 405(a) allows "have you heard" questions about specific acts with a good-faith basis. The examiner is stuck with the answer (Michelson).

## Final 7 · Q9 · Character Evidence — A defendant as a character witness
On trial for assault, Ivers testifies on direct, "I would never hit anybody." On cross, the prosecutor asks about a bar fight two years ago in which Ivers punched a man. The defense objects. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Overruled, because Ivers made himself a character witness on peacefulness.
B. Overruled, because a testifying defendant can be asked about any prior bad act.
C. Sustained, because a defendant's character can't be raised unless a witness is called.
D. Sustained, because Rule 609 is the only way to impeach a testifying defendant.
E. Sustained, because Rule 405(a) bars specific acts on any cross-examination.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. A defendant who claims he would never do such a thing offers a pertinent trait under Rule 404(a)(2)(A) and may be crossed on specific instances under Rule 405(a).
- B: That is too broad. The inquiry is allowed because of what he said on direct.
- C: A defendant's own broad claim about himself offers the pertinent trait.
- D: Rule 609 covers convictions. This is rebuttal of character evidence he offered.
- E: Rule 405(a) allows inquiry into relevant specific instances on cross of a character witness.
Rule: A defendant who testifies to a sweeping claim of good character makes himself a character witness, opening cross on specific instances under Rule 405(a).

## Final 7 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Bias: extrinsic proof allowed
A defense witness in a drug trial denies on cross that the defendant has been paying his rent. The prosecutor then calls the witness's landlord, who testifies that the defendant paid that rent for 14 months. The defense objects. What is the correct ruling?

A. Sustained, because Rule 608(b) bars extrinsic evidence of a witness's specific conduct.
B. Overruled, because bias is not a collateral matter and may be proved extrinsically.
C. Sustained, because the prosecutor is stuck with the witness's answer on cross.
D. Sustained, because bias may be shown only through the biased witness himself.
E. Overruled, because the payments are substantive evidence of the defendant's guilt.

Answer: B
- A: That bar applies only when the sole purpose is to attack character for truthfulness. Proof of bias isn't offered for that purpose (United States v. Abel).
- B: Correct. Under United States v. Abel, bias isn't character evidence and is never collateral, so a witness's denial can be answered with extrinsic proof.
- C: Being stuck with the answer is the rule for Rule 608(b) acts and collateral matters, not bias.
- D: No rule confines bias to cross-examination. United States v. Abel allowed another witness to prove it.
- E: The evidence is admitted because it impeaches the witness. Its relevance here is to credibility.
Rule: Bias may be shown by extrinsic evidence because it isn't collateral. Rule 608(b) bars extrinsic proof only when its sole purpose is to prove character for truthfulness (United States v. Abel).

## Final 7 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609(b): the clock runs from release
A defendant is on trial in 2026 for aggravated assault. If he testifies, the government wants to impeach him with his 2013 felony conviction for aggravated assault. He was released from prison in 2019. Which statement about the conviction’s admissibility is correct?

A. Rule 609(a)(1)(B) applies, and the crimes’ similarity makes the conviction more probative of truthfulness.
B. Rule 609(a)(1)(B) applies, because the ten years run from his 2019 release, and the crimes’ similarity weighs against admission.
C. The conviction must be admitted without balancing, because a violent felony bears on whether the defendant will respect his oath.
D. Rule 609(b) applies because the conviction is more than ten years old, so the government must show probative value substantially outweighing prejudice.
E. The conviction must be admitted, subject only to Rule 403, because aggravated assault is punishable by more than a year.

Answer: B
- A: Similarity cuts the other way. It raises the risk that the jury will use the conviction as propensity evidence, so it weighs against admission under the Gordon factors.
- B: Correct. 609(b)’s ten-year clock runs from the conviction or release from confinement, whichever is later (Brewer), so 609(b) doesn’t apply. Under 609(a)(1)(B), probative value on truthfulness must outweigh prejudice to the defendant. Among the Gordon factors, violent crimes say little about honesty, and a conviction for the same crime invites “he did it before” reasoning, so both point toward exclusion.
- C: Only 609(a)(2) crimes, those requiring proof of a dishonest act or false statement, come in automatically. Assault isn’t one of them.
- D: That was Brewer’s losing argument. The later of conviction or release starts the clock, and release in 2019 puts the conviction within ten years.
- E: 403’s lenient standard under 609(a)(1)(A) is for witnesses other than the accused. A testifying defendant gets the stricter 609(a)(1)(B) balance.
Rule: Rule 609(b)’s ten years run from the later of conviction or release (Brewer). For an accused, 609(a)(1)(B) applies, weighed with the Gordon factors: nature of the crime, time and later history, similarity, importance of the defendant’s testimony, and centrality of credibility.

## Final 7 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Ohler: drawing the sting
(Varies question 6.12.)
Same facts, except Boyd testifies and, to soften the blow, tells the jury about the conviction himself on direct. The government never mentions it. On appeal, he challenges the in limine ruling. What is the most likely result?

A. The claim is preserved, because he testified as Luce requires.
B. The claim is preserved, because he only responded to the ruling.
C. The claim is preserved if he objected again during his direct.
D. The claim is waived, because he introduced the conviction himself.
E. Reversal, because the ruling forced him to reveal his conviction.

Answer: D
- A: Testifying is necessary but not sufficient. Ohler adds that he can't introduce the conviction himself.
- B: Ohler rejected this reasoning, even though the defendant was responding to an adverse ruling.
- C: A further objection doesn't change who introduced the evidence.
- D: Correct. Under Ohler, a defendant who preemptively introduces his own conviction on direct can't challenge its admission on appeal.
- E: The conviction came from his own testimony, so there is no error by the court to review.
Rule: With Luce and Ohler together, a defendant preserves a Rule 609 objection only by testifying and letting the prosecution elicit the conviction.

## Final 7 · Q13 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 613(b): 2024 timing of the foundation
A prosecution eyewitness testifies the getaway car was blue. Defense counsel finishes cross without mentioning that the witness told an officer the car was green. The defense then calls the officer to prove that statement. The prosecutor objects. Under the current rules, what is the most likely ruling?

A. Overruled, because Rule 613(b) lets the foundation be laid at any time, even after the officer testifies.
B. Sustained, because a prior inconsistent statement may never be proved by extrinsic evidence.
C. Overruled, because Rule 613(b) applies only to a witness who is also a party.
D. Sustained, because the statement to the officer was not made under penalty of perjury.
E. Sustained, because unless the court orders otherwise the witness must first get to explain or deny.

Answer: E
- A: That was the rule in United States v. Barrett. Since the 2024 amendment, the opportunity must come before extrinsic proof unless the court orders otherwise.
- B: Rule 613(b) allows extrinsic proof on a non-collateral point once the foundation is laid.
- C: This gets the rule backward. Rule 613(b) doesn't apply to an opposing party's statement under Rule 801(d)(2).
- D: An oath matters to substantive use under Rule 801(d)(1)(A). It isn't required to impeach.
- E: Correct. As amended in 2024, Rule 613(b) bars extrinsic evidence of the statement until after the witness has had a chance to explain or deny it, unless the court orders otherwise.
Rule: Since Dec. 1, 2024, Rule 613(b) requires the witness's chance to explain or deny before extrinsic proof of the inconsistent statement, unless the court orders otherwise. Fisher (2022) and United States v. Barrett state the older rule.

## Final 7 · Q14 · Definition of Hearsay — Not for its truth: the testator's belief
Agnes Polk's will left nothing to her son Tom. Tom contests the will, claiming Agnes suffered from an insane delusion when she made it. The will's proponent offers testimony from Agnes's neighbor that, a month before signing the will, Agnes said, "Tom is a thief. He stole my mother's ring." The proponent concedes that Tom stole nothing. The statement is offered to show that Agnes had a reason for disinheriting Tom, believed or not.

How should the court treat the statement?

A. As hearsay with no exception, because Agnes is unavailable and her statement accuses Tom of a crime.
B. As admissible to show Agnes's feelings toward Tom, but not to prove that Tom stole anything.
C. As admissible to prove Tom stole the ring, because Rule 803(3) admits statements of belief.
D. As a statement against Agnes's interest, because disinheriting her own son was contrary to it.
E. As inadmissible character evidence, because it portrays Tom as a thief to explain the will.

Answer: B
- A: Offered to show what Agnes felt and believed, not the truth of the theft, the statement isn't hearsay, or else it falls within Rule 803(3) as a statement of her then-existing state of mind.
- B: Correct. The proponent offers the words for Agnes's feelings toward Tom and her reason for the gift, not to prove a theft. That is either a non-truth use or a statement of then-existing state of mind under Rule 803(3). A Rule 105 limiting instruction keeps the jury from using it to prove Tom stole.
- C: Rule 803(3) excludes "a statement of memory or belief to prove the fact remembered or believed" (except about the declarant's will). To prove Tom stole, the statement is hearsay with no exception.
- D: The statement isn't against Agnes's pecuniary or penal interest, and Rule 804(b)(3) requires an objective reasonable-person test.
- E: The statement isn't offered to show Tom acted in character on some occasion, so Rule 404 isn't triggered.
Rule: The same words can be hearsay for one purpose and not for another. Offered for the declarant's feelings or reasons, they come in (not for truth, or under Rule 803(3)). Offered to prove the fact believed, they are barred. Rule 105 instructs the jury on the limited use.

## Final 7 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Adoption by silence
At a diner, before any arrest, Gale's business partner says to her in front of two friends, "You and I both know we skimmed that cash." Gale, who plainly heard him, stares at her coffee and says nothing. At her fraud trial, the government offers the exchange. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admitted, because a business partner's remarks are always authorized.
B. Excluded, because silence can never be a statement.
C. Admitted, if the circumstances naturally called for a denial she could have given.
D. Excluded, because using her silence violates her constitutional right to remain silent.
E. Admitted, because anything said in a party's presence is her own statement.

Answer: C
- A: A partner's authority under Rule 801(d)(2)(C) needs proof. Nothing shows authority to confess fraud.
- B: Rule 801(d)(2)(B) covers a statement a party adopted or believed to be true, and silence can show adoption.
- C: Correct. Adoption by silence under Rule 801(d)(2)(B) requires that she heard and understood, was free to respond, the circumstances naturally called for a denial, and she didn't deny.
- D: Doyle concerns silence after Miranda warnings. Silence with a private accuser before arrest is unaffected.
- E: Presence isn't enough. The elements of adoption must be met.
Rule: Silence can adopt a statement under Rule 801(d)(2)(B) if the party heard and understood it, was free to respond, the circumstances naturally called for a denial, and the party didn't deny it.

## Final 7 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 613(b) doesn't apply to party statements
In a negligence suit over a car crash, defendant Noah Kerr testifies that the light was green when he entered the intersection. On cross, the plaintiff doesn't ask him about any earlier statement. Later, in rebuttal, the plaintiff calls Kerr's neighbor, who would testify that the day after the crash Kerr told him, "The light had already turned yellow, but I figured I could make it." Kerr objects that he was never given a chance to explain or deny the statement.

How should the court rule?

A. Sustain it, because since 2024 the witness must get a chance to explain or deny first.
B. Overrule it, but admit the statement only to impeach Kerr, not as proof.
C. Overrule it, because the foundation rule doesn't apply to an opposing party's statement.
D. Sustain it, because the light's color is a collateral matter on rebuttal.
E. Overrule it, because the judge may always order otherwise under Rule 613(b).

Answer: C
- A: Rule 613(b) expressly says it "does not apply to an opposing party's statement as defined in Rule 801(d)(2)."
- B: As Kerr's own statement offered against him, it is admissible substantively under Rule 801(d)(2)(A).
- C: Correct. Rule 613(b)'s foundation requirement excludes opposing parties' statements. Kerr's statement is admissible under Rule 801(d)(2)(A) to prove that the light was yellow, as well as to impeach him.
- D: The color of the light is a central fact, not a collateral one.
- E: The court's discretion exists ("unless the court orders otherwise"), but it isn't needed here because Rule 613(b) doesn't apply at all.
Rule: Rule 613(b) requires an opportunity to explain or deny before extrinsic proof of a witness's inconsistent statement, ordinarily first since 2024. It doesn't apply to an opposing party's statement under Rule 801(d)(2), which is also substantive.

## Final 7 · Q17 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(3): exculpating the accused
Tess is charged with robbery. She offers a statement by Vince, who invokes the Fifth Amendment, telling a friend the day after the robbery, "I did that job alone." Vince was seen near the bank that day and had the same model of getaway car. What must the court decide?

A. Whether a jury could find the statement more likely than not true.
B. Only whether the statement is against Vince's interest.
C. Whether corroborating circumstances clearly indicate trustworthiness.
D. Whether Vince was convicted of the robbery.
E. Nothing, because Rule 804(b)(3) doesn't apply to defendants.

Answer: C
- A: The standard is corroboration under Rule 804(b)(3)(B), decided by the court.
- B: In a criminal case, Rule 804(b)(3)(B) also requires corroboration.
- C: Correct. Rule 804(b)(3)(B) requires corroborating circumstances that clearly indicate trustworthiness, considering the totality of circumstances and any evidence that supports or undermines the statement (2024).
- D: No conviction is required. Vince's interest and corroboration are the questions.
- E: The corroboration rule applies to penal-interest statements offered by either side.
Rule: In a criminal case, a statement against penal interest needs corroborating circumstances clearly indicating trustworthiness. Since 2024, the court considers the totality of circumstances and any evidence supporting or undermining the statement.

## Final 7 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(3): Hillmon and third parties
On Friday, Paul told his roommate, "Tonight I'm meeting Victor at the quarry to buy his truck." Paul was found dead at the quarry on Saturday. At Victor's murder trial, the prosecution offers the statement through the roommate. What is the best statement of its admissibility?

A. Excluded, because Rule 803(3) covers only statements about past states of mind.
B. Admitted to prove Paul went, but using it to prove Victor went is contested.
C. Admitted without limit to prove that both Paul and Victor went to the quarry.
D. Excluded, because a statement of intent can't be used to prove later conduct.
E. Admitted as a dying declaration, because Paul died shortly after saying it.

Answer: B
- A: This reverses the rule. Rule 803(3) covers then-existing intent and plan, and it excludes backward-looking memory.
- B: Correct. Under Mutual Life Insurance, a statement of one's own intent may show the declarant acted on it. Use against another person is contested; United States v. Pheaster allowed it, and some courts require independent evidence.
- C: The third-party use depends on Paul's memory of Victor's plan, which is why it is contested.
- D: Mutual Life Insurance holds the opposite. Intent makes the later act more probable.
- E: Rule 804(b)(2) requires a belief that death was imminent. Paul had no such belief.
Rule: Rule 803(3) admits a statement of the declarant's own intent to show the declarant acted on it (Mutual Life Insurance). Using it to prove another person's conduct is contested (United States v. Pheaster).

## Final 7 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(6)(C): a one-off memo isn't regular practice
After the first fire in its 30-year history, Tarn Manufacturing's CEO asked the plant manager to "write up what you find out." Over two weeks, the plant manager interviewed workers and wrote a memo concluding that a contractor's welding torch caused the fire. Tarn has never prepared such a memo before. In Tarn's suit against the contractor, Tarn offers the memo through its records custodian.

Is the memo admissible as a business record?

A. Yes, because the plant manager had a duty to report and wrote it promptly.
B. Yes, unless the contractor shows that the memo's source or circumstances lack trustworthiness.
C. No, because making such a memo wasn't a regular practice of Tarn's business.
D. Yes, as a record of a factual investigation, like a public agency's findings.
E. No, because the plant manager who wrote it must testify in person to lay the foundation.

Answer: C
- A: Timeliness and duty are part of the foundation, but Rule 803(6) also requires that making the record be a regular practice of the activity.
- B: The opponent's burden under Rule 803(6)(E) arises only after Rules 803(6)(A)–(D) are met. Regular practice is missing.
- C: Correct. Rule 803(6)(B)–(C) requires that the record be kept in the course of a regularly conducted activity and that making it be a regular practice. A one-time memo commissioned after a unique event fails. Its preparation as a dispute loomed also raises the Palmer concern now in Rule 803(6)(E).
- D: Tarn is a private company. Rule 803(8) covers public offices.
- E: A custodian or other qualified witness, or a Rule 902(11) certification, can lay the foundation. The flaw is the lack of regular practice.
Rule: Rule 803(6) requires a record made at or near the time by someone with knowledge, kept in the course of a regularly conducted activity, as a regular practice, shown by a qualified witness or certification. The opponent then may show lack of trustworthiness. One-off and litigation-driven reports fail (Palmer).

## Final 7 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(18): learned treatises
An expert testifies that she relied on a standard surgical treatise, and she establishes it as authoritative. The proponent asks to give the jury the book as an exhibit. What is the correct ruling?

A. The passages stay out, because reliance doesn't make hearsay admissible.
B. The relevant passages may be read in, but the book isn't received.
C. The passages may be used only for impeachment on cross.
D. The book comes in only if the author is unavailable to testify.
E. The book comes in as an exhibit, because it is reliable.

Answer: B
- A: Rule 803(18) is the exception that makes these statements substantive.
- B: Correct. Rule 803(18) admits statements in a learned treatise relied on by an expert on direct, or called to the expert's attention on cross, if authoritative. They may be read but not received as an exhibit.
- C: Rule 803(18) also allows substantive use on direct.
- D: Rule 803 applies regardless of availability.
- E: Under Rule 803(18), statements are read in but not received as an exhibit.
Rule: Rule 803(18) admits authoritative treatises relied on by or called to the attention of an expert. The passages are read in, not received as an exhibit.

## Final 7 · Q21 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 805: the two layers
Facts: Nadia Voss is charged in federal court with burning down a warehouse she had insured. Investigators found a homemade chemical timer of an unusual design at the scene. The government calls Joel Tran, who would testify that his late mother, a nurse at a burn clinic, told him over dinner six years ago: "A patient named Voss came in with burned hands today. She told me she got them testing a chemical timer she built for a warehouse job." The defense moves to exclude the testimony.
Which analysis of the hearsay layers is correct?

A. Rule 803(4) covers both layers, because the nurse treated Voss.
B. Voss's words to the nurse need an against-interest finding to come in.
C. Voss's words are her own, but the nurse's words still need an exception.
D. No analysis is needed, because the whole account Tran gives is really Voss's own statement.
E. The nurse's death supplies an exception for her statement to her son.

Answer: C
- A: The nurse told her son over dinner, not to get treatment, so Rule 803(4) can't cover that layer.
- B: Rule 801(d)(2)(A) has no against-interest requirement.
- C: Correct. Under Rule 805, each layer needs its own route. Offered against her, Voss's words fit Rule 801(d)(2)(A). The nurse's dinner remark needs its own exception, and none clearly fits.
- D: Tran heard the nurse, not Voss. The nurse's remark is a separate layer.
- E: Death establishes unavailability under Rule 804(a). It isn't an exception.
