Rule: Rule 805 requires a route for each layer. An opposing party's statement solves the inner layer, but the outer layer needs its own exception, and unavailability alone isn't one.

## Final 7 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Smith v. Arizona: truth and testimonial are separate questions
At a drug trial, the analyst who tested the seized powder has left the lab. A substitute expert reviews the absent analyst's bench notes and tells the jury that, based on the notes' description of the test results, the powder was methamphetamine. The prosecution argues that the notes aren't offered for their truth but only to explain the basis of the substitute's opinion. It also argues that informal bench notes aren't testimonial.

What is the most accurate statement of the law?

A. They come in for their truth; whether they're testimonial is a separate question.
B. The notes aren't offered for their truth, because they only support an expert opinion.
C. The Clause is violated automatically whenever a substitute expert testifies.
D. The Clause isn't implicated, because the substitute expert can be cross-examined.
E. The notes are admissible, because experts may rely on facts others reasonably rely on.

Answer: A
- A: Correct, though it leaves the outcome unresolved. Smith v. Arizona held that when an expert conveys an absent analyst's statements in support of an opinion, and they support it only if true, they come in for their truth. Whether informal notes are testimonial was left open, and the Court remanded that question.
- B: This is the Williams v. Illinois plurality's basis theory, which Smith v. Arizona rejected.
- C: Smith didn't hold that every substitute expert violates the Clause. The statements conveyed must also be testimonial.
- D: The witnesses against the defendant include the absent analyst whose statements are conveyed. Cross of the substitute doesn't satisfy the Clause (Bullcoming).
- E: Rule 703 governs what an expert may rely on under the evidence rules. It can't override the Confrontation Clause.
Rule: Smith v. Arizona: basis statements conveyed by an expert that support the opinion only if true are offered for their truth. Whether they are testimonial (primary purpose and formality) is a separate question the Court left open.

## Final 7 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Smith v. Arizona: expert conveying an absent analyst
Analyst Kim tested seized powder, signed a report calling it fentanyl, and left the lab. At trial, the government calls supervisor Ortiz, who neither ran nor observed the test. Ortiz reads Kim's notes and report to the jury and then gives his "own opinion" that the powder was fentanyl. What is the best analysis under the Confrontation Clause?

A. There is no issue, because basis evidence isn't offered for its truth.
B. There is no issue, because Ortiz is on the stand and can be cross-examined.
C. There is no issue, because lab reports are business records and never testimonial.
D. Kim's statements come in for their truth, so the Clause applies if they are testimonial.
E. The Clause is violated whenever an expert relies on any part of another person's work in forming an opinion.

Answer: D
- A: That was the Williams v. Illinois plurality's theory, which Smith v. Arizona rejected.
- B: Bullcoming rejected surrogate testimony. Cross-examining a substitute doesn't replace the analyst.
- C: Melendez-Diaz held forensic reports aren't exempt merely because they are kept in the regular course.
- D: Correct. Under Smith v. Arizona, when an expert conveys an absent analyst's statements to support an opinion and they support it only if true, they come in for their truth. Whether they are testimonial is a separate question.
- E: This overstates the holding. An expert may rely on others' data under Rule 703; the problem is conveying an absent analyst's statements for their truth.
Rule: If an expert conveys an absent analyst's statements and the opinion depends on their truth, they are offered for their truth (Smith v. Arizona). Whether they are testimonial remains a separate question (Melendez-Diaz, Bullcoming).

## Final 7 · Q24 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Chambers: critical, trustworthy defense evidence
In a state murder trial, Floyd Kerr's defense is that Lyle Bates did the shooting. On the night of the killing, Bates separately told three close friends that he shot the victim. A fourth witness saw Bates holding a revolver of the type used minutes after the shots. Kerr calls Bates, who denies the killing. The state's voucher rule bars Kerr from cross-examining his own witness. The state's hearsay law has no exception for statements against penal interest, so the three friends are excluded. Kerr is convicted.

What is Kerr's strongest constitutional argument on appeal?

A. A Confrontation Clause violation, because Bates's out-of-court confessions were testimonial.
B. Due process: together, the rulings excluded critical, trustworthy defense evidence.
C. There is none, because states have complete authority to set their own rules of evidence.
D. A violation of Rule 804(b)(3), which binds state courts through the Fourteenth Amendment.
E. There is none, because Bates testified, so the jury heard his own account of the night.

Answer: B
- A: The Confrontation Clause protects the defendant against the prosecution's evidence. Kerr's complaint is that his own evidence was excluded, which is a due-process and compulsory-process claim.
- B: Correct. Chambers held that a voucher rule combined with a hearsay rule lacking a penal-interest exception denied due process. The excluded confessions were spontaneous, made to close friends soon after the crime, corroborated, and against interest, and the declarant was available for cross. Under Chambers, "the hearsay rule may not be applied mechanistically to defeat the ends of justice."
- C: States have broad latitude, but the Constitution guarantees "a meaningful opportunity to present a complete defense" (Holmes).
- D: The Federal Rules govern federal courts and don't bind a state trial. Rule 804(b)(3) also requires unavailability, and Bates testified.
- E: Hearing Bates's denial while being barred from confronting him with his confessions is precisely the problem.
Rule: Under Chambers, evidence rules may not be applied mechanistically to exclude critical, reliable defense evidence. Holmes adds that arbitrary or disproportionate exclusions violate the right to present a complete defense.

## Final 7 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 704(b) and Diaz
Rivera is charged with knowingly importing heroin hidden in a car door. Her defense is that she didn't know it was there. A government agent testifies as an expert. Which testimony is barred by Rule 704(b)?

A. "Most couriers in loads of this size know they are carrying drugs."
B. "Traffickers commonly hide drugs inside car doors on this route."
C. "Based on my experience, Ms. Rivera knew the drugs were in the car."
D. "This quantity would sell for about $400,000 at the street level."
E. "Organizations rarely trust an unknowing driver with a load this valuable."

Answer: C
- A: Diaz allows testimony about most people in the defendant's position. Rule 704(b) doesn't bar it.
- B: Concealment methods are general background, not an opinion on the defendant's mental state.
- C: Correct. Rule 704(b) bars an expert's opinion about whether the defendant had the mental state that is an element of the crime. This opinion is about Rivera herself.
- D: Value is outside Rule 704(b) altogether.
- E: This describes organizations' practices in general and stops short of an opinion on Rivera's own state of mind, so Diaz allows it.
Rule: Rule 704(b) bars an opinion that the defendant had or lacked the required mental state. Under Diaz, testimony about most people in the defendant's position is allowed.

## Final 7 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Kumho Tire: experience-based testimony still gets gatekeeping
In an arson-for-profit prosecution, the government calls a fire investigator with 25 years of experience. He would testify that "pour patterns" on the floor prove an accelerant was used. The defense moves for a Rule 702 hearing. It offers studies showing that such patterns often appear in accidental fires after flashover. The trial court denies the hearing, reasoning that the investigator's testimony rests on experience rather than science, so Daubert doesn't apply.

Was the ruling correct?

A. Yes, because Daubert's gatekeeping applies only to scientific testimony.
B. Yes, because 25 years of experience establishes reliability as a matter of law.
C. Yes, because challenges to an expert's method go to weight, not admissibility.
D. No, because the court must assess reliability of experience-based testimony too.
E. No, because fire investigation must be generally accepted under Frye before it is admitted.

Answer: D
- A: That was the Eleventh Circuit's view, which Kumho Tire reversed.
- B: Experience can qualify a witness, but the court must still find that the method is reliable and reliably applied (Rule 702(c)–(d)).
- C: Under Rule 702 as amended in 2023, reliability is a Rule 104(a) question for the court, decided by a preponderance.
- D: Correct. Kumho Tire holds that the gatekeeping duty applies to "scientific, technical, or other specialized knowledge." The court may use whichever Daubert factors fit, such as testing and error rates. It has discretion in how to test reliability, but not whether to. With studies showing that pour patterns appear in accidental fires, it must decide whether this method can reliably separate arson from accident.
- E: Daubert held that Frye didn't survive the Federal Rules. General acceptance is one factor, not a requirement.
Rule: Kumho Tire: Rule 702 gatekeeping covers all expert testimony. The court applies the Daubert factors that fit and has latitude in how to assess reliability, but it must assess it. Since 2023, the proponent proves each Rule 702 element by a preponderance.

## Final 7 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Ancient documents: Rule 901(b)(8) vs. Rule 803(16)
In a 2026 boundary dispute, a party offers a surveyor's letter dated 2003, found in the county's archived land files, stating where a boundary stone stood. The letter's condition raises no suspicion, and the files are where such letters would be kept. The opponent objects on authentication and hearsay grounds. No witness has knowledge of the letter, and no hearsay exception other than the ancient-documents exception could apply.

How should the court rule?

A. It is authenticated as an ancient document, but the hearsay exception covers only documents prepared before 1998.
B. Admit it, because a document more than 20 years old is both authenticated and excepted from the hearsay rule.
C. Exclude it, because the 20-year authentication rule was repealed when the hearsay exception was narrowed.
D. Admit it, because a document kept in a public office is self-authenticating and admissible as a public record.
E. Exclude it, because a document can't be authenticated without a chain of custody from its author.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Rule 901(b)(8) authenticates a document that is in a condition creating no suspicion, was in a place where it would likely be, and is at least 20 years old, so the 2003 letter is authenticated. But Rule 803(16) now covers only documents prepared before January 1, 1998, so the letter's assertions remain hearsay with no exception.
- B: That was true before December 1, 2017. Since then, Rule 803(16) covers only a document prepared before January 1, 1998, whose authenticity is established. Rule 901(b)(8)'s 20-year rule didn't change, so the two now diverge.
- C: The 2017 amendment narrowed only the hearsay exception in Rule 803(16). Rule 901(b)(8)'s 20-year authentication rule remains.
- D: A surveyor's private letter placed in a public file isn't a record of a public office's own activities or observations under Rule 803(8), and it isn't a certified copy under Rule 902(4).
- E: Ancient documents are authenticated by age, condition, and custody, without a strict chain of custody, as United States v. Stelmokas held for wartime archival records.
Rule: Authentication and hearsay are separate hurdles. Rule 901(b)(8) authenticates documents at least 20 years old found in natural custody and in unsuspicious condition. Rule 803(16) excepts only documents prepared before January 1, 1998.

## Final 7 · Q28 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 1002: event vs. content
A security camera recorded a bank robbery, and the video is at the police station. The government doesn't offer it. A teller testifies from memory that the robber wore a red cap. A detective who wasn't there testifies that the video shows the robber wearing a red cap. The defense raises Rule 1002 against both. What is the correct ruling?

A. Both are admitted, because each describes the same event.
B. Both are excluded, because the video is the best evidence of the robbery.
C. The teller is excluded, but the detective is admitted as an investigator.
D. The teller is admitted, but the detective is excluded.
E. Both are admitted, because a video is not a writing under Rule 1001.

Answer: D
- A: The detective knows only what the recording shows, so that testimony proves its content.
- B: The rule doesn't require the best evidence on every point. A witness may testify from memory even though a recording exists.
- C: This reverses the rule. The teller is proving the event; the detective is proving the content.
- D: Correct. The teller perceived the event and proves it from memory. The detective can only prove what the recording shows, so Rule 1002 requires the original or a Rule 1004 excuse, and the video is available.
- E: Rule 1001 covers recordings as well as writings and photographs.
Rule: Rule 1002 applies only when a party proves the content of a writing, recording, or photograph. An eyewitness may testify to the event from memory, but a witness who knows the event only through the recording proves its content.

## Final 7 · Q29 · Privileges — Lawyer-client: an unnecessary third party
Felix Ward met with his lawyer to discuss defending a fraud investigation. He brought his close friend, Tom, "for moral support." Tom has no role in the legal matter and isn't anyone's agent. In Tom's presence, Ward told his lawyer that he had backdated several invoices. A federal grand jury subpoenas Tom.

Must Tom testify about what Ward said?

A. No, because Ward intended the whole meeting to stay confidential.
B. No, because a client's statements about past acts are always privileged.
C. Yes, because a friend's presence meant the statement wasn't confidential.
D. No, because only the lawyer can be compelled to disclose what was said.
E. Yes, because the crime-fraud exception covers statements about fraud.

Answer: C
- A: Subjective intent isn't enough. The precautions taken bear on whether confidentiality was reasonable, and bringing a nonessential friend defeats it.
- B: The privilege protects only confidential communications.
- C: Correct, though Ward surely thought he was protected. The privilege covers confidential communications made to obtain legal services. A third party whose presence isn't needed to facilitate the legal advice, like a friend there for moral support, destroys confidentiality.
- D: If the communication isn't privileged, anyone who heard it may be compelled to testify.
- E: Disclosing past fraud to get a defense is protected. The crime-fraud exception reaches advice sought to further a future or ongoing crime.
Rule: The lawyer-client privilege covers confidential communications made to obtain legal services. The presence of a third party who isn't needed to facilitate the representation defeats confidentiality, and so the privilege recognized under Rule 501.

## Final 7 · Q30 · Privileges — Spousal privileges: marriage after the crime
Jon Reed is charged with a March bank robbery. In February, while they were dating, Reed privately told Wendy Cole, "I'm going to hit the bank on Main next month." In March, Cole saw Reed counting stacks of banded cash. They married in April, and the marriage is valid. At the federal trial in October, the prosecution calls Cole, who doesn't want to testify.

Which statement is correct?

A. Reed may prevent Cole from testifying, because the defendant-spouse holds the privilege.
B. Cole may refuse, but if she testifies, Reed can't block the statement or what she saw.
C. Cole must testify, because the privilege doesn't cover a marriage entered into after the crime.
D. Cole may refuse to testify about the February statement, but must testify to what she saw.
E. Reed may bar the February statement as a marital confidence, but Cole must testify to the cash.

Answer: B
- A: Trammel modified Hawkins. The witness-spouse alone holds the testimonial privilege.
- B: Correct. Under Trammel, the privilege against adverse spousal testimony belongs to the witness-spouse and requires a valid marriage when it is claimed, so Cole may refuse. If she testifies, Reed can't stop her. The marital-confidences privilege covers only communications made during the marriage, and the February statement was made before.
- C: The federal testimonial privilege looks to a valid marriage when it is claimed, not when the crime occurred.
- D: The testimonial privilege covers all adverse testimony, including acts observed. It isn't limited to communications.
- E: The February statement was made before the marriage, so it isn't a marital confidence. And the witness-spouse may refuse all adverse testimony.
Rule: Trammel: the witness-spouse holds the testimonial privilege, which requires a valid marriage when claimed and covers all adverse testimony in a criminal case against the spouse. The confidences privilege covers only confidential communications made during the marriage.

## Final 8 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Tanner: nonjuror evidence of misconduct
After Vera Holt's conviction for securities fraud, she moves for a new trial. She supports the motion with two affidavits. The first, from a juror, says four jurors drank pitchers of beer at lunch every day of trial and dozed through afternoon testimony. The second, from a waiter at the café across from the courthouse, says he served those jurors two pitchers of beer at lunch each day.

Which affidavit may the court consider?

A. Both, because intoxication during trial is an outside influence on the jury.
B. Neither, because Rule 606(b) bars all evidence of juror misconduct after a verdict.
C. Only the waiter's, because Rule 606(b) limits jurors' testimony, not others'.
D. Only the juror's, because a juror is the best witness to what happened among jurors.
E. Both, because the Sixth Amendment right to a competent jury overrides Rule 606(b).

Answer: C
- A: Tanner held that drugs and alcohol are "no more an 'outside influence' than a virus, poorly prepared food, or a lack of sleep."
- B: Rule 606(b) restricts a juror's own testimony and affidavits. It doesn't bar evidence from others.
- C: Correct. Under Tanner, juror testimony about juror intoxication is barred, because intoxication isn't an outside influence. Tanner relied on other safeguards: observation during trial, juror reports before the verdict, and nonjuror evidence of misconduct after it. The waiter's affidavit is that kind of evidence.
- D: The juror's affidavit is exactly what Rule 606(b) and Tanner exclude.
- E: Tanner held that Rule 606(b)'s bar doesn't violate the Sixth Amendment in this setting.
Rule: Rule 606(b) bars jurors from testifying about internal matters such as intoxication (Tanner). Misconduct may still be proved by nonjuror evidence, and by juror reports made before the verdict.

## Final 8 · Q2 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 401: relevance through corroboration (United States v. James)
A woman is charged with aiding and abetting a killing and claims she acted in fear for her and her son’s lives. She testifies that the deceased had bragged to her about beating two men so badly that they ended up in the hospital. The prosecution suggests in cross-examination that she made the stories up. She then offers certified police reports showing that the deceased did hospitalize two men in exactly the beatings she described. She concedes she never saw the reports before the killing. The prosecution objects that the reports are irrelevant. How should the court rule?

A. Sustain the objection, because she never saw the reports, so they could not have affected her fear.
B. Overrule the objection, because the reports show the deceased’s violent character, which is an essential element of self-defense under Rule 405(b).
C. Overrule the objection, because once the prosecution attacks her credibility, any supporting evidence comes in without limit.
D. Overrule the objection, because the reports corroborate her testimony about what he told her, which bears on her fear.
E. Sustain the objection, because her own testimony about what he told her is the only permissible evidence of her state of mind.

Answer: D
- A: That was the trial court’s framing, which the en banc court rejected. Relevance can run through corroboration of her account of what he told her, even though the reports themselves never reached her.
- B: A victim’s violent character is not an essential element of self-defense; what matters is the defendant’s reasonable fear. Specific acts don’t come in under 405(b) for that reason. The reports are relevant for a different, non-character purpose: corroboration.
- C: Relevance is the right ground, but “without limit” is wrong. The reports remain subject to Rule 403, which the dissent in United States v. James stressed because jurors may conclude the deceased deserved to die, and to a limiting instruction.
- D: Correct. This is United States v. James (en banc). The trial judge asked whether the reports were relevant to her state of mind, and they weren’t, since she never saw them. The right question was whether they were relevant to her testimony about her state of mind, and they were: if the beatings really happened, it’s more likely he told her about them and less likely she invented the stories. Rule 403 remains available (Judge Kleinfeld’s dissent).
- E: No rule limits proof of state of mind to the defendant’s own words. Any evidence with a tendency to make her account more or less probable is relevant under 401.
Rule: Rule 401: relevance can run through corroboration. In James, police reports she never saw were relevant to her testimony about her state of mind, though 403 remained available.

## Final 8 · Q3 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Flight: the Myers chain and Rule 403
Lionel Banks is charged with robbing a pharmacy on May 1. On May 22, two officers approached Banks outside his apartment to serve a bench warrant for unpaid child support. Banks knew about that warrant. He ran, and he was caught three blocks away. Nothing suggests he knew he was a suspect in the robbery. The prosecution offers the flight to show consciousness of guilt of the robbery, and Banks objects.

What is the court's best ruling?

A. Admit it, because flight from police always has some tendency to show consciousness of guilt.
B. Exclude it under Rule 404(b), because flight is a bad act offered to show the defendant's criminal character.
C. Admit it, because the weight of flight evidence is for the jury, and Rule 403 doesn't reach the accused's conduct.
D. Exclude it under Rule 403, because flight from an unrelated warrant says little about this robbery.
E. Exclude it as irrelevant, because flight three weeks after the crime can't make guilt any more probable.

Answer: D
- A: The evidence may clear Rule 401's low bar, but relevance isn't the end of the analysis. Rule 403 weighs how much it is worth.
- B: Flight is offered for a chain of inferences about consciousness of guilt, not to show character. Rule 404(b) isn't the right objection.
- C: Rule 403 applies to all relevant evidence, including the defendant's own conduct.
- D: Correct. The Myers chain needs each link to hold: behavior to flight, flight to consciousness of guilt, guilt of the crime charged, and actual guilt. The third link fails when Banks had another reason to run. Explaining that reason would force him to reveal another wrong. Low probative value and real unfair prejudice let the court exclude it under Rule 403.
- E: A three-week delay weakens the inference but doesn't eliminate it. Rule 401 requires only "any tendency."
Rule: Flight is relevant only through a chain of inferences: to flight, to consciousness of guilt, to guilt of the crime charged, and to actual guilt. When a link is weak, as when the defendant had another reason to flee, Rule 403 can exclude it.

## Final 8 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 407: firing an employee as a remedial measure
A Fleet Movers van ran a stop sign and struck cyclist Abby Chen. The next day, Fleet fired the driver. Chen sues Fleet, alleging that the driver was negligent and that Fleet is vicariously liable. Fleet admits that the driver was its employee acting within the scope of employment. Chen offers evidence of the firing to show that the driver was at fault.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit it, because Rule 407 covers only physical repairs and design changes.
B. Admit it, because firing the driver is Fleet's admission of fault by conduct.
C. Admit it to show that Fleet controlled the driver and could fire him.
D. Admit it, because it is offered to prove the driver's negligence, not Fleet's.
E. Exclude it, because firing him is a remedial measure offered to prove fault.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 407 covers any "measures" that would have made the injury less likely. Disciplining or firing a negligent employee qualifies.
- B: That is precisely the inference Rule 407 forbids: that a post-accident measure shows fault.
- C: Control is a permitted purpose only "if disputed." Fleet concedes employment and scope.
- D: Chen's claim against Fleet depends on the driver's negligence. Offering the firing to prove it is offering it to prove negligence or culpable conduct.
- E: Correct. Removing an unsafe driver is a measure that would have made the collision less likely. Rule 407 bars it to prove negligence or culpable conduct, and no permitted purpose is in dispute.
Rule: Rule 407 covers any post-injury measure that would have made the harm less likely, including discipline or firing. The "if disputed" purposes (ownership, control, feasibility) apply only when the opponent actually disputes them.

## Final 8 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 409: medical expenses only
Moments after backing into a cyclist in a parking lot, a driver jumps out and says: (1) “I’ll cover whatever your bike repairs cost,” (2) “and I’ll pay your doctor bills if you need to see someone,” and (3) “I never checked my mirror.” No demand had been made, and nothing was in dispute. The cyclist later sues. Which statements may the cyclist offer to prove the driver’s negligence?

A. Statements 1 and 3.
B. None of them, because Rules 408 and 409 together protect everything said while trying to make an injured person whole.
C. Statements 1, 2, and 3, because Rule 409 applies only after a claim has been made.
D. Statements 2 and 3.
E. Statement 3 only.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. 409 excludes only offers to pay “medical, hospital, or similar expenses,” so statement 2 is out, but the admission that came with it isn’t. Statement 1 offers to pay for property damage, which 409 doesn’t cover. Nor does 408: it protects offers to compromise a claim disputed as to validity or amount, and at the scene there was no claim and no dispute.
- B: Neither rule is that broad. 409 covers only medical-type expenses, and not the statements made with the offer; 408 covers only the compromise of a disputed claim.
- C: Rule 409 has no claim requirement: it excludes an offer to pay medical expenses whenever it is made, so statement 2 is out. The dispute requirement belongs to Rule 408, which is why statement 1 isn’t protected.
- D: Statement 2 is the one 409 squarely excludes: an offer to pay medical expenses resulting from the injury can’t prove liability. Statement 1 is the admissible offer.
- E: That treats the repair offer as protected. 409 is limited to medical and similar expenses, and 408 requires a disputed claim. An offer made before anyone has claimed or disputed anything isn’t a compromise.
Rule: Rule 409 excludes only offers to pay medical and similar expenses, not statements made with them or offers to pay other losses. 408 requires a claim disputed as to validity or amount.

## Final 8 · Q6 · Character Evidence — Identity: a common method isn’t a signature
A defendant is charged with robbing a convenience store at knifepoint. The clerk’s identification is shaky, and the defense is misidentification. The prosecution offers the defendant’s conviction, four years earlier, for robbing a gas station at knifepoint, arguing that it proves identity. How should the court rule?

A. Admit it, because identity is one of the exceptions to the propensity ban listed in Rule 404(b)(2), and identity is disputed here.
B. Admit it, because identity is disputed and both crimes were armed robberies of small stores committed with knives.
C. Admit it, because a conviction is reliable proof that the earlier robbery happened.
D. Admit it, because four years is recent enough that the earlier robbery stays probative of identity.
E. Exclude it, because a knifepoint robbery is too common a method to show that one person committed both crimes.

Answer: E
- A: The 404(b)(2) purposes are not exceptions. They are non-character routes to relevance, and a chain that runs through the propensity box is still barred (Zackowitz; Kirsch).
- B: A dispute over identity makes identity a proper purpose but doesn’t make every similar crime probative of it. The likeness must be idiosyncratic, not generic.
- C: Reliability of proof answers the Huddleston question, whether the other act happened. It doesn’t supply a non-propensity reason the act is relevant.
- D: Recency doesn’t create signature-level similarity. A knifepoint robbery is too common a method to show the same person did both, so the inference runs through propensity.
- E: Correct. Identity through modus operandi requires similarities so distinctive that the crime “could not be anyone else’s” (Trenkler). A single prosaic commonality like a knife isn’t enough (Pisari). Without a signature, the only route to identity is that he robbed before, so he robbed again, which is the forbidden inference.
Rule: Rule 404(b) identity (modus operandi) requires signature-level similarity, not prosaic commonalities (Trenkler vs. Pisari). The listed purposes aren’t exceptions.

## Final 8 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Identity: prosaic commonalities
Marek is charged with robbing a credit union. The robber wore a ski mask, showed a handgun, and passed the teller a note demanding cash. The government offers evidence that Marek robbed a bank in another city two years earlier, also wearing a ski mask, showing a handgun, and passing a note. It says the evidence proves identity. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admitted, because identity is listed in Rule 404(b)(2) and listing it ends the inquiry.
B. Admitted, because two crimes of the same type make it more likely the same person did both.
C. Excluded, because other crimes may never prove identity when the defendant denies being there.
D. Excluded, because two years is too remote to allow any use of a prior crime.
E. Excluded, because these common features leave only a propensity inference.

Answer: E
- A: Listing a purpose doesn't compel admission. The evidence must actually prove identity by a non-propensity chain, and Rule 403 still applies.
- B: That reasons from the defendant's type of crime, which is the propensity inference. United States v. Trenkler asks whether the crime could likely be anyone else's.
- C: Identity is a recognized purpose. United States v. Trenkler admitted another crime for this reason because the similarities were distinctive.
- D: Rule 404(b) has no fixed time limit. Remoteness is at most a factor in the balance.
- E: Correct. Identity requires characteristics sufficiently idiosyncratic to permit an inference of pattern. Under United States v. Trenkler, prosaic commonalities aren't enough, and masks, guns, and notes are common to many robberies.
Rule: To prove identity under Rule 404(b)(2), the other crime must share idiosyncratic features that work like a signature. Prosaic commonalities leave only propensity (United States v. Trenkler).

## Final 8 · Q8 · Character Evidence — Rule 405(b): self-defense isn’t an element
A defendant charged with assault claims he punched the alleged victim in self-defense. He offers testimony from two strangers that the victim beat each of them in separate bar fights last year. The defendant didn’t know about either fight when he threw the punch. Under which theory, if any, is the testimony admissible?

A. Rule 405(b), because a claim of self-defense puts the victim’s violent character directly in issue as an element.
B. Rule 404(a)(2)(B), which allows a defendant to prove the victim’s violent character by specific acts.
C. Rule 404(b), to show the victim’s intent to attack the defendant.
D. None. Self-defense doesn’t make the victim’s character an essential element, and he didn’t know of the fights.
E. Rule 406, because two bar fights in one year show the victim’s habit of violence.

Answer: D
- A: The rule says “person,” and some argue the victim’s character is an element, but the element of self-defense is the defendant’s reasonable belief. Character is at most circumstantial.
- B: 404(a)(2)(B) allows the trait to be offered, but 405(a) controls the method: reputation or opinion on direct, specific instances only on cross.
- C: Two unrelated bar fights show the victim’s intent only through propensity, the inference Rule 404(b)(1) bars. And since the defendant didn’t know of them, they can’t explain his fear either.
- D: Correct. Under 404(a)(2)(B) the defendant may offer the victim’s violent trait, but 405(a) limits him to reputation or opinion. 405(b) allows specific instances only when the trait is an essential element, and self-defense turns on the defendant’s reasonable fear, not on the victim’s actual character. Because he didn’t know of the fights, there’s no state-of-mind route either (contrast James, where the records corroborated what the victim had told her). Offering even reputation evidence would open the door to his own violent character under 404(a)(2)(B)(ii).
- E: Two fights aren’t a habit. Habit is a regular, semi-automatic response to a repeated situation, not a general disposition toward violence.
Rule: Rule 405(b) allows specific acts only when character is an essential element (entrapment, truth in defamation, negligent entrustment, custody), not in self-defense. The victim’s violence comes in by reputation or opinion under 404(a)(2)(B).

## Final 8 · Q9 · Character Evidence — Rule 405(b): essential element
In which situation may the proponent prove character on direct examination with specific instances of conduct?

A. A plaintiff suing for negligent entrustment proves the driver's earlier reckless crashes.
B. A defendant claiming self-defense proves the victim once stabbed a stranger during a bar argument over a debt.
C. A defendant charged with fraud proves he once returned a lost wallet.
D. A prosecutor rebuts a claim of peacefulness with the defendant's bar fight.
E. None of the above.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. In negligent entrustment the driver's character is an essential element of the claim, so Rule 405(b) allows specific instances.
- B: Character isn't an essential element of self-defense, which turns on the defendant's reasonable fear. Rule 405(a) limits this proof to reputation or opinion.
- C: Honesty is offered circumstantially here. It isn't an element, so Rule 405(a) applies.
- D: Rebuttal under Rule 404(a)(2)(A) uses reputation or opinion. Specific acts are for cross of the character witness.
- E: One of the other choices is correct: the negligent entrustment plaintiff may use specific instances under Rule 405(b).
Rule: Rule 405(b) allows specific instances only when character is an essential element of a charge, claim, or defense: entrapment, truth in defamation, negligent entrustment, custody. Self-defense isn't on that list.

## Final 8 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 608(a): bias isn't a character attack
A government informant testifies. The defense's whole cross suggests he is lying because the government paid him and dropped his charges. Later, the prosecutor calls a pastor to testify that the informant has a strong reputation for truthfulness. What is the correct ruling?

A. Admitted, because any cross-examination attacks a witness's credibility.
B. Admitted, because proof of bias is an attack on character for truthfulness.
C. Admitted, because Rule 608(a) lets a party support a witness before any attack.
D. Admitted, but only as opinion testimony and not as reputation testimony.
E. None of the above.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 608(a) requires an attack on character for truthfulness in particular, not just any attack on credibility.
- B: The Advisory Committee Note says bias isn't an attack on character for truthfulness.
- C: Rule 608(a) says truthful character evidence comes in only after the character has been attacked.
- D: Rule 608(a) treats reputation and opinion alike, and neither form is allowed here.
- E: Correct. The testimony should be excluded. Bias doesn't attack character for truthfulness, so Rule 608(a) doesn't open the door to good-character evidence.
Rule: Evidence of truthful character comes in only after an attack on character for truthfulness, by Rule 608 opinion or reputation, Rule 608(b) acts, or Rule 609 convictions. Bias isn't such an attack.

## Final 8 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609(c): a pardon and a later felony
In a federal fraud trial, the defendant seeks to impeach a prosecution witness with his eight-year-old felony burglary conviction. Five years ago, the governor pardoned him for that crime after a board found that he had been rehabilitated. Two years ago, he was convicted of felony assault. Is the burglary conviction admissible under Rule 609?

A. Yes, subject to Rule 403, because his later felony lifted the pardon’s bar.
B. No, because a pardon based on a finding of rehabilitation always bars use of the conviction.
C. No, because a prosecution witness may be impeached only with crimes of dishonesty.
D. No, because the pardon removed the conviction from his record for every purpose.
E. Yes, but only if its probative value substantially outweighs its prejudicial effect.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. 609(c)(1) bars a conviction pardoned on a finding of rehabilitation only if “the person has not been convicted of a later crime punishable by death or by imprisonment for more than one year.” The later assault removes the bar. The burglary is a felony offered against a witness other than the accused, so 609(a)(1)(A) applies, subject to 403.
- B: The bar has a built-in exception: a later felony conviction shows that the finding of rehabilitation didn’t hold.
- C: 609 applies to every witness, whoever calls him. Felonies come in under 609(a)(1)(A) against any witness other than the accused.
- D: Rule 609(c)(1) bars a conviction pardoned on a finding of rehabilitation only if the person hasn’t been convicted of a later felony. His later assault felony lifts the bar.
- E: That is 609(b)’s test for convictions more than ten years old. This one is eight years old, so the ordinary 403 test under 609(a)(1)(A) applies.
Rule: Rule 609(c): a pardon or equivalent based on rehabilitation bars the conviction unless the person was later convicted of a felony. One based on a finding of innocence always bars it.

## Final 8 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609(d): juvenile adjudications
In a criminal trial, the defense seeks to impeach a key prosecution witness with a juvenile adjudication for fraud. All other conditions of Rule 609 would be met if the witness were an adult. The prosecutor also wants to impeach the testifying defendant with his own juvenile adjudication. Which is correct?

A. Both may be used, but only with a limiting instruction under Rule 105 on each of the adjudications.
B. Only the prosecutor may use it, because the defendant chose to testify.
C. Both may be used, because the same test applies to every witness.
D. The defense may use the witness's if necessary; the prosecutor can't use the defendant's.
E. Neither may be used, because juvenile adjudications are never admissible.

Answer: D
- A: An instruction doesn't create admissibility, and Rule 609(d) doesn't reach the defendant.
- B: This reverses the rule, which protects the defendant.
- C: Rule 609(d) excludes the criminal defendant from its coverage.
- D: Correct. Rule 609(d) allows juvenile adjudications only in a criminal case, only against a witness other than the defendant, only if an adult conviction would be admissible, and only if necessary to fairly determine guilt or innocence.
- E: Rule 609(d) allows them in limited circumstances.
Rule: Rule 609(d): juvenile adjudications only in a criminal case, only against a witness other than the defendant, only if an adult conviction would be admissible, and only if necessary to fairly determine guilt or innocence.

## Final 8 · Q13 · Definition of Hearsay — Bridges: a statement offered to show knowledge
Seven-year-old Nora was lured from a playground into a stranger’s house and assaulted. When she got home an hour later, she described the place to her mother: a front room with a green couch, a fish tank with one large orange fish, a staircase with a broken bottom step, and a picture of a white horse over the fireplace.

Nora does not testify at the defendant’s trial. The prosecution calls the mother to repeat the description, and a detective then testifies that the defendant’s house, where Nora had never been before that day, contains each of those features. The defendant objects to the mother’s testimony as hearsay.

What is the best argument for admitting the mother’s testimony?

A. The description is a present sense impression, because Nora described the room as she remembered seeing it.
B. It isn’t offered to prove the house had those features, but to show Nora knew things she could have learned only inside it.
C. The description is an excited utterance, because Nora had just been assaulted.
D. The description is admissible under Rule 803(3) as a statement of Nora’s then-existing state of mind.
E. The description is a statement of identification, because it identifies the defendant’s house as the place Nora perceived earlier.

Answer: B
- A: A present sense impression must be made while or immediately after perceiving the event. Nora spoke an hour later, from memory.
- B: Correct. The statement is circumstantial evidence of Nora’s knowledge, which is the Bridges theory.
- C: An hour later, at home, giving a detailed description, Nora was not clearly under the stress of the event. This is weaker than the non-hearsay argument.
- D: Rule 803(3) excludes statements of memory offered to prove the fact remembered. A description of a room is memory.
- E: Rule 801(d)(1)(C) applies only if the declarant testifies and is subject to cross-examination. Nora does not testify.
Rule: A statement offered as circumstantial evidence of the declarant’s knowledge, rather than to prove the facts it describes, is not offered for the truth of the matter asserted. Bridges v. State.

## Final 8 · Q14 · Definition of Hearsay — Notice: admissible for one purpose only
A delivery driver's van lost its brakes and struck Ana Cole. She sues the van's owner, Swift Couriers, for negligent maintenance. Cole calls a gas-station attendant, who would testify that a week before the crash, the day after a Swift driver bought gas there, he phoned Swift's manager and said, "The brakes on van 9 are shot. I could hear them grinding when your driver pulled in yesterday." Cole wants to use the statement both to show that Swift knew of the problem and to prove that the brakes were bad.

What is the best ruling?

A. Admit it for both purposes, because the attendant had personal knowledge.
B. Exclude it entirely, because it is an out-of-court statement and so hearsay.
C. Admit it for both purposes as the attendant's present sense impression.
D. Admit it only to show notice, with a limiting instruction on request.
E. Admit it to prove the brakes were bad, because the manager adopted it by silence.

Answer: D
- A: Personal knowledge is required but doesn't make a statement offered for its truth admissible.
- B: Hearsay depends on purpose. For notice, the statement isn't offered for its truth.
- C: Rule 803(1) requires a statement made while or immediately after perceiving the event. The attendant called a day after hearing the grinding.
- D: Correct, though Cole would prefer the broader use. Offered to show that Swift was warned, the words aren't offered for their truth. For the truth of the brakes' condition, the statement is hearsay. Rule 105 requires a limiting instruction on timely request. Cole can prove the brakes' condition by other means, such as the mechanic or the wreck.
- E: Adoption by silence requires circumstances that would naturally call for a denial and proof of the manager's response. Nothing suggests he acquiesced.
Rule: The same words may be admissible to show notice or effect on the listener but hearsay if offered for their truth. Under Rule 105, the court admits them for the proper purpose and, on request, instructs the jury on the limit.

## Final 8 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Doyle: post-Miranda silence
After arrest and Miranda warnings, Hart says nothing to police. At trial, he testifies that he was framed. The prosecutor asks, "If you were framed, why didn't you tell the officers that night?" The defense objects. What is the correct ruling?

A. Overruled, because Rule 613 allows impeachment with an inconsistent omission.
B. Overruled, because silence in the face of an accusation is an adoptive admission.
C. Sustained, because no silence by a defendant may ever be mentioned at trial.
D. Overruled, because a testifying defendant may be impeached by any prior silence.
E. Sustained, because silence after Miranda warnings can't be used to impeach him.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 613 doesn't override the due-process rule of Doyle.
- B: Adoption by silence is limited when the accusation follows Miranda warnings.
- C: This is too broad. Pre-arrest silence may impeach, as may silence before Miranda warnings.
- D: Doyle limits this. Pre-arrest and pre-Miranda silence may impeach; post-Miranda silence may not.
- E: Correct. Doyle holds that using post-Miranda silence to impeach violates due process. The silence is insolubly ambiguous, and the warnings imply no penalty.
Rule: Post-Miranda silence may not be used to impeach (Doyle). Pre-arrest and pre-Miranda silence may be.

## Final 8 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 613(b): "unless the court orders otherwise"
In a 2026 product-liability trial, the defense's engineer testified and was excused. She then left for a two-month assignment overseas. The next day, the plaintiff discovers an email in which the engineer told a colleague that the product's design was "an accident waiting to happen." The plaintiff offers the email to impeach her. The defense objects that she was never given a chance to explain or deny it.

What is the most accurate statement?

A. It must be excluded, because the 2024 amendment requires the chance to explain first.
B. It is admissible, because the foundation requirement doesn't apply to experts.
C. It is admissible only as substantive evidence that the design was defective.
D. It must be excluded, because the engineer's opinion is a collateral matter.
E. The court has discretion to admit it without the usual foundation.

Answer: E
- A: The 2024 amendment made a prior opportunity the default, but the rule begins "unless the court orders otherwise." The ACN anticipates cases like a witness who is no longer available.
- B: Rule 613(b) applies to all witnesses. Only an opposing party's statement under Rule 801(d)(2) is exempt.
- C: Under the version of Rule 801(d)(1)(A) in effect for this 2026 trial, an unsworn email isn't substantive. It impeaches only.
- D: The engineer's view of the design's safety goes to the heart of her testimony. It isn't collateral.
- E: Correct. The default rule is sound but not absolute. Since 2024, extrinsic evidence ordinarily may come in only after the witness has had a chance to explain or deny. But the court has discretion to order otherwise, for example when the statement was discovered only after the witness left. The engineer isn't a party, so Rule 801(d)(2) doesn't remove the requirement.
Rule: Rule 613(b) (2024): unless the court orders otherwise, extrinsic evidence of a prior inconsistent statement comes in only after the witness has had an opportunity to explain or deny it. The court keeps discretion, and the rule doesn't apply to opposing parties' statements.

## Final 8 · Q17 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(a)(5)(B): try to get the testimony
Leo Marsh is on trial in federal court for fraud. He offers testimony that his former business partner, Rafael Cruz, told a friend, "I faked the invoices myself. Leo never knew." Cruz now lives in Brazil, beyond the reach of a subpoena. Marsh made no effort to arrange a deposition, though one could have been taken. The prosecution argues that Cruz isn't unavailable.

Is Cruz unavailable for purposes of the statement-against-interest exception?

A. Yes, because he lives abroad, beyond the reach of the court's subpoena power.
B. Yes, because any declarant who has moved out of the country is unavailable.
C. No, because Marsh also had to try to obtain Cruz's testimony, as by deposition.
D. It doesn't matter, because a statement against interest doesn't require unavailability.
E. Yes, because unavailability fails only when the proponent caused the declarant's absence.

Answer: C
- A: That would satisfy Rule 804(a)(5)(A) for former testimony, but for Rules 804(b)(2)–(4) the proponent must also seek the declarant's testimony.
- B: Absence isn't enough. The proponent must show that reasonable means of obtaining attendance or testimony failed.
- C: Correct. Under Rule 804(a)(5)(B), for exceptions (b)(2), (3), and (4), the proponent must show it couldn't procure the declarant's "attendance or testimony" by process or other reasonable means. Marsh didn't try to depose Cruz.
- D: Rule 804(b)(3) is among the exceptions that require unavailability.
- E: The proponent's wrongdoing is one way to fail Rule 804(a), but the proponent must also make the required showing.
Rule: Rule 804(a)(5) defines absence as unavailability only if the proponent couldn't procure attendance (for former testimony) or attendance or testimony (for dying declarations, statements against interest, and family history) by process or reasonable means.

## Final 8 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(6): acquiescence in wrongdoing
Wes Dunn, a member of a drug ring, testified before a federal grand jury against the ring's leader, Paul Kerr. A month before trial, another member, Cal Ruiz, told Kerr that Dunn "will be taken care of before he can testify." Kerr replied, "Do what you have to." Ruiz killed Dunn. At Kerr's trial, the government offers Dunn's grand jury testimony. The court finds these facts by a preponderance.

How should the court rule on Kerr's hearsay and Confrontation Clause objections?

A. Sustain both, because Kerr didn't personally cause Dunn's death or absence.
B. Sustain both, unless Kerr has first been convicted of murdering Dunn.
C. Overrule both, because Kerr forfeited them by acquiescing in the killing.
D. Overrule the hearsay objection, but sustain the confrontation objection.
E. Sustain both, because forfeiture must be proved by clear and convincing evidence.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 804(b)(6) reaches a party that "wrongfully caused—or acquiesced in wrongfully causing" the unavailability.
- B: No conviction is required. The judge decides the foundation under Rule 104(a).
- C: Correct. Under Rule 804(b)(6), a party that acquiesced in wrongdoing intended to make the declarant unavailable forfeits the hearsay objection. The 1997 ACN uses a preponderance under Rule 104(a). Forfeiture by wrongdoing with that intent also defeats the confrontation right (Giles), even for testimonial statements.
- D: Crawford and Giles recognize forfeiture as an exception to confrontation when the defendant intended to prevent testimony.
- E: The ACN to Rule 804(b)(6) uses a preponderance standard.
Rule: Rule 804(b)(6): a party that wrongfully caused or acquiesced in causing a declarant's unavailability, intending that result, forfeits the hearsay objection. The same intent forfeits the confrontation right (Giles). Preponderance under Rule 104(a).

## Final 8 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(3): memory and the will exception
Which statement, offered for its truth, is admissible under Rule 803(3)?

A. "My husband poisoned my coffee last night," offered in his trial to prove that he did it.
B. "I paid the rent on the first," offered to prove the rent was paid.
C. "Last spring I signed a will leaving all to Ann," offered in a dispute over that will.
D. "I believe the mechanic fixed the brakes," offered to prove they were fixed.
E. "I remember the light being green," offered to prove the light was green.

Answer: C
- A: As in Shepard, this looks backward to another person's act. Rule 803(3) excludes it.
- B: A statement of memory offered to prove the fact remembered falls outside Rule 803(3).
- C: Correct. Rule 803(3) excludes memory or belief offered to prove the fact remembered, except as it relates to the declarant's will.
- D: A statement of belief offered to prove the fact believed is excluded.
- E: This is memory offered to prove the fact remembered, the core exclusion in Rule 803(3).
Rule: Rule 803(3) covers then-existing state of mind, but not memory or belief offered to prove the fact remembered, unless it relates to the validity or terms of the declarant's will (Shepard).

## Final 8 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 902(11) certification for a business record
In a federal fraud trial, the government offers a bank's account statements to trace stolen funds. It calls no bank witness. Instead, it gave the defense written notice before trial and made available a certification from the bank's records custodian. The certification attests that the records were made at or near the time by people with knowledge, kept in the regular course of business, and made as a regular practice. The defense objects that no live witness laid a foundation and that the government hasn't shown the records are trustworthy.

How should the court rule?

A. Sustain it, because Rule 803(6) requires a custodian to testify in person.
B. Sustain it, because the government must prove the bank records are trustworthy.
C. Overrule it, because a Rule 902(11) certification can lay the foundation.
D. Overrule it, because a bank's records are public records under Rule 803(8).
E. Sustain it, because certified records are testimonial and require live testimony.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 803(6)(D) allows the foundation to be shown "by a certification that complies with Rule 902(11) or (12)."
- B: Since 2014, Rule 803(6)(E) puts the burden on the opponent to show untrustworthiness.
- C: Correct. With advance written notice and the record made available, a Rule 902(11) certification authenticates a domestic business record and satisfies Rule 803(6)(A)–(D). The opponent must then show lack of trustworthiness under Rule 803(6)(E).
- D: A private bank isn't a public office.
- E: Records kept in the ordinary course of business are generally nontestimonial, unlike lab certificates prepared for trial.
Rule: Rule 803(6)(D) can be met by a Rule 902(11) or (12) certification, with notice. Under Rule 803(6)(E), the opponent must show untrustworthiness. Ordinary business records are generally nontestimonial.

## Final 8 · Q21 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 807(b): the notice requirement
In a civil fraud case, the plaintiff learns during trial that a key witness has died. The plaintiff wants to offer the witness's detailed, corroborated letter to a state regulator. All agree the letter fits no Rule 803 or 804 exception. The letter is trustworthy, and it is the best available proof of the point. The plaintiff gives oral notice of the letter's substance and the writer's name in court, the moment it learns of the death. The defense objects.

What is the most accurate statement?

A. It is admissible, because trustworthiness and necessity are both satisfied.
B. It is inadmissible, because Rule 807 doesn't apply to near misses.
C. It is inadmissible, because Rule 807 notice must always come before trial.
D. It may come in for good cause, but the notice must be given in writing.
E. It is inadmissible, because only public officials' statements qualify.

Answer: D
- A: The substantive tests of Rule 807(a) are met, which makes this the tempting answer. But Rule 807(b) also requires reasonable written notice.
- B: Since 2019, a statement that narrowly misses another exception may still be considered.
- C: Rule 807(b) allows notice "during the trial or hearing if the court, for good cause, excuses a lack of earlier notice."
- D: Correct. Learning of the witness's death during trial is good cause for notice during trial. But Rule 807(b) still requires the notice to be "in writing," including the statement's substance and the declarant's name. The plaintiff should put its oral notice in writing. The admissibility answer depends on that procedure.
- E: Rule 807 has no such limit.
Rule: Rule 807 requires (a) trustworthiness and comparative necessity, and (b) reasonable notice in writing, stating the substance and the declarant's name, before trial or during trial if the court excuses the delay for good cause.

## Final 8 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Crawford: prior opportunity to cross
At a preliminary hearing, a witness testifies under oath, and defense counsel cross-examines her. She dies before trial. The prosecution offers a transcript of her testimony. Which is correct?

A. It is admissible if the judge finds that the testimony bears indicia of reliability.
B. It is barred, because the jury can't see the witness in person.
C. It is barred, because prior testimony is always testimonial.
D. It is admissible, because the Clause doesn't apply once a witness has died.
E. It is admissible, because the defense had a prior opportunity to cross-examine.

Answer: E
- A: That is the test of Ohio v. Roberts, which Crawford overruled for testimonial statements.
- B: Mattox allowed former testimony despite that loss.
- C: It is testimonial, but that isn't the end. Crawford allows it on unavailability plus a prior opportunity to cross.
- D: Death establishes unavailability. The prior opportunity to cross is still required.
- E: Correct. Crawford requires unavailability and a prior opportunity to cross-examine, both met here, consistent with Mattox. Rule 804(b)(1) handles the hearsay objection.
Rule: Testimonial statements of an absent witness are admissible only if the declarant is unavailable and the defendant had a prior opportunity to cross-examine (Crawford, Mattox).

## Final 8 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Rule 106 (2023): completing an oral admission
Morrow is charged with being a felon in possession of a firearm. At trial, Officer Jett testifies on direct examination that during a traffic stop, Morrow told him (orally, not recorded): “Yeah, the gun’s mine. It’s in the glove box.”

Defense counsel immediately asks to elicit on cross the rest of what Morrow said in the same breath: “But I took it off my nephew an hour ago because he was talking about hurting himself, and I’m driving it to the police station right now.” The government objects that Morrow’s self-serving statement is inadmissible hearsay when offered by Morrow.

How should the court rule?

A. Overrule the objection if fairness requires the remainder to correct a misleading impression, because Rule 106 permits completion over a hearsay objection.
B. Overrule the objection, because once the government introduces part of a defendant’s statement, the defendant is automatically entitled to introduce the rest of it.
C. Sustain the objection for now, because Morrow may offer the remainder, if at all, only during his own case.
D. Sustain the objection, because a party’s own statement escapes the hearsay rule only when offered by the opposing party, and no exception applies.
E. Sustain the objection, because Rule 106 applies only to writings and recorded statements.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. This states the fairness standard and the 2023 hearsay override.
- B: Completion is not automatic. The remainder must be needed in fairness, typically to correct a misleading impression.
- C: Rule 106 allows completion at that time, during the government’s presentation. The point is that the jury hears the explanation while it hears the admission.
- D: This was the government’s winning argument in some circuits before 2023. The amended rule allows completion over a hearsay objection.
- E: The 2023 amendment extended Rule 106 to oral statements.
Rule: If a party introduces all or part of a statement, an adverse party may require the introduction, at that time, of any other part, or any other statement, that in fairness ought to be considered at the same time. The adverse party may do so over a hearsay objection. FRE 106 (as amended 2023).

## Final 8 · Q24 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 701: apparent age as lay opinion
Kyle Doran is charged in federal court with sexual abuse of a minor, and the statute allows a defense that he reasonably believed the girl was at least 16. She was 14 at the time but is now 16 and looks it at trial. Doran calls three people who saw her often that summer, at the pool and the mall, to testify that she looked about 19 or 20. The government objects that only an expert can give an opinion about age.

How should the court rule?

A. Overrule the objection, because the opinions rest on what the witnesses saw and would help the jury.
B. Sustain the objection, because the opinions embrace the ultimate issue of whether Doran's belief was reasonable.
C. Sustain the objection, because the jurors can judge her appearance for themselves at trial.
D. Overrule the objection, but only if each witness first qualifies as an expert in human development.
E. Sustain the objection, because estimating a person's age from appearance requires specialized knowledge under Rule 702.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Rule 701 admits a lay opinion that is rationally based on the witness's perception, helpful, and not based on specialized knowledge. How old someone looked is a lay opinion, and it helps because the jury sees her only as she looks two years later. The Ninth Circuit held exclusion of such testimony was error on similar facts.
- B: Rule 704(a) abolished the ultimate-issue bar. In any event, the witnesses describe how she looked, not whether Doran's belief was reasonable.
- C: The jurors can see her only as she is now, two years older. Testimony about how she looked then is exactly what helps them decide what Doran reasonably believed.
- D: Rule 701(c) bars lay opinions based on specialized knowledge, but judging age from appearance isn't specialized. Requiring expert qualification confuses the two rules.
- E: Appearance, including apparent age, is a classic subject of lay opinion: an inference from perception that resists being broken down into separate facts. No specialized knowledge is needed.
Rule: Rule 701 admits lay opinion rationally based on the witness's perception, helpful to the jury, and not based on specialized knowledge. Appearance, age, speed, and emotional state are standard subjects of lay opinion.

## Final 8 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 702 as amended in 2023
In a toxic-exposure suit, the defense moves to exclude the plaintiff's toxicologist, arguing that her data are insufficient and that she didn't reliably apply her method. The plaintiff answers that those points go to weight and belong to the jury. How should the judge proceed?

A. Leave both points to the jury, because sufficiency of data and application of a method go to weight.
B. Decide whether the plaintiff has shown each Rule 702 requirement by a preponderance.
C. Exclude the testimony only if the defense proves the method is unreliable.
D. Ask only whether a reasonable jury could find that the requirements are met.
E. Decide whether the expert's ultimate conclusion is correct before admitting it.

Answer: B
- A: The 2023 Advisory Committee Note calls that approach an incorrect application of Rules 702 and 104(a).
- B: Correct. Since 2023, Rule 702 requires the proponent to demonstrate to the court that it is more likely than not that each requirement is met, under Rule 104(a).
- C: The burden is on the proponent, not the opponent.
- D: That is the Rule 104(b) standard. Rule 702's requirements are Rule 104(a) questions for the judge.
- E: The proponent must show reliability, not correctness. Once the requirements are met, remaining disputes go to weight.
Rule: Under Rule 702 (2023), the proponent must show the court, by a preponderance, sufficient facts or data, reliable methods, and a reliable application. These aren't weight questions for the jury. Fisher (2022) predates the amendment.

## Final 8 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Daubert's fit: valid science, wrong question
In a wrongful-death case, the plaintiff claims a nursing-home resident wandered outside because staff left a door unlocked. The defendant offers an astronomer who would testify, accurately, that the moon was full that night. It also offers a psychologist who would testify, citing a published, peer-reviewed study, that full moons increase nighttime restlessness in general populations. No study addresses dementia patients or wandering.

What is the strongest objection to the psychologist?

A. The study isn't generally accepted in the scientific community.
B. The psychologist isn't qualified to discuss the moon's effects.
C. The testimony doesn't fit the question of what this resident did.
D. The testimony is hearsay, because it relies on a published study.
E. The testimony states a legal conclusion about the home's negligence.

Answer: C
- A: General acceptance is one Daubert factor, and the study is published and peer-reviewed. Attacking it is weaker than attacking its relevance to this case.
- B: Nothing suggests she lacks qualifications for her field.
- C: Correct, though the science itself may be sound. Daubert requires that the testimony "fit" the issue. The opinion requires that the reasoning bridge the data to the question, and Joiner rejects too great an analytical gap. Daubert's own example: whether a night was dark (moon phase) is relevant, but moon phases don't show whether a person behaved irrationally that night.
- D: Experts may rely on studies of a kind experts reasonably rely on (Rule 703).
- E: Saying moons cause restlessness isn't a statement of law.
Rule: Under Daubert and Rule 702(a) and (d), expert testimony must fit the issue: it must help decide a fact in the case. Valid science that doesn't connect to the disputed fact (Joiner's analytical gap) is excludable.

## Final 8 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 901(b)(5): voice familiarity acquired for trial
Sasha Lin received an anonymous phone call demanding $20,000 and threatening her family. She didn't recognize the voice. Six months later, while preparing for trial, a detective played Lin a recorded police interview of the defendant, Pete Varn. Lin immediately recognized the voice as the caller's. At trial the prosecution offers Lin's identification to authenticate the call as Varn's. Varn objects.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because familiarity with a voice acquired for litigation can't authenticate it.
B. Exclude it, because identifying a voice requires a qualified expert in voice analysis.
C. Admit it only if the judge finds by a preponderance that the caller was Varn.
D. Admit it, because she heard Varn's voice under circumstances connecting it to him.
E. Exclude it, because the call wasn't recorded, so the best evidence rule bars testimony about it.

Answer: D
- A: That limit applies to a nonexpert's handwriting opinion under Rule 901(b)(2). Rule 901(b)(5) has no such limit for voices.
- B: Rule 901(b)(5) permits a lay opinion identifying a voice.
- C: Authentication is a Rule 104(b) question. The judge asks only whether a reasonable jury could find the caller was Varn.
- D: Correct. Rule 901(b)(5) allows identification of a voice "based on hearing the voice at any time under circumstances that connect it with the alleged speaker," even if the familiarity was acquired for trial. That is enough to support a finding. Varn can attack its reliability before the jury.
- E: The best evidence rule applies only to proof of the content of a writing, recording, or photograph. Lin is testifying to what she heard, and no recording exists.
Rule: Rule 901(b)(5) allows voice identification by anyone who has heard the voice at any time under connecting circumstances. Rule 901(b)(2) handwriting familiarity, by contrast, may not be acquired for the litigation. Authentication requires only evidence sufficient to support a finding.

## Final 8 · Q28 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 1004(a): bad-faith loss
A novelist sues for copying, claiming an early manuscript contained the disputed scenes. The court finds he burned the manuscript to hide that it was different. He offers a version rewritten from memory years later. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admitted, because the best evidence rule doesn't apply to copyright.
B. Admitted, because the rewritten version is a duplicate under Rule 1003.
C. Admitted, because the jury decides whether the original ever existed.
D. Admitted, because any lost original excuses production under Rule 1004.
E. Excluded, because originals lost in bad faith don't excuse production.

Answer: E
- A: Seiler applied the rule to a copyright claim, where content decides the case.
- B: A duplicate must be made by an accurate mechanical or electronic process, not by hand.
- C: Under Rule 1008 that is a jury question, but the Rule 1004 conditions are for the judge, and the court found bad faith.
- D: Rule 1004(a) excuses loss only when not caused by the proponent acting in bad faith.
- E: Correct. Like Seiler, content is at issue, and because the originals were destroyed in bad faith, Rule 1004(a) doesn't excuse them. Rule 1002 bars the reconstruction.
Rule: Rule 1004(a) excuses a lost original only if the proponent didn't lose or destroy it in bad faith (Seiler). Rule 1008 gives the Rule 1004 conditions to the judge.

## Final 8 · Q29 · Privileges — Rule 501: no federal physician-patient privilege
In a federal prosecution for health-care fraud, the government subpoenas the defendant's internist to testify that the defendant told him during a checkup that he “bills for a lot of visits that never happen.” The defendant invokes the physician-patient privilege of the state where the checkup took place, which would protect the statement in that state's courts.

Must the internist testify?

A. No, because the Full Faith and Credit Clause requires federal courts to honor state privileges.
B. Yes, because federal common law governs privilege here, and it has no physician-patient privilege.
C. No, because Jaffee v. Redmond recognized a privilege for confidential communications with any medical professional.
D. No, because federal courts apply the privilege law of the state where the communication took place.
E. Yes, because the physician-patient privilege never applies to a patient's statements about his own crimes.

Answer: B
- A: Full Faith and Credit concerns judgments and public acts, not evidentiary privileges in federal criminal trials. Rule 501 governs, and it points to federal common law.
- B: Correct. Under Rule 501, federal common law, interpreted “in the light of reason and experience,” governs privilege in criminal cases. The proposed rules omitted a physician-patient privilege, and federal courts haven't recognized one, though Jaffee recognized a psychotherapist-patient privilege.
- C: Jaffee recognized a privilege for confidential communications with licensed psychotherapists, including clinical social workers, in the course of psychotherapy. It didn't extend to general physicians.
- D: Rule 501 applies state privilege law only in a civil case, to a claim or defense for which state law supplies the rule of decision. In a federal criminal case, federal common law governs.
- E: States that recognize the privilege generally have no such exception. The statement is unprotected here because federal law recognizes no physician-patient privilege at all.
Rule: Rule 501: federal common law governs privilege, except in civil cases where state law supplies the rule of decision for the claim or defense. Federal courts recognize a psychotherapist-patient privilege (Jaffee) but no physician-patient privilege.

## Final 8 · Q30 · Privileges — Communications, not underlying facts
During a consultation, a driver tells her lawyer, "I was texting when I hit him." In the pedestrian's civil suit, she is asked at deposition (1) "Were you texting when you hit him?" and (2) "Did you tell your lawyer you were texting?" She invokes the lawyer-client privilege for both. How should the objections be resolved?

A. She need not answer either, because telling a lawyer a fact makes it privileged.
B. She must answer both, because the privilege doesn't apply to a party's deposition.
C. She must answer (2), but not (1), because only trial testimony is privileged.
D. She must answer both, because the communication relates to a tort, not a legal matter.
E. She must answer (1), but she need not answer (2).

Answer: E
- A: The privilege protects communications, not the underlying facts. Telling a lawyer doesn't shield a fact.
- B: The privilege applies in discovery and at trial alike.
- C: This reverses the rule and invents a trial-only limit.
- D: Getting advice about an accident is a legal matter. The communication is made to facilitate professional legal services.
- E: Correct. A client can't refuse to state a fact because she told it to her lawyer, but what she said to her lawyer in confidence for legal advice is privileged.
Rule: Under Rule 501, federal common law protects confidential lawyer-client communications made to obtain professional legal services. It doesn't protect the underlying facts.

## Final 9 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Rule 606(b)(2)(C): a clerical error, not a misunderstanding
A jury in a negligence case found the plaintiff 40% at fault and the defendant 60% at fault. It wrote "$300,000" on the verdict form's line for total damages, and the judge reduced that to $180,000 under the comparative-fault instruction. The next day, all twelve jurors sign affidavits. They say they agreed the plaintiff's total damages were $500,000, wrote $300,000 because they had already taken off the plaintiff's 40%, and never expected the judge to reduce it again. The plaintiff moves to correct the judgment to $300,000.

What is the court's best ruling?

A. Grant it, because it shows a mistake in entering the verdict on the form.
B. Grant it, because unanimous affidavits remove any concern about harassing jurors.
C. Deny it, because the jurors describe their own reasoning, not a clerical error on the form.
D. Deny it, because Rule 606(b) bars any juror testimony offered after a verdict.
E. Grant it, because the jurors' confusion was an outside influence on the verdict.

Answer: C
- A: This is the tempting answer, but the jury wrote the figure it meant to write. The 2006 ACN limits Rule 606(b)(2)(C) to errors in recording or transmitting the verdict actually agreed on, such as a transposed number. It doesn't cover jurors who misunderstood the legal effect of their answers.
- B: Unanimity doesn't create an exception. Rule 606(b)(1) bars testimony about mental processes however many jurors join it.
- C: Correct, even though it means the plaintiff loses $120,000 the jury meant to award. The form says what the jury agreed to write. What the jurors thought the judge would do with it is their "mental processes concerning the verdict," which Rule 606(b)(1) bars. Rule 606(b)(2)(C) covers only clerical mistakes in entering the agreed verdict (2006 ACN).
- D: Too broad. Rule 606(b)(2) allows juror testimony about extraneous information, outside influence, and clerical mistakes on the verdict form.
- E: An outside influence comes from outside the jury, like a threat or a bribe. Jurors' own confusion about an instruction is internal.
Rule: Rule 606(b)(2)(C) allows testimony that the verdict actually agreed on was entered on the form incorrectly. Jurors' misunderstanding of instructions or of how the court will use their answers is a mental process that Rule 606(b)(1) bars (2006 ACN).

## Final 9 · Q2 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 401(b): materiality from the substantive law
Joe Rinaldi, a bar owner, is charged under a statute making it a crime to sell alcohol to anyone under 21. The statute imposes strict liability, and the defendant's belief about the buyer's age isn't an element or a defense. Rinaldi offers the 19-year-old buyer's fake driver's license, showing her age as 23, to prove he reasonably believed she was of age.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit it, because it has some tendency to make Rinaldi's belief more probable.
B. Admit it with a limiting instruction that it bears only on Rinaldi's state of mind.
C. Exclude it, but only under Rule 403, because it might confuse the jury.
D. Exclude it as irrelevant, because his belief isn't a fact of consequence here.
E. Admit it, because a defendant always has a right to present evidence of good faith.

Answer: D
- A: Probativeness is half of Rule 401. The fact must also be "of consequence in determining the action" (materiality).
- B: An instruction can't make immaterial evidence relevant.
- C: The evidence fails the threshold of Rule 401, before any Rule 403 balancing.
- D: Correct. Rule 401(b) requires that the fact be of consequence, and the substantive law defines what is. Under a strict-liability statute, Rinaldi's belief about age is immaterial (compare Wingate and the Egelhoff voluntary-intoxication statute), so the license is irrelevant under Rule 402.
- E: The right to present a defense doesn't require admitting evidence on an issue the substantive law makes irrelevant (the Egelhoff case).
Rule: Under Rule 401, relevance requires both probativeness and materiality, and the substantive law decides which facts are of consequence. A statute can make a probative fact immaterial.

## Final 9 · Q3 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 403: stipulation outside Old Chief
Rosa Kell is charged with murdering her business partner. She claims she stabbed him once in self-defense when he lunged at her. Before trial she offers to stipulate to the victim's identity and the cause of death. The prosecution refuses and offers three autopsy photographs. Together they show 14 stab wounds, six of them in the victim's back. Kell objects under Rule 403 and cites Old Chief.

How should the court rule?
