A. Exclude them, because Old Chief requires the prosecution to accept a defendant's stipulation to any element.
B. Exclude them, because autopsy photographs of a stabbing are inherently more prejudicial than probative.
C. Admit them, because their value on the disputed self-defense claim isn't substantially outweighed.
D. Admit them, because Rule 403 doesn't apply to evidence of facts the defense has offered to stipulate.
E. Admit them only if the prosecution shows that no less inflammatory proof of the wounds is available.

Answer: C
- A: Old Chief is confined to proof of legal status, such as a prior-felony element. Ordinarily the prosecution may prove its case its own way.
- B: Rule 403 has no per se rule against gruesome photos. In Bocharski, four of six photos came in because they bore on disputed issues.
- C: Correct. The number and location of the wounds bear on the disputed claim of a single defensive blow. A stipulation to cause of death doesn't remove that probative value. Under Rule 403, exclusion requires that probative value be substantially outweighed. Bocharski excluded only photos with little value on any disputed issue.
- D: Rule 403 applies to all relevant evidence. A concession reduces probative value on the 403 scale. It doesn't take the evidence outside the rule.
- E: The availability of other proof is one factor in Rule 403 balancing. It isn't a threshold the prosecution must satisfy.
Rule: Under Rule 403, a concession lowers probative value only on the point conceded. Photos that bear on a disputed issue, such as self-defense, are admissible unless that value is substantially outweighed. Old Chief applies only to status elements.

## Final 9 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 407: feasibility put in dispute
A customer sues a ladder manufacturer, claiming the hinge on its Model 6 ladder was defectively designed. Two months after the customer's fall, the manufacturer changed the hinge. At trial, the manufacturer's engineer testifies on direct that the old hinge was the safest design that could practically have been built. The customer now offers the design change. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Excluded, because Rule 407 bars later design changes in any claim of a product defect.
B. Admitted to prove the defect, because Rule 407 does not reach strict-liability product claims.
C. Admitted, because the manufacturer disputed feasibility, with a limiting instruction on request.
D. Excluded, because a defense witness may be impeached only with measures taken before the injury.
E. Admitted for any purpose, because calling an engineer to testify waives the protection of Rule 407.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 407 bars the use only for negligence, culpable conduct, a defect, or a need for a warning. It allows another purpose, including feasibility if disputed.
- B: This is the view the 1997 amendment rejected. Rule 407 now expressly covers a defect in a product or its design.
- C: Correct. Rule 407 permits proof of feasibility if disputed. Saying nothing safer could practically be built disputes it, and Rule 105 limits the jury's use on request.
- D: A subsequent measure is exactly what Rule 407 lets a party use for impeachment or feasibility. Timing defines the rule; it doesn't limit impeachment this way.
- E: Opening the door permits only the other purpose. The evidence still can't be used to prove the defect.
Rule: Rule 407 bars subsequent measures to prove negligence, culpable conduct, a defect, or a need for a warning. They may come in for another purpose, such as impeachment or, if disputed, ownership, control, or feasibility.

## Final 9 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 409: the offer goes, the admission stays
Seconds after his car rear-ended Nia Grant's car, Owen Tate ran to her window and said, "I'm so sorry, I was reading a text. Don't worry, I'll pay your ambulance and hospital bills." Grant had not made any claim. In her negligence suit, she offers the whole statement through a bystander. Tate objects to all of it.

What is the proper ruling?

A. Exclude all of it under Rule 408, because it was an offer to resolve Grant's potential claim.
B. Exclude all of it under Rule 409, because statements made with an offer to pay are protected too.
C. Admit all of it, because Rule 409 covers only medical expenses actually paid, not promises to pay.
D. Admit his admission that he was reading a text, but exclude his offer to pay the bills.
E. Admit only the offer to pay, because an apology is inadmissible as an expression of sympathy.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 408 requires compromise of a claim disputed as to validity or amount. No claim existed yet, much less a dispute.
- B: Unlike Rule 408, Rule 409 doesn't protect statements that accompany the offer. The ACN explains that such offers are made from humanitarian impulses, while statements of fact are not.
- C: Rule 409 covers "furnishing, promising, or offering to pay" medical expenses, so the promise is protected.
- D: Correct. Rule 409 excludes the offer to pay medical and hospital expenses to prove liability. It doesn't reach the factual admission, which comes in as Tate's own statement under Rule 801(d)(2)(A).
- E: The Federal Rules have no apology exception, and Rule 409 is what bars the offer to pay. The slides contrast "Let me give you a bandaid" (barred) with "I'm so sorry" (admissible).
Rule: Rule 409 excludes only the offer or payment of medical expenses to prove liability. Accompanying admissions remain admissible, which contrasts with Rule 408's protection of statements made in compromise negotiations.

## Final 9 · Q6 · Character Evidence — Identity: a signature modus operandi
Three banks are robbed by a man wearing a rubber mask of a former president. Each time he hands the teller a note written on the back of a deposit slip from a different bank and leaves a single red carnation on the counter. Identity is disputed. The prosecution offers the defendant’s conviction for a robbery six years earlier in which he wore the same kind of mask, used the same kind of note, and left a red carnation. How should the court rule?

A. Admit it to prove identity, because the shared features are distinctive enough to amount to a signature.
B. Admit it as propensity evidence, because the crimes are so similar that he is likely to repeat them.
C. Exclude it, because the earlier robbery is more than five years old and therefore too remote to be probative.
D. Admit it, but only to show the defendant had the knowledge needed to commit the robberies.
E. Exclude it, because identity isn’t one of the exceptions to the propensity ban, so similar crimes stay out.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Modus operandi evidence proves identity when the similarities are idiosyncratic, so the crime “could not be anyone else’s” (Trenkler). A president mask, a note on another bank’s deposit slip, and a carnation together are a signature, not a prosaic commonality. The court then applies 403, weighing need, the strength of the similarity, how inflammatory the evidence is, and the risk of propensity reasoning, and gives a 105 instruction.
- B: That is the forbidden inference. The evidence is admissible only because the signature points to the same person, and the jury should be told not to use it to infer criminal character.
- C: No fixed time limit governs 404(b). Remoteness reduces probative value in the 403 balance, but six years doesn’t erase a signature this distinctive.
- D: Knowledge isn’t disputed, and robbing a bank takes no special know-how. The proper purpose is identity, which the signature features support (United States v. Trenkler).
- E: Identity is a permitted non-character purpose under 404(b)(2). The inference here is “this is his signature,” not “he is the robbing type.”
Rule: Rule 404(b) identity: signature-level similarities (“could not be anyone else’s”) are admissible, subject to 403 and a 105 instruction (Trenkler).

## Final 9 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Identity: a signature method
(Varies question 8.7.)
Same facts, except that in both robberies the robber wore a beekeeper's veil, handed the teller a rhyming demand typed in green ink, and left one chess piece on the counter. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Excluded, because similarity between crimes shows only propensity, however unusual the method.
B. Admitted for identity, subject to Rule 403, because the features work like a signature.
C. Admitted without Rule 403 review, because signature evidence falls outside the rule entirely.
D. Excluded, because the government must first prove the earlier robbery beyond a reasonable doubt.
E. Admitted as proof that Marek has a character trait for planning elaborate robberies.

Answer: B
- A: A distinctive method supports a non-character inference: the same person did both. That is the route United States v. Trenkler allowed.
- B: Correct. These details are idiosyncratic enough to suggest one author, so identity is a proper purpose under Rule 404(b)(2). Rule 403 and a limiting instruction still apply.
- C: Rule 404(b)(2) never compels admission. Rule 403 applies to every non-character use.
- D: Huddleston requires only evidence from which a jury could reasonably find the earlier act occurred.
- E: A trait for elaborate robberies is character. The permitted inference runs from the method to identity, not through character.
Rule: One changed fact decides the result: distinctive shared features turn a propensity argument into proof of identity, subject to Rule 403 (United States v. Trenkler).

## Final 9 · Q8 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(a)(2)(C): homicide victims
A defendant charged with murder offers no character evidence. He testifies that the victim lunged at him with a box cutter and that he fired in self-defense. In rebuttal, the prosecution calls a witness who testifies that the victim had a reputation in his neighborhood as a peaceful man. The defense objects that it never offered evidence of the victim’s character. How should the court rule?

A. Overrule the objection, because evidence that a homicide victim struck first lets the prosecution offer his peacefulness.
B. Overrule the objection, but only if the witness describes specific peaceful acts of the victim.
C. Sustain the objection, because the prosecution may never initiate character evidence in a criminal case.
D. Overrule the objection, but only if the defendant has also offered evidence of his own peaceful character.
E. Sustain the objection, because the prosecution may offer a victim’s character only after the defendant offers evidence of the victim’s character.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. 404(a)(2)(C) is the one prosecution-initiated character route outside 413–415. It is triggered by any evidence that the homicide victim was the first aggressor, including the defendant’s own testimony, not just by defense character evidence. The method is still reputation or opinion under 405(a).
- B: 405(a) limits proof to reputation or opinion. The witness’s reputation testimony is the right form.
- C: 404(a)(2)(C) is an exception that lets the prosecution offer the homicide victim’s peacefulness once first aggression is claimed.
- D: Rule 404(a)(2)(C) needs only evidence that the victim was the first aggressor. The defendant’s own character evidence isn’t a precondition.
- E: That describes 404(a)(2)(B). Subsection (C) is broader in homicide cases: evidence that the victim struck first, whatever its form, opens the door.
Rule: Rule 404(a)(2)(C): in a homicide case, the prosecution may offer the victim’s peacefulness to rebut any evidence that the victim was the first aggressor.

## Final 9 · Q9 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(a)(2): criminal cases only
In a civil battery suit, the defendant calls a witness to testify to the defendant's excellent reputation for peacefulness, offered to show he didn't strike the plaintiff. The plaintiff objects. What is the correct ruling?

A. Overruled, because a defendant may always offer a pertinent trait.
B. Overruled, because Rule 405(a) allows reputation testimony.
C. Sustained, because the defendant hasn't testified yet.
D. Overruled, because battery makes character an essential element.
E. None of the above.

Answer: E
- A: That exception, Rule 404(a)(2)(A), belongs to a defendant in a criminal case.
- B: Rule 405(a) governs the method of proof only after character is admissible.
- C: Testifying has no bearing here. Character evidence offered as propensity is barred either way.
- D: Peacefulness isn't an element of a battery claim, so Rule 405(b) doesn't apply.
- E: Correct. The testimony should be excluded. Rule 404(a)(1) bars character evidence to prove action in conformity, and the Rule 404(a)(2) exceptions apply only in criminal cases.
Rule: Only the accused can open the door to character under Rule 404(a)(2), and only in a criminal case. In civil cases the propensity ban holds.

## Final 9 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Olden: bias and Rule 412(b)(1)(C)
A defendant claims a complainant invented an assault to keep her fiancé from learning she was seeing someone else. The court bars all evidence of the other relationship under Rule 412. The fiancé's testimony is the only corroboration. On appeal, what is the strongest argument?

A. Her sexual history makes her less believable as a witness.
B. Reputation evidence is always admissible in a criminal trial.
C. Exclusion cut off proof of her motive to lie, which Rule 412(b)(1)(C) protects.
D. The evidence of her other relationship shows she was more likely to have consented.
E. Rule 412 doesn't apply in cases with a consent defense.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 412 rejects the old idea that unchastity shows untruthfulness.
- B: Rule 412(b)(1) has no reputation route in criminal cases.
- C: Correct. Olden holds the Confrontation Clause protects cross-examination showing a prototypical form of bias. Rule 412(b)(1)(C) admits evidence whose exclusion would violate constitutional rights.
- D: That is the propensity inference Rule 412 forbids.
- E: Rule 412 applies in proceedings involving alleged sexual misconduct, whatever the defense.
Rule: When exclusion under Rule 412 cuts off proof of a motive to fabricate, Rule 412(b)(1)(C) and the Confrontation Clause can require admission (Olden).

## Final 9 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Valencia: Rule 404(b) and Rule 609 are separate tests
A defendant is charged with distributing cocaine. In its case-in-chief, the government offered his three-year-old felony conviction for possessing cocaine for sale under Rule 404(b), and the judge excluded it under Rule 403. The defendant then testifies and denies the charge. On cross-examination, the government offers the same conviction under Rule 609(a)(1)(B). The defendant argues that because the conviction failed the more lenient 403 test, it must fail 609’s stricter test as well. How should the court respond?

A. Reject the argument and weigh the conviction anew, because 609 measures its bearing on truthfulness, not on guilt.
B. Exclude the conviction, because a drug conviction is never probative of truthfulness.
C. Exclude the conviction, because once the court finds evidence fails Rule 403, that ruling binds it for the rest of the trial.
D. Admit the conviction without weighing, because a testifying defendant’s felony convictions are automatically admissible.
E. Admit the conviction under ordinary Rule 403, because the defendant chose to testify.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. This is Problem 4.4 (United States v. Valencia, 8th Cir.). The 404(b)/403 ruling measured probative force on substantive guilt; the 609(a)(1)(B) ruling measures probative force on truthfulness. Losing one doesn’t dictate the other, and appellate courts defer to the trial judge on both. (Whether a drug conviction says much about honesty is fair to question.)
- B: “Never” overstates it. Under Rule 609(a)(1)(B), the court weighs probative value on truthfulness against prejudice using the Gordon factors, and a drug felony can survive that balance.
- C: An in limine or case-in-chief ruling under one rule doesn’t control a later ruling for a different purpose under another rule.
- D: Only crimen falsi convictions under 609(a)(2) come in without balancing. A testifying defendant’s other felonies go through 609(a)(1)(B)’s special balance.
- E: 403 governs non-defendant witnesses under 609(a)(1)(A). For an accused, the conviction comes in only if probative value outweighs prejudicial effect to the defendant.
Rule: Rule 609(a)(1)(B): a testifying accused’s felony comes in only if its probative value on truthfulness outweighs its prejudicial effect to him. That’s a separate question from the 404(b)/403 ruling (Valencia).

## Final 9 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — False accusations fall outside Rule 412
A defendant is charged with molesting his 11-year-old stepdaughter, and there is no physical evidence. The defense wants to cross-examine her about an accusation she made two years ago that a music teacher touched her, which a classmate says she later admitted she “made up to get out of lessons.” The prosecution moves to exclude the cross-examination under the rape-shield rule. How should the court analyze the motion?

A. Rule 412 applies, but the cross-examination fits its exception for another source of injury.
B. The defense may cross-examine and, if she denies making it up, may call the classmate to prove the lie under Rule 608(b).
C. Rule 412 doesn’t apply; the court asks whether a jury could find the accusation false, then applies Rules 403 and 608.
D. The defense may cross-examine only if the judge first finds by a preponderance that the earlier accusation was false.
E. Rule 412 bars the cross-examination, because the earlier accusation concerned sexual contact.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 412 doesn’t apply at all, because a false accusation isn’t sexual behavior (State v. Smith). And the source-of-injury exception concerns physical evidence, not credibility.
- B: 608(b) permits inquiry on cross but bars extrinsic evidence of specific instances offered to attack character for truthfulness. The defense must take her answer unless some other theory, such as bias, applies.
- C: Correct. State v. Smith held that past false allegations aren’t “past sexual behavior,” so the shield rule doesn’t bar them; the ACN to FRE 412 agrees. The question is impeachment, governed by 608(b) and 403. The threshold is Huddleston’s: whether reasonable jurors could find she made a false accusation, not whether the judge believes it was false (Problem 5.4).
- D: That is what the Smith trial judge effectively did, and it was error. The judge doesn’t decide falsity; she asks only whether a jury could reasonably find it.
- E: 412 bars evidence offered to prove a victim’s other sexual behavior or predisposition. A false accusation is offered to show she lies about such matters, not that she engaged in sexual behavior.
Rule: Prior false accusations aren’t “sexual behavior” (Smith; ACN), so they fall outside 412 and are governed by 608(b) and 403. Threshold: could a jury reasonably find the accusation false?

## Final 9 · Q13 · Definition of Hearsay — Rule 801(a): unintended implied assertions (United States v. Zenni)
Federal agents execute a search warrant at Donnelly’s apartment, which the government contends is the hub of an illegal sports-betting business. During the two-hour search, the apartment’s landline rings eleven times. Agent Ruiz answers each call. Each caller, unprompted, says something like “Put two hundred on the Eagles, minus three” or “Give me the over on the Sixers.” None of the callers is identified.

At Donnelly’s trial for operating an illegal gambling business, the government calls Agent Ruiz to recount the calls to prove that the apartment was being used to accept bets. Donnelly objects on hearsay grounds.

How should the court rule?

A. Overrule the objection, because each call is a present sense impression describing the caller’s own conduct while he was engaged in it.
B. Overrule the objection, but only if the court finds the callers unavailable, because each caller’s attempt to place an illegal bet was a statement against penal interest.
C. Sustain the objection, because the callers’ words are offered to prove their belief that bets could be placed at the apartment, and that belief is probative only if it is accurate.
D. Overrule it, because the callers didn’t intend to assert that the apartment took bets, so their words aren’t offered for anything they asserted.
E. Sustain the objection unless the government produces at least one caller for cross-examination, because the sincerity of anonymous declarants cannot otherwise be tested.

Answer: D
- A: A present sense impression must describe or explain an event or condition. A betting instruction describes nothing, and no exception is needed for words that are not hearsay in the first place.
- B: Rule 804(b)(3) requires unavailability and, in a criminal case, corroborating circumstances. More basically, it presupposes hearsay. The calls are not hearsay, so the exception is beside the point.
- C: This is the common-law reasoning of Wright v. Doe d. Tatham, which treated implied assertions as hearsay. The Federal Rules rejected that approach by defining “statement” by reference to the declarant’s intent to assert.
- D: Correct. The callers did not intend to assert that the apartment was a betting operation, so their utterances are not hearsay when offered to prove it.
- E: Nothing in the hearsay rule requires producing a declarant whose words are not hearsay. Anonymity can bear on weight or Rule 403, but it does not turn non-hearsay into hearsay.
Rule: A “statement” is a person’s oral or written assertion, or nonverbal conduct the person intended as an assertion. FRE 801(a). Hearsay is a statement offered to prove the truth of the matter asserted in it. FRE 801(c). Under the Federal Rules, the declarant’s intent controls: words or conduct not intended to assert the fact they are offered to prove are not hearsay, even when they reveal the declarant’s belief in that fact.

## Final 9 · Q14 · Definition of Hearsay — Effect on the listener, and the limit on that use
At a drug trial, Officer Grey testifies that he stopped the defendant's car because the dispatcher radioed, "Be on the lookout for a red Camaro, plate ending 42, driver is carrying cocaine for the Southside crew." The prosecution offers the dispatch to explain why Grey stopped the car. In closing, the prosecutor plans to argue that the dispatch shows the defendant was a Southside courier.

What is the best ruling?

A. Admit the dispatch for all purposes, because it explains the investigation.
B. Exclude any mention of the dispatch, because it is hearsay.
C. Admit it for all purposes as the dispatcher's present sense impression.
D. Admit only what explains the stop, not its truth.
E. Admit it all, because the defendant can cross-examine Officer Grey.

Answer: D
- A: Explaining the investigation is a non-truth use. But the prosecutor's planned argument uses it for its truth, which is hearsay (and testimonial).
- B: For the limited purpose of explaining why Grey acted, the dispatch isn't hearsay. The problem is scope, not admissibility of any reference.
- C: The dispatcher was relaying information, not describing an event she perceived.
- D: Correct, though the prosecution wants more. Words offered for their effect on the listener aren't hearsay. But the accusation's only real value is its truth. Rule 403 and Rule 105 call for admitting no more than the background needed and barring use for the truth. That use would also raise a confrontation problem.
- E: Cross of Grey can't test the dispatcher's or the informant's knowledge.
Rule: A statement offered to explain a listener's conduct isn't hearsay, but the court should admit only what that purpose requires and bar argument from its truth (Rules 105 and 403). Accusatory background can become hearsay, and testimonial, if used for its truth.

## Final 9 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(2)(D): a fired employee’s statement
Paloma sues Ridgeline Freight, Inc. and its former driver, Hatch, for injuries suffered when a Ridgeline tractor-trailer that Hatch was driving on Ridgeline business ran a red light and struck her car. Ridgeline fired Hatch the morning after the crash. That afternoon, a newspaper reporter visited Hatch at home, and Hatch said: “Look, it was on me. I was texting dispatch and never saw the light change.”

Hatch and Ridgeline are tried together. Paloma calls the reporter to repeat Hatch’s statement. Both defendants object.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit it against both defendants, because the statement concerned a matter, the operation of the truck, that was within the scope of Hatch’s employment.
B. Admit it against both defendants as a statement against interest, because it exposed Hatch to civil liability.
C. Exclude it entirely, because by the time Hatch spoke he had been fired, so the statement could not have been against his interest when made.
D. Admit it against Hatch only and, on request, instruct the jury not to consider it against Ridgeline.
E. Exclude it as to Hatch, because a party’s statement is admissible only if made on personal knowledge, and a driver who “never saw” the light change cannot know when it changed.

Answer: D
- A: The scope-of-employment element is satisfied, but the timing element is not. Rule 801(d)(2)(D) requires the statement to be made while the employment relationship existed, and Hatch had already been fired.
- B: Rule 804(b)(3) applies only if the declarant is unavailable. Hatch is a party sitting in the courtroom. And an against-interest statement would not be binding on Ridgeline as an admission in any event.
- C: Opposing-party statements need not be against interest when made. That requirement belongs to Rule 804(b)(3). The termination matters only to whether the statement can be attributed to Ridgeline.
- D: Correct. The statement is Hatch’s own admission, but it cannot be attributed to Ridgeline because it was made after the employment ended.
- E: The advisory committee notes free party admissions from the firsthand-knowledge requirement. Hatch also had knowledge of what matters most here: he knew he was texting and not watching the light.
Rule: A party’s own statement is not hearsay when offered against that party. FRE 801(d)(2)(A). A statement by a party’s agent or employee is attributable to the party only if it concerns a matter within the scope of the relationship and was made “while it existed.” FRE 801(d)(2)(D). When evidence is admissible against one party but not another, the court must, on timely request, restrict it to its proper scope. FRE 105.

## Final 9 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(1)(A) as amended December 1, 2026
Dorsey is charged with murdering Vic and claims self-defense. Two days after the shooting, eyewitness Wren signed an unsworn statement for a detective: “Vic’s hands were empty and raised when Dorsey shot him.” The case is filed in January 2027.

At trial, Wren testifies that Vic lunged at Dorsey with a knife. The prosecutor asks Wren about the statement, and Wren denies making it. The prosecution then offers the statement through the detective.

Under Rule 801(d)(1)(A) as amended effective December 1, 2026, how should the court rule?

A. Admit it as substantive evidence, because it is inconsistent with Wren’s testimony and Wren is subject to cross-examination about it.
B. Admit it as substantive evidence, and instruct the jury that it is sufficient by itself to support a conviction.
C. Exclude the detective’s testimony, because Rule 613(b) bars extrinsic evidence once a witness denies a prior statement.
D. Admit it only to impeach Wren, with a limiting instruction, because it was not given under penalty of perjury in a proceeding.
E. Exclude it, because a witness who denies making a statement is not subject to cross-examination about it.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. The amended rule requires only a testifying declarant, cross-examination about the statement, and inconsistency.
- B: The Committee Note says the rule governs admissibility, not sufficiency.
- C: Rule 613(b) requires only that the witness first get a chance to explain or deny the statement. Wren had that chance, and the amendment leaves 613(b) unchanged.
- D: That is the rule before December 1, 2026. The amendment deletes the oath-and-proceeding requirement, and the Committee Note says no limiting instruction is needed.
- E: The Committee Note rejects this premise: there is effective cross-examination in the very denial, citing Nelson v. O’Neil.
Rule: As amended effective December 1, 2026, a prior statement is not hearsay if the declarant testifies and is subject to cross-examination about it and the statement is inconsistent with the declarant’s testimony. No oath or proceeding is required. Rule 613(b) still governs extrinsic evidence of the statement. FRE 801(d)(1)(A); FRE 613(b).

## Final 9 · Q17 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(a): who is unavailable
Which declarant is NOT unavailable under Rule 804(a)?

A. One who refuses to testify in spite of a court order to do so.
B. One who is excused from testifying by a court's privilege ruling.
C. One who takes the stand and testifies to not remembering the subject.
D. One who has died or is too ill to appear at the trial or hearing.
E. One whom the proponent paid to leave the country to avoid testifying.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 804(a)(2) covers this declarant.
- B: Rule 804(a)(1) covers this declarant.
- C: Rule 804(a)(3) covers this declarant.
- D: Rule 804(a)(4) covers this declarant.
- E: Correct. Rule 804(a) doesn't apply if the proponent procured or wrongfully caused the unavailability in order to prevent the declarant from testifying.
Rule: Rule 804(a) lists privilege, refusal, lack of memory, death or illness, and absence despite reasonable efforts. A proponent who wrongfully caused the absence can't rely on it.

## Final 9 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(6): forfeiture
Before trial, the defendant has a friend threaten a witness, who then flees. The prosecution offers the witness's earlier recorded statement to police. Who decides forfeiture, and under what standard?

A. No one decides, because threats automatically forfeit the objection.
B. The judge decides, by a preponderance under Rule 104(a).
C. The jury decides, if the evidence could support a finding.
D. The judge decides, by clear and convincing evidence.
E. The jury decides, using proof beyond a reasonable doubt.

Answer: B
- A: The proponent must prove the wrongdoing and the intent.
- B: Correct. The proponent proves the wrongdoing and the intent by a preponderance under Rule 104(a). Rule 804(b)(6) reaches a party that caused or acquiesced in the unavailability, intending that result.
- C: That is Rule 104(b). Hearsay foundations fall under Rule 104(a).
- D: The standard is a preponderance.
- E: Forfeiture is a preliminary question for the judge.
Rule: Forfeiture under Rule 804(b)(6) requires wrongdoing, or acquiescence in it, intended to make the declarant unavailable, proved by a preponderance under Rule 104(a). The same intent forfeits confrontation (Giles).

## Final 9 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(4): a doctor consulted only for litigation
Rennick sues a delivery company for injuries suffered when one of its vans rear-ended her car. A month before trial, at her lawyer’s request, Rennick sees Dr. Abbas, an orthopedist retained only to evaluate her and testify. He will provide no treatment.

Rennick tells Dr. Abbas: “My lower back has had a shooting pain down my left leg ever since the delivery van slammed into me from behind. The driver was staring at his phone and never even braked.” At trial, Rennick offers Dr. Abbas’s testimony repeating her statement.

What is the proper ruling?

A. Admit only the description of her present pain, because statements about past symptoms and medical history are excluded.
B. Admit the entire statement, because everything Rennick described concerned the cause of her injuries.
C. Exclude the entire statement, because Rennick is a party and may not offer her own out-of-court statements.
D. Exclude the entire statement, because statements to a physician consulted only for litigation are not covered by the medical-diagnosis exception.
E. Admit her pain and that a van hit her from behind, but exclude that the driver was on his phone and never braked.

Answer: E
- A: The rule expressly covers medical history and past symptoms.
- B: General cause is covered; fault is not. The driver’s distraction is not pertinent to diagnosing a back injury.
- C: Rule 801(d)(2) is unavailable to Rennick for her own statement, but a party may offer her own statement under any applicable hearsay exception, including 803(4).
- D: The Federal Rules cover statements made for diagnosis alone, including to a doctor retained to testify.
- E: Correct. General cause comes in; statements about fault do not.
Rule: A statement made for, and reasonably pertinent to, medical diagnosis or treatment that describes medical history, past or present symptoms or sensations, their inception, or their general cause is admissible. FRE 803(4).

## Final 9 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(8) limits can't be evaded through Rule 803(6)
At a federal assault trial, the officer who arrested the defendant has died. The prosecution offers his written incident report, which records his observations at the scene: the defendant standing over the victim with a bloody pipe. The records custodian testifies that the department routinely prepares such reports. The prosecution argues the report is a business record under Rule 803(6).

What is the best ruling?

A. Admit it, because the report meets every element of Rule 803(6).
B. Admit it, because Rules 803(6) and 803(8) are independent exceptions.
C. Admit it, because the officer who wrote it has died and is unavailable.
D. Exclude it, because Rule 803(8)'s limit can't be evaded through Rule 803(6).
E. Exclude it as testimonial, though the hearsay rule would allow it.

Answer: D
- A: The report may satisfy Rule 803(6)'s elements on their face, which is what makes this tricky. Courts refuse to let it be used to evade Rule 803(8)'s specific limit.
- B: Generally true, but not when one exception's specific restriction would be defeated.
- C: Unavailability doesn't create a hearsay exception. And the Rule 803 exceptions don't depend on it.
- D: Correct. Rule 803(8)(A)(ii) excludes, in a criminal case, matters observed by law-enforcement personnel. The outline notes that police reports offered against a defendant can't evade that limit through Rule 803(6). (The report would also raise Confrontation Clause problems.)
- E: The hearsay ruling itself is wrong: the report fails the hearsay rule because of Rule 803(8). The confrontation problem is an additional ground.
Rule: Rule 803(8)(A)(ii) bars use, against a criminal defendant, of matters observed by law-enforcement personnel. The prosecution can't evade it by offering the same report under Rule 803(6).

## Final 9 · Q21 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 807: near misses
A hardware supplier's sales ledger narrowly fails Rule 803(6) because its sole owner has died and no qualified witness can lay the foundation. The ledger's entries are corroborated by bank deposits, and it is the best available proof of the sales. The proponent gave reasonable written notice. Can it come in under Rule 807?

A. No, because a statement that narrowly misses an exception can't use Rule 807.
B. Yes, because the owner's death by itself satisfies Rule 804(b) and so Rule 807.
C. Yes, if it is trustworthy and the most probative evidence reasonably obtainable.
D. No, because Rule 807 applies only to statements of unavailable public officials.
E. Yes, because any hearsay that seems reliable to the judge is admissible.

Answer: C
- A: The 2019 amendment ended the near-miss debate. Rule 807 asks whether the statement is not admissible under Rule 803 or 804.
- B: Death shows unavailability only. It satisfies no exception.
- C: Correct. Rule 807(a) requires sufficient guarantees of trustworthiness and that the statement be more probative than other evidence obtainable with reasonable efforts. Near misses qualify (United States v. Laster).
- D: Rule 807 has no such limit.
- E: Rule 807(a) has two requirements, and courts use it rarely. General reliability isn't enough.
Rule: Rule 807(a) requires trustworthiness, considering the totality of circumstances and corroboration, and comparative necessity. Rule 807(b) requires reasonable written notice. A near miss may still be considered.

## Final 9 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Nontestimonial private remark
Facts: Nadia Voss is charged in federal court with burning down a warehouse she had insured. Investigators found a homemade chemical timer of an unusual design at the scene. The government calls Joel Tran, who would testify that his late mother, a nurse at a burn clinic, told him over dinner six years ago: "A patient named Voss came in with burned hands today. She told me she got them testing a chemical timer she built for a warehouse job." The defense moves to exclude the testimony.
Set aside the hearsay rules. Does the Confrontation Clause bar the nurse's remark to her son?

A. Yes, because medical professionals routinely report suspicious injuries to the authorities.
B. Probably not, because a private remark isn't a substitute for trial testimony.
C. Yes, because the nurse is dead and was never cross-examined by Voss.
D. No, because the Clause doesn't apply in federal prosecutions.
E. No, because Voss herself made the original statement to the nurse.

Answer: B
- A: The remark to her son wasn't a report, and reporting duties don't make someone police (Ohio v. Clark).
- B: Correct. Under Ohio v. Clark, a statement to a family member with no primary purpose of creating trial testimony is nontestimonial, so only the hearsay rules apply (Whorton).
- C: Unavailability and prior cross matter only for testimonial statements.
- D: The Sixth Amendment applies directly in federal prosecutions.
- E: The question concerns the nurse's own remark, a separate statement.
Rule: Informal remarks to acquaintances are nontestimonial. Nontestimonial statements face only the hearsay rules (Crawford, Whorton).

## Final 9 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Rule 106: a later denial doesn't complete an admission
At a robbery trial, the prosecution plays part of Dean Wolfe's recorded police interview: "Yeah, I was at First Federal that afternoon." Two hours later in the same interview, Wolfe had said, "I didn't rob anybody. You've got the wrong guy." The defense asks the court to require that the denial be played immediately under Rule 106. The prosecution objects that it is self-serving hearsay.

What is the best ruling?

A. Play it now, because Rule 106 now overrides a hearsay objection to any other part.
B. Play it now, but only for context, with an instruction not to use it for its truth.
C. Decline, because the denial only contradicts the admission; it corrects no misimpression.
D. Play it now, because since 2023 Rule 106 applies to oral and recorded statements.
E. Decline, because Rule 106 never permits the introduction of hearsay statements.

Answer: C
- A: The amendment lets completion overcome a hearsay objection only when fairness requires it, because the remainder corrects a misimpression.
- B: Completion comes in only if fairness requires it. There is no misimpression to put in context.
- C: Correct. Rule 106 is narrow: the remainder must be needed to correct a misimpression. "I was at the bank" isn't made misleading by omitting a general denial of guilt hours later. The ACN, citing a 2019 Second Circuit decision, says a denial doesn't come in merely because it contradicts an admission.
- D: Rule 106 does now cover oral statements, but the threshold of fairness still applies.
- E: Since 2023, Rule 106 completion may be admitted "over a hearsay objection" when fairness requires it.
Rule: Rule 106 (as amended in 2023) covers oral statements and overrides hearsay objections, but only for a remainder that in fairness ought to be considered to correct a misimpression. A general denial doesn't complete an admission (ACN, citing the Second Circuit).

## Final 9 · Q24 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 701: an experienced user identifies a drug
At Ray Tolan's trial for distributing cocaine, the drugs were never recovered. The government calls Gina Pace, who bought from Tolan. She testifies that she used cocaine almost daily for six years, knows its look, smell, and numbing effect, and that what Tolan sold her looked, smelled, and felt like the cocaine she had always used. She has no scientific training. Tolan objects that whether a substance is cocaine can be proved only by chemical analysis or expert testimony.

How should the court rule?

A. Overrule the objection, because the drug's identity is an ultimate issue that Rule 704(a) leaves to any witness.
B. Overrule the objection, because a lay witness with enough familiarity may identify a drug from her own perception.
C. Overrule the objection, but only if Pace first qualifies as an expert by experience under Rule 702.
D. Sustain the objection, because identifying a controlled substance is always specialized knowledge under Rule 701(c).
E. Sustain the objection, because without the drugs there's no evidence that Pace has personal knowledge.

Answer: B
- A: Rule 704(a) only removes the ultimate-issue objection. A witness still needs a proper basis under Rule 701 or 702, which Pace has here.
- B: Correct. The 2000 Advisory Committee Note to Rule 701 approves cases letting a lay witness with enough experience identify a drug as cocaine. Pace's opinion rests on her own perceptions, helps the jury, and draws on everyday familiarity rather than specialized training.
- C: Experience can qualify an expert, but Pace needn't be one. Her testimony fits Rule 701 because it rests on her own perceptions and everyday familiarity.
- D: Not always. The Committee Note distinguishes reasoning “familiar in everyday life” from specialized knowledge, and it approves lay identification of drugs by experienced users.
- E: Personal knowledge under Rule 602 can be shown by the witness's own testimony. Pace bought and used the drugs herself, which is ample foundation.
Rule: Rule 701 admits lay opinion based on perception and everyday experience. A witness familiar with a drug through long use may identify it as a lay witness (Advisory Committee Note, 2000); specialized knowledge triggers Rule 702.

## Final 9 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 702: qualified by experience
In a cargo-injury suit, the plaintiff offers a retired dockworker with no degree but 30 years of supervising cargo stacking. He would opine on how pallets on this dock should have been stacked. The defense objects that he has no formal training. What is the best analysis?

A. He may testify, because qualification goes to weight and the judge doesn't screen it.
B. He can't testify, because Rule 702 requires formal scientific training.
C. He may testify on any subject touching ports and shipping, because he qualifies as an expert generally.
D. He may qualify by experience for this opinion, subject to the other Rule 702 requirements.
E. He can't testify, because a lay witness may not give opinions at all.

Answer: D
- A: Qualification is a Rule 104(a) question for the judge.
- B: Rule 702 lists knowledge, skill, experience, training, or education.
- C: Qualification is topic-specific, not a general license.
- D: Correct. Rule 702 allows qualification by experience, and qualification is specific to the opinion offered. The proponent must also meet the remaining requirements.
- E: He is offered as an expert, and lay witnesses may give opinions under Rule 701 anyway.
Rule: Experience can qualify an expert under Rule 702, but qualification is topic-specific, and it is a Rule 104(a) question for the judge.

## Final 9 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Kumho Tire: non-scientific experts
An insurer defends a claim with a roofer with 30 years' experience who concludes from a visual inspection that wear, not hail, damaged a roof. The policyholder challenges the method. The insurer responds that Daubert applies only to scientists. How should the court proceed?

A. Admit the testimony, because experience-based experts are outside the gatekeeping duty.
B. Exclude the testimony, because experience alone can never qualify a witness.
C. Apply general acceptance in the roofing trade as the only test.
D. Apply every Daubert factor mechanically, and exclude if any one is missing.
E. Serve as gatekeeper, using the Daubert factors where they reasonably measure reliability.

Answer: E
- A: Kumho Tire applies the gatekeeping duty to all expert testimony, including technical and experience-based.
- B: Rule 702 lists experience as a basis for qualifying. Experience can qualify a witness.
- C: Daubert held that Frye's general-acceptance test didn't survive the Federal Rules. Acceptance is only one factor.
- D: The Daubert factors are non-exclusive and flexible. The judge decides how to test reliability.
- E: Correct. Under Kumho Tire, the gatekeeping duty applies to all expert testimony, and the judge may use the Daubert factors where they fit. The question is whether this method can reliably answer this question.
Rule: Daubert's gatekeeping applies to all expert testimony (Kumho Tire). The factors are flexible, and the proponent must meet Rule 702 by a preponderance.

## Final 9 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 902(6) self-authentication isn't a hearsay exception
In a 2026 slip-and-fall case against Corner Mart, the plaintiff offers a 2022 local newspaper article. It reports that city inspectors cited the store for "standing water near the coolers on several occasions." She offers it to prove that the store had a recurring water problem. The paper's masthead and date appear on the clipping. The store objects.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit it, because a newspaper is self-authenticating under Rule 902(6).
B. Exclude it, because a newspaper must be authenticated by testimony from its publisher.
C. Admit it under the ancient-documents exception, as a dated news article.
D. Exclude it as hearsay, even though the article is self-authenticating.
E. Admit it as a public record of what the city inspectors found.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 902(6) satisfies authentication only. The article must still pass the hearsay rule.
- B: Under Rule 902(6), printed material purporting to be a newspaper needs no extrinsic evidence of authenticity.
- C: Rule 803(16) covers only documents prepared before January 1, 1998.
- D: Correct. Rule 902(6) makes the article self-authenticating, but authentication is only the first gate. Offered to prove recurring water, it is the reporter's hearsay about what inspectors found, with another layer from the inspectors themselves. No exception covers the article.
- E: The newspaper isn't a record of a public office. The city's own inspection records might qualify under Rule 803(8), but the reporter's account of them doesn't.
Rule: Under Rule 902, self-authenticating items need no extrinsic proof of genuineness, but they must still satisfy the hearsay rule and the best evidence rule. A modern newspaper offered for its truth is hearsay.

## Final 9 · Q28 · Privileges — Psychotherapist privilege: threats the patient knew would be relayed
Gene Rusk, in court-ordered therapy, repeatedly told his psychologist he would “take out” the claims adjuster handling his disability benefits if his payments were cut. At the first session the psychologist had explained, and Rusk had acknowledged in writing, that she would warn anyone he threatened. She warned the adjuster, and Rusk was charged with making threats. At trial the government calls the psychologist to repeat his statements. Rusk invokes the psychotherapist-patient privilege.

How should the court rule?

A. Reject the privilege, because Rusk knew his threats would be passed on, so they weren't confidential.
B. Uphold the privilege unless the court finds, by balancing, that the government's need outweighs Rusk's privacy.
C. Uphold the privilege, because a duty to warn permits a warning but never testimony in a prosecution.
D. Reject the privilege, because federal courts have never recognized a psychotherapist-patient privilege.
E. Uphold the privilege, because Jaffee recognized an absolute privilege for statements made in psychotherapy.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. The psychotherapist-patient privilege covers only confidential communications. Rusk was told, and acknowledged, that threats would be passed on to their targets, so he couldn't have expected these statements to stay confidential. The Fifth Circuit so held in a case like this one.
- B: Jaffee rejected case-by-case balancing: making the privilege depend on a later weighing of need would eviscerate its effectiveness.
- C: That is the strongest argument for the privilege, and it has support. But on similar facts the Fifth Circuit held the threats unprivileged because the patient knew they would be relayed.
- D: Jaffee v. Redmond recognized the psychotherapist-patient privilege under Rule 501 and extended it to licensed clinical social workers.
- E: Jaffee protects confidential communications made in the course of psychotherapy. Confidentiality is an element, and a statement the patient knows will be disclosed lacks it.
Rule: The psychotherapist-patient privilege (Jaffee) protects confidential communications made in the course of psychotherapy, without case-by-case balancing. A statement the patient knows will be disclosed is not confidential.

## Final 9 · Q29 · Privileges — Rule 502(b): inadvertent disclosure
In a federal suit, a company produces 20,000 pages and one privileged memo slips through. Its lawyers learn of this three months later, send one clawback letter, and do nothing for two years. The company offers only an unsworn statement that a paralegal reviewed the documents. The plaintiff argues waiver. What is the most likely result?

A. No waiver, because any inadvertent disclosure preserves the privilege no matter what the holder did next.
B. Waiver, because any production to an opponent waives the privilege.
C. No waiver, because the plaintiff bears the burden of proving waiver.
D. Waiver, because the company didn't show reasonable steps to prevent and rectify.
E. No waiver, because a clawback letter ends the inquiry under Rule 502.

Answer: D
- A: Inadvertence is only the first of three requirements in Rule 502(b).
- B: Rule 502(b) protects qualifying inadvertent disclosures, so production alone doesn't waive.
- C: Under Williams v. District, the holder bears the burden on each element.
- D: Correct. As in Williams v. District, the holder must show inadvertence, reasonable steps to prevent disclosure, and prompt steps to rectify. Unsworn assertions and one letter followed by silence fall short.
- E: Rule 502(b)(3) requires prompt, reasonable steps. A single letter followed by silence doesn't meet it.
Rule: Under Rule 502(b), inadvertent disclosure doesn't waive if the holder took reasonable steps to prevent it and promptly took reasonable steps to rectify it. The holder bears the burden (Williams v. District).

## Final 9 · Q30 · Privileges — Marital confidences: Trammel doesn't let the spouse waive
Federal prosecutors charge Ian Holt with tax evasion. His wife, Mira, is willing to testify against him, and they remain married. The prosecution wants her to testify that (1) one night at home Ian told her privately, "I'm hiding income in the Cayman account. Don't tell anyone," and (2) she saw him shredding bank statements in the garage. Mira had no part in the scheme. Ian claims privilege as to both.

What is the correct ruling?

A. Mira may testify to both, because under Trammel the witness-spouse alone decides.
B. Mira may testify to both, because communications about a crime aren't privileged.
C. Mira may testify to neither, because Ian holds the spousal testimonial privilege.
D. Ian may block (1) as a marital confidence, but Mira may testify to (2).
E. Ian may block (1), but only if he and Mira are divorced before trial.

Answer: D
- A: Trammel governs the adverse-testimony privilege. The separate marital-confidences privilege lets either spouse prevent disclosure of a confidential marital communication.
- B: The joint-crime exception requires the holder's complicity (United States v. Rakes). Mira wasn't a participant.
- C: Under Trammel, the testimonial privilege belongs to the witness-spouse, and Mira waives it by choosing to testify.
- D: Correct. The marital-confidences privilege covers confidential communications during a valid marriage, and either spouse may prevent disclosure. Because Mira wasn't complicit, the joint-crime exception doesn't apply (Rakes). Observations of conduct aren't communications. Mira holds and waives the testimonial privilege, so she may describe the shredding.
- E: The confidences privilege survives divorce. It doesn't depend on it.
Rule: Two spousal privileges: the testimonial privilege (the witness-spouse holds it, in criminal cases, during a valid marriage: Trammel) and the confidences privilege (either spouse, for confidential communications during the marriage, surviving divorce, with joint-crime and crimes-against-family exceptions: Rakes).

## Final 10 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Warger: prove voir dire dishonesty without deliberations
In voir dire for a medical-malpractice trial, the court asked whether any juror had a family member harmed by a doctor's error. Juror Dana Pike said no. After a defense verdict, another juror tells the plaintiff's lawyer that during deliberations Pike described how a surgeon's mistake had killed her father, and urged the others not to "ruin a doctor's life over an accident." The plaintiff's investigator also finds Pike's public social-media posts from before trial blaming her father's surgeon for his death.

What is the plaintiff's best path to a new trial?

A. Offer the juror's affidavit, because a lie in voir dire is extraneous information.
B. Offer the juror's affidavit, because Rule 606(b) yields whenever a juror was biased.
C. Use only the posts to show a dishonest voir dire answer.
D. Call Pike to testify about how her father's death affected her vote.
E. None, because Rule 606(b) bars all challenges based on a juror's bias.

Answer: C
- A: Warger held that a juror's own experiences aren't extraneous information, and that Rule 606(b) bars deliberation testimony even when offered to show voir dire dishonesty.
- B: The only constitutional exception is for clear racial bias in a criminal case (Peña-Rodriguez).
- C: Correct. The juror's affidavit is the more direct proof, but it can't be used. Under Warger, a party may still get a new trial by showing that a juror failed to answer honestly a material voir dire question and that a correct answer would have supported a challenge for cause. That must be proved with evidence outside deliberations, like the pre-trial posts.
- D: Rule 606(b)(1) bars testimony about the effect of anything on a juror's vote.
- E: The voir dire route remains open with evidence that isn't barred by Rule 606(b).
Rule: Warger: Rule 606(b) bars deliberation testimony even to show voir dire dishonesty, and a juror's own experiences aren't extraneous information. A dishonest material answer that would have supported a for-cause challenge must be proved with other evidence.

## Final 10 · Q2 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 401: a brick is not a wall
Rita Coe is charged with poisoning her husband with thallium. The prosecution offers a receipt showing that, two years before his death, Coe bought a rat poison that contained thallium until it was reformulated a year later. Coe objects that the purchase was too long ago and that thousands of people bought that product.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it as irrelevant, because a purchase two years earlier proves nothing.
B. Admit it, because it makes it somewhat more probable that she had access to thallium.
C. Exclude it under Rule 404(b), because it shows a propensity to use poison.
D. Exclude it unless the prosecution proves she still had the poison later.
E. Admit it, but only if other evidence also connects Coe to the death.

Answer: B
- A: This conflates relevance with sufficiency. Under the ACN, "a brick is not a wall": evidence need not prove the point by itself.
- B: Correct, though it's a weak link in the chain. Rule 401 requires only "any tendency" to make access to the poison more probable. Remoteness and the product's popularity go to weight, and Rule 403 weighs any unfair prejudice, which seems modest here.
- C: Buying a household product isn't a bad act offered to show character.
- D: Rule 104(b) might matter if relevance depended on a specific fact, but access remains more probable even if she later discarded it. Retention goes to weight.
- E: Each item of evidence is judged for relevance on its own. Admission doesn't depend on other evidence.
Rule: Rule 401's threshold is low: any tendency to make a fact of consequence more or less probable. Weakness and remoteness go to weight and to Rule 403, not to relevance.

## Final 10 · Q3 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Collins: assumed probabilities
No eyewitness saw the face of a hit-and-run driver. Witnesses said the vehicle was a red pickup with a dog in the cab and a ponytailed male driver. The defendant owns a red pickup and a dog and wears a ponytail. The prosecution calls a statistics professor. Using estimates the prosecutor supplied, with no supporting studies (1 in 20 pickups are red, 1 in 8 have a dog, 1 in 10 drivers have a ponytail), the professor multiplies them. She testifies that the chance a random truck shares all three traits is 1 in 1,600. The defense objects.

What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admit it, because probability evidence is admissible whenever an expert does the math correctly.
B. Admit it, because the defense can expose any weakness in the numbers on cross-examination.
C. Exclude it, because probability evidence is never admissible to prove a person's identity.
D. Admit it, with an instruction that the jury may give the figure whatever weight it deserves.
E. Exclude it, because the numbers were assumed and the traits weren't shown to be independent.

Answer: E
- A: Correct arithmetic on invented inputs produces a precise-sounding number with no basis.
- B: People v. Collins reasoned that an impressive expert figure the defense can't refute is exactly what distracts a jury. Cross-examination doesn't cure a number with no foundation.
- C: Probability evidence built on real data, such as DNA population frequencies, is admissible.
- D: An instruction doesn't supply the missing foundation.
- E: Correct. Collins reversed because the probabilities were simply assumed and the factors weren't independent (a ponytail and a dog in a pickup may well correlate). The figure also risked overwhelming the jury. Even an accurate figure wouldn't show that this defendant was the driver.
Rule: Under Collins, probability evidence needs real data and independent factors. Assumed numbers multiplied together lack a foundation and invite overvaluation, which is unfair prejudice under Rule 403.

## Final 10 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 408: no impeachment, but bias is allowed
During settlement talks in a product suit, the defendant's chief engineer says, “Between us, the brake design was rushed.” At trial he testifies the design was thoroughly tested. Separately, a former co-defendant settled with the plaintiff for $200,000 and then testified for the plaintiff. Which use is permitted?

A. The co-defendant's settlement may show bias, but the engineer can't be impeached with his statement.
B. Both uses are permitted, because impeachment is not an attempt to prove the claim's validity.
C. The engineer may be impeached, because he is a witness rather than a party to the negotiations.
D. The engineer may be impeached, because a side remark made “between us” wasn't part of the formal negotiations.
E. Neither use is permitted, because 408 bars all evidence of compromise for every purpose.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. 408(a) bars impeachment by prior inconsistent statement (since 2006), while 408(b) allows proof of a witness's bias or prejudice.
- B: 408 expressly bars impeachment by prior inconsistent statement or contradiction.
- C: The statement was made during the party's compromise negotiations; the bar applies.
- D: A remark made during settlement talks is a statement made during compromise negotiations, however informal. Rule 408(a) bars using it to impeach by a prior inconsistent statement or contradiction.
- E: 408(b) allows other purposes such as bias.
Rule: Rule 408(a) bars compromise offers and statements made during negotiations when offered to prove or disprove a disputed claim or to impeach by a prior inconsistent statement or contradiction. Rule 408(b) allows them for another purpose, such as proving a witness's bias.

## Final 10 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 411: insurance to show bias
A cyclist sues a delivery-van driver for negligence. The defense calls an accident-reconstruction expert who testifies that the cyclist ran a stop sign. On cross-examination, the plaintiff seeks to ask the expert whether he has been retained and paid in more than forty cases by the driver’s liability insurer, which is funding the defense. The defense objects under Rule 411. How should the court rule?

A. Sustain the objection, because who pays an expert is irrelevant once the expert is qualified.
B. Sustain the objection, because Rule 411 bars any mention of liability insurance in front of the jury.
C. Overrule the objection, because the jury is entitled to know the defendant can pay any judgment.
D. Sustain the objection, because a witness’s credibility may be attacked only by reputation or opinion under Rule 608.
E. Overrule the objection, because Rule 411 permits insurance evidence offered to show a witness’s bias.

Answer: E
- A: Who pays an expert, and how often, is classic bias evidence. Rule 411 expressly allows insurance evidence offered to prove a witness’s bias, subject to Rule 403.
- B: 411’s second sentence expressly allows insurance evidence for other purposes, including proving a witness’s bias or prejudice, agency, ownership, or control.
- C: That is the forbidden inference: insurance used to suggest that liability or a large award is painless. The evidence comes in only for bias, and the defense may get an instruction saying so.
- D: 608 governs attacks on character for truthfulness. Bias is a separate, non-character mode of impeachment that may be shown on cross and by extrinsic evidence (Abel).
- E: Correct. 411 bars insurance evidence only when offered to prove that a person acted negligently or otherwise wrongfully. An expert’s ongoing financial relationship with the insurer paying for the defense is classic bias, and here, unlike the adjuster in Lacher v. Anderson, it has real probative force.
Rule: Rule 411: insurance can’t prove negligence or wrongdoing, but may show bias, agency, ownership, or control (subject to 403).

## Final 10 · Q6 · Character Evidence — Doctrine of chances: repeated “accidents”
A home health aide is charged with murdering an elderly diabetic patient with an insulin overdose. She testifies that the patient must have injected himself twice by mistake. After giving pretrial notice, the prosecution offers evidence that in the past three years two other patients in the aide’s care suffered unexplained insulin overdoses, one of them fatal. She was never charged in either incident. How should the court rule on her objection?

A. Exclude it, because the doctrine of chances still asks the jury to infer that she is the kind of person who harms patients.
B. Admit it as habit evidence, because three overdoses show her routine practice in giving insulin.
C. Exclude it, because she was never charged or convicted in the earlier incidents, so the jury would be trying uncharged crimes.
D. Admit it under Rule 404(a)(2), because a defendant who claims accident puts her character for carefulness in issue.
E. Admit it to show absence of accident, because so many unexplained overdoses among one caregiver’s patients are unlikely to be innocent.

Answer: E
- A: Scholars debate whether the doctrine of chances escapes propensity, but courts have long accepted it as proof of absence of accident (Rex v. Smith, the brides in the bath). It isn’t barred per se; 403 does the screening.
- B: Three unexplained incidents over three years aren’t a regular, semi-automatic response under Rule 406. The proper route is Rule 404(b)(2)’s absence of accident, through the doctrine of chances.
- C: Other-act evidence needs no charge or conviction. Under Huddleston the question is whether a jury could reasonably find, by a preponderance, that the acts happened and she did them.
- D: Claiming accident doesn’t open a character exception. 404(a)(2)(A) applies only when the defendant offers her own pertinent trait. This evidence comes in, if at all, around the box under 404(b)(2).
- E: Correct. This is the doctrine of chances (Rex v. Smith; United States v. Woods). The inference turns on how improbable the coincidence is rather than on what kind of person she is, and 404(b)(2) lists absence of mistake and lack of accident. The judge must still balance under 403 and give a 105 instruction on request.
Rule: Rule 404(b)(2) absence of accident and the doctrine of chances: repeated unusual “accidents” are objectively improbable. No conviction is needed (Huddleston); 403 and notice still apply.

## Final 10 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Reverse 404(b)
A defendant is charged with mugging a jogger on a park trail. He offers testimony from another jogger who was mugged nearby a week later in a similar way and who says the defendant wasn't his attacker. What standard governs the defense evidence?

A. The crimes need only be similar enough to suggest one person did both, then pass Rule 403.
B. The crimes must share features so idiosyncratic that they could not likely be anyone else's doing.
C. The evidence is barred, because Rule 404(b) forbids any proof of another crime.
D. The defendant must prove by a preponderance that the same person did both crimes.
E. The defendant must first offer evidence of his own good character for peacefulness.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. United States v. Stevens sets a lower bar for reverse 404(b) evidence: ordinary Rule 401 similarity, followed by Rule 403.
- B: That high standard from United States v. Trenkler protects defendants from the government's use. It doesn't apply to evidence offered by the defendant.
- C: The evidence isn't offered to show anyone's character. It points away from the defendant as the attacker.
- D: No such burden exists. The question is similarity under Rule 401, then Rule 403.
- E: Nothing makes this evidence depend on opening the door under Rule 404(a)(2)(A).
Rule: Defendants may offer other-crimes evidence that points away from them on a lower showing: enough similarity under Rule 401 to suggest one person did both, then Rule 403 (United States v. Stevens).

## Final 10 · Q8 · Character Evidence — Rule 405(b): character as an element
A local blog calls a roofing contractor “a con man who takes deposits and never shows up.” The contractor sues for defamation. The blogger pleads truth and offers testimony from three homeowners that the contractor took their deposits and never did the work. The contractor objects that character evidence is inadmissible in a civil case and that, in any event, character may be proved only by reputation or opinion. How should the court rule?

A. Admit the testimony under Rule 404(b) to show the contractor’s plan or scheme to defraud homeowners.
B. Exclude the testimony, because specific instances may be used only on cross-examination of a character witness.
C. Admit the testimony, because his character is an essential element of the truth defense, so specific instances may prove it.
D. Admit only reputation or opinion testimony about his honesty, because Rule 405(a) governs every use of character evidence.
E. Exclude the testimony, because the exceptions in Rule 404(a)(2) apply only in criminal cases.

Answer: C
- A: No one is trying to prove that the contractor acted a certain way on one occasion, so 404(b)’s purposes are beside the point. The right route is 405(b), because character is itself in issue.
- B: Rule 405(a) allows specific instances on cross, but Rule 405(b) independently allows them whenever character is an essential element of a claim or defense, as truth is here.
- C: Correct. 404(a)(1) bars character offered to prove that a person acted in accordance with it on a particular occasion. Here character is itself what must be proved: whether he is in fact a con man. When a trait is an essential element of a charge, claim, or defense, 405(b) allows proof by relevant specific instances, in civil and criminal cases alike.
- D: 405(a) is the general method, but 405(b) adds specific instances when character is an essential element. Reputation and opinion remain available too.
- E: Those exceptions matter only when character is offered to prove conduct on a particular occasion. When character is an element, as in a truth defense to defamation, 404(a)(1) doesn’t apply at all.
Rule: Rule 405(b): when a trait is an essential element of a charge, claim, or defense (truth in defamation, entrapment, negligent entrustment, custody), it may be proved by specific instances, in civil or criminal cases.

## Final 10 · Q9 · Character Evidence — Rule 406: habit vs. general carefulness
A driver is killed when a train hits her car at a rural crossing with no gates. In her estate’s wrongful-death suit, the railroad offers (1) a coworker’s testimony that, riding with her to work twice a day for five years, he saw her cross those tracks hundreds of times without ever slowing or looking, and (2) her sister’s testimony that she was “a careless, distracted driver in general.” No one saw the crash. Which testimony is admissible to show how she drove that day?

A. Both, because in a wrongful-death case the decedent’s carefulness is an essential element under Rule 405(b).
B. Item 2 only, because a decedent’s general carefulness is the best evidence of how she drove that day.
C. Neither, because character evidence can’t be used to prove conduct in a civil case.
D. Item 1 only, as evidence of habit.
E. Item 1 only, but only because no eyewitness saw the crash.

Answer: D
- A: Carefulness is relevant to negligence, but character isn’t an essential element of the claim, as it would be in negligent entrustment or defamation.
- B: General carelessness is a character trait, and Rule 404(a)(1) bars it to prove conduct in a civil case. The coworker’s specific, repeated observations are habit under Rule 406.
- C: That is true of item 2, but habit isn’t character. 406 is a separate rule that applies in civil and criminal cases.
- D: Correct. 406 admits a person’s habit to prove conduct on a particular occasion. Habit is a specific, regular, semi-automatic response to a repeated situation, like crossing the same tracks the same way hundreds of times (compare Halloran’s regular use of a coil). Item 2 is a general trait offered to prove conduct in conformity, which 404(a)(1) bars.
- E: 406 says habit evidence may be admitted “regardless of whether it is corroborated or whether there was an eyewitness.” The lack of an eyewitness isn’t a requirement.
Rule: Rule 406: habit (a specific, regular, semi-automatic response) is admissible to prove conduct, with or without an eyewitness or corroboration. General traits are 404(a) character.

## Final 10 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 608(a): witnesses only
In a civil real-estate fraud suit, the plaintiff never takes the stand; her case rests on the closing documents and her broker’s testimony. The defense calls a witness who testifies that the plaintiff “has a terrible reputation for truthfulness around town.” The plaintiff objects. How should the court rule?

A. Overrule the objection, because reputation testimony is always admissible in a fraud case.
B. Overrule the objection, because Rule 608 lets a party in a civil case attack the opposing party’s reputation for truthfulness.
C. Overrule the objection, because a fraud plaintiff’s honesty is an essential element of her claim under Rule 405(b).
D. Sustain the objection, because Rule 608 applies only in criminal cases.
E. Sustain it, because Rule 608(a) applies only to witnesses, and the plaintiff hasn’t testified.

Answer: E
- A: Character isn’t an essential element of a fraud claim, so 405(b) doesn’t apply, and 404(a)(1) bars character offered to show she acted dishonestly. Fraud cases have no special character rule.
- B: That was counsel’s sloppy statement of the rule in the Easerly clip. 608 applies in civil and criminal cases, to any witness, but not to a party who never takes the stand.
- C: A fraud plaintiff’s own truthfulness isn’t an element of her claim; the defendant’s alleged fraud is. And Rule 608(a) reaches only witnesses and hearsay declarants.
- D: 608 applies in civil and criminal cases alike, unlike the 404(a)(2) exceptions.
- E: Correct. 608(a) speaks of “a witness’s credibility.” Character for truthfulness matters only when a person’s testimony is before the jury, and being a party doesn’t make the plaintiff a witness. Offered against a party who hasn’t testified, the evidence is just character offered to prove conduct, barred by 404(a)(1). That was the point of the Easerly v. Lettwin clip.
Rule: Rule 608(a): character for truthfulness may be attacked by reputation or opinion, in any case, but only for someone who has testified (or whose out-of-court words are admitted).

## Final 10 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609 in a civil case: felonies vs. crimen falsi
In a civil contract suit, a key defense witness has three convictions: (1) a five-year-old felony assault conviction (punishable by up to three years); (2) a three-year-old misdemeanor conviction for filing a false police report (maximum 90 days); and (3) a two-year-old misdemeanor petty-theft conviction (maximum six months). The plaintiff offers all three to impeach. Which statement is correct?

A. All three must be admitted, because theft and assault both reflect on the witness’s willingness to lie under oath.
B. Item 2 must be admitted, item 1 comes in unless Rule 403 calls for exclusion, and item 3 is out.
C. Item 2 is admissible only if it survives Rule 403, because misdemeanors always require balancing.
D. Items 1 and 2 are admissible only if their probative value outweighs their prejudicial effect.
E. Items 1 and 3 come in subject to Rule 403, and item 2 is out because it was only a misdemeanor.

Answer: B
- A: Theft and robbery aren’t crimen falsi; the crime’s elements must require deceit or false statement. A misdemeanor theft therefore fits neither 609(a)(1) nor (a)(2).
- B: Correct. 609(a)(2) requires admitting any conviction whose elements required proving a dishonest act or false statement, and a false police report qualifies, with no 403 discretion. A felony offered against a witness in a civil case falls under 609(a)(1)(A), admitted subject to 403. Petty theft is a misdemeanor and isn’t crimen falsi, so neither branch applies.
- C: 609(a)(2) applies “regardless of the punishment” and says the evidence “must be admitted.” Such convictions are “peculiarly probative” and always admitted, though 609(b)’s ten-year limit still applies.
- D: That’s the 609(a)(1)(B) test for an accused. This witness isn’t a criminal defendant, and 609(a)(2) convictions aren’t balanced at all.
- E: Backwards. A false-report conviction is crimen falsi, admissible under Rule 609(a)(2) whatever the punishment. Petty theft is a misdemeanor that isn’t crimen falsi, so Rule 609 doesn’t admit it at all.
Rule: Rule 609(a)(2) crimen falsi convictions must be admitted, with no balancing, whatever the punishment. 609(a)(1)(A) felonies offered against a non-accused witness are subject to 403. Theft isn’t crimen falsi.

## Final 10 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 412(b)(2): civil cases
An employee sues her former supervisor and employer for sexual harassment, seeking damages for emotional distress. She makes no claim of harm to her reputation. The defense moves, in a timely sealed filing, to offer (1) evidence that she had a consensual relationship with a coworker, to suggest she welcomed sexual banter in the office, and (2) testimony that she had a reputation around the office for promiscuity. Which ruling is correct?

A. Both are admissible if their probative value substantially outweighs the harm, because Rule 412(b)(2) treats reputation like other evidence.
B. Both are admissible under ordinary Rule 403 balancing, because this is a civil case.
C. Both are barred, because Rule 412 contains no exceptions at all in civil cases.
D. Item 1 is admissible only if it passes Rule 412(b)(2)’s test, and item 2 is inadmissible.
E. Item 1 is barred, because Rule 412(b)(1)(B) allows only sexual behavior with the accused.

Answer: D
- A: Reputation faces an extra hurdle: in a civil case it is admissible only if the victim has placed it in controversy, and she hasn’t.
- B: Civil cases get a stricter test than 403: probative value must substantially outweigh harm and unfair prejudice. Reputation also requires that the victim put it in controversy.
- C: 412(b)(2) supplies a civil exception, a weighing test heavily tilted against admission, but not an absolute bar.
- D: Correct. 412(b)(2) reverses the usual 403 balance in civil cases: the evidence must substantially outweigh the danger of harm to any victim and of unfair prejudice to any party. Reputation evidence is admissible only if the victim has placed it in controversy (compare Jones v. Clinton, Problem 5.9, where her claim of reputational damage arguably did). This plaintiff hasn’t.
- E: 412(b)(1) lists the criminal-case exceptions. Civil cases use 412(b)(2)’s balancing test instead.
Rule: Rule 412(b)(2), civil cases: probative value must substantially outweigh harm to the victim and unfair prejudice to any party. Reputation comes in only if the victim placed it in controversy.

## Final 10 · Q13 · Definition of Hearsay — Implied assertion of a plan; Rule 803(3)
Grant Hale died after falling from a hotel balcony. His life-insurance policy excludes suicide. His widow sues the insurer, which claims he jumped. The widow offers Hale's email to a friend, sent the night before he died: "Just booked our Alaska cruise for June. Can't wait to show Mia the glaciers!" She offers it to prove that Hale didn't intend to kill himself. The insurer objects that it is hearsay.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because no exception admits a statement offered to prove what the declarant would later do.
B. Admit it as a present sense impression, because Hale described the booking right after making it.
C. Admit it, because an implied assertion is never hearsay under the Federal Rules of Evidence.
D. Exclude it, because a dead declarant's statements come in only as dying declarations under Rule 804.
E. Admit it under Rule 803(3) as a statement of Hale's then-existing plan and emotion.

Answer: E
- A: Under the Hillmon doctrine, Rule 803(3) admits a statement of the declarant's own intent or plan to prove that the declarant later acted on it.
