- B: The email is offered to show Hale's plans and outlook, not to describe an event he was perceiving. Rule 803(1) is a poor fit.
- C: Under Rule 801(a), a statement exists whenever the declarant intended an assertion. Hale intentionally communicated his plans and eagerness, so offering them for the belief behind them is hearsay. The outline uses a "Planning my retirement home" example on this point.
- D: A dying declaration under Rule 804(b)(2) is one route, but Rule 803(3) applies whether or not the declarant is available.
- E: Correct. Hale's statement of his plan and eagerness is hearsay when offered to show he didn't intend suicide. Rule 803(3) admits statements of the declarant's then-existing intent, plan, and emotional state, and they may be used to show his later conduct conformed (Mutual Life Insurance).
Rule: A statement of the declarant's own plan, motive, or emotion is admissible under Rule 803(3) to prove that state of mind and, through it, the declarant's later conduct (Mutual Life Insurance). Statements of memory or belief offered to prove the fact remembered or believed are excluded.

## Final 10 · Q14 · Definition of Hearsay — Verbal acts
A restaurant owner is charged with bribing a health inspector. A waiter testifies that he heard the inspector tell the owner, "Pay me $500 and this violation disappears." The inspector doesn't testify. The defense objects on hearsay grounds. What is the best ruling?

A. Sustained, because the inspector is not on the stand and can't be cross-examined.
B. Sustained, unless the prosecution first proves the inspector is unavailable.
C. Overruled, because the statement is a present sense impression of the visit.
D. Overruled, because the waiter personally heard the words and can be cross-examined.
E. Overruled, because the words are a verbal act with independent legal significance.

Answer: E
- A: The inspector's absence matters only if the words are offered for their truth. They aren't.
- B: Unavailability is a condition for Rule 804. The statement isn't hearsay at all.
- C: An offer doesn't describe an event, and no exception is needed for words that aren't hearsay.
- D: The waiter's knowledge covers only what was said. That doesn't answer a hearsay objection to a statement offered for truth.
- E: Correct. The offer has legal effect because it was said, whether or not it was sincere. It isn't offered to prove the truth of an assertion under Rule 801(c)(2).
Rule: Verbal acts such as contract words, threats, and offers have legal effect because they were said. They aren't hearsay under Rule 801(c).

## Final 10 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(2)(D): the audience doesn't matter
A Rand Freight truck rear-ended a car on the interstate. That night, the truck's driver, Matt Cole, told his roommate over dinner, "I think I dozed off for a second before I hit that car." The injured motorist sues only Rand Freight and calls the roommate to repeat the statement. Rand objects that it is hearsay. Cole is still a Rand driver.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because Cole made it to his roommate, not while doing his job.
B. Exclude it, because an employee's statement binds the employer only if made at work.
C. Admit it, but only against Cole, who isn't a party to the suit.
D. Exclude it, because Cole had no authority to speak for Rand about the crash.
E. Admit it against Rand, since it concerns his driving.

Answer: E
- A: The tempting answer. But Rule 801(d)(2)(D) asks whether the statement concerns "a matter within the scope of that relationship" and was made "while it existed," not whether making the statement was part of his job or to whom it was said.
- B: Rule 801(d)(2)(D) has no audience requirement. Statements to friends, family, or strangers qualify if they concern a matter within the scope of the employment.
- C: Cole isn't a party, and the motorist offers the statement against Rand.
- D: Authority to speak is the test under Rule 801(d)(2)(C). Under (D), no speaking authority is needed.
- E: Correct, even though it was a private remark at home. How Cole drove the truck is within the scope of his employment as a driver, and he was still employed. Rule 801(d)(2)(D) requires nothing more: not authority to speak, not personal knowledge (Mahlandt), not any particular audience.
Rule: Rule 801(d)(2)(D) admits an agent's or employee's statement against the principal if it concerns a matter within the scope of the relationship and was made while the relationship existed. Authority to speak (Rule 801(d)(2)(C)) and a business audience aren't required.

## Final 10 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(1)(A): old text and the 2026 amendment
At trial, a robbery victim testifies that the robber was short. She earlier said the robber was tall (1) under oath before a grand jury, and (2) in an unsworn interview with a detective. The government offers both for their truth. Which statement is correct about the two versions of Rule 801(d)(1)(A)?

A. (1) is substantive under both; (2) only impeaches under the old text but is substantive under the new.
B. (1) and (2) are both substantive under both versions, because she is on the stand and subject to cross-examination.
C. (1) only impeaches under both versions; (2) is substantive only under the new text.
D. (1) and (2) only impeach under both versions, because a grand jury isn't a proceeding.
E. (1) is substantive under both versions; (2) only impeaches under both versions.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Grand jury testimony is sworn testimony in a proceeding, so it qualifies either way. The Dec. 1, 2026 amendment drops the oath requirement, so the unsworn statement becomes substantive only under the new text.
- B: Before the amendment, an unsworn statement didn't qualify, however available the witness is for cross.
- C: Sworn grand jury testimony met the old text and meets the new one.
- D: A grand jury is an "other proceeding" under the old text, and the new text doesn't require one at all.
- E: This states the old rule for both versions. The amendment changes the result for the unsworn statement.
Rule: Old Rule 801(d)(1)(A) required a statement under penalty of perjury at a proceeding or deposition. The amendment effective Dec. 1, 2026 requires only inconsistency with the testimony. Ask which version the exam uses, and give both results for an unsworn statement.

## Final 10 · Q17 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(1): similar motive at the grand jury
A federal grand jury investigating a bid-rigging cartel subpoenas Vance, a cement contractor. Under a grant of immunity, Vance testifies that Corrigan never attended any cartel meeting. The prosecutors already believed Vance was lying but did not confront him, because they did not want to reveal their wiretap evidence while the investigation was ongoing.

Corrigan is later indicted. At trial, Vance, who now faces perjury exposure, invokes the Fifth Amendment, and the court sustains the privilege. Corrigan offers the transcript of Vance’s grand jury testimony. The government objects.

What is the court’s proper approach?

A. Admit the testimony, because the government chose to call Vance before the grand jury and, as a matter of adversarial fairness, cannot now avoid testimony it elicited.
B. Admit the testimony, because statements made under oath and under a grant of immunity carry sufficient guarantees of trustworthiness.
C. Exclude the testimony, because a declarant who invokes a privilege is not “unavailable” when the government has the power to immunize him again.
D. Admit it only if the government had a similar motive to develop Vance’s testimony, a question its reasons for not confronting him bear on.
E. Exclude the testimony, because a grand jury proceeding is not a “trial, hearing, or lawful deposition.”

Answer: D
- A: The Supreme Court rejected this adversarial-fairness argument in Salerno. The text of 804(b)(1) requires similar motive with no exception.
- B: Oath and immunity bear on reliability, but 804(b)(1) turns on similar motive, not general trustworthiness. That argument belongs under Rule 807, which has its own requirements.
- C: A sustained privilege claim makes the declarant unavailable under 804(a)(1). The government’s refusal to immunize again does not change that.
- D: Correct. This is the Salerno and DiNapoli framework: no adversarial-fairness shortcut, and a fact-specific similar-motive inquiry.
- E: Courts treat grand jury testimony as testimony given at a hearing for purposes of 804(b)(1). The fight is over motive, not the type of proceeding.
Rule: Former testimony of an unavailable declarant is admissible if, in a criminal case, it is offered against a party who had an opportunity and similar motive to develop it by direct, cross, or redirect examination. FRE 804(b)(1). A witness who properly invokes a privilege is unavailable. FRE 804(a)(1).

## Final 10 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 806: attacking a declarant
The government introduces a coconspirator's recorded statement under Rule 801(d)(2)(E). The coconspirator doesn't testify. The defense offers proof of the coconspirator's conviction for perjury five years ago. The government objects that the declarant never took the stand. What is the correct ruling?

A. Overruled, because a perjury conviction is substantive proof of innocence.
B. Overruled, because the declarant may be impeached as if the declarant had testified.
C. Sustained, because Rule 806 doesn't apply to coconspirators' statements.
D. Sustained, because the declarant wasn't given a chance to explain or deny the conviction.
E. Sustained, because only a testifying witness may be impeached.

Answer: B
- A: The conviction is relevant to the declarant's credibility. It proves nothing directly about guilt.
- B: Correct. Under Rule 806, once a statement under Rule 801(d)(2)(C), (D), or (E) or a hearsay statement is admitted, the declarant's credibility may be attacked as if the declarant had testified. Rule 609 convictions may be used.
- C: Rule 806 expressly names Rule 801(d)(2)(C), (D), and (E) statements.
- D: Rule 806 removes the foundation requirement of Rule 613(b) for an absent declarant.
- E: Rule 806 exists to allow impeachment of absent declarants.
Rule: Rule 806 lets a party attack an absent declarant's credibility as if the declarant had testified, including with Rule 608 and Rule 609 evidence. The Rule 613(b) foundation isn't required.

## Final 10 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(4): litigation-only physician; fault not pertinent
Ben Ochoa sues for injuries from a rear-end collision. His lawyer sends him to Dr. Lane, who will not treat him and was hired only to examine him and testify. Ochoa tells Dr. Lane: (1) "Since the crash I've had shooting pains down my left leg"; (2) "The other car slammed into me from behind"; and (3) "The other driver was obviously drunk." Dr. Lane is asked to repeat all three statements at trial.

Which statements are admissible under Rule 803(4)?

A. None, because the exception covers only statements made to a doctor consulted for treatment.
B. All three, because each one describes the cause of Ochoa's injuries in some way.
C. Statements (1) and (2), because symptoms and general cause are pertinent but fault is not.
D. Statement (1) only, because statements about cause are never pertinent to diagnosis.
E. None, but Dr. Lane may disclose them to the jury as the basis of her opinion.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 803(4) covers statements made for "medical diagnosis or treatment." The ACN extends it to a physician consulted only to testify, which rejects the older common-law limit.
- B: The other driver's intoxication goes to fault, not to the cause of the injury. It isn't reasonably pertinent to diagnosis.
- C: Correct. Rule 803(4) covers symptoms and their general cause if reasonably pertinent to diagnosis, and diagnosis for litigation counts. Being hit from behind bears on the mechanism of injury. Whether the other driver was drunk is fault, which is ordinarily not pertinent.
- D: The rule expressly covers "their general cause."
- E: Statements (1) and (2) are substantively admissible under Rule 803(4), so they don't need Rule 703's restrictive disclosure route.
Rule: Rule 803(4) admits statements reasonably pertinent to diagnosis or treatment, including a consultation solely for testifying. That covers history, symptoms, inception, and general cause, but not fault or identity.

## Final 10 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(6)(E): the opponent must show untrustworthiness
In a slip-and-fall suit, Valley Market offers its floor-inspection logs, which show an employee initialed "aisle clear" every 30 minutes, including 10 minutes before the fall. A store manager testifies that the logs are kept every day as a regular practice. The plaintiff argues the logs are self-serving and that the store hasn't shown they are trustworthy. She offers no evidence that the entries were falsified.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude the logs, because records favoring their maker are untrustworthy.
B. Exclude the logs, because they are like the accident report in Palmer.
C. Exclude the logs unless the store proves they are trustworthy.
D. Admit the logs, because the plaintiff hasn't shown they're untrustworthy.
E. Admit the logs only if the employee who initialed them testifies.

Answer: D
- A: A record's being favorable to its maker doesn't make it untrustworthy. Regular business practice is the guarantee.
- B: Palmer concerned a one-time report prepared after an accident for litigation. Routine logs kept every day for safety are part of regular operations, though the plaintiff may argue the point.
- C: Since 2014, Rule 803(6)(E) puts the burden on the opponent.
- D: Correct, though the store's own logs are self-serving. The manager lays the Rule 803(6)(A)–(D) foundation, and the plaintiff offers nothing beyond suspicion. She can still attack the logs' weight with evidence or on cross.
- E: A custodian or other qualified witness may lay the foundation.
Rule: Rule 803(6): once the foundation is laid by a qualified witness or certification, the record comes in unless the opponent shows that the source or circumstances indicate a lack of trustworthiness. Routine records differ from one-off litigation reports (Palmer).

## Final 10 · Q21 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Davis v. Washington and Hammon: no ongoing emergency
Police respond to a report of a domestic disturbance at the Voss home. They find Lena Voss alone on the front porch, appearing “somewhat frightened” but saying nothing is wrong. Inside, they see a shattered glass space heater. One officer keeps Curtis Voss in the kitchen while another questions Lena in the living room. Lena describes how Curtis shoved her into the heater and punched her. The officer then has her write and sign a “battery affidavit.”

Curtis is charged with domestic battery. Lena is subpoenaed but does not appear, and the prosecution cannot locate her. The prosecution calls the officer to recount Lena’s oral statements and offers the affidavit. Curtis objects under the Confrontation Clause.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude both unless the prosecution proves Lena is unavailable, in which case both are admissible.
B. Admit both, because Lena’s statements qualify as excited utterances, and a firmly rooted hearsay exception satisfies the Confrontation Clause.
C. Admit the oral statements but exclude the affidavit, because only formalized, sworn statements are testimonial.
D. Exclude both, because the primary purpose of the questioning was to establish past events for a possible prosecution, not to meet an ongoing emergency.
E. Admit both, because Lena is the alleged victim, and the Confrontation Clause is directed at accusatory statements by accomplices and other suspects.

Answer: D
- A: Unavailability alone is not enough. Crawford requires both unavailability and a prior opportunity for cross-examination.
- B: The “firmly rooted exception” test comes from Ohio v. Roberts, which Crawford overruled for testimonial statements. Qualifying as an excited utterance does not answer the Confrontation question.
- C: Hammon held the oral statements testimonial too. Formality matters, but a structured police interview about past events is formal enough without an oath.
- D: Correct. With the emergency over and the questioning directed at past events, the statements are testimonial under Davis and Hammon.
- E: The Clause protects against all testimonial statements of absent witnesses, whatever the declarant’s role. Victims’ statements to police are the paradigm case.
Rule: The Confrontation Clause bars testimonial statements of a witness who does not appear at trial unless the witness is unavailable and the defendant had a prior opportunity to cross-examine. Crawford v. Washington. Statements to police are testimonial when the circumstances objectively indicate that there is no ongoing emergency and the primary purpose of the interrogation is to establish or prove past events potentially relevant to later prosecution. Davis v. Washington; Hammon v. Indiana.

## Final 10 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Rule 106 works against the defendant too
In a sexual-harassment suit, the defendant reads to the jury one line of a text the plaintiff sent a coworker: "Honestly, I thought his jokes were kind of funny at first." The next line of the same text read: "until he cornered me in the supply room and grabbed me." The plaintiff asks the court to require that the second line be read now. The defense objects that the second line is self-serving hearsay.

What is the best ruling?

A. Deny it, because a party can't offer her own out-of-court statements.
B. Deny it now, but let the plaintiff try a hearsay exception on redirect.
C. Grant it, because fairness requires the next line to correct a misimpression.
D. Grant it, but only for context, not for the truth of what she said.
E. Deny it, because Rule 106 covers only recorded statements, not texts.

Answer: C
- A: Generally true, but Rule 106 overrides the hearsay objection when completion is needed to correct a misimpression the other side created.
- B: Rule 106 entitles the plaintiff to completion "at that time." Waiting would let the misimpression take hold.
- C: Correct, though it lets in the plaintiff's own self-serving words. Reading "his jokes were kind of funny" alone suggests she welcomed his conduct. The next line corrects that. Under the 2023 amendment, completion overrides a hearsay objection. Because the remainder corrects the misimpression only if true, it comes in for its truth.
- D: The ACN says the completing statement comes in for its truth when it corrects the misimpression only if true, as here.
- E: A text is a written statement, and since 2023, Rule 106 covers oral statements too.
Rule: Rule 106 (2023): when a party introduces part of a statement, the adverse party may require introduction at that time of any other part that in fairness ought to be considered, over a hearsay objection, and for its truth when that is needed to correct the misimpression.

## Final 10 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Holmes: an arbitrary third-party-guilt rule
In a state murder trial, DNA and fiber evidence point strongly to Calvin Royce. Royce offers a witness who saw another man, Earl Biggs, running from the victim's building with bloody clothes minutes after the killing. He also offers a witness who heard Biggs say he "had to take care of" the victim. The trial court excludes all of it under a state rule that bars third-party-guilt evidence whenever the prosecution's forensic evidence, viewed alone, strongly supports guilt.

How will a reviewing court most likely rule?

A. Affirm, because states may exclude third-party-guilt evidence that raises only a bare suspicion.
B. Affirm, because strong forensic evidence makes the defense evidence too weak to matter.
C. Reverse, because judging defense evidence only by the prosecution's case is arbitrary.
D. Reverse, because the Confrontation Clause guarantees the right to offer third-party-guilt evidence.
E. Affirm, because the Federal Rules of Evidence don't govern a state murder trial.

Answer: C
- A: Bare-suspicion rules remain valid, but this rule doesn't evaluate the defense evidence at all. It looks only at the prosecution's case.
- B: This is the reasoning Holmes rejected. The strength of one side's evidence doesn't show that the other side's is weak.
- C: Correct. Holmes v. South Carolina struck down this kind of rule as arbitrary. It doesn't serve the legitimate ends of rules that exclude marginal third-party evidence, and it denies "a meaningful opportunity to present a complete defense."
- D: The right comes from due process and compulsory process (Crane; Holmes). The Confrontation Clause protects against the prosecution's witnesses.
- E: That's true but beside the point. The constitutional limit binds state evidence rules.
Rule: Under Holmes (with the Washington and Rock cases), evidence rules that are arbitrary or disproportionate to their purposes violate the right to present a complete defense. Chambers adds a test for valid rules applied mechanistically to critical, reliable evidence.

## Final 10 · Q24 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 701: lay opinion vs. specialized knowledge
In a dram-shop suit, the bartender who served Glen Ash testifies: (1) "When he left at 11, he was drunk. He was slurring, stumbling, and smelled strongly of beer." (2) "Based on the eight pints I poured him in two hours, his blood-alcohol level had to be at least .15." The bartender has no training in toxicology.

Which testimony is admissible?

A. Both, because the bartender personally perceived how much Ash drank and how he acted.
B. Neither, because intoxication is a medical conclusion that requires a qualified expert.
C. Only (2), because it rests on specific facts rather than on general impressions.
D. Only (1), because estimating blood-alcohol content requires specialized knowledge.
E. Both, because opinions on an ultimate issue are expressly allowed by Rule 704(a).

Answer: D
- A: Perception is necessary but not sufficient. Rule 701(c) bars a lay opinion based on specialized knowledge.
- B: Intoxication with a foundation of observed signs is a classic lay opinion under the 2000 ACN.
- C: Converting drinks to a blood-alcohol figure requires pharmacology, the kind of knowledge Rule 701(c) reserves to experts.
- D: Correct. An opinion that someone was drunk, based on observed signs, is rationally based on perception and helpful (Rule 701(a)–(b)). A blood-alcohol estimate depends on scientific or specialized knowledge, which Rule 701(c) excludes from lay opinion. That opinion requires a qualified expert under Rule 702.
- E: Rule 704(a) removes the ultimate-issue objection, but it doesn't make a lay witness competent to give a specialized opinion.
Rule: Rule 701 lay opinions must be rationally based on perception, helpful, and not based on specialized knowledge. Rule 701(c) keeps an expert in lay clothing from evading Rule 702 and its disclosure rules.

## Final 10 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Guilbert and Joiner: error, abuse of discretion, harmlessness
At a robbery trial, four eyewitnesses who had known the defendant for years identified him. A fifth, a stranger, identified him after a brief, high-stress encounter at gunpoint. The defense offered an expert to testify about weapon focus, stress, and cross-racial identification. The trial court excluded the expert as describing "common sense." Surveillance video and the defendant's fingerprints on the getaway car also implicate him. He was convicted.

What is the most likely appellate result in a court that follows Guilbert?

A. Reversal, because excluding a qualified eyewitness expert is always reversible.
B. Affirmance, because eyewitness experts are never helpful to jurors.
C. Reversal, because the expert would have said the stranger was mistaken.
D. Affirmance, because rulings on experts aren't reviewable on appeal.
E. Affirmance, because the exclusion was an error but a harmless one.

Answer: E
- A: Even an erroneous exclusion is reviewed for harmlessness.
- B: Guilbert rejected the "common sense" rationale, noting a near-national consensus that such testimony can help.
- C: Under Guilbert, the expert may describe general factors but may not opine on whether a particular eyewitness is accurate.
- D: Rulings admitting or excluding experts are reviewed for abuse of discretion (General Electric Co. v. Joiner).
- E: Correct, though it leaves the conviction standing. State v. Guilbert held that excluding the expert was an abuse of discretion only as to the stranger. Four acquaintance identifications and physical evidence made the error harmless. Review is for abuse of discretion, then harmlessness.
Rule: Under Guilbert, eyewitness-identification experts are admissible when helpful. They may explain general factors but may not opine on a particular eyewitness's accuracy. Exclusion is reviewed for abuse of discretion (Joiner), then harmlessness.

## Final 10 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Daubert: acceptance is one factor
In federal court, a plaintiff offers a new diagnostic technique that isn't yet generally accepted. It has been tested, has been published in peer-reviewed journals, and has a known low error rate. The defense says it fails because it lacks general acceptance. What is the best analysis?

A. It must be admitted, because a peer-reviewed technique is always reliable.
B. It may be admitted, because general acceptance is one flexible factor.
C. It must be excluded, because every Daubert factor must be met.
D. It must be excluded, because Frye still governs in federal court.
E. It is for the jury alone, because the judge has no gatekeeping role.

Answer: B
- A: Peer review is a factor, not a guarantee. The proponent must still meet Rule 702.
- B: Correct. Daubert held that Frye didn't survive the Rules. General acceptance is one non-exclusive factor, along with testing, peer review, and error rate.
- C: The factors are non-exclusive and flexible.
- D: Daubert replaced Frye in federal courts.
- E: Daubert makes the judge a gatekeeper under Rule 104(a).
Rule: Under Daubert, the judge considers testing, peer review, error rate, standards, and general acceptance as flexible factors.

## Final 10 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 1003: a duplicate when authenticity is genuinely in question
In a contract dispute, the buyer offers a photocopy of the signed contract showing a delivery date of June 1. The seller testifies that the original he signed said July 1, that the buyer kept the original, and that the "6" in the photocopy looks altered. A document examiner for the seller notes toner irregularities around the date. The buyer refuses to produce the original, saying the copy is good enough.

What is the best ruling on the photocopy?

A. Admit it, because a duplicate is admissible to the same extent as an original.
B. Admit it, because the seller's challenge goes only to its weight.
C. Exclude it, because only an original may ever prove a contract's terms.
D. Admit it, because the seller's own testimony proves the content under Rule 1007.
E. Exclude it, because the original's authenticity is genuinely in question.

Answer: E
- A: That is Rule 1003's general rule, but it has an exception for a "genuine question ... about the original's authenticity."
- B: For the general authenticity question this would be right (Rule 104(b)), but Rule 1003's exception makes a genuine dispute a reason to require the original.
- C: Duplicates and other exceptions under Rules 1003–1007 may prove content. The original isn't the only route.
- D: The seller's testimony contradicts the buyer's version. Rule 1007 lets the proponent use the opponent's statement about content, and the seller's says July 1.
- E: Correct. Rule 1003's preference for duplicates yields when "a genuine question is raised about the original's authenticity." With testimony and expert evidence of alteration, and the buyer holding the original, the original should be produced.
Rule: Under Rule 1003, a duplicate is admissible like the original unless there is a genuine question about the original's authenticity or admitting the duplicate would be unfair. Then the original (or a Rule 1004 excuse) is required.

## Final 10 · Q28 · Privileges — Jaffee: social worker, no balancing
In a federal civil-rights suit alleging that Officer Dana Price used excessive force, the plaintiff subpoenas the notes of a licensed clinical social worker who counseled Price in 40 sessions after the incident. The plaintiff argues that the notes are the only contemporaneous record of Price's account, and that the need for them outweighs any confidentiality interest. Price claims privilege.

How should the court rule?

A. Order production, because the privilege covers only psychiatrists and psychologists.
B. Order production, because the plaintiff's need for unique evidence outweighs Price's privacy.
C. Review the notes in camera and release the parts whose value outweighs the harm to Price.
D. Uphold the privilege, which covers licensed clinical social workers and isn't balanced away.
E. Order production, because Rule 501 makes federal courts apply state privilege law in civil cases.

Answer: D
- A: Jaffee extended the privilege to licensed clinical social workers.
- B: Jaffee rejected balancing: "An uncertain privilege ... is little better than no privilege at all."
- C: This is a balancing approach in another form, which Jaffee rejected.
- D: Correct. Under Rule 501's "reason and experience," Jaffee recognized a psychotherapist-patient privilege that covers licensed clinical social workers. It rejected ad hoc balancing of the evidentiary need against the patient's privacy.
- E: State privilege law governs only claims for which state law supplies the rule of decision. A federal civil-rights claim is governed by federal common law.
Rule: Jaffee recognized a federal psychotherapist-patient privilege under Rule 501, covering licensed clinical social workers, with no case-by-case balancing. In civil cases, state privilege law applies only to state-law claims.

## Final 10 · Q29 · Privileges — Fees, identity, and preexisting documents
A subpoena asks a lawyer to produce (1) the name of a client and the fee paid, and (2) old business records the client handed over to get advice, which were not protected in the client's hands. Ordinarily, what is protected?

A. Neither, because none of these are confidential communications.
B. Only the records, because they were transferred in confidence.
C. Only the name and fee, because they are confidential.
D. Both, because anything given to a lawyer becomes privileged.
E. Both, unless the client waives the privilege in writing.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Identity and fees are ordinarily not privileged, absent the narrow exception. Records unprotected in the client's hands don't become protected by being handed to a lawyer.
- B: Preexisting documents gain protection in the lawyer's hands only if they were protected in the client's hands.
- C: Identity and fees are ordinarily facts, not confidential communications.
- D: The privilege protects communications, not everything a lawyer receives.
- E: No written waiver is needed where no privilege attaches.
Rule: Under Rule 501 common law, neither fee arrangements nor client identity are ordinarily privileged. Preexisting documents are protected in a lawyer's hands only if they would have been protected in the client's hands.

## Final 10 · Q30 · Privileges — No federal parent-child privilege
A federal grand jury investigating a warehouse fire subpoenas Diane Moore to testify about what her 17-year-old son told her in their kitchen the night after the fire. She believes he confessed to setting it. She moves to quash, arguing that compelling a parent to reveal a child's confidences would destroy family trust. She notes that her state's law recognizes a parent-child privilege.

How should the court rule?

A. Quash it, because Jaffee recognized privileges for relationships of trust and confidence.
B. Quash it, because the son was a minor when he confided in his mother.
C. Quash it, because federal courts must apply state privilege law under Rule 501.
D. Quash it, because the marital-confidences privilege extends to the whole family.
E. Deny the motion, because federal common law recognizes no parent-child privilege.

Answer: E
- A: Jaffee rested on "reason and experience," including all 50 states' recognition. Courts have refused to extend it to parents and children for lack of that consensus.
- B: The court in In re Grand Jury Proceedings rejected a privilege for minor children too.
- C: State privilege law applies only in civil cases to claims governed by state law. A federal grand jury investigation is governed by federal common law.
- D: The marital-confidences privilege protects spouses only.
- E: Correct. In re Grand Jury Proceedings (3d Cir. 1997) held there is no parent-child privilege: almost every court rejects it, few states recognize it, it didn't exist at common law, and any such privilege should come from Congress. In this federal criminal proceeding, Rule 501 calls for federal common law.
Rule: Under Rule 501, federal common law governs privilege in federal criminal proceedings. Without consensus in "reason and experience," courts decline to recognize new privileges such as parent-child, which contrasts with Jaffee.

## Final 11 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Rule 606(b): outside influence
After a guilty verdict, the defense offers juror testimony to overturn it. Which testimony may the court consider?

A. The foreperson pressured two holdouts until they changed their votes.
B. A juror described her own experience of being robbed during deliberations.
C. A juror says she voted guilty only because she was exhausted and wanted to go home.
D. A juror says several jurors drank heavily at lunch and slept through testimony.
E. A bailiff told the jurors during a break that the defendant had confessed.

Answer: E
- A: Pressure among jurors is a statement or incident during deliberations, which Rule 606(b)(1) bars.
- B: A juror's own life experience isn't extraneous information (Warger), and it was a statement during deliberations.
- C: Rule 606(b)(1) bars testimony about the effect of anything on a juror's vote or mental processes.
- D: Tanner treats intoxication and sleeping as internal matters, no more an outside influence than a virus or lack of sleep.
- E: Correct. A bailiff's remark brings extraneous prejudicial information or an outside influence to the jury, so Rule 606(b)(2) lets a juror testify about it.
Rule: Rule 606(b) bars juror testimony about deliberations, votes, and mental processes. A juror may testify about extraneous prejudicial information, an outside influence, or a mistake on the verdict form. Internal vs. external turns on the nature of the matter (Tanner).

## Final 11 · Q2 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 104(b): relevance depends on knowledge
Dale is charged with burning a rival's shop. To show motive, the State proves that a week earlier the rival filed a sealed complaint that could cost Dale his license. Dale objects that he never knew about it. The State shows that Dale's brother works at the licensing board and had dinner with Dale three times that week. Which standard governs?

A. The judge must personally find by a preponderance that Dale knew of the complaint.
B. The evidence comes in if a jury could reasonably find that Dale knew of the complaint.
C. The State must prove Dale's knowledge beyond a reasonable doubt before the jury hears it.
D. The State must offer direct evidence that someone told Dale about the complaint.
E. The evidence comes in with no showing, because Rule 401 requires only any tendency.

Answer: B
- A: That is the Rule 104(a) standard. Conditional relevance goes to the jury under Rule 104(b).
- B: Correct. Motive depends on knowledge, so this is conditional relevance. Under Rule 104(b) and Huddleston, the proof need only support a finding, as in Cox.
- C: Rule 104(b) is far lower than reasonable doubt.
- D: Circumstantial evidence is enough. In Cox, regular contact with someone who knew supported the finding.
- E: The complaint is relevant to motive only if Dale knew of it, so Rule 104(b) requires proof of that fact.
Rule: When relevance depends on a fact, Rule 104(b) requires proof sufficient to support a finding. The judge asks only whether a jury could reasonably find the fact by a preponderance (Huddleston, Cox).

## Final 11 · Q3 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Old Chief doesn't reach a 404(b) purpose
Rex Hale is charged in one indictment with (1) possessing cocaine with intent to distribute and (2) being a felon in possession of a firearm. His prior felony is a 2019 conviction for cocaine distribution. Hale offers to stipulate that he is a convicted felon. His defense on count 1 is that he was only a visitor in the apartment and didn't know the drugs were there. The government wants to prove the nature of the 2019 conviction to show knowledge and intent on count 1.

Which ruling is correct?

A. Accept the stipulation on count 2, but allow the prior on count 1 to show knowledge and intent.
B. Under Old Chief, the government may never mention the nature of the conviction once Hale stipulates.
C. Reject the stipulation and let the government prove the conviction's nature on count 2 as it chooses.
D. Exclude the conviction on count 1, because a prior drug crime can show only propensity.
E. Admit the conviction on both counts with no instruction, because Hale put his knowledge in issue.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Old Chief requires the court to accept a stipulation to legal status on the felon-in-possession count. But its holding is confined to status elements. On count 1, a prior distribution conviction may be offered under Rule 404(b)(2) to show knowledge and intent, which Hale disputes. It must pass the Huddleston steps and Rule 403, and Rule 105 entitles him to an instruction on request.
- B: Old Chief addresses proof of status. It doesn't bar the same evidence offered for a separate, proper purpose on another count.
- C: For a status element, Old Chief holds that refusing the stipulation and proving the name of the offense is an abuse of discretion.
- D: Knowledge and intent are proper Rule 404(b)(2) purposes when the defendant claims he didn't know about the drugs.
- E: On count 2, the stipulation controls. On count 1, Hale is entitled to a limiting instruction on request.
Rule: Old Chief requires accepting a stipulation to a prior-felony status element. It doesn't bar the prior offered for a Rule 404(b)(2) purpose on a disputed issue, which remains subject to Rule 403 and a Rule 105 instruction.

## Final 11 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 408: a report prepared for the talks
After a warehouse fire destroys a tenant’s inventory, the tenant sends the landlord a letter demanding $400,000 and blaming the landlord’s old wiring. The landlord denies fault. During the settlement talks that follow, the landlord hires an electrical consultant to inspect the building and prepare a report for use in the negotiations. The report concludes that the wiring violated the electrical code. Talks collapse, the tenant sues, and the tenant obtains the report and offers it to prove the wiring was defective. How should the court rule?

A. Admit it, because the report was written by an outside consultant rather than by the landlord or its lawyers.
B. Admit it, because no lawsuit had been filed when the consultant prepared the report.
C. Admit it, because Rule 408 protects only offers of money, not factual findings made along the way.
D. Exclude it, because the report is a statement made during compromise negotiations.
E. Admit it, because the tenant could have hired its own consultant to discover the same facts.

Answer: D
- A: The consultant acted as the landlord’s agent in the negotiation process. 408 protects statements made in the course of negotiations, whoever the speaker is.
- B: 408 requires a disputed claim, not a filed complaint. The demand letter and the landlord’s denial created one.
- C: 408(a)(2) expressly covers “conduct or a statement made during compromise negotiations.” Since 2006 that has included factual admissions.
- D: Correct. This is Problem 2.4 (Hotel Inspection). A report prepared by the party’s agent for use in the settlement process counts as a statement made “during compromise negotiations.” Otherwise parties would be afraid to hire such consultants. 408 doesn’t make the underlying facts undiscoverable, so the tenant can hire its own expert.
- E: That other evidence could prove the same facts doesn’t strip protection from a statement made during negotiations. The tenant may hire its own expert, but it can’t use the landlord’s negotiation report to prove the claim.
Rule: Rule 408(a)(2): conduct or statements made during compromise negotiations over a disputed claim, including an agent’s report prepared for the talks, can’t prove validity or amount. The claim needn’t have been filed.

## Final 11 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 411: insurance to prove control
Ella Brandt was hurt when a rotten board gave way on a boat dock behind Gary Lund's lake house. Lund denies that he owns or controls the dock, which he says belongs to his neighbor. Brandt offers Lund's homeowner's liability policy, which lists "dock and boat lift" as insured premises. Lund objects under Rule 411.

What is the proper ruling?

A. Exclude it, because evidence of liability insurance is inadmissible for any purpose.
B. Admit it to prove that Lund carelessly failed to maintain the dock he insured.
C. Exclude it unless Lund first opens the door by testifying that he has no insurance.
D. Admit it to prove ownership or control, with an instruction against inferring negligence.
E. Exclude it, because insurance evidence is admissible only to show a witness's bias.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 411 bars only one purpose: proving negligence or wrongful conduct.
- B: That is the barred purpose.
- C: Rule 411 has no such prerequisite. Ownership and control are independent permitted purposes.
- D: Correct. Rule 411 permits insurance evidence for "another purpose," including "agency, ownership, or control." Lund disputes control, and insuring the dock tends to show it. A Rule 105 instruction limits the jury's use.
- E: Bias is one example. Rule 411 also lists agency, ownership, and control.
Rule: Rule 411 bars insurance evidence only to prove negligence or wrongful conduct. It may be admitted for another purpose, such as bias, agency, ownership, or control, subject to Rule 403 and a limiting instruction.

## Final 11 · Q6 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(b): the purpose must be in dispute
An undercover officer buys heroin from a man in a hooded jacket on a dark street corner. The defendant is arrested an hour later with no drugs or marked money on him. At trial, the defense concedes that whoever made the sale knew the substance was heroin and intended to sell it; the only issue is whether the defendant was the seller. The prosecution offers the defendant’s prior conviction for selling heroin, arguing that it shows “knowledge of the drug trade” and “intent.” How should the court rule?

A. Admit it with a limiting instruction, because a Rule 105 instruction cures any propensity concern.
B. Admit it, because intent is an element of every distribution charge, and a not-guilty plea keeps it in issue.
C. Exclude it, because with knowledge and intent conceded, the prior sale bears on identity only through propensity.
D. Admit it, because knowledge is a permitted purpose listed in Rule 404(b)(2).
E. Admit it, but only if the prior conviction is less than ten years old.

Answer: C
- A: A limiting instruction can’t create a proper purpose. With knowledge and intent conceded, the only path to identity is propensity, which Rule 404(b)(1) bars.
- B: The defense has expressly conceded that the seller intended to sell. With that concession, the only live issue is identity, and the prior sale reaches identity only through character.
- C: Correct. This is Problem 3.2 (Drug Seller). A 404(b)(2) purpose must connect to a fact actually in issue. With knowledge and intent conceded, labeling the evidence “knowledge” just disguises the propensity chain to identity. 403 would also weigh heavily against it.
- D: Listing a purpose doesn’t make it relevant in a given case. Where knowledge isn’t contested, the evidence has no non-propensity probative value on that point.
- E: The ten-year limit belongs to Rule 609(b), which governs impeachment by conviction. It has nothing to do with substantive other-act evidence under 404(b).
Rule: Rule 404(b): the non-propensity purpose must bear on an issue actually in dispute. “Knowledge” or “intent” can’t be a label for propensity reasoning toward identity.

## Final 11 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Zackowitz: weapons unconnected to the crime
Dunn is charged with stabbing a man outside a bar with a pocketknife, which police recovered. The State offers a collection of a dozen combat knives found in Dunn's garage, unconnected to the stabbing, to show he is a dangerous man. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admitted, because possessing knives is not a crime, so Rule 404(b) doesn't apply.
B. Admitted, subject to a limiting instruction that the jury may not punish him for them.
C. Excluded, because a defendant's lawful property can never be used against him at trial.
D. Admitted, because weapons in the home show he had the means to commit the crime.
E. Excluded, because the collection proves only character and has no non-propensity link.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 404(b) reaches any other crime, wrong, or act, and Rule 404(a)(1) bars character evidence generally.
- B: An instruction can't rescue evidence whose only purpose is forbidden.
- C: This is too broad. Items tied to the crime, or to preparation for it, can come in.
- D: The knife used was recovered, so the collection adds nothing on means. Its only force is that Dunn is a dangerous type.
- E: Correct. As in People v. Zackowitz, weapons unconnected to the crime prove only a dangerous character. Rule 404(b)(1) bars that use.
Rule: Other acts need a non-character route under Rule 404(b)(2). Evidence that shows only the defendant is a dangerous type falls within the propensity ban (People v. Zackowitz).

## Final 11 · Q8 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(a)(2): criminal cases only
A bar patron sues another patron for battery, claiming the defendant hit him with a beer bottle without provocation. The defendant, who says he never threw a punch, calls his longtime neighbor to testify that the defendant has a reputation in the neighborhood as a peaceful, gentle man. The plaintiff objects. How should the court rule?

A. Sustain the objection, because reputation testimony is hearsay.
B. Overrule the objection, because a battery claim makes the defendant’s peacefulness an essential element.
C. Sustain it, because the exceptions letting a defendant offer his pertinent trait apply only in criminal cases.
D. Overrule the objection, because a battery claim alleges a crime, so he may defend his good name as an accused could.
E. Overrule the objection, because reputation is a proper way to prove character under Rule 405(a).

Answer: C
- A: Reputation testimony about character is admissible over a hearsay objection under FRE 803(21). The problem is 404(a)(1), not hearsay.
- B: Peacefulness isn’t an element of a battery claim or defense. Rule 405(b) applies only when character itself is at issue, as with truth in defamation or negligent entrustment.
- C: Correct. 404(a)(2) applies only in criminal cases. The 2006 amendment rejected decisions like Perrin v. Anderson, which had let civil defendants facing quasi-criminal charges offer good character. In a civil battery suit the evidence is barred by 404(a)(1).
- D: That was the reasoning of Perrin v. Anderson, which the 2006 amendment rejected. The “special dispensation” belongs only to criminal defendants, whose liberty is at stake.
- E: 405(a) governs how character is proved once some rule lets it in. It doesn’t create a right to offer character. Here no exception applies, so the method never comes into play.
Rule: The 404(a)(2)(A)–(C) exceptions apply only in criminal cases (2006 amendment). In a civil case, character offered to prove conduct is barred.

## Final 11 · Q9 · Character Evidence — Rule 406: an organization's routine practice
Harbor Mutual claims it mailed Lena Ruiz a policy cancellation notice on June 3, before her house burned. No employee remembers mailing that notice. Harbor's office manager testifies that for 11 years every cancellation notice has been printed, logged, stamped, and placed in a tray. A postal carrier collects the tray daily. The log shows a notice to Ruiz on June 3. Ruiz objects that the testimony is improper character evidence and is uncorroborated.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because Rule 404(a) bars an organization's character offered to prove conduct on one occasion.
B. Admit it to prove the notice was mailed, as evidence of an organization's routine practice.
C. Exclude it, because Rule 406 requires an eyewitness or other corroboration that the practice was followed.
D. Admit the log under Rule 803(6), but exclude the manager's description of the mailing practice.
E. Exclude it, because only individuals have habits; organizations act through individual decisions.

Answer: B
- A: A routine practice isn't character. Rule 406 specifically admits it to prove conduct on a particular occasion.
- B: Correct. Rule 406 admits evidence of an organization's routine practice to prove that it acted accordingly on a particular occasion, "regardless of whether it is corroborated or whether there was an eyewitness." A regular, specific mailing procedure is the classic example.
- C: Rule 406 expressly dispenses with corroboration and eyewitnesses.
- D: The log may be a business record, but the manager's testimony about the practice is independently admissible under Rule 406.
- E: Rule 406 expressly covers "an organization's routine practice."
Rule: Rule 406 admits a person's habit or an organization's routine practice to prove action in conformity on a particular occasion. No corroboration or eyewitness is needed. The practice must be regular, specific, and semi-automatic.

## Final 11 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 608(a): no bolstering before an attack
In a civil suit over a bar fight, the plaintiff calls an eyewitness who testifies that she saw the defendant swing a pool cue at the plaintiff. The defense’s cross-examination asks only about the lighting and her distance from the pool table. The plaintiff then calls the eyewitness’s neighbor to testify that the eyewitness has an excellent reputation in the neighborhood for truthfulness. The defense objects. How should the court rule?

A. Sustain the objection, because Rule 608 applies only to criminal defendants who testify.
B. Overrule the objection, because Rule 608(a) lets any party support a witness’s credibility with reputation evidence.
C. Overrule the objection, because the neighbor testifies to reputation, a form Rule 608(a) permits.
D. Overrule the objection, because the defense’s cross-examination challenged the eyewitness’s credibility.
E. Sustain the objection, because truthful character can be supported only after it has been attacked.

Answer: E
- A: 608 applies to every witness, in civil and criminal cases alike, unlike 404(a)(2).
- B: 608(a) allows support, but its last sentence limits evidence of truthful character to after an attack on character for truthfulness.
- C: Reputation is a proper form, but Rule 608(a) admits evidence of truthful character only after the witness’s character for truthfulness has been attacked. Questions about lighting and distance attack perception, not character.
- D: Not every challenge to credibility is an attack on character for truthfulness. Doubting perception, bias, or a single contradiction usually doesn’t open the door to good-character evidence (Problem 4.6).
- E: Correct. Under 608(a), support comes only after an attack. Questions about lighting and distance attack her perception, the “that’s an error” mode of impeachment, not her character for truthfulness. Offering good character now is bolstering (Problem 4.1).
Rule: Rule 608(a): evidence of truthful character comes in only after an attack on character for truthfulness. Attacks on perception or bias don’t count, and bolstering is improper.

## Final 11 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609(a)(1)(B): same-crime prior; the Gordon factors
Andre Fisk is charged in federal court with burglary of a dwelling. His defense is that he was at a friend's apartment, and he is the only witness who can say so. If Fisk testifies, the prosecution wants to impeach him with his six-year-old state felony conviction for residential burglary. That conviction's elements required breaking and entering with intent to commit theft. No other impeachment of Fisk is available.

What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admit it, because every felony conviction must be admitted to impeach a testifying witness.
B. Admit it, because burglary with intent to steal is a crime of dishonesty under Rule 609(a)(2).
C. Exclude it, because its probative value doesn't outweigh its prejudicial effect to Fisk.
D. Admit it unless Fisk shows that its prejudice substantially outweighs its probative value.
E. Exclude it, because only crimes of dishonesty may be used against a testifying defendant.

Answer: C
- A: Under Rule 609(a)(1)(B), a testifying criminal defendant's felony comes in only if the court finds that its probative value outweighs the prejudice to that defendant.
- B: Theft and burglary are generally not crimen falsi. Proving their elements doesn't require a dishonest act or false statement.
- C: Correct. Rule 609(a)(1)(B) uses a balance that favors the defendant, and the proponent bears the burden. Under the Gordon factors applied in United States v. Brewer, a prior identical to the charge invites propensity reasoning. A defendant whose testimony is central should not be deterred from giving it. Both factors weigh against admission, so the conviction is likely excluded.
- D: That is the ordinary Rule 403 test, which applies to witnesses other than the accused under Rule 609(a)(1)(A). It isn't the standard for the defendant.
- E: Felonies may impeach a defendant under Rule 609(a)(1)(B) if they pass its balancing test.
Rule: Rule 609(a)(1)(B) admits a testifying defendant's felony only if its probative value outweighs its prejudicial effect to the defendant. Similarity to the charge and the importance of the defendant's testimony weigh against admission (the Gordon factors, applied in Brewer).

## Final 11 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 412(b)(1)(B): behavior with the accused
A defendant charged in federal court with sexual assault claims the encounter was consensual. By timely written motion, he seeks to offer (1) testimony that he and the complainant had a consensual sexual relationship that ended two months before the incident; (2) testimony that on the night in question she wore a revealing dress to the bar; and (3) testimony that she flirted with several other men at the bar before leaving with him. After an in camera hearing, which items may the court admit?

A. None of them, because Rule 412 bars all evidence of a victim’s sexual history in criminal cases.
B. Items 1 and 2, because what she wore that night isn’t sexual behavior.
C. All of them, because once the defendant claims consent, the complainant’s character becomes a pertinent trait under Rule 404(a)(2)(B).
D. Items 1 and 3, because flirting that same night bears directly on her state of mind.
E. Item 1 only.

Answer: E
- A: 412(b)(1) has three criminal-case exceptions, and past sexual behavior with the accused, offered to prove consent, is one of them.
- B: Dress is predisposition evidence, which Rule 412(a)(2) bars as surely as sexual behavior. Only item 1, specific behavior with the accused offered to prove consent, fits Rule 412(b)(1)(B).
- C: 404(a)(2)(B) is expressly “subject to the limitations in Rule 412.” Closing off that use of the victim-character exception is why 412 exists.
- D: Flirting with other men is offered to show a disposition toward sexual activity, the inference 412 forbids. Only behavior with the accused fits (b)(1)(B).
- E: Correct. 412(b)(1)(B) admits specific instances of the victim’s sexual behavior with the accused, offered by the defendant to prove consent. Items 2 and 3 are offered to suggest she was the type to consent, which is “sexual predisposition.” The Advisory Committee’s Note says the rule bars evidence of a complainant’s mode of dress, speech, or lifestyle offered for that purpose.
Rule: Rule 412(b)(1)(B): in a criminal case, specific instances of sexual behavior with the accused may be offered by the defendant to prove consent, or by the prosecutor. Dress, speech, or lifestyle evidence is barred predisposition evidence.

## Final 11 · Q13 · Definition of Hearsay — Absence of complaints: nonassertive silence
In a products suit claiming that the Zephyr blender's lid flies off during use, the manufacturer calls its customer-service director. She personally handles every complaint the company receives. She testifies that in three years of sales, about 40,000 Zephyr blenders sold under similar conditions, no customer has reported a lid coming off. The plaintiff objects that the testimony is hearsay.

How should the court rule?

A. Overrule it, because silence the customers didn't intend as an assertion isn't hearsay.
B. Sustain it, because the silence implies the customers believed the lids were safe.
C. Sustain it unless the company offers its complaint records under Rule 803(7).
D. Overrule it, because a manufacturer's good safety record is admissible character evidence.
E. Sustain it, because absence of complaints can never be relevant to a product's safety.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under Rule 801(a), nonverbal conduct is a statement only if intended as an assertion. Customers who simply didn't complain intended to assert nothing, like the paramedics' silence in the course examples. The testimony isn't hearsay, and the director has personal knowledge. Relevance depends on similar conditions, subject to Rule 403.
- B: The Federal Rules rejected the implied-assertion approach of Wright. Unintended inferences from conduct aren't statements.
- C: Rule 803(7) is one route for proving absence through records, but the director testifies from personal knowledge, and the silence isn't hearsay in the first place.
- D: This isn't character evidence about a person. Its admissibility turns on hearsay and relevance.
- E: With a foundation showing similar conditions and a reliable reporting channel, the absence of complaints has a tendency to show safety.
Rule: Under Rule 801(a), only conduct intended as an assertion is a statement. Silence or nonassertive conduct isn't hearsay, though it must still be relevant and pass Rule 403.

## Final 11 · Q14 · Definition of Hearsay — Rule 805: two spontaneous layers
A 911 operator testifies that a caller, breathing hard, said: "My neighbor just ran over here screaming, 'Ray shot Tom in the garage! He's still got the gun!'" Neither the caller nor the neighbor testifies at the civil wrongful-death trial. The defense objects to the recording as double hearsay.

What is the most accurate analysis?

A. It is admissible, because 911 calls are business records of the service.
B. It is admissible if the neighbor's and the caller's words each fit an exception.
C. It is inadmissible, because multiple hearsay is never admissible.
D. It is admissible, because the caller knew what the neighbor said.
E. It is inadmissible, because no one can identify the neighbor.

Answer: B
- A: The recording might be a record, but the caller and neighbor had no business duty. Their statements need their own exceptions (Johnson v. Lutz; Rule 805).
- B: Correct. Rule 805 admits hearsay within hearsay if each part fits an exception. The neighbor screamed about a shooting while under its stress (Rule 803(2)). The caller described what she was perceiving, the neighbor's arrival and words, while or immediately after perceiving it (Rule 803(1)), and while excited. This is a civil case, so there is no Confrontation Clause issue.
- C: Rule 805 permits multiple hearsay when each layer is covered.
- D: The caller's personal knowledge covers only what the neighbor said. That neighbor's statement still needs its own exception for its truth.
- E: Identity isn't required if the circumstances show perception.
Rule: Rule 805: split multi-level hearsay and fit each layer to an exception. A caller's description of what she is hearing now can be a present sense impression, and the inner statement needs its own route.

## Final 11 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(2)(D): no personal knowledge needed
A forklift strikes a visitor at a warehouse. The next day supervisor Vance, who was off site when it happened and heard about it secondhand, emails the company's owner: "Our operator hit a visitor because he was going too fast." Reporting incidents to the owner is part of Vance's job. In the visitor's suit against the company, the visitor offers the email. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Excluded, because Rule 602 requires every declarant to have personal knowledge.
B. Excluded, because a statement to the employee's own employer isn't made to outsiders.
C. Admitted, because it is an employee's statement within the scope of her employment.
D. Admitted only if the company authorized Vance to speak for it to the public.
E. Admitted, because any email kept by a company is a record of regularly conducted activity.

Answer: C
- A: Under Mahlandt, Rule 801(d)(2) has no personal-knowledge requirement.
- B: Mahlandt admitted statements made inside an organization. An opposing party's statement doesn't have to reach outsiders.
- C: Correct. Rule 801(d)(2)(D) covers a statement by a party's employee on a matter within the scope of the relationship while it existed. Mahlandt requires no personal knowledge.
- D: Authority to speak is Rule 801(d)(2)(C). Rule 801(d)(2)(D) needs only the employment relationship and scope.
- E: Rule 803(6) requires a regular practice and a qualified foundation. An email isn't a business record just because a company kept it.
Rule: Rule 801(d)(2)(D) admits an employee's statement against the employer if it concerns a matter within the scope of employment and was made while employed. Personal knowledge isn't required (Mahlandt).

## Final 11 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(1)(B)(ii): rebutting a memory attack
Lucia Reyes, the only eyewitness to a hit-and-run, testifies that the car was a silver hatchback. On cross-examination, defense counsel brings out that it was dark, that two years have passed, and that Reyes is now “filling in” details she can't really remember. Counsel doesn't suggest that Reyes has any motive to lie. The prosecution then offers a recorded call Reyes made to a friend two hours after the crash, in which she said the car was a silver hatchback.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because a prior consistent statement is admissible only if it was given under penalty of perjury.
B. Admit it as substantive evidence, because any prior consistent statement of a testifying witness is not hearsay.
C. Admit it as substantive evidence under Rule 801(d)(1)(B)(ii), because it rebuts the attack on her memory.
D. Admit it only to rehabilitate Reyes's credibility, with an instruction that it is not evidence of the car's type.
E. Exclude it, because under Tome a consistent statement is admissible only if made before a motive to fabricate arose.

Answer: C
- A: Only the old Rule 801(d)(1)(A), for inconsistent statements, had an oath requirement, and the December 1, 2026 amendment removed it. Rule 801(d)(1)(B) never required one.
- B: A consistent statement is not hearsay only when it rebuts a charge of fabrication or improper motive (clause (i)) or rehabilitates against another kind of attack (clause (ii)). Without an attack, offering it would be improper bolstering.
- C: Correct. Since 2014, Rule 801(d)(1)(B)(ii) admits a testifying witness's prior consistent statement, as substantive evidence, to rehabilitate credibility attacked on another ground, such as faulty memory. A statement made two hours after the crash, when her memory was fresh, rebuts the claim that she is now filling in details.
- D: Before 2014 that was the usual approach for consistent statements outside clause (i). The amendment made statements that properly rehabilitate under clause (ii) substantive evidence, so no limiting instruction is needed.
- E: Tome's premotive rule applies to clause (i), statements offered to rebut a charge of recent fabrication or improper motive. The defense attacked memory, not motive, so clause (ii) applies.
Rule: Rule 801(d)(1)(B): a testifying declarant's prior consistent statement is not hearsay if offered (i) to rebut a charge of recent fabrication or improper influence or motive (made before the motive arose, under Tome) or (ii) to rehabilitate credibility attacked on another ground, such as faulty memory.

## Final 11 · Q17 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(1) and Crawford: an opportunity not used
At a preliminary hearing on a robbery charge against Nate Cole, eyewitness Gina Pratt testified for the prosecution that she saw Cole take the money. As a matter of strategy, Cole's lawyer chose not to cross-examine her. By trial, Pratt herself has been charged in an unrelated case. When called, she invokes her privilege against self-incrimination, and the court upholds it. The prosecution offers the transcript of her preliminary-hearing testimony.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because Cole never actually cross-examined Pratt at the preliminary hearing.
B. Exclude it, because a witness who invokes a privilege isn't unavailable under Rule 804(a).
C. Admit it under the hearsay rule, but exclude it under the Confrontation Clause as testimonial.
D. Admit it, because Pratt is unavailable and Cole had an opportunity and similar motive to cross.
E. Admit it, because testimony given at a preliminary hearing isn't testimonial under Crawford.

Answer: D
- A: Both Rule 804(b)(1) and Crawford ask whether the party had an opportunity to cross-examine. A strategic choice not to use the opportunity doesn't defeat it.
- B: Rule 804(a)(1) defines a declarant as unavailable when the court rules that a privilege exempts her from testifying.
- C: Crawford is satisfied where the declarant is unavailable and the defendant had a prior opportunity to cross-examine. Both conditions are met.
- D: Correct. Pratt is unavailable under Rule 804(a)(1). Under Rule 804(b)(1), her former testimony is admissible against Cole, who had an opportunity and a similar motive to develop it at the hearing on the same charge. The testimony is testimonial, but Crawford is satisfied by her unavailability and Cole's prior opportunity to cross.
- E: Prior testimony at a preliminary hearing is at the core of what Crawford calls testimonial.
Rule: Rule 804(b)(1) and Crawford both turn on an opportunity to cross-examine, not on whether it was used. A privilege ruling makes the declarant unavailable under Rule 804(a)(1).

## Final 11 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(1): a calm 911 call
At 4:12 p.m., a motorist calls 911 and, in an even, unhurried voice, says: “There’s a red Silverado, plate JKT-4471, that just passed me on I-95 northbound doing probably a hundred, weaving across all three lanes. I’m at mile marker 12.” The caller gives no name and hangs up.

At 4:21 p.m., a red Silverado with that plate crosses the median near mile marker 19 and kills another driver. In the estate’s wrongful-death suit against the Silverado’s driver, the estate offers the 911 recording, properly authenticated, to prove that the Silverado was speeding and weaving. The caller has never been located.

Is the recording admissible?

A. No, because the caller’s calm demeanor shows that he was not under the stress of excitement caused by a startling event.
B. Yes, as a present sense impression, because the caller described the truck’s driving while or immediately after perceiving it.
C. No, because a call made to a government 911 operator is testimonial.
D. No, because an unidentified declarant’s personal knowledge cannot be established.
E. Yes, but only if the estate shows that it could not locate the caller despite reasonable efforts.

Answer: B
- A: Calm demeanor would hurt an excited-utterance argument under 803(2), but 803(1) has no excitement requirement.
- B: Correct. The statement describes an event while or immediately after the declarant perceived it.
- C: The Confrontation Clause applies only in criminal prosecutions. Even in a criminal case, a 911 call reporting an ongoing danger is typically nontestimonial under Davis v. Washington.
- D: Personal knowledge can be inferred from the content and circumstances of the statement. Courts scrutinize anonymous declarants carefully, but anonymity is not a per se bar.
- E: Availability is immaterial under Rule 803. Unavailability matters only for the Rule 804 exceptions.
Rule: A present sense impression is a statement describing or explaining an event or condition, made while or immediately after the declarant perceived it. FRE 803(1). Unlike an excited utterance, it requires no startling event and no excitement; its reliability comes from contemporaneity, which leaves no time for calculated misstatement. Rule 803 exceptions apply whether or not the declarant is available.

## Final 11 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(4): statements by a parent; identity of the abuser
In a civil battery suit brought on behalf of a four-year-old against her stepfather, the plaintiff calls the emergency-room doctor. She testifies that the child's mother brought the child in and said, "Her left arm has been swollen and she's cried every time it's touched since Tuesday. It happened when her stepfather yanked her up by that arm." The doctor's records don't reflect any discussion of the child's safety at home, and no one has testified about how the stepfather's identity bore on treatment.

Which part of the mother's statement is admissible under Rule 803(4)?

A. None, because Rule 803(4) covers only statements made by the patient herself.
B. All of it, because an abuser's identity is always pertinent to a child's treatment.
C. All of it, because the doctor actually relied on it in treating the child's arm.
D. None, because the mother had an obvious motive to blame the stepfather.
E. All but the stepfather's identity, absent a foundation that it bore on treatment.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 803(4) isn't limited to the patient. The ACN covers statements by a family member seeking care for the patient.
- B: Some courts admit the identity of a household abuser, but the proponent must show how identity affected treatment, and this record has no such foundation (State v. Smith).
- C: Reliance doesn't extend the exception to statements not reasonably pertinent to diagnosis or treatment.
- D: A motive to fabricate is a 403 or weight argument. It doesn't defeat a statement that fits the exception.
- E: Correct. The symptoms, when they began, and the general cause (being yanked by the arm) are pertinent to diagnosing an arm injury. Who did it is ordinarily not pertinent. Without testimony that identity mattered to treatment, such as safety planning, it doesn't qualify (State v. Smith).
Rule: Rule 803(4) covers statements, including by a parent for a child, describing history, symptoms, inception, and general cause, if reasonably pertinent to diagnosis or treatment. Fault and identity require a showing of pertinence (State v. Smith).

## Final 11 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 805: outsider in a business record
Facts: Wren sues Harbor Ferry Co. after falling on a gangway. Deckhand Ito completed the company's incident log an hour after the fall. Harbor Ferry requires a log entry after every incident, and its claims manager tells deckhands to fill them out "so we have our side on paper if there is a lawsuit." One entry reads: "Passenger Alvarez told me the plaintiff was running when she fell." Another reads: "I checked the gangway chain ten minutes before the fall and it was hooked." Alvarez, a passenger with no tie to the company, cannot be found. Ito has died.
Harbor Ferry offers the first log entry to prove Wren was running. Assume Rule 803(6) covers Ito's entry. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admitted, because the entire entry is a record of a regularly conducted activity.
B. Admitted, because Alvarez is unavailable and so the exception for his statement is met.
C. Excluded, because a business record may never contain anyone else's statement.
D. Admitted, because Ito had a business duty to record what he was told.
E. Excluded unless Alvarez's statement has its own exception, because he had no business duty.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 803(6) covers the layer supplied by people in the business. It doesn't carry an outsider's statement with it (Johnson v. Lutz).
- B: Unavailability under Rule 804(a) is only a precondition. It isn't itself an exception.
- C: A record can hold an insider's report or an outsider's statement that has its own exception. Rule 805 asks only that each layer qualify.
- D: Ito's duty covers his own layer. The informant must also have a business duty or another exception must apply.
- E: Correct. Under Rule 805, each layer needs its own route. As in Johnson v. Lutz, an outsider has no business duty, so Alvarez's statement needs its own exception.
Rule: Under Rule 805, each layer of hearsay needs its own exception. An outsider's statement in a business record is a separate layer because the outsider has no business duty to report (Johnson v. Lutz).

## Final 11 · Q21 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Michigan v. Bryant: an armed shooter at large
At 3:25 a.m., officers responding to a “man down” call find Rico lying in a gas station parking lot with a gunshot wound to the abdomen, in obvious pain and having difficulty breathing. The shooting happened about 25 minutes earlier, six blocks away; Rico drove himself to the station. Before the paramedics arrive, officers ask, “What happened? Who shot you? Where did it happen?” Rico answers: “Bricks shot me, through the back door of his house on Seventh. I was talking to him through the door and he shot.”

Rico survives, but at Bramlett’s (“Bricks”) trial for attempted murder, Rico refuses to testify despite a court order and a contempt citation. The prosecution offers the officers’ testimony repeating Rico’s answers. Bramlett objects on hearsay and Confrontation Clause grounds.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude the statements, because statements made in response to police questioning cannot be excited utterances.
B. Admit the statements as dying declarations, because Rico’s grave wound shows that he believed he was dying.
C. Exclude the statements, because statements identifying a perpetrator to police are testimonial per se.
D. Admit them, because they are excited utterances made to help police meet an ongoing emergency involving an armed shooter at large.
E. Exclude the statements, because 25 minutes had passed and Rico had driven six blocks, so the statements were the product of reflection.

Answer: D
- A: Responding to a question does not destroy spontaneity. Courts routinely admit excited answers to questions like “What happened?”
- B: Rule 804(b)(2) applies only in homicide prosecutions and civil cases. This is a prosecution for attempted murder. The record also does not show that Rico believed death was imminent.
- C: Bryant rejected any per se rule. Identification of an armed assailant at large can serve the emergency purpose.
- D: Correct. This is the Bryant analysis paired with a straightforward Rule 803(2) foundation.
- E: There is no fixed time limit for excited utterances. The question is whether the declarant was still under the stress of the event, and Rico plainly was.
Rule: An excited utterance is a statement relating to a startling event made while the declarant was under the stress of excitement it caused. FRE 803(2). Statements to police are nontestimonial when, viewed objectively from the perspectives of both the declarant and the questioners, their primary purpose is to enable police to meet an ongoing emergency. Michigan v. Bryant. A dying declaration is admissible only in a homicide prosecution or a civil case. FRE 804(b)(2).

## Final 11 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Rule 106 as amended in 2023
The prosecution has an officer testify that the defendant said in an unrecorded interview, "I owned the gun." The defendant asks to have the officer add the rest of the sentence: "but I sold it months ago." The prosecution objects on hearsay grounds. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Overruled, because anything a defendant says is admissible when offered for the defense.
B. Sustained, because a defendant must testify to explain a statement.
C. Sustained, because a defendant can't offer his own statement.
D. Overruled, because Rule 106 now reaches oral statements and overrides hearsay.
E. Sustained, because Rule 106 covers only recorded and written statements.

Answer: D
- A: The rule is narrow. The statement must actually correct a misimpression.
- B: Nothing in Rule 106 requires a defendant to testify.
- C: Rule 801(d)(2) doesn't help him, but Rule 106 overrides the hearsay objection.
- D: Correct. As amended in 2023, Rule 106 covers oral statements and allows completion over a hearsay objection when fairness requires it. The ACN uses this gun example.
- E: That was the old text. Since 2023, Rule 106 covers oral statements too.
Rule: Since 2023, Rule 106 reaches oral statements and overrides a hearsay objection, but only where the completing statement corrects a misimpression. Fisher (2022) prints the old text.

## Final 11 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Scheffer: a per se polygraph ban survives
At his federal trial for drug possession, a defendant offers results of a polygraph examination that he passed. The test was administered by a qualified examiner, and the defendant argues that excluding it violates his constitutional right to present a defense. The court excludes it under a rule barring all polygraph evidence.

How will a reviewing court most likely rule?

A. Reverse, because excluding reliable defense evidence violates Chambers.
B. Reverse, because Holmes forbids rules that judge defense evidence categorically.
C. Reverse, because Daubert requires case-by-case review of every technique.
D. Affirm, because the defendant chose not to take the stand himself.
E. Affirm, because a per se ban on polygraph evidence is a rational rule.

Answer: E
- A: Chambers addressed rules applied mechanistically to critical, trustworthy evidence. Polygraph evidence isn't shown to be trustworthy.
- B: Holmes struck a rule that ignored the defense evidence's own strength. A polygraph ban rests on doubts about the evidence itself.
- C: Daubert governs admission under Rule 702. It doesn't make a categorical ban unconstitutional.
- D: The defendant's decision whether to testify doesn't decide the constitutional question.
