- E: Correct, though it means the jury never hears exculpatory test results. The Scheffer case upheld a per se ban on polygraph evidence. The outline notes that polygraph evidence is still excluded almost everywhere. Contrast Holmes's arbitrary rule.
Rule: The right to present a defense yields to rules that aren't arbitrary or disproportionate to their purposes. Under the Scheffer case, a per se polygraph ban is permissible. Chambers and Holmes condemn mechanistic or arbitrary exclusions.

## Final 11 · Q24 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 701: a business owner's particularized knowledge
In a suit against a contractor whose construction blocked the entrance to her bakery for five months, owner Mae Lin testifies to her lost profits. She has run the bakery for 12 years, keeps its books herself, and bases her estimate on her own sales records, seasonal patterns, and costs. She isn't designated as an expert, and the contractor objects that lost-profit projections require expert testimony.

How should the court rule?

A. Sustain it, because any projection of lost profits requires specialized knowledge.
B. Overrule it, because her opinion rests on firsthand knowledge of her own business.
C. Sustain it, because a lay opinion may not address damages, an ultimate issue.
D. Overrule it, but only if Lin's records are admitted under Rule 803(6) first.
E. Sustain it, because a party can't give opinion testimony about her own claim.

Answer: B
- A: The 2000 ACN to Rule 701 recognizes a business owner's lay opinion based on particularized knowledge of the business.
- B: Correct. Under Rule 701 and the 2000 ACN, a business owner may give lay opinion on her business's value or projected profits. Such opinions are based on particularized knowledge of her own business, not on specialized expertise. They are rationally based on perception and helpful.
- C: Rule 704(a) allows opinions on ultimate issues. Damages may be the subject of a lay opinion with a proper foundation.
- D: Lin testifies from her own knowledge. Admitting the records isn't a prerequisite.
- E: Parties may testify and give otherwise-proper lay opinions. Interest goes to credibility.
Rule: A business owner may give lay opinion under Rule 701 on her own business's value or lost profits, based on particularized knowledge of it, as distinct from specialized expertise under Rule 702.

## Final 11 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Eyewitness experts: Guilbert
Defendant Ross is identified by a stranger who saw him during a robbery at gunpoint. Ross offers a psychologist to explain that weapon focus and stress impair memory and that confidence doesn't track accuracy. In a jurisdiction following Guilbert, which testimony is proper?

A. General research on factors that affect identification.
B. An opinion that this witness's identification is mistaken.
C. An opinion that Ross was not the person who robbed the store.
D. An instruction on how the jury must weigh the identification.
E. None of the above, because eyewitness memory is common knowledge.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under State v. Guilbert, an expert may describe general factors relevant to an identification that many jurors wouldn't expect.
- B: State v. Guilbert bars an opinion on whether a particular eyewitness is accurate.
- C: This goes further than general factors and tells the jury what result to reach.
- D: Instructions on the law come from the judge, not the expert (Hygh).
- E: State v. Guilbert overruled the earlier view that this subject is common knowledge. General research is the correct choice.
Rule: Eyewitness experts may explain counterintuitive findings but may not vouch for or against a particular witness (State v. Guilbert).

## Final 11 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Forensic testimony: limits
A firearms examiner plans to testify that a bullet came from the defendant's gun "with absolute certainty and a zero error rate." The method is subjective, and no error-rate study was offered. Based on the 2023 ACN and recent practice, what is the most likely outcome?

A. The jury must decide reliability, because the method is subjective.
B. The examiner may testify with limits, without claims of absolute certainty.
C. The field is excluded, because all forensic evidence is unreliable.
D. The testimony comes in as stated, because certainty goes to weight.
E. The testimony comes in, because courts accepted it under Frye for decades.

Answer: B
- A: Reliability is a Rule 104(a) question for the judge.
- B: Correct. The 2023 ACN says forensic experts should avoid claims of absolute certainty for a subjective method, and courts usually admit forensic testimony with such limits.
- C: Courts usually limit forensic testimony rather than exclude it outright.
- D: Under Rule 702(d) as amended, each opinion must stay within what a reliable application supports.
- E: Frye's acceptance by practitioners doesn't meet Rule 702 today.
Rule: Under Rule 702(d), an opinion must stay within what a reliable application supports. Forensic examiners shouldn't claim absolute certainty for a subjective method.

## Final 11 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Photograph: a witness who didn't take it
In a premises-liability case, the plaintiff offers a photograph of a broken stair tread. The photographer is unknown. The building's maintenance supervisor, who inspected the stairs the morning after the fall, testifies that the photo fairly and accurately shows the stairs as he saw them that morning. The defense objects that no one can say who took the photo, when, or with what camera.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because only the photographer can authenticate a photo.
B. Admit it, because the supervisor says it fairly shows what he saw.
C. Exclude it, because no one has shown that the camera worked reliably.
D. Admit it, because photographs are self-authenticating under Rule 902.
E. Exclude it unless the original negative or file is produced.

Answer: B
- A: Under the pictorial-testimony approach, any witness who saw the scene can authenticate the photo.
- B: Correct. The defense's points sound substantial, but under the "pictorial testimony" theory (Rule 901(b)(1)), a witness with knowledge who testifies that the image fairly and accurately shows what he saw is enough. Who took it doesn't matter. Timing questions go to weight.
- C: The silent-witness foundation (Rule 901(b)(9)) is needed when no witness saw what the image shows. Here the supervisor did.
- D: Photographs aren't listed in Rule 902.
- E: The plaintiff isn't proving the content of the photo as such, and a print is an "original" under Rule 1001(d) anyway.
Rule: Photos and videos are authenticated either by a witness who saw the scene and says the image is fair and accurate ("pictorial testimony") or, without an eyewitness, by showing a reliable process ("silent witness," Rule 901(b)(9); State v. Luke).

## Final 11 · Q28 · Privileges — Rule 501: a state privilege for a state-law claim
In a federal diversity suit for defamation under state law, the plaintiff seeks to depose the defendant's father about what his 30-year-old son told him in confidence about the statements at issue. The state where the court sits recognizes a parent-child privilege by statute. Federal common law recognizes none.

What is the best ruling?

A. He must testify, because federal courts recognize no parent-child privilege.
B. He must testify, because the son was an adult when he confided.
C. He may refuse, because state privilege law governs this state-law claim.
D. He may refuse, because Jaffee requires adopting any state's privilege.
E. He must testify, because the privilege applies only in criminal cases.

Answer: C
- A: True of federal common law (In re Grand Jury Proceedings), which makes this tempting. But Rule 501's second sentence sends state-law claims in civil cases to state privilege law.
- B: Whether the state's privilege covers adult children depends on the state statute, and the facts say the state recognizes it here.
- C: Correct, though the federal rule rejects any parent-child privilege. Rule 501 provides that "in a civil case, state law governs privilege regarding a claim or defense for which state law supplies the rule of decision." Defamation under state law qualifies.
- D: Jaffee looked to state consensus as "experience" in shaping federal common law. It didn't require adopting every state's privileges.
- E: The scope of the state's privilege is a question of state law. Nothing suggests it is limited to criminal cases.
Rule: Under Rule 501, federal common law governs privilege, except that in civil cases, state law governs privilege for claims or defenses governed by state law. No federal parent-child privilege exists (In re Grand Jury Proceedings).

## Final 11 · Q29 · Privileges — Crime-fraud: advice about past acts vs. future crimes
Pharmacist Omar Stone consults a lawyer and asks, in confidence, two questions: (1) whether he could be prosecuted for opioid prescriptions he filled last year without checking the prescribers, and (2) how he could structure cash deposits from future sales so the bank wouldn't file reports. The lawyer answers both, not realizing that Stone plans to keep selling illegally. A grand jury later subpoenas the lawyer.

What must the lawyer disclose?

A. Both conversations, because once a client discusses crimes, the privilege is forfeited for the whole consultation.
B. Nothing, because the lawyer didn't know of Stone's plan, and the exception requires the lawyer's knowing participation.
C. Nothing, until the government proves beyond a reasonable doubt that Stone committed a crime.
D. The conversation about last year's prescriptions, because the privilege doesn't protect admissions of completed crimes.
E. The conversation about future deposits, but not the one about last year's prescriptions.

Answer: E
- A: The exception applies communication by communication. Seeking advice about past conduct remains fully protected, even in the same meeting.
- B: The exception turns on the client's purpose. A client who seeks advice to further a crime or fraud loses the privilege for that communication even if the lawyer is unaware of the plan.
- C: Zolin set a lower threshold for in camera review, and lower courts use standards such as a prima facie showing or probable cause to establish the exception. Proof beyond a reasonable doubt isn't required.
- D: That reverses the rule. Confidential discussion of past crimes, to get legal advice, is exactly what the privilege protects.
- E: Correct. The privilege's purpose ceases where the advice sought concerns future wrongdoing rather than past wrongdoing (Wigmore, quoted in United States v. Zolin). Asking how to avoid bank reporting on future illegal proceeds seeks help with a future crime, so that conversation isn't privileged. Asking about exposure for last year's conduct is the heart of the privilege.
Rule: The crime-fraud exception removes protection from communications made to obtain advice or help in committing a future or ongoing crime or fraud. Communications about past crimes remain privileged, and the client's purpose controls (United States v. Zolin).

## Final 11 · Q30 · Privileges — Spousal privilege: crimes against a child of either spouse
Federal prosecutors charge Dwight Hale with abusing his wife's nine-year-old son on a military base. His wife, Carla, saw some of the abuse. She is subpoenaed by the government and asserts her privilege not to testify adversely against her husband. The government argues the privilege doesn't apply.

What is the most likely ruling?

A. Carla may refuse, because under Trammel the witness-spouse alone decides.
B. Carla may refuse, because the defendant-spouse holds the privilege.
C. Carla must testify to what she saw, but may refuse to repeat his statements.
D. Carla may refuse, because the privilege applies in every federal criminal case.
E. Carla must testify, because the charge is a crime against her child.

Answer: E
- A: Trammel does give her the choice in the usual case, which makes this the tempting answer. But most courts recognize an exception for crimes against the spouse or a child of either.
- B: Trammel gave the privilege to the witness-spouse, not the defendant.
- C: The confidential-communications privilege has a similar exception for crimes against the spouse or a child of either. Her testimony about Dwight's statements is likely compellable too.
- D: Too broad. The exception for crimes against family members limits the privilege.
- E: Correct, though it compels a spouse to testify against her will. The outline notes that most courts deny the privilege when the charge is a crime against the other spouse or a child of either. The proposed (never enacted) spousal-privilege rule included the same exception. The policy of preserving marital harmony carries little weight against protecting the child.
Rule: The testimonial privilege belongs to the witness-spouse (Trammel), but most courts deny it, and the confidences privilege, when the defendant-spouse is charged with a crime against the other spouse or a child of either.

## Final 12 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Rule 603: an affirmation instead of an oath
In a federal civil-rights trial, the plaintiff calls Ruth Okafor, who saw the arrest. When the clerk asks her to swear to tell the truth “so help me God,” Okafor says her faith forbids swearing oaths, but she will solemnly promise to tell the truth and understands that lying would be perjury. Defense counsel objects that she can't testify without an oath. The judge agrees and excuses her.

Was the ruling correct?

A. Yes, because her religious objection could be used to impeach her, so the court may keep her off the stand.
B. No, because Rule 603 allows an affirmation in a form designed to impress the duty on her conscience.
C. No, but only because state law, which governs competency in every civil case, allows affirmations.
D. Yes, because Rule 603 requires an oath, and a witness who refuses one is incompetent under Rule 601.
E. No, because a witness with personal knowledge may testify without any oath or affirmation if she prefers.

Answer: B
- A: Rule 610 forbids using a witness's religious beliefs to attack or support her credibility. And a possible impeachment is never a ground to keep a competent witness off the stand.
- B: Correct. Rule 603 requires “an oath or affirmation to testify truthfully” in “a form designed to impress that duty on the witness's conscience.” A solemn promise backed by an understanding of perjury satisfies it, and under Rule 601 every person is competent unless a rule provides otherwise.
- C: Rule 601's second sentence applies state competency law only to a claim or defense for which state law supplies the rule of decision. A federal civil-rights claim is governed by federal law, and Rule 603 already allows the affirmation.
- D: Rule 603 expressly permits an affirmation. Okafor is willing to give one, so there is no basis for treating her as incompetent.
- E: Rule 603 requires an oath or affirmation before testifying. Personal knowledge under Rule 602 is a separate requirement, not a substitute.
Rule: Under Rule 601 every person is competent unless a rule provides otherwise. Rule 603 requires an oath or affirmation in a form designed to impress the duty to tell the truth on the witness's conscience, and Rule 610 bars using religious beliefs to attack or support credibility.

## Final 12 · Q2 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 104(a) vs. Rule 104(b)
Four preliminary questions come up in a federal drug-conspiracy trial. Which one does the judge decide for herself, by a preponderance of the evidence, without being bound by the rules of evidence other than privilege?

A. Whether the defendant actually signed a letter the government offers against him.
B. Whether the government’s chemist is qualified to testify as an expert.
C. Whether the defendant knew about a threat that supplies his supposed motive.
D. Whether the defendant made an uncharged drug sale that the government offers under Rule 404(b).
E. Whether the defendant wrote a drug ledger found in a co-conspirator’s house.

Answer: B
- A: Authorship goes to authenticity, a conditional-relevance question under Rules 104(b) and 901(a). The judge asks only whether a jury could reasonably find that the defendant signed it.
- B: Correct. 104(a) gives the judge questions about whether “a witness is qualified, a privilege exists, or evidence is admissible.” She decides them herself by a preponderance (Bourjaily) and may consider inadmissible evidence. The other questions ask whether a fact exists on which relevance depends, which 104(b) leaves to the jury, subject only to a sufficiency screen.
- C: Knowledge is a fact on which the relevance of the motive evidence depends. Under 104(b) and Cox, the judge asks only whether a reasonable jury could find it.
- D: Huddleston held that whether the other act happened is a 104(b) question. The judge asks whether a jury could reasonably find it by a preponderance, without deciding it herself.
- E: Authorship is a fact on which the ledger’s relevance depends. The judge decides only whether there is enough evidence for a jury to find that he wrote it.
Rule: Rule 104(a): the judge decides qualification, privilege, and admissibility by a preponderance, unbound by the evidence rules except privilege. 104(b): when relevance depends on a fact, the judge asks only whether a jury could reasonably find it.

## Final 12 · Q3 · Relevance and Rule 403 — A computer animation: foundation beats Rule 403
In a manslaughter trial, the prosecution offers a lifelike computer animation of the fatal confrontation, prepared by its accident-reconstruction expert to illustrate her testimony. The animation shows the victim with his hands raised in surrender. No witness testified to the victim's posture, and the expert's report doesn't address it. The defense objects.

What is the defense's best objection?

A. The animation isn't shown to fairly and accurately reflect the evidence.
B. It is unfairly prejudicial, because lifelike animations persuade more than testimony.
C. It is hearsay, because it repeats the expert's out-of-court conclusions.
D. It violates the best evidence rule, because a video of the real event is required.
E. It is improper character evidence that portrays the defendant as violent.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. The Rule 403 argument feels stronger because the animation is so vivid, but an illustrative animation must first be shown to fairly and accurately depict what the evidence shows. The class's Chauvin animation example makes this point: authentication may be the better objection. An animation that invents the victim's raised hands has no foundation for that detail. That is a cleaner ground than Rule 403, which tilts toward admission.
- B: A real objection, and the court will weigh it (Rule 107 applies the 403 dangers to illustrative aids). But persuasiveness alone rarely substantially outweighs probative value. The missing foundation is the stronger point.
- C: An animation illustrating an expert's live testimony isn't an out-of-court statement offered for its truth.
- D: The best evidence rule applies only when a party proves the content of a writing, recording, or photograph. No recording of the event exists, and the animation doesn't claim to be one.
- E: Nothing about the animation suggests propensity.
Rule: Demonstrative and illustrative evidence must first fairly and accurately depict what the evidence shows (Rule 901; Rule 107 for illustrative aids), then pass Rule 403. When a detail has no basis in the evidence, the foundation objection is usually stronger than prejudice.

## Final 12 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rules 408 and 409: what each protects
Right after a rear-end collision, Driver A walks to Driver B’s window and says, “Are you hurt? I’ll pay for your ER visit. I’m so sorry; I was looking at my GPS.” Two months later, B’s lawyer sends A a letter demanding $25,000. A’s lawyer disputes both fault and the amount. At a settlement meeting, A says, “Fine, I wasn’t watching the road, but $25,000 is absurd. I’ll pay $7,000.” Talks fail and B sues. Which of A’s statements may B introduce to prove A was negligent?

A. None of them, because every statement concerned the same claim and the parties later tried to settle it.
B. “I was looking at my GPS” and “I wasn’t watching the road.”
C. “I’m so sorry; I was looking at my GPS” only.
D. All of them, because Driver A volunteered each statement before any lawsuit was filed.
E. “I’ll pay for your ER visit” and “I was looking at my GPS.”

Answer: C
- A: The later negotiations don’t reach back to protect A’s statements at the scene. No claim was disputed then, and 409 protects only the offer to pay medical expenses.
- B: The second admission was made during compromise negotiations after a demand and a dispute over fault and amount. 408(a)(2) excludes conduct or statements made in those talks, not just the dollar offer. This is broader than 409.
- C: Correct. The offer to pay the ER visit is excluded by 409, but 409 reaches only the offer itself, not statements made with it, so the apology and admission at the scene come in. Everything A said at the settlement meeting, both the admission and the $7,000 offer, falls under 408 because it was said in compromise negotiations over a disputed claim.
- D: Filing doesn’t matter. Rule 409 excludes the offer to pay the ER bill, and Rule 408 excludes statements made in negotiations over a claim disputed as to fault and amount, which A’s settlement-meeting statement was.
- E: Offering to pay medical expenses is exactly what 409 excludes when offered to prove liability. Only the accompanying statement of fault survives.
Rule: Rule 409 excludes only offers to pay medical expenses, not statements made with them. 408 excludes offers and statements made in compromise negotiations over a disputed claim.

## Final 12 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 411: redact the insurance, keep the admission
Immediately after his car hit a cyclist, Pete Sharp told her, "Don't worry, my insurance will cover everything. I wasn't watching the road." In her negligence suit, the cyclist offers the whole statement. Sharp objects to all of it under Rule 411.

What is the best ruling?

A. Admit all of it, because every word is Sharp's own statement.
B. Exclude all of it, because the statement mentions liability insurance.
C. Admit all of it, because Rule 411 covers policies, not statements.
D. Exclude all of it under Rule 409, because it promises to pay.
E. Admit the admission of fault, but redact the mention of insurance.

Answer: E
- A: The hearsay objection fails as to all of it, but Rule 411 is a separate objection to the insurance reference.
- B: Rule 411 bars only insurance evidence offered to prove negligence. It doesn't sweep away an admission of fault in the same sentence.
- C: Rule 411 covers "evidence that a person was or was not insured against liability," in any form.
- D: Saying insurance will cover it isn't an offer to pay medical expenses, and Rule 409 wouldn't reach the admission of fault anyway.
- E: Correct, though the cyclist would rather the jury hear it all. The admission of fault comes in under Rule 801(d)(2)(A). The insurance reference would invite a decision on an improper basis, so it is redacted where it can be severed without distorting the meaning.
Rule: Rule 411 bars insurance evidence offered to prove negligence. When insurance is mentioned alongside an admission, the usual result is to admit the admission and redact the insurance reference.

## Final 12 · Q6 · Character Evidence — Reverse 404(b): still no propensity
Police stop a car and find a handgun under the driver’s seat. The driver, a convicted felon, is charged with unlawful possession. He offers evidence that his passenger was convicted three years earlier of possessing a different handgun, arguing that it shows the gun was the passenger’s. The prosecution objects. How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because it shows only that the passenger tends to carry guns, which is propensity reasoning.
B. Exclude it, because a defendant may never offer evidence of another person’s crimes or bad acts.
C. Admit it, because Stevens applies a relaxed standard to any other-act evidence a defendant offers.
D. Admit it, because the propensity ban protects only criminal defendants, not third parties like the passenger.
E. Admit it under Rule 404(a)(2), because a defendant may offer evidence of a third party’s pertinent trait.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. This is Problem 3.10 (United States v. Williams, misreading Stevens). Reverse 404(b) evidence gets a lower similarity threshold, but the defense still must show a non-propensity inference, such as enough similarity to suggest that the same person committed both crimes (Stevens). A prior possession of a different gun shows only propensity.
- B: Reverse 404(b) evidence is admissible in a proper case. In Stevens, a similar robbery nearby whose victim said Stevens wasn’t the attacker was improperly excluded.
- C: Stevens relaxed the degree of similarity needed (no “signature” required) and the 403 balance, but it didn’t drop the requirement of a non-propensity theory of relevance.
- D: 404(b)(1) bars other-act evidence about “a person,” not just the defendant. Williams rejected the idea that Stevens licenses defense propensity evidence.
- E: Rule 404(a)(2)(A)–(B) concern the defendant’s and the victim’s character. The passenger is neither, and Rule 404(b)(1) bars the propensity inference about “a person.”
Rule: Reverse 404(b) (Stevens): a lower similarity threshold and a 401 + 403 (waste, confusion) analysis, but still no propensity inference (Williams).

## Final 12 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Plan vs. pattern
In which case is the other act best offered to prove a plan under Rule 404(b)(2)?

A. A child-abuse defendant has abused other children in the same church.
B. A bank-robbery defendant stole a van the day before and used it for the getaway.
C. A forgery defendant has a long history of signing other people's checks.
D. A bank-robbery defendant robbed three other banks over the past five years.
E. A shoplifting defendant has shoplifted from similar stores many times before.

Answer: B
- A: This is the argument State v. Kirsch rejected: a systematic course of conduct isn't a plan.
- B: Correct. Plan requires acts that are steps toward one overall scheme. Stealing a getaway van is preparation for this robbery.
- C: A history of similar acts is propensity unless linked to one scheme.
- D: Repeated robberies show a pattern, not parts of one scheme (State v. Kirsch).
- E: A mere pattern is insufficient to establish a plan. This shows only a tendency to shoplift.
Rule: Plan under Rule 404(b)(2) requires that the acts be parts of one overall scheme. A pattern or systematic course of conduct isn't enough (State v. Kirsch).

## Final 12 · Q8 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(a)(2)(B): the accused’s same trait
A defendant charged with assault claims the alleged victim attacked first. He calls a witness who testifies that the victim has a reputation for violence. In rebuttal, the prosecution offers (1) a witness who testifies that the victim has a reputation as a peaceful person, (2) a witness who testifies that the defendant has a reputation for violence, and (3) a bartender who testifies that the defendant broke a man’s jaw in a bar fight last year. The defendant didn’t offer evidence of his own character. Which rebuttal evidence is admissible?

A. Items 1 and 2 only.
B. None of them, because only a homicide victim’s peacefulness may be offered by the prosecution.
C. Item 1 only, because the defendant never put his own character in issue.
D. Items 1 and 3, because specific acts may rebut, but the defendant’s reputation isn’t at issue.
E. Items 1, 2, and 3, because the defendant opened the door to violence.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Once the defendant offers the victim’s pertinent trait under 404(a)(2)(B), the prosecution may (i) rebut it and (ii) offer evidence of the defendant’s same trait, even though he never offered his own character (the 2000 amendment). Under 405(a) both must be by reputation or opinion. Item 3 is a specific instance offered on direct, which 405(a) doesn’t allow.
- B: That describes 404(a)(2)(C), which lets the prosecution go first in a homicide case. In any criminal case, once the defendant offers the victim’s trait, 404(a)(2)(B) lets the prosecution respond.
- C: 404(a)(2)(B)(ii) was added in 2000 precisely so that an accused who attacks the victim’s trait opens the door to the same trait in himself.
- D: Backwards. Rule 404(a)(2)(B)(ii) opens the accused’s same trait, but Rule 405(a) limits proof to reputation or opinion, so the bartender’s account of a specific bar fight stays out.
- E: Opening the door doesn’t change the method of proof. Specific acts come in only on cross of a character witness, or where character is an essential element under 405(b).
Rule: Rule 404(a)(2)(B): if the accused offers the victim’s pertinent trait, the prosecution may rebut it and offer the accused’s same trait, by reputation or opinion only (405(a)).

## Final 12 · Q9 · Character Evidence — Weil: deliberate conduct isn't habit
Ada Price sues her allergist, Dr. Seltz, alleging that for years he gave her steroid pills while telling her they were antihistamines. To prove he deceived her, she offers testimony from six other long-term patients that Dr. Seltz told each of them the same thing about the same pills. Dr. Seltz has treated thousands of patients.

What is the most accurate statement about the evidence?

A. It is habit evidence under Rule 406, because the doctor told several patients the same thing.
B. It isn't habit; it may come in only for a non-propensity purpose under Rule 404(b).
C. It is inadmissible, because Rule 404 bars all evidence of other acts in civil cases like this one.
D. It is admissible to show propensity, because the character ban applies only in criminal cases.
E. It is admissible as the routine practice of the doctor's medical office under Rule 406.

Answer: B
- A: Repetition alone doesn't make habit. Habit is a regular, semi-automatic response to a recurring situation.
- B: Correct. Weil held that a doctor's deceptions of eight patients weren't habit: they were deliberate, they varied by patient, and eight out of a large practice isn't "regular." Such evidence may come in, if at all, under Rule 404(b)(2) for a purpose such as intent or plan, subject to Rule 403.
- C: Rule 404 applies in civil cases, but Rule 404(b)(2) permits other acts for non-propensity purposes.
- D: The character ban in Rule 404 applies in civil cases too.
- E: An organization's routine practice must be regular and systematic. A few instances out of thousands of patients don't qualify.
Rule: Under Rule 406, habit is a specific, regular, semi-automatic response to a recurring situation. Deliberate, morally charged conduct is usually character (Weil), admissible only through a Rule 404(b)(2) purpose.

## Final 12 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rehabilitation: only after a character attack
A defense alibi witness testifies that he ate lunch with the defendant at a deli at the time of the robbery. Consider four things that happen on the prosecution’s cross-examination: (1) he admits he is in business with the defendant’s father; (2) he concedes the deli was closed that day and says they must have eaten next door; (3) he admits that he falsely claimed on a car-loan application that he had no other debts; (4) he admits that he and the defendant are close friends. After which of these may the defense offer evidence that the witness has a good reputation for truthfulness?

A. Items 1, 3, and 4.
B. Items 2 and 3.
C. Item 3 only.
D. Items 1 and 4, because bias suggests the witness would lie for the defendant.
E. All four, because each casts doubt on the witness’s credibility.

Answer: C
- A: Items 1 and 4 show a motive to favor the defendant. Bias suggests he is lying now for a reason, not that he is a liar, so it doesn’t open the door (compare Bonner, where the professor suggested “perhaps both courts erred”).
- B: Exposing one contradiction is generally not an attack on character for truthfulness, though pervasive contradiction might be (ACN to 608(a): it “must depend upon the circumstances”).
- C: Correct. 608(a) allows support only after an attack on character for truthfulness. Lying on a loan application (a 608(b) act) is such an attack. Business ties and friendship show bias, which the ACN to 608(a) says isn’t a character attack, and a single contradiction usually isn’t one either (Problem 4.6).
- D: Bias attacks the witness’s motive in this case, not his character for truthfulness, so it doesn’t open the door to Rule 608(a) rehabilitation. Only the false loan application, a Rule 608(b) attack, does.
- E: Credibility can be attacked many ways, but only attacks on character for truthfulness, such as 608(a) opinion or reputation, 608(b) acts, and 609 convictions, open the door to good-character evidence.
Rule: Rule 608(a) rehabilitation is allowed only after a character attack (608(a), 608(b), or 609). Bias and a single contradiction don’t open the door.

## Final 12 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609(b): old crimen falsi
In a civil fraud suit, the defendant wants to impeach the plaintiff's key witness with her conviction, 12 years ago, for filing a false insurance claim. The offense was a state misdemeanor, and she was fined and never confined. Its elements required proof of a knowingly false statement. The defendant gave written notice two months before trial.

What standard governs admission of the conviction?

A. It must be admitted with no balancing, because its elements required proof of a false statement.
B. It is inadmissible, because a misdemeanor can never be used to impeach a witness's truthfulness.
C. It is inadmissible, because Rule 609 bars all convictions that are more than 10 years old.
D. It is admitted unless its probative value is substantially outweighed by unfair prejudice.
E. It comes in only if its probative value, shown by specific facts, substantially outweighs its prejudicial effect.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 609(a)(2) has no balancing, but Rule 609(b) overrides it for old convictions. The time limit applies to crimen falsi too.
- B: Under Rule 609(a)(2), any crime whose elements required a dishonest act or false statement qualifies, whatever the punishment.
- C: Rule 609(b) isn't an absolute bar. It raises the bar and requires notice.
- D: That is Rule 403's standard. It governs recent felonies for non-defendant witnesses, not convictions more than 10 years old.
- E: Correct. More than 10 years have passed since the conviction, and there was no confinement to restart the clock. Rule 609(b) applies even to crimen falsi. It admits the conviction only if probative value, supported by specific facts and circumstances, substantially outweighs prejudicial effect, and the proponent gave reasonable written notice.
Rule: Rule 609(b) applies to any conviction more than 10 years past the later of conviction or release, including crimen falsi. It is a reverse 403 test: probative value must substantially outweigh prejudice, and the proponent must give written notice.

## Final 12 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 412(b)(1)(A): another source of injury
A defendant is charged in federal court with sexual assault and claims he wasn’t the attacker. The examining nurse found bruising and abrasions consistent with intercourse. By written motion filed a month before trial, the defense seeks to offer (1) testimony that the complainant had intercourse with her boyfriend several hours before the alleged assault, to explain the injuries, and (2) testimony that she “was sleeping around with several men” that month. Which ruling is correct?

A. Item 1 is admissible only if the prosecutor offers it, because the accused may not offer evidence under Rule 412(b)(1)(A).
B. Item 1 may be admitted under Rule 412(b)(1)(A) after an in camera hearing, but item 2 is barred.
C. Both are barred, because Rule 412 excludes all evidence of a victim’s other sexual behavior in a criminal case.
D. Item 2 may be admitted to show she had many possible partners, but item 1 is barred as predisposition.
E. Both are admissible, because a defendant claiming misidentification may offer any evidence about other possible sources of the injury.

Answer: B
- A: The source-of-physical-evidence exception is typically invoked by the defense. The “offered by the prosecutor” language belongs to 412(b)(1)(B), on sexual behavior with the accused.
- B: Correct. 412(b)(1)(A) permits specific instances of a victim’s sexual behavior offered to show that someone other than the defendant was the source of semen, injury, or other physical evidence, and the motion followed 412(c)’s procedure (written motion at least 14 days before trial, notice, in camera hearing). Item 2 is general conduct or predisposition, not a specific instance, so it is barred (compare Walker, Problem 5.2).
- C: 412(b)(1) has three criminal-case exceptions, and explaining the source of physical evidence is the first.
- D: Reversed. Item 1 is a specific instance offered to explain the injuries (Rule 412(b)(1)(A)). Item 2 is general evidence of sexual predisposition, which Rule 412 bars.
- E: Only specific instances qualify. General evidence of sexual activity with others is exactly the inference 412 was designed to block.
Rule: Rule 412(b)(1)(A): in criminal cases, specific instances may show another source of semen, injury, or physical evidence; general conduct stays barred. 412(c) requires a written motion 14 days before trial and an in camera hearing.

## Final 12 · Q13 · Definition of Hearsay — Wright v. Doe d. Tatham under the Federal Rules
In a will contest, Arthur Blaine's relatives claim he lacked capacity when he signed his will. The will's proponent offers a dozen letters that Blaine's longtime business associates wrote to him during the year before the will. In them, the associates asked his advice on complex investment and tax matters. The proponent offers the letters to show that people who dealt with him regularly treated him as fully competent.

How should the court treat the letters?

A. As hearsay, because they are implied assertions that the writers believed Blaine competent.
B. As admissible under Rule 803(3), because they show the writers' state of mind about Blaine.
C. As not hearsay, because the writers didn't intend to assert Blaine's competence.
D. As verbal acts, because the letters asking for advice had independent legal effect.
E. As inadmissible evidence of Blaine's character offered to prove his conduct.

Answer: C
- A: That was the common-law answer in Wright v. Doe d. Tatham. The Federal Rules reject it by defining a statement through the declarant's intent to assert.
- B: Using the writers' belief to prove the fact believed (competence) is barred by Rule 803(3)'s exclusion of belief offered to prove the fact believed. The letters aren't hearsay in the first place.
- C: Correct. Rule 801(a) defines a statement as an assertion, or conduct, that the person intended as an assertion. The associates intended to ask advice, not to assert anything about Blaine's competence. As in Zenni, unintended implications aren't statements under the Federal Rules.
- D: Asking for advice creates no legal rights or obligations.
- E: The letters aren't offered to prove Blaine acted in conformity with a character trait.
Rule: Under Rule 801(a), only an intended assertion is a statement. Implied beliefs behind conduct or words not meant to assert them aren't hearsay, which rejects Wright v. Doe d. Tatham (Zenni).

## Final 12 · Q14 · Definition of Hearsay — A witness's own earlier words
In her suit over an intersection collision, Hannah Lowe testifies that the defendant ran a red light. She then adds: "The next morning I told my sister, 'That other driver ran a red light.'" Lowe offers the sister conversation to prove the light was red. The defense hasn't suggested that Lowe fabricated her account or that her memory is faulty.

Is the testimony about what she told her sister admissible?

A. Yes, because Lowe is testifying and can be cross-examined about her own earlier statement.
B. Yes, as a present sense impression, because she described what she had seen.
C. Yes, as an opposing party's statement, because Lowe is a party to the suit.
D. Yes, as a prior consistent statement, because Lowe now testifies consistently with it.
E. No, because it is hearsay, and no exclusion or exception covers it.

Answer: E
- A: Hearsay is a statement the declarant doesn't make "while testifying at the current trial." A witness repeating her own earlier words is still offering hearsay.
- B: Rule 803(1) requires a statement made while or immediately after perceiving the event. The next morning is too late.
- C: Rule 801(d)(2) applies only to statements offered against the party who made them. Lowe is offering her own statement.
- D: Rule 801(d)(1)(B) admits a consistent statement only to rebut a charge of fabrication or improper motive, or to rehabilitate after an attack on another ground. Neither has occurred.
- E: Correct. Lowe's out-of-court statement, offered to prove the light was red, is hearsay even though she now testifies. It fits no exception: it's too late for Rule 803(1), not offered against her, and not offered to rebut an attack under Rule 801(d)(1)(B).
Rule: A testifying witness's own prior statement offered for its truth is hearsay unless it fits Rule 801(d)(1) or an exception. Prior consistent statements need an attack on credibility, and party statements must be offered against the party.

## Final 12 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(2)(D): relationship must exist
(Varies question 11.15.)
Same facts, except the company fired Vance before she sent the email, and she wrote it from home after her employment ended. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admitted, because the subject of the email falls within her former duties.
B. Excluded, because she was no longer an employee when she made the statement.
C. Admitted, because Rule 801(d)(2) has no timing requirement for agents.
D. Excluded, because a fired employee is presumed to be biased against the company.
E. Admitted as a statement against interest, because it exposed the company to liability.

Answer: B
- A: The subject matter is only half the test. The statement must also be made while the relationship existed.
- B: Correct. Rule 801(d)(2)(D) requires that the statement be made while the employment existed. After she was fired, it isn't the company's statement.
- C: Rule 801(d)(2)(D) expressly says "while it existed."
- D: There is no such presumption. Bias goes to weight, and the failure here is the timing.
- E: Rule 804(b)(3) asks whether the statement was against the declarant's interest and requires unavailability. Neither is shown.
Rule: An employee's statement counts against the employer only if made while the employment existed, under Rule 801(d)(2)(D). Scope alone isn't enough.

## Final 12 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Tome: a consistent statement made after the motive arose
Dalton is charged with sexually abusing his eight-year-old daughter, M., on a military base. M. and her mother lived with Dalton until a bitter custody fight began in January, when Dalton sought sole custody. M. testifies to the abuse. On cross-examination, defense counsel suggests that M.’s mother has coached her since January to fabricate the accusations so that the mother would win custody.

The prosecution then calls M.’s babysitter, who testifies that in March, M. told her the same account M. gave on the stand. The defense objects.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit the testimony only to rehabilitate M.’s credibility, not as substantive evidence of the abuse.
B. Admit the testimony, because M. testified and is subject to cross-examination about the prior statement, which is all Rule 801(d)(1) requires.
C. Admit the babysitter’s testimony as substantive evidence, because the 2014 amendment to Rule 801(d)(1)(B) eliminated the requirement that a consistent statement precede the alleged motive to fabricate.
D. Exclude the testimony under Rule 801(d)(1)(B), because M.’s statement was made after the alleged motive to fabricate arose.
E. Exclude the testimony, because a prior consistent statement is admissible only if it was given under penalty of perjury.

Answer: D
- A: Since 2014, a statement that properly rehabilitates under 801(d)(1)(B) comes in substantively. And a post-motive statement does not rehabilitate against a fabrication charge at all.
- B: Being subject to cross is necessary but not sufficient. Clause (B) also requires the statement to be offered for a permitted purpose and, under Tome, to predate the motive.
- C: The 2014 amendment did not disturb Tome. It extended substantive use to consistent statements that rehabilitate on other grounds, and the note says the premotive rule still governs fabrication charges.
- D: Correct. Under Tome, a consistent statement offered to rebut a charge of improper influence must predate that influence.
- E: Only prior inconsistent statements ever needed an oath, under 801(d)(1)(A), and the December 1, 2026 amendment removes that requirement too.
Rule: A testifying declarant’s prior consistent statement is not hearsay if offered (i) to rebut an express or implied charge that the declarant recently fabricated it or acted from a recent improper influence or motive, or (ii) to rehabilitate the declarant’s credibility when attacked on another ground. FRE 801(d)(1)(B). Under Tome v. United States, a statement offered under clause (i) must have been made before the alleged improper motive arose.

## Final 12 · Q17 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(1): predecessor in interest
Ames sued a neighbor over a boundary. A surveyor testified at that trial, and Ames's lawyer cross-examined him at length about the same line. Ames later sold the land to Bell. Bell now sues the same neighbor over the same line. The surveyor has died, and the neighbor offers the transcript against Bell. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Excluded, because Bell herself never had a chance to cross-examine the surveyor.
B. Admitted, because any testimony given under oath qualifies as former testimony.
C. Excluded, because Rule 804(b)(1) requires the same parties in both cases.
D. Admitted, because the transcript is a prior statement under Rule 801(d)(1)(A).
E. Admitted, because Bell's predecessor in interest had an opportunity and similar motive.

Answer: E
- A: In a civil case, a predecessor in interest's opportunity is enough under Rule 804(b)(1).
- B: An oath isn't enough. The rule requires an opportunity and similar motive to develop the testimony.
- C: The rule expressly reaches a predecessor in interest in civil cases.
- D: Rule 801(d)(1) requires a declarant who testifies at the current trial, and the surveyor is dead.
- E: Correct. Rule 804(b)(1) applies because the surveyor is unavailable and Ames had the same stake and a similar motive (Volland-Golden).
Rule: Rule 804(b)(1) admits former testimony against a party who had, or in a civil case whose predecessor in interest had, an opportunity and similar motive to develop it (Volland-Golden).

## Final 12 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Present sense impression: calm is fine
In a negligence suit, the plaintiff offers testimony from a woman who was standing at a bus stop. Seconds before the collision, the man next to her said in a calm, even voice, "That blue car is going way too fast. It's not going to stop for that light." The man can't be identified.

What is the best basis for admitting the statement?

A. As an excited utterance, because a speeding car is a startling event.
B. As a present sense impression, because he described the car as he saw it.
C. As then-existing state of mind, because it reveals the man's belief.
D. As a statement against interest, because the man had nothing to gain.
E. None; it is inadmissible, because the declarant is unidentified.

Answer: B
- A: This is the instinctive answer, but the man spoke "in a calm, even voice." Rule 803(2) requires that the declarant be under the stress of excitement. Nothing shows he was.
- B: Correct. Rule 803(1) requires no excitement, only a statement describing an event made while or immediately after perceiving it. His near-simultaneous description of the car's speed qualifies. (His prediction about the light is close enough to be part of the same description.)
- C: Rule 803(3) excludes statements of belief offered to prove the fact believed: here, that the car was speeding.
- D: Rule 804(b)(3) requires unavailability and a statement against the declarant's own interest. Neither fits.
- E: An unidentified declarant's statement can qualify if the circumstances show perception.
Rule: Rule 803(1) present sense impressions require timing (while or immediately after perceiving), not excitement. Rule 803(2) excited utterances require stress, not exact timing. Choose by the facts: a calm, contemporaneous description is a present sense impression.

## Final 12 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(4): cause vs. fault and identity
An injured tenant tells an emergency-room doctor, "My head is pounding. I was hit with a bottle. My landlord Cruz did it because he wants me evicted." In the tenant's civil suit, the doctor is asked to repeat the statement. Nothing shows the landlord's identity affected treatment. What part is admissible under Rule 803(4)?

A. All of it, because everything said to a doctor in an emergency room qualifies.
B. None of it, because the tenant is the party offering the statement.
C. Only "My head is pounding," because the rule covers present symptoms only.
D. The pain and being hit with a bottle, but not Cruz's identity or motive.
E. All of it, because the identity of an attacker is always pertinent to treatment.

Answer: D
- A: The statement must be reasonably pertinent to diagnosis or treatment. The setting isn't enough.
- B: Rule 803 exceptions apply regardless of who offers the statement.
- C: Rule 803(4) also reaches medical history and the inception or general cause of symptoms.
- D: Correct. Symptoms and general cause are pertinent. Fault and identity ordinarily aren't, and the proponent must show how identity affected treatment (State v. Smith).
- E: State v. Smith requires foundation testimony. Identity isn't automatically pertinent.
Rule: Rule 803(4) covers statements made for and reasonably pertinent to diagnosis or treatment, including general cause. Fault and identity ordinarily aren't covered without foundation testimony (State v. Smith).

## Final 12 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(6)(E): prepared with litigation in view
Facts: Wren sues Harbor Ferry Co. after falling on a gangway. Deckhand Ito completed the company's incident log an hour after the fall. Harbor Ferry requires a log entry after every incident, and its claims manager tells deckhands to fill them out "so we have our side on paper if there is a lawsuit." One entry reads: "Passenger Alvarez told me the plaintiff was running when she fell." Another reads: "I checked the gangway chain ten minutes before the fall and it was hooked." Alvarez, a passenger with no tie to the company, cannot be found. Ito has died.
Harbor Ferry offers the second log entry through its records custodian to prove the chain was hooked. What is Wren's strongest objection?

A. The company failed to prove the record is trustworthy, as the proponent must.
B. The custodian did not personally make the entry and can't lay the foundation.
C. The circumstances of its preparation show it is untrustworthy, as in Palmer.
D. A party may never offer its own employee's statement in its favor.
E. Ito's death means the entry can come in only under Rule 804.

Answer: C
- A: Since 2014, the opponent bears the burden of showing untrustworthiness under Rule 803(6)(E).
- B: Rule 803(6)(D) lets a custodian or another qualified witness lay the foundation. The custodian need not have written the entry.
- C: Correct. The stated purpose, to have the company's side on paper for a lawsuit, lets Wren show under Rule 803(6)(E) that the method or circumstances indicate a lack of trustworthiness. Palmer is the ancestor of that clause.
- D: That limit applies to Rule 801(d)(2), which runs only against a party. Rule 803(6) can be used by anyone.
- E: Rule 803 applies whether or not the declarant is available.
Rule: A record that meets Rule 803(6)(A)–(D) still fails if the opponent shows that the source or the method or circumstances of preparation indicate a lack of trustworthiness. Litigation-driven reports are the classic example (Palmer).

## Final 12 · Q21 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Davis: ongoing emergency
Mia calls 911: "He's hitting me, he's still in the house, send someone!" She gives the dispatcher her husband's name. She doesn't testify at his assault trial, and the defense has never cross-examined her. The prosecution offers the recording. How does the Confrontation Clause apply?

A. It bars the recording, because statements to a 911 operator are always testimonial.
B. It doesn't bar the recording, because the primary purpose was to meet an ongoing emergency.
C. It bars the recording, because she was unavailable and there was no prior cross-examination.
D. It doesn't bar the recording, because an excited utterance is firmly rooted and reliable.
E. It doesn't bar the recording, because a 911 dispatcher is not a law enforcement officer.

Answer: B
- A: Davis v. Washington rejects a blanket rule. A 911 call can be nontestimonial, and it can become testimonial once the emergency ends.
- B: Correct. Under Davis v. Washington, statements are nontestimonial when the objective primary purpose is to enable police to meet an ongoing emergency. Hearsay rules still apply.
- C: Unavailability plus prior cross-examination is required only for testimonial statements (Crawford).
- D: That reasoning comes from Ohio v. Roberts, which Crawford overruled for testimonial statements. Reliability isn't the test.
- E: Davis v. Washington assumed 911 operators' questions could count as police interrogation. The decision rests on purpose, not on the operator's status.
Rule: Under Davis v. Washington, a statement is nontestimonial when the objective primary purpose is to meet an ongoing emergency. Nontestimonial statements face only the hearsay rules.

## Final 12 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Bruton redaction: Gray and Samia
Ash and Bly are tried jointly for robbing a pharmacy. After his arrest, Ash gave police a written confession: “Bly and I planned it for a week. Bly had the gun; I drove.” Neither defendant will testify. Other evidence, including surveillance footage of a getaway car and phone records, connects Bly to Ash on the day of the robbery. The prosecution proposes to introduce Ash’s confession.

Which proposal is most likely constitutional as to Bly?

A. Introduce it with Bly’s name replaced by “the other guy,” with a limiting instruction, even if other evidence suggests who that is.
B. Introduce the confession unredacted against both defendants, because each defendant’s confession is an opposing-party statement.
C. Introduce the confession unredacted, with an instruction that the jury may consider it only against Ash.
D. Introduce the confession unredacted, because Ash’s confession interlocks with a confession that Bly also gave.
E. Introduce the confession with Bly’s name replaced by a blank space or the word “deleted,” with a limiting instruction.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. This is the neutral-substitution redaction approved in Samia.
- B: Ash’s confession is an opposing-party statement only against Ash. Rule 801(d)(2)(A) does not make it admissible against Bly.
- C: Bruton held that a limiting instruction cannot cure a facially incriminating confession by a non-testifying co-defendant.
- D: The Supreme Court has rejected an interlocking-confession exception: a codefendant’s confession that names the defendant is not cured by the defendant’s own similar confession.
- E: Gray v. Maryland treats obvious deletions as equivalent to naming the defendant.
Rule: At a joint trial, admitting a non-testifying co-defendant’s confession that facially incriminates the defendant violates the Confrontation Clause, even with a limiting instruction. Bruton v. United States. A confession redacted to eliminate any reference to the defendant may be admitted with a limiting instruction (Richardson v. Marsh), but obvious redactions such as blanks or “deleted” are treated like unredacted confessions (Gray v. Maryland). A neutral substitute such as “the other person,” with a limiting instruction, is permissible even if other evidence links it to the defendant. Samia v. United States (2023).

## Final 12 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Holmes: bare-suspicion rules remain valid
In a state murder trial, the defendant offers evidence that the victim's business partner had a motive to kill him: the partner stood to collect a $1 million life-insurance payout. The defendant has no evidence placing the partner near the crime or otherwise connecting him to it. The trial court excludes the evidence under a state rule that third-party-guilt evidence must do more than raise a bare suspicion. The defendant claims the exclusion violated Holmes v. South Carolina.

How will a reviewing court most likely rule?

A. Reverse, because Holmes forbids excluding third-party-guilt evidence.
B. Reverse, because a defendant may always present another person's motive.
C. Reverse, because Chambers requires admitting evidence favorable to the defense.
D. Affirm, because evidence raising only a bare suspicion may be excluded.
E. Affirm, because the prosecution's evidence of guilt was strong.

Answer: D
- A: Holmes struck a rule that judged the defense evidence by the strength of the prosecution's case, not every limit on such evidence.
- B: The right to present a defense is subject to "established rules of evidence" that aren't arbitrary (Crane).
- C: Chambers involved critical, trustworthy evidence excluded mechanistically. Motive alone isn't critical evidence of guilt.
- D: Correct, though the jury won't hear an alternative suspect. Holmes expressly approved rules excluding third-party evidence that is speculative or remote and raises only a bare suspicion. That rule looks at the defense evidence itself. Motive with no other connection is a classic bare suspicion.
- E: That reasoning is what Holmes condemned. The strength of the prosecution's case is no basis for excluding defense evidence.
Rule: Holmes distinguishes valid rules excluding third-party-guilt evidence that raises only a bare suspicion, measured by its own probative value, from arbitrary rules that judge it by the strength of the prosecution's case.

## Final 12 · Q24 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 701: lay identification from a surveillance photo
A bank's surveillance camera recorded a robber's partially obscured face. At trial, the defendant has a new beard and has gained weight. The prosecution calls the defendant's former parole officer, who met with him weekly for two years. She would testify that, in her opinion, the person in the surveillance photo is the defendant. The defense objects that the jury can compare the photo with the defendant itself.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude it, because identifying a person in a photo is the jury's job.
B. Exclude it, because only a facial-recognition expert may give that opinion.
C. Admit it, because her familiarity with his former look makes it helpful.
D. Admit it, because Rule 704(a) allows opinions on the ultimate issue.
E. Exclude it, because a parole officer is biased against the defendant.

Answer: C
- A: Often true, but not when the witness knows something about the defendant's appearance the jury doesn't, as here, given his changed appearance and the obscured image.
- B: Identification from familiarity is a classic lay opinion under Rule 701.
- C: Correct, though jurors have the photo and can see the defendant. Under Rule 701, her opinion is rationally based on two years of weekly perception and helpful because the jury lacks that familiarity. Revealing that she was his parole officer raises a Rule 403 concern, and the court can avoid it by describing her neutrally.
- D: Rule 704(a) removes one objection but doesn't establish helpfulness.
- E: Possible bias goes to weight and cross, not admissibility.
Rule: Lay opinion identifying a person in a photo is admissible under Rule 701 when the witness's familiarity makes it helpful, for example when the defendant's appearance changed or the image is unclear. Rule 403 handles prejudice from the source of the familiarity.

## Final 12 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 703: what experts in the field rely on
In a civil commitment hearing, two psychiatrists testify that Ellis Ward is dangerous to others. Their opinion rests largely on Ward's brother's report that Ward attacked him with a hammer. The brother doesn't testify. Psychiatrists routinely rely on reports from family members in assessing dangerousness. The judge excludes the opinions, saying, “I wouldn't trust a family member's story in a case like this, so I won't let experts rely on it.”

Was the ruling correct?

A. No, because once an opinion is admitted, the expert may tell the jury everything the brother said.
B. Yes, because the judge must personally find every fact underlying an expert's opinion true by a preponderance.
C. Yes, because relying on a family member's report makes the expert a mere conduit for hearsay.
D. Yes, because an expert may rely only on facts that would themselves be admissible in evidence.
E. No, because Rule 703 asks whether experts in the field reasonably rely on such reports, not whether the judge would.

Answer: E
- A: Admitting the opinion doesn't admit its basis. Under Rule 703, the proponent may disclose inadmissible basis facts only if their value in helping the jury evaluate the opinion substantially outweighs their prejudicial effect, a presumption against disclosure.
- B: The judge decides under Rule 104(a) whether the bases satisfy Rules 702(b) and 703, not whether every underlying fact is true.
- C: An expert is a conduit when she merely passes along what others said. A psychiatrist who applies clinical judgment to a family history is using the discipline's tools, which is what Rule 703 allows.
- D: Rule 703 provides the opposite: if experts in the field would reasonably rely on the kind of facts or data, they need not be admissible for the opinion to be admitted.
- E: Correct. Rule 703 asks whether “experts in the particular field would reasonably rely on those kinds of facts or data.” Courts check the bases for minimum reliability under Rule 104(a), but the standard is what the discipline reasonably relies on, not what the judge would. The Advisory Committee Note lists statements by patients and relatives among the bases physicians use.
Rule: Rule 703 lets an expert rely on inadmissible facts or data if experts in the field would reasonably rely on that kind of information; the standard is the discipline's practice, checked for minimum reliability. Disclosing inadmissible bases to the jury requires that their value in evaluating the opinion substantially outweigh their prejudicial effect.

## Final 12 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Experts and credibility: perception science vs. vouching
Toby Grant sues two police officers, claiming they shot him in the back as he ran away. The officers say he turned toward them with something in his hand before they fired. The officers call an expert in the science of perception and reaction time.

Which part of the expert's proposed testimony is most likely admissible?

A. That the officers' accounts are more credible than Grant's, because officers are trained to observe accurately.
B. That the shooting was objectively reasonable under the Fourth Amendment standard for deadly force.
C. That in her opinion the officers are telling the truth, because a lie would be too easily disproved by the forensic evidence.
D. That jurors generally overestimate their own ability to tell whether a witness is lying.
E. That research on reaction time explains how a man turning toward officers could still be hit in the back.

Answer: E
- A: Ranking witnesses' credibility is the jury's job. An expert's aura of special reliability makes such vouching especially improper.
- B: An expert may not instruct the jury on the law or tell it what legal conclusion to reach. Hygh excluded testimony that force was “not justified” for exactly this reason.
- C: This is the kind of opinion the Second Circuit rejected in Nimely v. City of New York: an expert's view that witnesses aren't lying usurps the jury's role of judging credibility.
- D: Courts tell jurors they are the lie detectors in the courtroom. Expert testimony about jurors' general ability to spot lies invites them to give up that role and is routinely excluded.
- E: Correct. An expert may explain counterintuitive scientific phenomena that help the jury evaluate the evidence, here how the timing of a turn and a shot can reconcile the officers' account with a wound in the back. What she may not do is tell the jury whom to believe (Nimely; State v. Batangan).
Rule: An expert may explain counterintuitive phenomena that help the jury evaluate evidence, but may not vouch for or against a witness's credibility (Nimely; State v. Batangan) or state legal conclusions (Hygh).

## Final 12 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Texts: prove the author
The State offers threatening text messages from a phone on the defendant's account. He says his roommates often used the phone. What is the strongest way to satisfy Rule 901(a) on authorship?

A. Show that the texts refer to details about the defendant and sit among his personal messages.
B. Offer the phone bill and the carrier's records, showing the account has always been in the defendant's name.
C. Offer a carrier's certification that the number was active that week.
D. Prove that no one but the defendant ever touched the phone.
E. Ask the judge to find authorship herself by a preponderance.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under State v. Benton, content, timing, and context are the proof of authorship under Rule 901(b)(4).
- B: Ownership of the device alone isn't enough to prove who wrote the texts (State v. Benton).
- C: An active number proves the device worked, not who used it.
- D: That demands too much. Rule 901(a) needs only evidence sufficient to support a finding.
- E: Authentication is a Rule 104(b) question. The judge screens, and the jury decides.
Rule: Authenticating texts means proving the author, not just the device, through content, timing, and context (State v. Benton). The Rule 901(a) standard is evidence sufficient to support a finding.

## Final 12 · Q28 · Privileges — Rule 501: state law in civil cases
A patient sues a surgeon for malpractice in federal court on diversity jurisdiction, with state law supplying the rule of decision. The state recognizes a physician-patient privilege. A witness invokes it. Which law governs the privilege?

A. Federal common law, and there is no federal physician-patient privilege.
B. The proposed privilege rules, because they are binding federal common law.
C. State law, because it supplies the rule of decision on the claim.
D. Federal common law, because the case is in federal court.
E. Rule 502, because it applies to all privileges in federal court.

Answer: C
- A: This would be right in a federal-question or criminal case, but not on a state-law claim.
- B: The proposed rules were never enacted. They are respected statements of the common law, not binding rules.
- C: Correct. Under Rule 501, in a civil case state law governs privilege regarding a claim or defense for which state law supplies the rule of decision.
- D: Rule 501's second sentence carves out civil cases governed by state law, even in federal court.
- E: Rule 502 covers only attorney-client privilege and work-product waiver.
Rule: Rule 501: federal common law governs privilege unless state law supplies the rule of decision in a civil case. There is no federal physician-patient privilege.

## Final 12 · Q29 · Privileges — Rakes: crime-fraud requires complicity
Paul Ostrow owns a liquor store. In 2019 a gang boss threatens to kill him unless he sells the store to an associate for a fraction of its value. Ostrow tells his wife, alone at home, about the threat and that he is going to give in. He sells. Years later Ostrow tells a grand jury that he sold willingly and was never threatened, and he is charged with perjury. Invoking the crime-fraud exception, the government seeks to have his now ex-wife testify to the conversations. Ostrow invokes the marital confidences privilege.

How should the court rule?

A. Uphold the privilege, because the crime-fraud exception never applies to marital communications.
B. Reject the privilege, because the conversations concerned extortion, a crime.
C. Reject the privilege, because the couple is now divorced.
D. Reject the privilege, because communications about financial matters aren't intended to be confidential.
E. Uphold the privilege, because handing over property under threat isn't culpable participation in a crime.

Answer: E
- A: Rakes assumed a crime-fraud exception could apply to marital communications. It held only that the exception requires the holder's culpable participation.
- B: Discussing a crime isn't enough. Assuming a crime-fraud exception applies to marital communications, it requires the privilege holder's wrongful complicity, not innocent or involuntary action.
- C: The marital confidences privilege survives divorce. What matters is that the communication was made in confidence during the marriage.
- D: Rakes rejected a general exception for financial matters. Money talk may sometimes not be meant as confidential, but these conversations plainly were.
- E: Correct. On similar facts, United States v. Rakes held the conversations privileged. They were confidential and made during the marriage, and an extortion victim who hands over property isn't a participant in the crime. Any exception takes the holder's wrongful complicity.
Rule: The marital confidences privilege survives divorce. Any crime-fraud exception requires the holder's wrongful complicity, not innocent or involuntary action, and there is no general exception for financial matters (United States v. Rakes).

## Final 12 · Q30 · Privileges — Marital confidences: children present, divorce later
While married, Carl and Dana Wren had a conversation at home in which Carl confided that he had been forced by threats to sign over his share of a business. Their two-year-old and infant were in the room. They later divorced. In a civil suit, the business's buyer calls Dana to testify about the conversation. Carl objects. Dana is willing to testify.

What is the best ruling?

A. Dana may testify, because the privilege ended with the divorce.
B. Dana may testify, because the children's presence destroyed confidentiality.
C. Dana may testify, because the witness-spouse decides whether to testify.
D. Dana may testify, because talk about finances isn't privileged.
E. Carl may prevent it, because the privilege outlasts the divorce.

Answer: E
- A: The marital-confidences privilege survives divorce. (The testimonial privilege, by contrast, needs a valid marriage when claimed.)
- B: Rakes held that the presence of infant children didn't defeat confidentiality.
- C: That is Trammel's rule for the testimonial privilege, which applies only in criminal cases. Under the confidences privilege, either spouse may prevent disclosure.
- D: Rakes rejected a general exception for financial matters.
- E: Correct, though Dana wants to testify. United States v. Rakes, on similar facts, held such conversations privileged: divorce and the presence of very young children didn't matter, and there is no exception for financial matters. The privilege applies in civil cases and either spouse may assert it.
Rule: Marital-confidences privilege: confidential communications during a valid marriage; either spouse may prevent disclosure; it survives divorce; it applies in civil and criminal cases; young children's presence doesn't defeat it (Rakes). Exceptions cover joint crimes and crimes against family members.

## Final 13 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Rule 605: the presiding judge as a witness
During a federal products-liability trial over a forklift crash at a warehouse across the street from the courthouse, the presiding judge mentions to the jury that she happened to see the crash from her chambers window and that the forklift “was clearly going too fast.” Defense counsel, startled, makes no objection. After a plaintiff's verdict, the defendant appeals on this ground.

What is the appellate court most likely to hold?

A. The issue was forfeited, because counsel had to object at the time to preserve any error under Rule 103.
B. There was error only if the judge's account was false, because a truthful account can't have prejudiced the defendant.
C. There was no error, because remarks from the bench are comments on the evidence, not testimony.
D. There was no error, because a judge with personal knowledge may testify like any other witness under Rule 602.
E. There was error, and it is preserved without an objection, because Rule 605 bars the presiding judge from testifying.

Answer: E
- A: Ordinarily a party must object to preserve error, but Rule 605 makes an exception: “A party need not object to preserve the issue.” Objecting to the very judge who is testifying puts counsel in an impossible position.
- B: The rule doesn't depend on accuracy. The problem is that the judge's account can't be tested by cross-examination and carries the authority of the bench with the jury.
- C: A judge's limited power to comment on the evidence doesn't extend to supplying new facts she saw herself. That is testimony, and Rule 605 forbids it.
- D: Rule 605 is a specific bar on the presiding judge's testimony. Personal knowledge doesn't overcome it.
- E: Correct. Rule 605 provides that the presiding judge may not testify as a witness at the trial and that a party need not object to preserve the issue. Telling the jury what she saw is testimony in substance.
Rule: Rule 605: the presiding judge may not testify as a witness at the trial, and no objection is needed to preserve the issue. Rule 606(a) likewise bars a juror from testifying before the other jurors.

## Final 13 · Q2 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 104(b): knowledge as a conditional fact (Cox)
A diner owner is charged with setting fire to a rival restaurant. To prove motive, the prosecution calls the rival’s owner, who testifies that two days before the fire he told a newspaper he planned to open a second restaurant directly across the street from the defendant’s diner. The prosecution shows that the article ran the next morning and that the defendant subscribed to the paper. The defendant testifies that he never reads it. The defense objects that the rival’s plan is irrelevant unless the defendant knew about it. What should the judge do?

A. Decide herself under Rule 104(a) whether the defendant more likely than not read the article, and exclude the testimony unless she is persuaded.
B. Admit the testimony if a reasonable jury could find, by a preponderance, that the defendant knew of the plan.
C. Exclude the testimony, because the defendant’s sworn denial creates a factual dispute that makes the evidence speculative.
D. Admit the testimony without any showing of knowledge, because a rival’s plans are always relevant to motive.
E. Admit the testimony only if the prosecution proves beyond a reasonable doubt that the defendant read the article.

Answer: B
- A: 104(a) is for preliminary questions such as privilege and competency. When relevance depends on a fact, 104(b) applies, and the jury decides whether the fact exists.
- B: Correct. This is conditional relevance under 104(b), as in Cox. The judge doesn’t weigh credibility or make her own finding (Huddleston). She asks only whether a jury could reasonably find the conditional fact. The subscription and the article’s timing are enough, as Cox’s near-daily visits to Hammer’s mother’s house were in that case.
- C: A denial doesn’t defeat conditional relevance. If a reasonable jury could find knowledge, the evidence comes in and the jury weighs the denial.
- D: The plan supplies a motive only if the defendant knew about it. That is the missing link 104(b) exists to police, and without a sufficient showing the evidence should be excluded or struck.
- E: Conditional relevance needs only evidence from which a reasonable jury could find the fact by a preponderance. Proof beyond a reasonable doubt applies to the elements at the end of the case, not to preliminary facts.
Rule: Rule 104(b) conditional relevance: admit if a reasonable jury could find the conditional fact by a preponderance. The judge doesn’t weigh credibility (Huddleston, Cox).

## Final 13 · Q3 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 408: preexisting documents
During settlement talks over a forklift accident at a warehouse, the company’s lawyer hands the injured worker’s lawyer a set of the company’s pre-existing maintenance logs, which show that the forklift’s brakes failed inspection twice in the month before the accident. Talks fail. At trial the worker offers the logs, which he also obtained in discovery, to prove the company’s negligence. The company objects under Rule 408. How should the court rule?

A. Admit the logs, because pre-existing records aren’t shielded just by being shown in settlement talks.
B. Exclude the logs, because the company showed them voluntarily, hoping to settle.
C. Admit the logs, because Rule 408 protects only offers of money, not documents or statements.
D. Exclude the logs, because the company disputed both liability and damages.
E. Exclude the logs, because Rule 408 bars every document exchanged during compromise negotiations.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. 408 protects offers and the conduct or statements made during negotiations. A record created in the ordinary course before the talks is independent evidence. The Advisory Committee explained that a party can’t immunize otherwise admissible information, such as a pre-existing document, by disclosing it in compromise negotiations. What stays out is the fact that the logs were handed over in the talks.
- B: The company’s motive doesn’t matter. Rule 408 doesn’t let a party immunize preexisting evidence by presenting it during negotiations; the logs were otherwise discoverable and were in fact obtained in discovery.
- C: 408(a)(2) also protects “conduct or a statement made during compromise negotiations.” A report prepared for the talks, like the one in Hotel Inspection, would be protected. The logs differ because they existed independently.
- D: A dispute is needed for 408 to apply at all, but it doesn’t stretch the rule to cover independent evidence.
- E: That reading would let parties shield damaging records by producing them at a settlement meeting. The rule protects the negotiations, not evidence that exists independently of them.
Rule: Rule 408 protects compromise offers and conduct or statements made in negotiations, not pre-existing evidence that happened to be disclosed during them.

## Final 13 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 411: bias exception
