In a slip-and-fall suit, the store's key witness is an investigator who testifies the floor was dry. On cross, the plaintiff seeks to show the investigator works for the store's liability insurer. The store objects under Rule 411. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Sustained, because Rule 411 bars all mention of liability insurance anywhere in a negligence trial.
B. Overruled, because the evidence shows the witness's bias, with an instruction on request.
C. Overruled, because being insured makes it more likely the store acted carelessly.
D. Sustained, because bias may be shown only by the witness's own financial stake.
E. Overruled, because Rule 411 protects only parties who have no liability insurance.

Answer: B
- A: Rule 411 bars insurance evidence only to prove negligence or wrongful conduct. It allows other purposes.
- B: Correct. Rule 411 expressly permits insurance evidence for another purpose, such as a witness's bias or prejudice. Rule 105 limits the jury's use on request.
- C: That is the inference Rule 411 forbids.
- D: Employment by an interested insurer is a recognized source of bias. No rule limits bias to a direct financial stake.
- E: Rule 411 applies whether a person was or was not insured.
Rule: Rule 411 bars proof of insurance to show negligence or wrongful conduct. It may come in for another purpose, such as bias, agency, ownership, or control.

## Final 13 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 410(a)(2): a nolo plea in a later civil suit
After a fatal boating collision, Ray Kent pleaded nolo contendere to a state charge of negligent operation of a vessel and was fined. The victim's family now sues Kent for wrongful death in federal court and offers the nolo plea as an admission that he was negligent.

What is the best ruling?

A. Admit it as Kent's own statement, offered against him by his opponent.
B. Admit it, because the resulting conviction is a public record.
C. Admit it only to impeach Kent if he testifies that he wasn't negligent.
D. Exclude it, because a nolo plea can't be used against the person who made it.
E. Exclude it, because the criminal case involved a state, not federal, charge.

Answer: D
- A: A plea is Kent's own statement, which makes this tempting. But Rule 410(a)(2) bars "a nolo contendere plea" against the defendant who made it, whatever the hearsay analysis.
- B: The plea itself, not a public record, is what is offered, and Rule 410 excludes it.
- C: Congress removed the impeachment use from Rule 410 in 1975.
- D: Correct, though Kent was in fact convicted. A nolo plea is designed to resolve a criminal charge without an admission usable elsewhere. Rule 410(a)(2) bars it in later civil cases.
- E: Rule 410(a)(3) covers state proceedings, and Rule 410(a)(2) applies to any nolo plea.
Rule: Rule 410(a) bars, against the defendant, withdrawn guilty pleas, nolo pleas, statements in plea proceedings about them, and statements in failed plea discussions with a prosecutor, in civil and criminal cases alike.

## Final 13 · Q6 · Character Evidence — Zackowitz: preparation vs. arsenal
At 9:00 p.m., Gil Moran argued with Victor Shaw at a bar, and Shaw insulted Moran's wife. Moran left, saying, "You'll be sorry." At 10:15 he bought a revolver at a pawnshop, and at 11:00 Shaw was shot dead outside the bar. Moran is charged with murder and claims he was home all night. The prosecution offers:

I. The pawnshop clerk's testimony about the 10:15 purchase.
II. Three rifles and a stun gun found in Moran's basement, none connected to the shooting.
III. A receipt showing Moran bought the stun gun two years earlier.

Which items are admissible over a character objection?

A. I only.
B. II only.
C. I and II only.
D. I and III only.
E. I, II, and III.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. A gun bought an hour after the quarrel and before the shooting shows preparation and opportunity, purposes Rule 404(b)(2) allows. Unconnected weapons, and the old receipt for one of them, show only that Moran is the "desperate type" who keeps an arsenal. That is the propensity inference People v. Zackowitz condemned.
- B: The basement weapons are exactly what Cardozo held inadmissible in People v. Zackowitz: they prove only character.
- C: Item II proves only a dangerous disposition.
- D: Item III, an old stun-gun purchase, has no link to the shooting.
- E: Items II and III have no non-propensity chain of inference.
Rule: Under Rule 404(b)(2), another act is admissible only through a non-character chain of inference. Arming oneself right after a quarrel shows preparation. A general collection of weapons shows only character (People v. Zackowitz).

## Final 13 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Other act to prove identity: distinctive device
Facts: Nadia Voss is charged in federal court with burning down a warehouse she had insured. Investigators found a homemade chemical timer of an unusual design at the scene. The government calls Joel Tran, who would testify that his late mother, a nurse at a burn clinic, told him over dinner six years ago: "A patient named Voss came in with burned hands today. She told me she got them testing a chemical timer she built for a warehouse job." The defense moves to exclude the testimony.
Set the hearsay objections aside. Why might the evidence of the earlier chemical timer avoid the propensity ban?

A. Because Rule 404(b) never applies to acts over five years old.
B. Because a statement to a nurse is privileged in federal court.
C. Because Voss has shown a general willingness to break the law.
D. Because people who burn themselves tend to commit arson.
E. Because the unusual device suggests the same person made both.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 404(b) has no time limit. Age goes to probative value.
- B: There is no federal physician-patient privilege, and privilege doesn't address propensity.
- C: A general willingness to break the law is the forbidden inference under Rule 404(b)(1).
- D: This is also a character inference. It runs through what kind of person Voss is.
- E: Correct. Under United States v. Trenkler, an idiosyncratic method supports identity under Rule 404(b)(2). Knowledge of how to build the device is another route. Rule 403 and Huddleston still apply.
Rule: A distinctive technique can show identity or knowledge under Rule 404(b)(2). The court then applies Huddleston, Rule 403, and a limiting instruction on request.

## Final 13 · Q8 · Character Evidence — Pertinent traits, by reputation or opinion only
A defendant is charged with filing fraudulent insurance claims. She calls four character witnesses. Witness 1 testifies that she has a reputation in her church for honesty. Witness 2 testifies that in his opinion she is a law-abiding person. Witness 3 testifies that she has a reputation as a calm, peaceful person. Witness 4 testifies that last year she found a wallet with $900 in it and returned it to its owner. The prosecution objects to each. Which testimony is admissible?

A. Witnesses 1, 2, and 4.
B. Witnesses 1, 2, and 3.
C. Witness 1 only.
D. Witnesses 2 and 4.
E. Witnesses 1 and 2.

Answer: E
- A: The returned wallet bears on honesty, but 405(a) allows specific instances only on cross-examination of a character witness, or under 405(b) when character is an element. The ACN notes that specific acts, though most convincing, carry the greatest risk of prejudice, confusion, surprise, and delay.
- B: The accused may offer only a trait pertinent to the charge. Peacefulness doesn’t bear on whether she lied to an insurer.
- C: The Federal Rules allow opinion as well as reputation (405(a) added opinion to the common law’s reputation-only rule), and being law-abiding is generally accepted as a pertinent trait.
- D: Witness 4’s wallet story is a specific instance, which Rule 405(a) doesn’t allow on direct, and Witness 1’s testimony to a reputation for honesty, a pertinent trait, is admissible.
- E: Correct. 404(a)(2)(A) lets the accused offer a pertinent trait. Honesty is pertinent to fraud, and being law-abiding is generally treated as pertinent to any crime. Peacefulness has nothing to do with a fraud charge. Witness 4 describes a specific instance, and on direct examination 405(a) limits proof to reputation or opinion.
Rule: Rule 404(a)(2)(A): the accused may offer a pertinent trait, proved on direct only by reputation or opinion (405(a)). Specific instances come in only on cross or when character is an element.

## Final 13 · Q9 · Character Evidence — Rule 406: habitual negligence can still be habit
Pedestrian Kim Soto was struck at the corner of Elm and Fifth at 7:40 a.m. by a car driven by Gil Doran, who says he came to a full stop at the stop sign. No one saw whether he stopped. Soto offers testimony from a crossing guard who has worked that corner every school morning for two years. The guard would say that on nearly every one of those mornings, at about 7:40, Doran's car rolled through the stop sign at Elm without stopping.

How should the court rule on Doran's objection?

A. Admit it as habit, a regular, specific response to the same stop sign.
B. Exclude it as character evidence, because it shows Doran is a careless driver.
C. Exclude it, because habit must be corroborated by an eyewitness to the accident.
D. Admit it, but only to impeach Doran's testimony that he came to a full stop.
E. Exclude it, because a habit of negligence can't be used to prove negligence.

Answer: A
- A: Correct, even though it feels like proof that Doran is a bad driver. The testimony isn't about general carelessness but about a specific, regular, semi-automatic response to a recurring situation: this stop sign, at this time, nearly every morning. That is "predictable and predictive" habit under Rule 406, admissible to prove what he did that morning.
- B: This would be right for testimony that Doran is "a careless driver." Specificity and regularity turn a trait into a habit.
- C: Rule 406 admits habit "regardless of whether it is corroborated or whether there was an eyewitness."
- D: Habit evidence is substantive under Rule 406. It isn't limited to impeachment.
- E: Rule 406 has no exception for negligent habits.
Rule: Rule 406 admits habit to prove conduct on a particular occasion, without corroboration. The line between habit and character is specificity and regularity: a semi-automatic response to a specific recurring situation, not a general disposition.

## Final 13 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — United States v. Whitmore: foundation and good faith
A federal agent is the only witness who saw the defendant toss a gun. The defense offers (1) a criminal defense lawyer who will testify that the agent has a bad reputation for truthfulness in the “courthouse community,” based on talks with three other defense lawyers, and that in his own opinion the agent is a liar because the agent testified against his clients; and (2) cross-examination of the agent about his failure to disclose a bankruptcy on his security-clearance form. The defense’s only support for item 2 is a credit-bureau printout that would itself be inadmissible hearsay. Which rulings fall within the court’s discretion?

A. Admitting both and allowing the defense to introduce the credit-bureau printout if the agent denies the omission.
B. Admitting the defense lawyer’s opinion but not his reputation testimony, because opinion needs no foundation.
C. Excluding both, because character for truthfulness may be proved only by reputation.
D. Barring the cross-examination, because the defense has no admissible proof that the agent omitted the bankruptcy.
E. Excluding the defense lawyer’s testimony for lack of foundation, while allowing the cross-examination.

Answer: E
- A: 608(b) forbids extrinsic evidence of specific instances offered to attack character for truthfulness. The defense may ask but can’t prove the omission with the printout.
- B: Opinion testimony also needs an adequate basis of acquaintance, and an opinion grounded in the agent’s testifying against the lawyer’s clients reflects bias, not knowledge of character. United States v. Whitmore upheld excluding such witnesses.
- C: 608(a) permits opinion as well as reputation, and 608(b) separately permits cross about specific instances probative of untruthfulness.
- D: Whitmore rejected exactly this reasoning. The questioner needs only a reasonable, good-faith basis. The bar on extrinsic evidence means the defense must take the agent’s answer, not that it can’t ask.
- E: Correct. Whitmore upheld excluding character witnesses whose knowledge of the reputation was too thin or too remote, and the lawyer here spoke with only a few colleagues and based his opinion on testimony against his own clients. But 608(b) cross needs only a good-faith basis, not admissible proof, and omitting a bankruptcy on an official form is probative of untruthfulness. Barring that cross was the reversible error in Whitmore, where the officer was the key witness.
Rule: Rule 608(a) witnesses need an adequate foundation (Whitmore). 608(b) cross requires only a good-faith basis, and no extrinsic evidence is allowed.

## Final 13 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609(e): a conviction on appeal
Cole Dunbar is on trial for assault and testifies in his own defense. Fourteen months ago he was convicted of federal mail fraud, which requires proof of a scheme to defraud by false representations, and his appeal of that conviction is pending. The prosecution offers the conviction to impeach. Dunbar asks that it be excluded until the appeal is decided, or else that he be allowed to tell the jury about the appeal.

What is the correct ruling?

A. Exclude the conviction until the appeal is resolved, because it isn't yet final.
B. Admit the conviction only if its probative value outweighs its prejudicial effect to Dunbar.
C. Admit the conviction, but bar any mention of the appeal as confusing to the jury.
D. Exclude the conviction, because mail fraud isn't similar to the assault charge.
E. Admit the conviction, and allow Dunbar to tell the jury that his appeal of the conviction is still pending.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 609(e) says a conviction is admissible "even if an appeal is pending."
- B: Mail fraud's elements require proof of a false statement, so Rule 609(a)(2) mandates admission without balancing.
- C: Rule 609(e) expressly says that "evidence of the pendency is also admissible."
- D: Rule 609(a)(2) admits crimen falsi regardless of how similar it is to the charge. And here the lack of similarity would only favor admission.
- E: Correct. Mail fraud is crimen falsi, so Rule 609(a)(2) requires admission with no balancing. Under Rule 609(e), a pending appeal doesn't bar use of the conviction, and the pendency is also admissible.
Rule: Under Rule 609(a)(2), a conviction whose elements required a dishonest act or false statement must be admitted. Under Rule 609(e), a pending appeal doesn't bar it, and the pendency is admissible too.

## Final 13 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 412(b)(1)(C): motive to fabricate
A church youth volunteer is charged with sexually abusing a 13-year-old boy. The defense theory is that the boy made up the accusation to deflect attention from his own serious trouble at home: months earlier, his parents had discovered that he sexually abused his younger sister and sent him to counseling. The defense seeks to cross-examine the boy about the incident with his sister. The prosecution objects under Rule 412. How should the court analyze the objection?

A. The evidence must be excluded, because Rule 412 has no exceptions in cases involving child victims.
B. It is admissible under Rule 412(b)(1)(A), because it explains the source of the boy’s sexual knowledge.
C. The evidence must be admitted, because evidence offered to show bias is always constitutionally protected.
D. Rule 412 doesn’t apply, because the evidence concerns the boy’s misconduct toward someone else, not his own victimization.
E. It is other sexual behavior under Rule 412, admissible only if exclusion would violate the right of confrontation.

Answer: E
- A: 412’s exceptions, including the constitutional exception in 412(b)(1)(C), apply in every criminal case, whatever the victim’s age.
- B: Rule 412(b)(1)(A) covers another source of semen, injury, or other physical evidence, not knowledge. The defense theory is a motive to fabricate, so the route is the constitutional exception.
- C: Olden protects cross on a prototypical form of bias, but the constitutional exception isn’t automatic. The court weighs how strongly the evidence supports the motive and whether the defense can make its point without it.
- D: The rule covers any “other sexual behavior” of the alleged victim, not only behavior in which he was a willing participant or a victim.
- E: Correct. 412(a) reaches evidence “that a victim engaged in other sexual behavior,” whoever the other person was. The defense’s route is 412(b)(1)(C), evidence whose exclusion would violate the defendant’s constitutional rights. Olden and Davis protect cross-examination showing a prototypical motive to lie. The court must decide whether this theory is strong enough to require admission over the shield.
Rule: Rule 412(b)(1)(C): evidence otherwise barred by 412 comes in if excluding it would violate the defendant’s constitutional rights, as with cross-examination on a prototypical motive to fabricate (Olden, Davis).

## Final 13 · Q13 · Definition of Hearsay — Verbal act vs. party statement: the more precise reason
Gwen Hart sues Ivan Lake for breach of contract. She testifies that when she offered to sell him her boat for $40,000, Lake replied, "I accept. I'll bring a cashier's check Friday." Lake objects that his reply is hearsay.

What is the most accurate reason the objection fails?

A. It is admissible as Lake's present sense impression of the deal.
B. It isn't hearsay at all, because words of acceptance are a verbal act.
C. It is admissible as a statement against Lake's pecuniary interest.
D. It is admissible because Lake is available to testify about it.
E. It is admissible as an opposing party's statement under Rule 801(d)(2)(A).

Answer: B
- A: Lake wasn't describing an event he perceived.
- B: Correct. The party-statement route also works, which is what makes this tricky. But "I accept" has legal effect simply because it was said: it forms the contract. It isn't offered to prove the truth of an assertion, so it isn't hearsay at all, and no exclusion or exception is needed. (Words like "I'll bring a check Friday" are part of the same operative act.)
- C: Rule 804(b)(3) requires unavailability, which isn't present, and the statement isn't being offered for its truth anyway.
- D: Availability doesn't make hearsay admissible. The statement isn't hearsay in the first place.
- E: True, and an opposing party may always offer the other side's statement. But Rule 801(d)(2) is an exclusion for statements offered for their truth. Words of acceptance aren't offered for their truth, so the more precise answer is that they aren't hearsay at all.
Rule: Verbal acts, words with independent legal significance such as an offer, acceptance, threat, or defamatory statement, aren't hearsay because they aren't offered for their truth. Rule 801(d)(2) exclusions matter when a party's words are offered for their truth.

## Final 13 · Q14 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Mahlandt: admissibility is party-specific
A bus company and its driver, Poe, are co-defendants in a crash suit. The company's board minutes say, "Poe ran the light." Poe didn't attend the meeting and didn't see the minutes. The plaintiff offers the minutes against both. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admitted against Poe only, because Poe is the actor.
B. Admitted against both, because they are co-defendants in one case.
C. Excluded against both, because the board didn't see the crash.
D. Excluded, because internal statements aren't admissions.
E. Admitted against the company only, with a limiting instruction.

Answer: E
- A: The minutes aren't Poe's statement. They were made by the company's agents, not by Poe.
- B: Rule 801(d)(2) is applied party by party, so joinder doesn't extend it.
- C: Under Mahlandt, personal knowledge isn't required.
- D: Mahlandt admitted statements made inside an organization.
- E: Correct. In Mahlandt the minutes came in against the organization, not against the individual who neither made nor adopted them. Rule 105 limits the use.
Rule: Rule 801(d)(2) is party-specific. A statement comes in only against the party who made, adopted, or is responsible for it (Mahlandt).

## Final 13 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(1)(B)(ii): rehabilitation after a memory attack
In a robbery trial, eyewitness Paula Grey identifies Joel Marks as the robber. On cross, defense counsel suggests only that her memory is unreliable: the robbery happened two years ago, at dusk, and she wasn't wearing her glasses. Counsel doesn't suggest she has any motive to lie. The prosecution then offers Grey's written statement to police, made an hour after the robbery. It describes the robber exactly as she did in her testimony.

How may the jury use the statement?

A. Not at all, because a prior consistent statement may be used only to rebut a charge of fabrication.
B. Only to evaluate Grey's credibility, not as substantive proof of what the robber looked like.
C. Not at all, because it wasn't made before a motive to fabricate could have arisen.
D. As substantive evidence, but only if Grey made the statement under oath at a proceeding.
E. As substantive evidence, if it actually rebuts the attack on Grey's memory.

Answer: E
- A: Since 2014, Rule 801(d)(1)(B)(ii) also covers statements offered to rehabilitate a witness attacked "on another ground," such as faulty memory.
- B: A statement that qualifies under Rule 801(d)(1)(B) isn't hearsay and comes in substantively. The 2014 amendment eliminated the line between rehabilitation and substance.
- C: Tome's pre-motive requirement governs a charge of fabrication under (B)(i). No motive to lie was charged here.
- D: Prior consistent statements need not be sworn. Only the old version of Rule 801(d)(1)(A) required an oath.
- E: Correct. Grey testifies and is subject to cross. Her consistent statement, made soon after the event, rehabilitates her against the attack on her memory. Rule 801(d)(1)(B)(ii) makes such a statement non-hearsay, usable for its truth.
Rule: Rule 801(d)(1)(B)(i) admits consistent statements to rebut a charge of fabrication or improper motive, and only pre-motive statements qualify (Tome). Rule 801(d)(1)(B)(ii) admits them to rehabilitate against other attacks, such as faulty memory. Both are substantive.

## Final 13 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 806: impeaching a deposition with an unsworn statement
In a negligence suit filed in March 2027, bystander Owen Hale testified at his deposition that the defendant's truck ran a red light. Hale died before trial, and the plaintiff reads the deposition into evidence as former testimony. The defendant then offers testimony from an insurance adjuster that, the day after the crash, Hale told her in an unsworn conversation that the truck “had the green.” The defendant asks the court to let the jury consider the adjuster's account as proof that the light was green.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit it as substantive evidence, because Rule 801(d)(1)(A), as amended in 2026, no longer requires an oath.
B. Exclude it, because Hale was never given an opportunity to explain or deny the earlier statement.
C. Admit it only to impeach the deposition, because Rule 801(d)(1)(A) applies only if the declarant testifies.
D. Admit it as substantive evidence under Rule 804(b)(1), because Hale is now unavailable.
E. Exclude it, because an unsworn statement can't be used to impeach testimony given under oath.

Answer: C
- A: The 2026 amendment removed the oath and proceeding requirement, but Rule 801(d)(1) still applies only when the declarant testifies and is subject to cross-examination about the prior statement. Hale is dead and isn't testifying at this trial.
- B: Rule 806 provides that the court may admit a declarant's inconsistent statement regardless of whether the declarant had an opportunity to explain or deny it. Rule 613(b)'s foundation doesn't apply.
- C: Correct. Rule 806 lets a party attack the credibility of a declarant whose hearsay has been admitted by any evidence that would be admissible if he had testified, including an inconsistent statement. Because Hale doesn't testify, Rule 801(d)(1)(A) can't make the statement substantive, so it comes in to impeach, with a limiting instruction on request.
- D: Rule 804(b)(1) covers former testimony given at a trial, hearing, or deposition. Hale's conversation with the adjuster wasn't testimony.
- E: Any inconsistent statement, sworn or not, may impeach. The oath matters only to whether a statement can be substantive evidence.
Rule: Rule 806: once a hearsay statement is admitted, the declarant's credibility may be attacked as if he had testified, including with an inconsistent statement, without the Rule 613(b) foundation. Rule 801(d)(1)(A) makes an inconsistent statement substantive only when the declarant testifies at the trial and is subject to cross-examination about it.

## Final 13 · Q17 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(2): only the cause and circumstances
Teddy is on trial for the murder of Vance, who was stabbed in an alley. Paramedics found Vance bleeding heavily. As they worked on him, Vance said: “I’m done for. Tell my mom I love her. Teddy stabbed me over the eight hundred he owes me. And tell the cops Teddy’s the one who killed Ray Moss last summer, too.” Vance died in the ambulance.

The prosecution offers all of Vance’s statement.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit the statements that Teddy stabbed Vance over the money he owed, but exclude the statement about Ray Moss.
B. Exclude the entire statement, because Vance’s belief that death was imminent must be shown by evidence other than his own words.
C. Admit only “Teddy stabbed me.” The reference to the debt must be excluded as a statement of motive, which falls outside the exception.
D. Admit the entire statement as a dying declaration, because Vance believed his death was imminent.
E. Exclude the entire statement, because statements to paramedics are testimonial and Vance cannot be cross-examined.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Identity and motive are part of the cause and circumstances; another killing is not.
- B: The declarant’s belief can be proven by his own words together with the nature of his injuries.
- C: “Circumstances” of the death include why the declarant was attacked. Motive is within the exception.
- D: The exception is limited to the cause or circumstances of the declarant’s own death. The Ray Moss accusation is outside it.
- E: Statements to paramedics during emergency treatment are nontestimonial in purpose, and dying declarations may be a recognized historical exception to the Clause anyway.
Rule: In a homicide prosecution or civil case, a statement that the declarant, while believing death to be imminent, made about its cause or circumstances is admissible if the declarant is unavailable. FRE 804(b)(2).

## Final 13 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Excited utterance vs. present sense impression
Twenty minutes after a car crash, a passenger still shaking and crying tells a paramedic, "The truck ran the red light!" In a civil suit, the injured driver offers the statement through the paramedic to prove the truck ran the light. The passenger is available but isn't called. What is the most likely basis for admission?

A. Rule 803(1), because the statement describes an event the passenger perceived.
B. Rule 803(2), because the passenger was still under the stress of the startling event.
C. Rule 803(4), because the statement was made to a medical professional at the scene.
D. Rule 804(b)(2), because the passenger believed the crash was life-threatening.
E. No exception applies, because the passenger is available to testify.

Answer: B
- A: Rule 803(1) requires the statement to be made while or immediately after perceiving the event. Twenty minutes is too long.
- B: Correct. Rule 803(2) turns on continuing stress, not a fixed time limit. Shaking and crying twenty minutes later supports the foundation.
- C: Rule 803(4) reaches statements pertinent to diagnosis or treatment. Who ran a light is fault, not treatment.
- D: Rule 804(b)(2) needs an unavailable declarant who believed death was imminent. Neither is shown.
- E: Rule 803 exceptions apply regardless of availability.
Rule: Rule 803(1) requires near-simultaneity. Rule 803(2) requires only that the declarant remain under the stress of the startling event, so it tolerates a longer gap. Both apply whether or not the declarant is available.

## Final 13 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 612: refreshing with an inadmissible writing
A police officer testifying at a robbery trial can't remember the license-plate number of the getaway car. The prosecutor hands him a copy of an unidentified bystander's handwritten note with a plate number, which the officer collected at the scene. The officer reads it silently, sets it down, and says, "Yes, now I remember. It was 7KXR224." The defense objects that the note is inadmissible hearsay.

What is the best ruling?

A. Sustain it, because a writing used to refresh memory must itself be admissible.
B. Sustain it, because the officer must first lay a recorded-recollection foundation.
C. Overrule it, and admit the note as an exhibit offered by the prosecution.
D. Sustain it, because the officer didn't write or adopt the note himself.
E. Overrule it, because anything may refresh memory; the defense may inspect the note.

Answer: E
- A: Refreshing material needn't be admissible. The evidence is the witness's revived memory, not the writing.
- B: Rule 803(5) applies when memory isn't revived. Here the officer says it was, so he testifies from present memory.
- C: Under Rule 612, the adverse party, not the proponent, may introduce relevant portions.
- D: Refreshing material needn't be the witness's own writing.
- E: Correct, though the defense will suspect the "memory" comes from the note. That suspicion is for cross-examination and the jury. Under Rule 612(b), the adverse party may inspect the writing, cross-examine about it, and introduce relevant portions.
Rule: Under Rule 612, any writing may refresh memory. The witness reads it silently and testifies from revived memory, and the adverse party gets inspection, cross, and the option to introduce it. If memory isn't revived, Rule 803(5) is the route.

## Final 13 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 803(8) and Rule 805: a bystander inside a police report
After an intersection collision, Officer Breen arrives within minutes. Her report, prepared under a department regulation requiring officers to document every collision, states: (1) “Measured 62 feet of skid marks behind Vehicle 2”; and (2) “Witness Lena Ortiz, a pedestrian, stated: ‘The blue car (Vehicle 2) ran the red.’” Breen did not record when Ortiz made the statement or her demeanor. Ortiz cannot be located.

In the civil suit by the driver of Vehicle 1 against the driver of Vehicle 2, the plaintiff offers a certified copy of the report without calling Officer Breen. The defendant objects to the entire report.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude the entire report, because a public record is admissible only through the testimony of its author or custodian.
B. Admit the skid-mark measurement, but exclude Ortiz’s statement unless it independently satisfies a hearsay exception.
C. Exclude the entire report, because Rule 803(8) excludes matters observed by law-enforcement personnel.
D. Admit the entire report as a business record, because the police department regularly prepares such reports.
E. Admit the entire report, because Officer Breen had a legal duty to record accurately what witnesses told her.

Answer: B
- A: Public records can be authenticated by certification under Rule 902(4). No live witness is required.
- B: Correct. The officer’s observation is admissible under 803(8)(A)(ii). The bystander’s statement is a separate layer that needs its own exception under Rule 805.
- C: The law-enforcement exclusion in 803(8)(A)(ii) applies only in criminal cases. This is a civil suit.
- D: Rule 803(6) has the same limitation: a statement by an outsider who is not part of the business’s regular reporting chain is not covered. Johnson v. Lutz was itself a police-report case under the business-records statute.
- E: Breen’s duty extends to her own observations, not to the truth of what a bystander told her. The informant must also be acting under a duty for the inner statement to ride along; that is the lesson of Johnson v. Lutz.
Rule: A public record setting out a matter observed while under a legal duty to report is admissible, except that in a criminal case it may not include matters observed by law-enforcement personnel. FRE 803(8)(A)(ii). When a record contains a statement by someone who had no duty to report, that inner statement is a second layer of hearsay and must satisfy its own exception. FRE 805; Johnson v. Lutz. A certified copy of a public record is self-authenticating. FRE 902(4).

## Final 13 · Q21 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Hammon: past events, no emergency
(Varies question 12.21.)
Same facts, except that when officers arrive, the scene is calm. One officer stays with the husband in the kitchen while another asks Mia in the living room what happened. She describes the assault and signs a statement. She doesn't testify, and the prosecution offers the signed statement. How does the Confrontation Clause apply?

A. It bars the statement, because its primary purpose was to prove past events for prosecution.
B. It doesn't bar the statement, because she made it within an hour of the assault.
C. It doesn't bar the statement, because a signed statement taken at the scene carries indicia of reliability.
D. It bars the statement, because anything said to police officers is testimonial.
E. It doesn't bar the statement, because the husband was in the home when she spoke.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. As in Hammon, there was no ongoing emergency, and the objective purpose was to prove past events. With no unavailability plus prior cross, Crawford bars it.
- B: Timing matters only as evidence of an emergency. Here the emergency was over.
- C: Crawford replaced reliability with cross-examination for testimonial statements.
- D: The result is right but the reason is overbroad. Michigan v. Bryant held that some statements to police are nontestimonial.
- E: Presence of the accused doesn't create confrontation. The Clause requires cross-examination.
Rule: One fact changes the result: with no ongoing emergency, a statement to police about past events is testimonial (Hammon). Crawford then requires unavailability and a prior opportunity to cross-examine.

## Final 13 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Bruton: the confessing codefendant testifies
Nadia Cole and Ben Alvarez are tried jointly before one jury for a carjacking. A detective testifies that after her arrest Cole confessed: “Ben and I took the car. Ben had the gun.” The court instructs the jury to consider the confession only against Cole. Cole then takes the stand in her own defense, denies ever making the confession, and testifies that she was home that night. Alvarez's counsel cross-examines her. Alvarez is convicted and argues on appeal that admitting Cole's confession violated Bruton.

What result?

A. Reversal, because Cole's denial made any cross-examination about the confession meaningless.
B. No violation, because Cole's confession is admissible against Alvarez as an opposing party's statement.
C. Reversal, unless the confession was redacted to remove every reference to Alvarez.
D. No violation, because Cole testified and was subject to cross-examination, which satisfies the Confrontation Clause.
E. Reversal, because Bruton holds that limiting instructions are never adequate for a codefendant's confession.

Answer: D
- A: Nelson v. O'Neil holds that when the codefendant takes the stand and is cross-examined, the Confrontation Clause is satisfied even if she denies making the confession. Cross can still test her account and her credibility.
- B: Cole's confession is admissible against Cole as her own statement under Rule 801(d)(2)(A), not against Alvarez. That is why Bruton is a concern in the first place. What saves the conviction is that Cole testified.
- C: Redaction is one way to avoid Bruton when the codefendant doesn't testify. It is unnecessary when she testifies and is cross-examined.
- D: Correct. Bruton applies only to a nontestifying codefendant's confession. When the confessing codefendant testifies and can be cross-examined, there is no confrontation problem, even if she denies the confession (Nelson v. O'Neil; California v. Green). That is one of the prosecution's ways around Bruton.
- E: Bruton's rule rests on the defendant's inability to cross-examine the confessing codefendant. Once Cole testified and was cross-examined, that premise is gone.
Rule: Bruton bars a nontestifying codefendant's facially incriminating confession at a joint trial, even with a limiting instruction. If the codefendant testifies and is subject to cross-examination, there is no confrontation problem, even if she denies the confession (Nelson v. O'Neil).

## Final 13 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Testifying witness with memory loss
A witness gave police a detailed statement but at trial takes the stand and says she remembers nothing about the event. The prosecution uses an admissible prior statement of hers. The defense argues confrontation is violated because she can't be meaningfully cross-examined. What is the most likely result?

A. No violation, because the Clause guarantees only an opportunity for effective cross.
B. A violation, because the statement to police was testimonial when she made it to them.
C. A violation, because a forgetful witness is treated as absent from the trial.
D. No violation, because police statements are nontestimonial.
E. A violation, unless she can answer every question on cross.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. United States v. Owens says the Clause guarantees an opportunity for effective cross-examination, not cross that is effective in whatever way the defense might wish. A declarant who testifies removes the problem (California v. Green).
- B: The statement may be testimonial, but the Clause doesn't bar statements the declarant is cross-examined about at trial.
- C: United States v. Owens rejected treating memory loss as absence.
- D: A statement to police describing past events is usually testimonial.
- E: The Clause doesn't require answers to every question.
Rule: When the declarant takes the stand and can be cross-examined, the Clause is satisfied even with memory loss (United States v. Owens, California v. Green).

## Final 13 · Q24 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 701(c): specialized knowledge
Which testimony is most likely to be excluded as improper lay opinion?

A. A neighbor says the driver seemed drunk, having watched him slur and stumble.
B. A bystander estimates a car was going about 60 miles per hour.
C. A sister identifies the voice on a recording as her brother's.
D. A shop owner estimates lost profits from her knowledge of her own business.
E. A bystander says the crash resulted from metal fatigue in the brake master cylinder.

Answer: E
- A: Intoxication with a foundation is a classic lay opinion under Rule 701.
- B: Speed is an ordinary inference from perception, permitted under Rule 701(a).
- C: Familiarity with a person supports identity testimony. It is rationally based on perception.
- D: A business owner may give opinions based on particularized knowledge of the business.
- E: Correct. Metal fatigue depends on specialized knowledge, so Rule 701(c) excludes it from lay testimony. It must come through a qualified witness under Rule 702.
Rule: Rule 701 lay opinion must be rationally based on perception, helpful, and not based on specialized knowledge. Rule 701(c) stops an expert in lay clothing from evading Rule 702.

## Final 13 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 703: an untraceable note and the conduit problem
A child suffers brain damage after heart surgery. The only evidence of negligence is a chart note by a neurology resident: “during surg. episode of aortic cannula accidently out x 40–60 secs.” The resident wasn't in the operating room and can't say who told him. No other record or witness mentions such an episode. The plaintiff's expert, who calls the note “unusual,” proposes to testify that the cannula came out and caused the injury, relying solely on the note.

How should the court rule?

A. Admit the opinion, because the note is a hospital business record and admissible whatever its source.
B. Exclude the opinion, because an expert may not rely on medical records prepared by another doctor.
C. Exclude the opinion, because an expert can't serve as a mere conduit for an untraceable note of unknown source.
D. Admit the opinion and let the jury weigh the note, because questions about an expert's basis go to weight.
E. Admit the opinion, because Rule 703 lets an expert rely on hearsay that wouldn't itself be admissible.

Answer: C
- A: Rule 803(6) requires a record made by, or from information transmitted by, someone with knowledge. A resident recording what an unknown person told him is multiple hearsay with no known source of knowledge.
- B: Doctors routinely rely on records made by other physicians. The problem is this note's unknown source, not that another doctor wrote it.
- C: Correct. The First Circuit affirmed exclusion on similar facts. The expert would add no expertise; he would simply relay the note to the jury. Experts don't reasonably rely on an untraceable entry as their only basis, and Rule 703 doesn't let an expert serve as a conduit for hearsay.
- D: Under Rules 702(b) and 104(a), the judge must find by a preponderance that the opinion rests on sufficient facts or data. The 2023 Committee Note rejects treating basis questions as automatically going to weight.
- E: Rule 703 allows reliance on inadmissible facts only if experts in the field would reasonably rely on that kind of information. An unexplained note from an unknown source, met by silence in every other record, isn't that kind of data.
Rule: Rule 703 allows reliance on inadmissible facts only if experts in the field would reasonably rely on them, and an expert may not serve as a mere conduit for hearsay. Rule 702(b) requires the judge to find a sufficient basis by a preponderance.

## Final 13 · Q26 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Vouching and legal conclusions
At a child sexual-abuse trial, the prosecution calls a psychologist. Which testimony is proper?

A. In my opinion, this child is telling the truth about what happened.
B. The child's account shows that abuse occurred.
C. Children who have been abused commonly delay reporting or recant.
D. The defendant fits the psychological profile of a child abuser.
E. The defendant's conduct meets the legal definition of sexual abuse.

Answer: C
- A: This is vouching. Jurors judge credibility (State v. Batangan).
- B: State v. Batangan bars a conclusory opinion that abuse occurred.
- C: Correct. Under State v. Batangan, an expert may explain seemingly bizarre behavior such as delayed reporting and recantation.
- D: This asks the jury to infer guilt from a type of person, which raises the character problem of Rule 404(a)(1).
- E: This is a legal conclusion that tells the jury what result to reach (Hygh).
Rule: An expert may explain counterintuitive behavior but may not vouch for a witness (State v. Batangan), state a legal conclusion, or tell the jury what result to reach (Hygh).

## Final 13 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Chain of custody
An officer identifies a seized pistol by its serial number, which she recorded at the scene. Separately, the State offers a bag of white powder, and the log has a two-hour gap in the middle of its chain. Which is correct?

A. The pistol needs no chain, and the powder's gap goes to weight.
B. Neither needs a chain, because exhibits are self-authenticating.
C. Both must be authenticated by the judge beyond a reasonable doubt.
D. The pistol needs a chain, but powder may be identified by sight.
E. Both need an unbroken chain, and the gap keeps out the powder.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. A unique item may be identified by a witness who recognizes it. A fungible item needs a chain, but it need not be perfect, and gaps usually go to weight.
- B: Exhibits aren't self-authenticating unless Rule 902 says so.
- C: Rule 901(a) requires evidence sufficient to support a finding, a Rule 104(b) standard.
- D: This reverses the rule. Fungible items need the chain.
- E: The chain need not be perfect, and a unique item can be identified by a witness who recognizes it.
Rule: Unique items can be identified by recognition under Rule 901(b)(1). Fungible items need a chain from seizure to court, and gaps usually go to weight.

## Final 13 · Q28 · Privileges — Jaffee: psychotherapist privilege
In a federal civil-rights suit, a police officer saw a licensed clinical social worker for counseling after a shooting. The plaintiff demands the session notes and argues the need for them outweighs privacy here. What is the most likely result under Rule 501?

A. The notes aren't privileged, because social workers are not psychiatrists.
B. The notes are privileged, and there is no case-by-case balancing.
C. The judge weighs the plaintiff's need against the officer's privacy.
D. The notes are privileged only if the officer sought counseling voluntarily.
E. The notes aren't privileged, because Rule 501 recognizes no new privileges.

Answer: B
- A: Jaffee expressly extends the privilege to licensed clinical social workers.
- B: Correct. Under Jaffee, confidential communications with a licensed psychotherapist are privileged, the privilege covers licensed clinical social workers, and there is no ad hoc balancing.
- C: Jaffee rejected balancing: an uncertain privilege is little better than none.
- D: Jaffee adds no such limit.
- E: Rule 501 directs courts to use reason and experience, which is how Jaffee recognized it.
Rule: Rule 501 lets courts recognize privileges through reason and experience. Jaffee recognized the psychotherapist privilege, including licensed clinical social workers, and rejected case-by-case balancing.

## Final 13 · Q29 · Privileges — Zolin: the threshold for in camera review
The IRS seeks recordings of meetings between a company's executives and their lawyers, claiming the crime-fraud exception. It offers lawfully obtained partial transcripts suggesting the executives asked how to structure transactions "so the auditors never see the losses." The company argues that the court can't look at the recordings at all unless the IRS first proves the exception applies, using evidence independent of the recordings.

What is the best ruling?

A. The court may not review them, because review would itself breach the privilege.
B. The court must order disclosure, because the transcripts already prove the exception.
C. The court may review them in camera on a lower, good-faith showing.
D. The court may review them only on clear and convincing proof of the exception.
E. The court may not consider the transcripts, because they came from privileged meetings.

Answer: C
- A: Zolin held that in camera review doesn't destroy the privilege and is permitted.
- B: The transcripts justify a look but haven't yet established the exception. That is the second step.
- C: Correct, though the company argued for a stricter test. United States v. Zolin set a lower threshold for in camera review than for defeating the privilege: a good-faith belief by a reasonable person that review may reveal evidence establishing the exception. Advice sought to hide losses from auditors in the future fits the exception's focus on ongoing or future wrongdoing.
- D: No heightened standard applies at the review stage.
- E: Zolin allows the threshold showing to rest on any lawfully obtained evidence not adjudicated privileged, including partial transcripts.
Rule: Zolin's two steps: (1) in camera review requires a factual basis supporting a reasonable good-faith belief that review may reveal crime-fraud evidence; (2) the exception then must actually be established. It covers advice sought for future or ongoing wrongdoing.

## Final 13 · Q30 · Privileges — Trammel vs. marital confidences
Hal is on trial in federal court for tax fraud. His wife Nora, who took no part in the fraud, is willing to testify against him. She would say (1) she saw him burn ledgers and (2) he whispered to her alone in their kitchen, "I hid the money offshore." Hal objects to both. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Hal can block both, because a defendant-spouse holds the testimonial privilege.
B. Hal can't block either, because after Trammel the witness-spouse alone holds every marital privilege.
C. Nora may testify to (1), but Hal can block (2) as a confidential marital communication.
D. Hal can block (1) as an observation in the home, but not (2).
E. Hal can block both, because the marriage still exists when the privilege is claimed.

Answer: C
- A: Trammel rejected this. The witness-spouse alone decides whether to give adverse testimony.
- B: Trammel addresses only the testimonial privilege. The marital confidences privilege is separate, and either spouse may invoke it.
- C: Correct. Under Trammel, the defendant can't keep a willing spouse off the stand, so the observed act comes in. The whispered statement is a confidential marital communication that either spouse may prevent her from disclosing.
- D: This reverses the two privileges. Observations aren't communications, and whispered words alone are.
- E: A valid marriage matters to the testimonial privilege, but that privilege belongs to Nora, who is willing to testify.
Rule: Federal testimonial privilege: the witness-spouse decides (Trammel). Marital confidences: either spouse may block confidential communications made during the marriage, but not observations of conduct.

## Final 14 · Q1 · Jury Secrecy and Competency — Rules 601–603: a young child's competency
In a federal prosecution for abusive sexual contact, the government calls the five-year-old victim. At a competency hearing, she says that telling a lie is "saying something that didn't happen" and that lying is bad. She promises to tell the truth and describes the events, though her account of the time of day and the room's furniture varies from an earlier interview. The defense argues she is too young and too inconsistent to testify.

How should the court rule?

A. She may not, because children under seven are presumed incompetent to testify.
B. She may not unless the court finds by a preponderance that her account is accurate.
C. She may not unless a psychologist certifies she can tell fantasy from reality.
D. She may testify, but only through a recorded forensic interview, not live.
E. She may, because every person is presumed competent; inconsistencies go to weight.

Answer: E
- A: The age-based common-law presumptions are gone. Rule 601 makes every person competent unless a rule provides otherwise, and a federal statute presumes children competent.
- B: The judge doesn't decide whether testimony is accurate. Personal knowledge needs only evidence sufficient to support a finding (Rule 602), and credibility is for the jury.
- C: No expert certification is required. The court asks about the ability to perceive, remember, communicate, and understand the duty to tell the truth.
- D: A competent child testifies live, subject to cross.
- E: Correct. Rule 601 presumes competence, and the child shows she understands the duty to tell the truth. She can affirm it in a form suited to her age (Rule 603), and she perceived the events (Rule 602). Inconsistent details are for cross-examination and the jury.
Rule: Rule 601 presumes every witness competent, and federal law presumes children competent. Rule 602 requires personal knowledge sufficient to support a finding, and Rule 603 an oath or affirmation. Inconsistencies go to credibility.

## Final 14 · Q2 · Relevance and Rule 403 — Rule 104(b): when the connection never comes
In an extortion trial, the prosecution offers a threatening letter signed only “J.” Defense counsel objects that nothing ties the letter to the defendant. The prosecutor says a handwriting examiner will testify later that the defendant wrote it, and the judge admits the letter on that condition. The prosecution then rests without calling the examiner, and no other evidence links the defendant to the letter. What should the court do on the defendant’s motion?

A. Let the letter stand, because once evidence is admitted, its weight is for the jury.
B. Strike the letter and tell the jury to disregard it, because the condition for admitting it was never met.
C. Let the letter stand if the judge personally believes, by a preponderance, that the defendant wrote it.
D. Declare a mistrial automatically, because no instruction can cure conditionally admitted evidence that fails.
E. Deny the motion, because the defense waived the issue by not objecting again when the prosecution rested.

Answer: B
- A: Conditional admission is just that. If no evidence would let a reasonable jury find the conditional fact by a preponderance, the letter has no proper path to relevance and can’t stay before the jury.
- B: Correct. The letter is relevant only if the defendant wrote it. 104(b) lets the court admit evidence “on the condition that the proof be introduced later.” When that proof never comes, the evidence fails the test of conditional relevance, and the court must instruct the jury to disregard it (or declare a mistrial if an instruction can’t cure the harm).
- C: Under 104(b) the judge doesn’t decide the conditional fact. She asks only whether a reasonable jury could find it (Huddleston). With no evidence of authorship, no jury could.
- D: Under Rule 104(b) the usual remedy is to strike the evidence and instruct the jury to disregard it. A mistrial is for the rare case where an instruction can’t cure the prejudice, and it isn’t automatic.
- E: The defense objected when the letter was offered, and a motion to strike is the proper way to raise the failure to connect it up.
Rule: Rule 104(b): evidence whose relevance depends on a fact may be admitted on condition of later proof. If the proof never comes, the court strikes it and tells the jury to disregard it.

## Final 14 · Q3 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 408(a)(2): the public-office exception
Which of the following statements is admissible over a Rule 408 objection?

A. A homeowner’s admission, during negotiations with his neighbor over a property-line dispute, that he knew he was building on her land, offered in a later criminal trespass prosecution.
B. The same chemical company’s offer to pay a $2 million civil penalty, offered in the criminal prosecution to show consciousness of guilt.
C. A driver’s statement in negotiations with a pedestrian’s insurer, offered by the driver to show he acted reasonably.
D. A chemical company’s admission to the EPA, made while negotiating to settle a civil enforcement action, offered in a later criminal prosecution for the same dumping.
E. A CEO’s statement at a settlement conference that “we knew the brakes were bad,” offered to impeach his trial testimony that the company had no reason to suspect a defect.

Answer: D
- A: The criminal-case exception applies only to negotiations with a public office exercising regulatory, investigative, or enforcement authority, not with a private party.
- B: The criminal-case exception covers only conduct or statements under 408(a)(2). Offers to compromise under 408(a)(1) stay excluded even in criminal cases, since an offer may reflect a desire for peace.
- C: Rule 408 bars compromise evidence offered “by any party” to prove or disprove the validity of a disputed claim, so the driver can’t use his own negotiation statement either.
- D: Correct. 408(a)(2) excludes statements made in compromise negotiations, “except when offered in a criminal case and when the negotiations related to a claim by a public office in the exercise of its regulatory, investigative, or enforcement authority.” A party talking to a regulator is on notice that its statements may be used by prosecutors.
- E: Since 2006, 408 bars compromise statements offered “to impeach by a prior inconsistent statement or a contradiction.” That’s The Verdict clip: the bishop’s “tragic accident” can’t impeach his “act of God” testimony.
Rule: Rule 408(a)(2) exception: statements (not offers) made in negotiations with a public office are admissible in a later criminal case. 408 bars compromise statements used to impeach by prior inconsistent statement or contradiction.

## Final 14 · Q4 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Mezzanatto: a waiver triggered by argument
Before a proffer session, a defendant signs an agreement allowing the government to use his statements "to rebut any evidence or argument offered by or on behalf of the defendant that is inconsistent with them." In the session he admits he was at the warehouse on the night of the theft. At trial he does not testify, but in opening statement his lawyer tells the jury that the defendant "was never at that warehouse." What should the court do with the proffer statements?

A. Admit them to rebut the argument, because Rule 410's protections may be waived by a knowing and voluntary agreement.
B. Admit them only in a later perjury prosecution, because Rule 410 allows plea statements to be used only in that setting.
C. Exclude them, because Rule 410 reflects a public policy favoring plea bargaining that defendants cannot waive.
D. Exclude them, because a lawyer's opening statement isn't evidence and so can't trigger the waiver.
E. Exclude them, because a waiver can be enforced only to impeach a defendant who testifies inconsistently on the stand.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Mezzanatto upheld waivers of 410. Many circuits enforce rebuttal waivers like this one, triggered by defense argument and not only by the defendant's testimony.
- B: This confuses the 410(b)(2) exception with the separate route of waiver by agreement.
- C: Mezzanatto rejected the claim that the rule cannot be waived.
- D: The agreement covers “evidence or argument offered by or on behalf of the defendant,” and courts enforce such waivers when defense counsel's opening contradicts the proffer.
- E: The waiver's text reaches defense arguments as well. Mezzanatto approved an impeachment waiver, and courts have since enforced broader ones.
Rule: Rule 410's protections may be waived by a knowing and voluntary agreement (United States v. Mezzanatto). Courts enforce proffer agreements that let the government rebut inconsistent evidence or argument offered for the defendant.

## Final 14 · Q5 · Specialized Relevance Rules — Rule 410: prosecutor vs. police
An FBI agent tells Jules she has no authority to make deals. Jules says, "I took the money. I'd plead to something if you drop the rest." Later, in plea discussions with the Assistant U.S. Attorney, Jules again says, "I took the money." No plea results, and there is no waiver. Which statement may the government offer in its case-in-chief?

A. Neither, because Rule 410 protects every offer to plead.
B. Both, because each is an opposing party's statement.
C. Only the statement to the prosecutor, because it was counseled.
D. Only the statement to the agent.
E. Neither in its case-in-chief, but both to impeach.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 410(a)(4) covers discussions with an attorney for the prosecuting authority. The agent said she had no authority.
- B: Rule 801(d)(2) answers only the hearsay objection. Rule 410 independently bars the statement to the prosecutor.
- C: This reverses the rule. The statement to the prosecutor is the protected one.
- D: Correct. Rule 410(a)(4) protects plea discussions with a prosecutor, and the agent said she had no authority. The statement to her is Jules's own under Rule 801(d)(2)(A).
- E: Congress removed the impeachment use of Rule 410 statements in 1975, absent a waiver (United States v. Mezzanatto).
Rule: Rule 410(a)(4) protects statements in plea discussions with a prosecutor (or a designated agent), not statements to police without authority. It has no impeachment exception, though the protection can be waived (United States v. Mezzanatto).

## Final 14 · Q6 · Character Evidence — Rule 404(b)(3): notice in a criminal case
Midway through a federal wire-fraud trial in which Lena Hart claims she didn't know the investment was a sham, the prosecutor seeks to prove that Hart ran a similar scheme in 2021, to show intent and knowledge. The prosecutor gave no pretrial notice. She explains that she had the 2021 file for months but "didn't think we'd need it." The defense never asked for notice.

What is the proper ruling?

A. Admit it, because the prosecution must give notice of other-acts evidence only on request.
B. Admit it, because intent is always at issue in a fraud prosecution, so notice is unnecessary.
C. Exclude it, because the notice requirement applies to other-acts evidence in all cases.
D. Admit it, because the notice rule applies only to evidence offered under Rules 413–415.
E. Exclude it, because there was no pretrial written notice and no good cause for the delay.

Answer: E
- A: That was the rule before 2020. Rule 404(b)(3) now requires notice in every criminal case, with no request needed.
- B: Intent may be a proper purpose, but that doesn't excuse the notice requirement.
- C: Rule 404(b)(3) applies only "in a criminal case."
- D: Rules 413–415 have their own 15-day disclosure rule. Rule 404(b)(3) separately governs ordinary other-acts evidence.
- E: Correct. Rule 404(b)(3) requires the prosecutor to give reasonable written notice before trial, stating the permitted purpose and the reasoning behind it. Notice during trial is allowed only if the court excuses pretrial notice for good cause, and having the file all along isn't good cause.
Rule: Rule 404(b)(3) requires the prosecutor in a criminal case to give reasonable written notice of other-acts evidence before trial, stating the permitted purpose and the reasoning, unless the court excuses it for good cause. No defense request is needed.

## Final 14 · Q7 · Character Evidence — Huddleston: the Rule 104(b) threshold
A defendant is charged with knowingly receiving 500 stolen laptops, which he bought for $40 each from a man in a parking lot. To prove he knew they were stolen, the government offers evidence that two months earlier he sold 200 new smartphones at a sixth of their retail price, from the same supplier, with no receipts or bill of sale. The government has no direct proof that the phones were stolen. What standard does the judge apply in deciding whether the jury may hear about the phone sales?

A. The judge must find by clear and convincing evidence that the phones were stolen.
B. The judge asks whether a jury could reasonably find, by a preponderance, that the phones were stolen.
C. The judge needs no finding at all, because Rule 404(b) evidence is admitted without any threshold showing.
D. The judge must herself find by a preponderance under Rule 104(a) that the phones were stolen.
E. The judge must exclude it unless the government proves beyond a reasonable doubt the phones were stolen.

Answer: B
- A: Some states once used a clear-and-convincing standard, but the federal rule after Huddleston is 104(b) sufficiency.
- B: Correct. Huddleston holds that whether the other act happened, here that the goods were stolen, is a question of conditional relevance under 104(b). The judge looks at all the evidence and asks whether a jury could reasonably find the fact. Low price, large quantity, and no bill of sale were enough in Huddleston itself.
- C: Huddleston requires a threshold: enough evidence for a jury reasonably to find that the act occurred and that the defendant did it. Without that, the evidence is irrelevant.
- D: Huddleston rejected a 104(a) finding by the judge. The judge doesn’t weigh credibility or make the finding; she asks only whether a reasonable jury could.
- E: The other act is not a charged offense. Even an acquittal doesn’t bar other-act evidence (Dowling), because the 104(b) standard is far lower.
Rule: Huddleston: other-act evidence comes in if a jury could reasonably find, by a preponderance, that the act occurred and the defendant did it (104(b)). Then 403 and 105 apply.

## Final 14 · Q8 · Character Evidence — Michelson: cross on specific acts, no extrinsic proof
A bookkeeper charged with embezzlement calls a coworker, who testifies that in her opinion the bookkeeper is an honest person. On cross, the prosecutor, who has the former employer’s termination letter in hand, asks, “Did you know he was fired from his last job for padding his expense reports?” The coworker says no. The prosecutor then offers the former employer’s HR director to testify about the padding. Which ruling is correct?

A. Exclude the question, because an opinion witness may be asked only “Have you heard...?” and not “Did you know...?”
B. Allow the question but exclude the HR director’s testimony.
C. Exclude both, because the defendant was never charged or convicted for the padding.
D. Allow both, because the defendant opened the door to his character for honesty.
E. Exclude the question, because Rule 608(b) allows questions about specific acts only of the defendant himself.

Answer: B
- A: Under 405(a), the old “have you heard” (reputation) versus “do you know” (opinion) distinction no longer controls admissibility. Both forms are allowed so long as the question tests the witness’s basis.
- B: Correct. Once a defendant offers his own pertinent trait under 404(a)(2)(A), 405(a) allows cross-examination of the character witness about relevant specific instances, here to test the basis of her opinion, if the prosecutor has a good-faith basis (Michelson). Specific acts may be raised only on cross, though, and the questioner must take the answer. Proving the padding through the HR director would be extrinsic evidence of a specific act.
- C: Michelson allowed questions about an arrest, which proves less than a conviction does. What’s required is a good-faith basis, and the termination letter supplies one.
- D: Opening the door lets the prosecution rebut, but under 405(a) rebuttal witnesses are limited to reputation or opinion. Specific instances may be raised only on cross-examination of the character witness.
- E: Rule 608(b) governs a witness’s character for truthfulness, not cross-examination of a Rule 404(a)(2)(A) character witness. Under Rule 405(a), the prosecutor may ask the character witness about relevant specific instances with a good-faith basis.
Rule: Rule 405(a): character is proved by reputation or opinion. On cross of the character witness, specific instances may be asked about with a good-faith basis (Michelson), but there’s no extrinsic proof.

## Final 14 · Q9 · Character Evidence — Rule 406: habit or routine practice
An insurer claims it mailed a cancellation notice. Which evidence best qualifies under Rule 406?

A. An agent is known around the office as careful and conscientious about paperwork.
B. A claims adjuster has misled eight customers over ten years about their coverage.
C. A manager usually gets angry and sends harsh letters when customers complain.
D. A clerk drinks heavily on most weekends and is often late on Monday mornings.
E. The mailroom stamps and mails every notice at 4 p.m. daily, without exception.

Answer: E
- A: That is a general character trait, not a specific response to a specific situation.
- B: Deliberate, morally charged conduct rarely qualifies, and eight instances isn't regular (Weil).
- C: Anger is a general disposition. The response isn't semi-automatic or specific enough.
- D: This shows a general tendency, not a regular response to a specific recurring situation.
- E: Correct. A specific, regular, semi-automatic response to a recurring situation is an organization's routine practice under Rule 406.
Rule: Habit or routine practice is a specific, regular, semi-automatic response to a specific recurring situation. Deliberate or morally charged conduct is usually character (Rule 406; Weil).

## Final 14 · Q10 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rules 608(b) and 609: impeaching an accused
At his 2026 trial for grand theft, the defendant testifies that he was out of state when the theft occurred. In 2022 he shot a man in the arm during a bar fight and was convicted of felony assault with a dangerous weapon. In 2024 he was convicted of misdemeanor turnstile jumping and served 30 days. The prosecutor proposes four ways to impeach him. Which may the court permit?

A. Evidence of the 2022 assault conviction, if it passes Rule 609(a)(1)(B)’s balancing test.
B. Testimony from a bartender who saw him shoot the man in the arm during the 2022 fight.
C. Cross-examination asking whether he was convicted of turnstile jumping in 2024.
D. Cross-examination asking whether he shot a man in the arm during the 2022 bar fight.
E. Evidence of the 2024 turnstile conviction, because evading a fare is a crime of dishonesty.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. A felony conviction of a testifying accused is admissible under 609(a)(1)(B) if it passes that special balance. Violent crimes are weak on honesty, so admission isn’t guaranteed, but it’s the only route the rules allow among these options.
- B: This is extrinsic evidence of a specific act. 608(b) bars it as proof of character for truthfulness, and offered to show violent character it’s propensity barred by 404.
- C: A misdemeanor qualifies under 609 only if it is crimen falsi, which fare evasion isn’t, and it isn’t the kind of deceitful act 608(b) reaches either.
- D: 608(b) permits cross only about acts probative of truthfulness, and a shooting isn’t one. Asking about the underlying act would also be a back door around 609’s protections for convictions.
- E: Fare evasion is a misdemeanor whose elements don’t require proving a dishonest act or false statement, so it isn’t crimen falsi under Rule 609(a)(2).
Rule: Impeaching an accused: felony convictions go through 609(a)(1)(B). 608(b) reaches only acts probative of truthfulness, on cross, with no extrinsic proof, and it isn’t a back door around 609.

## Final 14 · Q11 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 609(a)(1): punishable by exactly one year
In a civil trial, the defendant seeks to impeach the plaintiff's witness with her conviction, three years ago, for misdemeanor shoplifting. The offense is punishable by up to one year in jail, and she was sentenced to 30 days. The offense's elements require taking property with intent to deprive the owner, but not any false statement.

Is the conviction admissible to impeach her?

A. Yes, because theft is inherently a crime of dishonesty under Rule 609(a)(2).
B. Yes, subject to Rule 403, because she was actually sentenced to time in jail.
C. Yes, because any conviction less than 10 years old may be used to impeach.
D. No, because it isn't punishable by over a year and isn't a crime of dishonesty.
E. Yes, but only by asking about the act under Rule 608(b), not by proving it.

Answer: D
- A: The ACN (2006) limits Rule 609(a)(2) to crimes whose elements require proof of a dishonest act or false statement, such as fraud or perjury. Theft generally doesn't qualify.
- B: Rule 609(a)(1) looks to the maximum punishment, which must exceed one year. The sentence imposed doesn't matter.
- C: Recency matters only once a conviction qualifies under Rule 609(a).
- D: Correct. A crime punishable by exactly one year isn't "punishable by ... imprisonment for more than one year" under Rule 609(a)(1). Shoplifting's elements don't require a dishonest act or false statement, so Rule 609(a)(2) doesn't apply either.
- E: Rule 608(b) allows inquiry only into acts probative of truthfulness. Courts generally don't treat simple theft as such an act, and the conviction itself is governed by Rule 609.
Rule: Rule 609(a)(1) requires a crime punishable by death or imprisonment for more than one year. Rule 609(a)(2) requires elements involving a dishonest act or false statement. Theft and shoplifting ordinarily satisfy neither.

## Final 14 · Q12 · Impeachment and the Rape Shield — Rule 412(b)(2): civil reverse balancing
Kara Dunn sues her former employer for sexual harassment by her supervisor. The employer moves, 20 days before trial, to offer coworkers' testimony that Dunn often told sexually explicit jokes at work and described her sex life at office parties. The employer argues this shows the supervisor's advances were not unwelcome.

What standard governs admission?

A. It is inadmissible, because Rule 412 permits only reputation evidence about a victim in civil cases.
B. It is admissible unless unfair prejudice substantially outweighs its probative value under Rule 403.
C. It is admissible only if its value substantially outweighs harm to Dunn and unfair prejudice.
D. It is inadmissible, because it fits none of the three exceptions listed in Rule 412(b)(1).
E. It is admissible, because Rule 412 applies only in criminal proceedings for sexual offenses.

Answer: C
- A: In a civil case, specific instances may come in under the Rule 412(b)(2) balancing test. Reputation is the more restricted form: it comes in only if the victim has placed it in controversy.
- B: That is Rule 403's balance, which favors admission. Rule 412(b)(2) reverses it and places the burden on the proponent.
- C: Correct. Rule 412(b)(2) admits evidence of a victim's sexual behavior or predisposition in a civil case only if its probative value substantially outweighs the danger of harm to any victim and of unfair prejudice to any party. The employer filed the motion more than 14 days before trial, as Rule 412(c) requires, and the court holds a sealed hearing.
- D: Rule 412(b)(1) lists the exceptions for criminal cases. Civil cases use the balancing test in Rule 412(b)(2).
- E: Rule 412(a) applies "in a civil or criminal proceeding involving alleged sexual misconduct."
Rule: In civil cases, Rule 412(b)(2) admits a victim's other sexual behavior or predisposition only if its probative value substantially outweighs the harm to the victim and unfair prejudice to any party. Reputation is admissible only if the victim placed it in controversy, and Rule 412(c) procedures apply.

## Final 14 · Q13 · Definition of Hearsay — Nonassertive conduct
Residents sue a town, claiming its well water was contaminated. To prove the water was safe, the town offers testimony that its chief engineer, alone in the pump house and unaware anyone could see, read the test results, filled a cup from the well, and drank it. The residents object on hearsay grounds. Is the evidence hearsay?

A. Yes, because conduct revealing a person's belief is treated as a statement of that belief.
B. No, because the hearsay rule applies only to spoken and written words.
C. No, because the engineer did not intend the act as an assertion.
D. Yes, because a party can't offer its own employee's conduct for its truth.
E. No, because drinking the water is a verbal act with independent legal significance.

Answer: C
- A: That is the common-law view of Wright v. Doe d. Tatham. Rule 801(a) replaced it by asking whether the person intended an assertion.
- B: Rule 801(a) includes nonverbal conduct if the person intended it as an assertion.
- C: Correct. Under Rule 801(a), conduct is a statement only if intended as an assertion. Alone and unobserved, the engineer wasn't telling anyone anything.
- D: A party can't use Rule 801(d)(2) for its own statements, but there is no statement here to begin with.
- E: A verbal act is words with legal effect, like a contract offer. This conduct has no legal effect of that kind.
Rule: Under Rule 801(a), nonverbal conduct is a statement only if the person intended it as an assertion. Nonassertive conduct isn't hearsay even when offered to show the actor's belief.

## Final 14 · Q14 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(2)(E): a post-arrest confession
Agents arrest Darius Kell at a stash house holding 10 kilograms of cocaine. In a recorded interview after his arrest, Kell says: “The cocaine belongs to Vic Lomax. He fronts it to me every month and I sell it for him.” Lomax is later tried alone for conspiracy. Kell invokes the Fifth Amendment and refuses to testify. Independent evidence, including phone records and cash deposits, shows that Kell and Lomax conspired until Kell's arrest. The government offers Kell's interview against Lomax.

How should the court rule?

A. Exclude it, because invoking the privilege doesn't make Kell unavailable when the government could immunize him.
B. Admit it as a co-conspirator statement, because independent evidence shows the conspiracy and Lomax's membership in it.
C. Admit it, because under Bourjaily the judge may rely on the statement itself to find that it furthered the conspiracy.
D. Admit it as a statement against Kell's penal interest, because Kell is unavailable and admitted selling cocaine.
E. Exclude it, because a statement to agents after arrest was not made during and in furtherance of the conspiracy.

Answer: E
- A: A witness who properly invokes the Fifth Amendment is unavailable under Rule 804(a)(1). The statement fails for other reasons.
- B: Proving the conspiracy is only part of Rule 801(d)(2)(E). The statement must also be made during and in furtherance of the conspiracy, and a confession to agents after arrest serves the police, not the conspiracy.
- C: Bourjaily lets the judge consider the statement itself in deciding the preliminary facts under Rule 104(a). It doesn't change the requirement that the statement further the conspiracy, which a confession to police doesn't.
- D: Under Williamson, only the parts of a statement that are themselves self-inculpatory qualify under Rule 804(b)(3). Naming Lomax as the owner shifts blame and isn't against Kell's interest. And a recorded interview with agents is testimonial, so the Confrontation Clause would bar it anyway.
- E: Correct. Rule 801(d)(2)(E) requires a statement made during and in furtherance of the conspiracy. A post-arrest confession to agents does neither. It is also testimonial under Crawford, and Lomax has had no chance to cross-examine Kell.
Rule: Rule 801(d)(2)(E) admits a co-conspirator's statement only if made during and in furtherance of the conspiracy. Confessions to police after arrest almost never qualify, and they are testimonial under Crawford.

## Final 14 · Q15 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(1)(B): pre-motive statement
A government witness testifies against his former partner. On cross, the defense suggests he invented his story to get the plea deal he signed on March 1. The government offers two consistent statements for their truth: a January letter to his sister and an April interview with an agent. Which is admissible?

A. Both, because each is consistent and the witness is subject to cross-examination.
B. Only the April interview, because it was made to a law enforcement officer.
C. Neither, because Rule 801(d)(1) requires a prior statement given under oath.
D. Only the January letter, because it was made before the alleged motive arose.
E. Both, but only to support credibility and not for their truth.

Answer: D
- A: Being consistent isn't enough. Under Tome, the statement must come before the alleged motive arose.
