- B: The audience doesn't matter, and the April statement came after the motive, so it doesn't rebut the charge.
- C: An oath requirement appears only in the pre-amendment Rule 801(d)(1)(A). Rule 801(d)(1)(B) has never required one.
- D: Correct. Rule 801(d)(1)(B)(i) requires rebuttal of a charge of recent fabrication or improper motive. Under Tome, only a statement made before the motive arose does that.
- E: Rule 801(d)(1)(B) makes a qualifying statement substantive evidence, and the April statement doesn't qualify anyway.
Rule: A consistent statement offered to rebut a charge of fabrication or improper motive must predate that motive (Tome, Rule 801(d)(1)(B)(i)). If it qualifies, it is substantive evidence.

## Final 14 · Q16 · Opposing Party and Prior Statements — Rule 801(d)(2): 2024 successor sentence
An estate sues on a decedent's injury claim. The defendant offers the decedent's statement, made before death, "The crash was my fault." The estate argues that the decedent isn't the opposing party. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admitted, because the estate's claim derives from the decedent.
B. Admitted, because any statement by a dead person is admissible.
C. Excluded, because the party is the estate, not the decedent.
D. Excluded, because the decedent can't be cross-examined about it.
E. Admitted only if it meets every part of Rule 804(b)(3).

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under the 2024 sentence of Rule 801(d)(2), a statement admissible against the declarant is also admissible against a party whose claim is directly derived from that declarant.
- B: Death isn't an exception. The route is the successor sentence.
- C: The 2024 amendment closes this gap for a successor whose claim derives from the declarant.
- D: A party can't complain of being unable to cross-examine itself, and the estate stands in the decedent's shoes.
- E: Rule 801(d)(2) makes Rule 804(b)(3) unnecessary here.
Rule: Since 2024, a statement admissible against a declarant under Rule 801(d)(2) is also admissible against a party whose claim, defense, or potential liability is directly derived from that declarant.

## Final 14 · Q17 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 804(b)(2): not a homicide case
Bleeding heavily from gunshot wounds, Tom Hale told a paramedic, "I'm dying. Jeff Long shot me over the money." Hale survived but suffered brain damage and now can't remember anything about the shooting. Long is prosecuted in federal court for attempted murder. The prosecution offers Hale's statement as a dying declaration.

What is the best ruling on that ground?

A. Admit it, because Hale believed his death was imminent when he spoke.
B. Exclude it as a dying declaration, because this isn't a homicide case.
C. Exclude it, because Hale survived and a dying declarant must actually die.
D. Exclude it, because Hale is alive and so isn't unavailable.
E. Admit it, because dying declarations are admissible in every case.

Answer: B
- A: Hale's belief and subject meet those elements. But Rule 804(b)(2) is limited to "a prosecution for homicide or ... a civil case."
- B: Correct, though every other element is satisfied: he is unavailable through memory loss (Rule 804(a)(3)), believed death imminent, and spoke about its cause. Attempted murder isn't homicide. The prosecution should look elsewhere, for example Rule 803(2), given the stress of the moment.
- C: The declarant need not die, only be unavailable. Hale's memory loss suffices.
- D: Testifying to not remembering the subject matter makes him unavailable under Rule 804(a)(3).
- E: The rule's case-type limit is explicit.
Rule: Rule 804(b)(2) requires (1) a homicide prosecution or civil case, (2) unavailability (death isn't required), (3) belief in imminent death, and (4) a statement about its cause or circumstances.

## Final 14 · Q18 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 807 (2019): a near miss of Rule 803(2)
Ames sues a property owner for the drowning death of her son, Jace, at an unfenced retention pond. Three hours after the drowning, after Teo, Jace’s nine-year-old playmate, had stopped crying and was sitting calmly with a neighbor, Teo told the neighbor: “Jace slipped on the broken boards by the pond. The fence part was missing.” Teo’s family has since moved abroad and cannot be reached. Photographs taken that evening show missing fence panels and broken boards exactly where Teo said.

Two weeks before trial, Ames served written notice identifying Teo, the neighbor, and the substance of the statement, and stating that she would offer it under the residual exception. The court finds that the statement is not an excited utterance. The owner objects.

Which is the most accurate statement of the court’s authority?

A. The court may admit the statement only if Ames also shows that Teo is unavailable, because Rule 807 is limited to unavailable declarants.
B. The court must exclude the statement, because the residual exception may not be used to admit a statement that nearly misses a specific exception such as Rule 803(2).
C. The court must exclude the statement, because Rule 807 requires circumstantial guarantees of trustworthiness equivalent to those of the enumerated exceptions, and a child’s statement made after reflection cannot meet that standard.
D. The court may admit it if, considering the totality of the circumstances and the photographs, the statement is trustworthy and more probative than other reasonably obtainable evidence.
E. The court may not admit the statement, because notice under Rule 807 must be served at least 30 days before trial.

Answer: D
- A: Rule 807 has no unavailability requirement. Availability can matter only to whether the statement is more probative than other obtainable evidence.
- B: The 2019 committee note rejects the near-miss theory. A statement may come in under Rule 807 even if it narrowly fails a specific exception.
- C: The “equivalent guarantees” language was deleted in 2019, and no category of declarant is categorically excluded.
- D: Correct. This tracks the current text of Rule 807.
- E: Rule 807 requires reasonable notice in writing before trial, or during trial if the court excuses the lack of earlier notice for good cause. There is no fixed 30-day period.
Rule: A hearsay statement not admissible under Rule 803 or 804 may be admitted if (1) it is supported by sufficient guarantees of trustworthiness, after considering the totality of circumstances under which it was made and any evidence corroborating it; and (2) it is more probative on the point for which it is offered than any other evidence the proponent can obtain through reasonable efforts. The proponent must give reasonable notice in writing before trial, including the substance of the statement and the declarant’s name. FRE 807 (as amended 2019).

## Final 14 · Q19 · Hearsay Exceptions — Rule 612: writings used to refresh
A witness reviews a report before testifying, and then, while on the stand, takes out a second memo to refresh her memory. The adverse party demands both. Which is correct?

A. The report must be produced, but the memo stays private.
B. Both must be produced automatically, without any exceptions or decision by the court.
C. Neither must be produced unless the proponent offers them.
D. The memo must be produced, and the report may be if justice requires.
E. Neither must be produced, because refreshing isn't evidence.

Answer: D
- A: This reverses the rule. Rule 612 is stronger for a writing used while testifying.
- B: A writing used before testifying is produced only if the court decides justice requires it.
- C: The adverse party's rights under Rule 612 don't depend on the proponent's offering them.
- D: Correct. Under Rule 612, the adverse party gets a writing used while testifying, and one used before testifying if the court decides justice requires it.
- E: Refreshing isn't evidence, but Rule 612 still gives the adverse party rights.
Rule: Rule 612: the adverse party may inspect, cross on, and introduce relevant parts of a writing used to refresh while testifying, and of one used before testifying if justice requires.

## Final 14 · Q20 · Hearsay Exceptions — Beech Aircraft and Rule 803(8) in a criminal case
Holt is prosecuted for setting fire to his own warehouse. The state fire marshal’s office, an agency authorized by statute to investigate fires, issued a report concluding after investigation that the fire “was most likely accidental, caused by faulty wiring in the east wall.” The same report records the observation of a police detective who toured the scene: “strong odor of gasoline near the loading door.”

Consider two offers:
(I) Holt offers the marshal’s conclusion that the fire was most likely accidental.
(II) The prosecution offers the detective’s observation of a gasoline odor, as recorded in the report, without calling the detective.

Which is admissible under Rule 803(8)?

A. I only.
B. Neither, because Rule 803(8) applies only in civil cases.
C. Both I and II.
D. II only.
E. Neither, because opinions and conclusions in public reports are never admissible.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Findings may be used against the government in a criminal case; police observations may not be used by the prosecution.
- B: Rule 803(8) applies in criminal cases, subject to the law-enforcement exclusion and the against-the-government limit on findings.
- C: The police observation is excluded in a criminal case under 803(8)(A)(ii).
- D: This reverses the rule’s asymmetry.
- E: Beech Aircraft held that conclusions and opinions based on a factual investigation are within “factual findings.”
Rule: A public record setting out factual findings from a legally authorized investigation is admissible in a civil case or against the government in a criminal case. FRE 803(8)(A)(iii). Matters observed by law-enforcement personnel are excluded in criminal cases. FRE 803(8)(A)(ii). In either case, the opponent may show that the source of information or other circumstances indicate a lack of trustworthiness. FRE 803(8)(B).

## Final 14 · Q21 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Bryant: emergency to the public
Officers find a man mortally shot in a parking lot. He says, "Deshawn shot me at the bar down the street." The shooter hasn't been found, and no one knows where he is. The man dies before trial, and the prosecution offers the statement. Is it testimonial?

A. No, because the objective primary purpose was to meet an ongoing emergency.
B. No, because he was dying, so every dying declaration falls outside the Clause.
C. Yes, because police asked questions at the scene, which is an interrogation.
D. Yes, because all statements to police about past events are testimonial.
E. No, because the Confrontation Clause applies only to statements in court.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under Michigan v. Bryant, a shooter at large with a gun threatens the public as well as the victim. Courts consider the location, the participants' purposes, the medical condition, and formality.
- B: Crawford left dying declarations open as a possibility. Bryant doesn't rest on that ground.
- C: Questioning at the scene isn't automatically testimonial. The primary purpose decides.
- D: Michigan v. Bryant shows that a description of a recent past event can still serve an ongoing emergency.
- E: Crawford covers out-of-court testimonial statements, such as police interrogations.
Rule: An ongoing emergency depends on the threat, and a gun with a shooter at large threatens the public. Courts weigh location, purposes, medical condition, and formality objectively (Michigan v. Bryant).

## Final 14 · Q22 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Bruton: a joint bench trial
Jae Kim and Lou Fenn are charged together with arson. Kim confessed to police, naming Fenn as his partner, and Kim won't testify. Fenn validly waives his right to a jury, and the two are tried together before the judge alone. The prosecution offers Kim's confession only against Kim. Fenn objects.

What is the best ruling?

A. Exclude the confession entirely, because Bruton applies with full force in any joint trial.
B. Admit it against Kim only if it is redacted to remove Fenn's name, as Gray requires.
C. Admit the confession against both defendants, because the rules of evidence are relaxed in bench trials.
D. Admit the confession against Kim only, because a judge is presumed able to disregard it in deciding Fenn's guilt.
E. Sever the trials, because a judge who hears Kim's confession must recuse herself from Fenn's case.

Answer: D
- A: Bruton's concern is a jury that can't follow instructions. Before a judge alone, courts presume the evidence will be considered only against its maker.
- B: Redaction under Richardson, Gray, and Samia addresses a jury's inability to ignore the confession. It isn't required when a judge is the factfinder.
- C: The rules of evidence apply in federal bench trials (Rule 1101), and Kim's confession is admissible only against Kim (Rule 801(d)(2)(A)). Against Fenn it is testimonial hearsay he can't cross-examine.
- D: Correct. Bruton rests on the risk that a jury can't follow a limiting instruction. Judges are presumed able to keep evidence in separate boxes, so a bench trial is one of the prosecution's options for using a codefendant's confession, as long as the defendant voluntarily waives a jury.
- E: Hearing evidence admissible against one defendant doesn't disqualify a judge from deciding the other's case. Judges routinely hear evidence and then disregard it.
Rule: Bruton bars a nontestifying codefendant's facially incriminating confession at a joint jury trial. In a bench trial the judge is presumed able to consider it only against its maker, so the problem disappears if the defendant voluntarily waives a jury.

## Final 14 · Q23 · Confrontation, Bruton, and the Defense — Confrontation in a civil case; Rule 803(8)(A)(iii)
In a civil wrongful-death suit against a nursing home, the plaintiff offers the factual findings of a state health-department investigator. After a formal investigation, the investigator concluded that the home's staff failed to turn the decedent as required. The investigator has since died. The nursing home objects that admitting the findings violates its right to confront witnesses against it, and that the findings are hearsay.

Which ruling is correct?

A. Exclude them, because they are testimonial and the home never cross-examined the investigator.
B. Exclude them, because Rule 803(8) applies only when the official who made the record testifies.
C. Exclude them, because Rule 803(8) bars investigative findings offered against a private party.
D. Admit them, but only if the plaintiff first shows that the investigator is unavailable.
E. Admit them under Rule 803(8) unless the home shows they are untrustworthy.

Answer: E
- A: The Confrontation Clause guarantees rights "to the accused" in criminal prosecutions, not to civil litigants.
- B: Rule 803 exceptions apply regardless of the declarant's availability, and the record itself is the evidence.
- C: Rule 803(8)(A)(iii) admits factual findings in civil cases against any party. Its limit applies only in criminal cases, where findings may be used only against the government.
- D: Unavailability isn't required under Rule 803.
- E: Correct. Rule 803(8)(A)(iii) admits factual findings from a legally authorized investigation in a civil case, and Beech Aircraft extends that to opinions and conclusions. The opponent bears the burden of showing untrustworthiness. The Sixth Amendment protects only the accused.
Rule: The Confrontation Clause protects only criminal defendants. In civil cases, Rule 803(8)(A)(iii) admits investigative findings, including conclusions (Beech Aircraft), unless the opponent shows untrustworthiness.

## Final 14 · Q24 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 704(a): legal criteria and the capacity example
In a will contest, the contestants call the testator's treating neurologist. Counsel proposes two questions. Question 1: “Did Mr. Bell have the capacity to make a will?” Question 2: “Did Mr. Bell have enough mental capacity to understand the nature and extent of his property and the natural objects of his bounty, and to form a rational plan for distributing it?” The proponents object to both.

How should the court rule?

A. Allow Question 2 but exclude Question 1, which asks for an opinion under an unexplained legal standard.
B. Exclude both, because whether a testator had capacity is a legal question for the court, not a witness.
C. Allow both, but only if the neurologist first testifies to every fact on which her opinion rests.
D. Allow both, because Rule 704(a) abolished the objection that an opinion embraces an ultimate issue.
E. Allow Question 1 but exclude Question 2, because Question 2 merely restates the jury instruction.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. This is the Advisory Committee Note's own example under Rule 704. “Did T have capacity to make a will?” is excluded because it invites an answer under a legal standard the witness and jury may understand differently. The detailed question states the actual criteria, so it is allowed even though it embraces the ultimate issue.
- B: An expert may give an opinion on the facts that make up capacity, even in the ultimate-issue form of Question 2. The jury, applying the court's instructions, decides whether the legal standard was met.
- C: Rule 705 lets an expert state an opinion and its reasons without first testifying to the underlying facts, unless the court orders otherwise. The facts can be explored on cross.
- D: Rule 704(a) removed the ultimate-issue bar, but the Advisory Committee Note says it doesn't admit all opinions. Opinions phrased in terms of inadequately explored legal criteria may still be excluded as unhelpful under Rules 701, 702, and 403.
- E: That reverses the Committee Note's example. The detailed question is allowed precisely because it spells out the criteria the law uses, so the answer helps the jury instead of telling it what result to reach.
Rule: Rule 704(a) abolished the ultimate-issue objection, but opinions that merely tell the jury what result to reach or rest on inadequately explored legal criteria may be excluded as unhelpful (Advisory Committee Note; Hygh). Rule 705 doesn't require disclosing the underlying facts first.

## Final 14 · Q25 · Lay and Expert Opinion — Rule 703 bases: hypothetical questions; no personal knowledge
In a medical-malpractice trial, the plaintiff's expert, a neurologist, never examined the plaintiff. She was in the courtroom for the plaintiff's witnesses. She bases her opinion on (1) the plaintiff's hospital chart, already in evidence, and (2) a hypothetical question whose assumed facts the trial evidence supports. She then states her opinion that the delay in diagnosis caused permanent nerve damage. The defense objects that she has no personal knowledge and didn't first lay out every underlying fact.

How should the court rule?

A. Sustain it, because an expert must examine a patient before opining on her condition.
B. Sustain it, because the Federal Rules abolished the use of hypothetical questions.
C. Overrule it, but only if every fact she relied on is independently admissible.
D. Sustain it, because an expert must testify to the underlying facts before her opinion.
E. Overrule it, because experts need no personal knowledge and may give the opinion first.

Answer: E
- A: Rule 703 lets experts base opinions on facts "made aware of," and Rule 602's personal-knowledge requirement expressly doesn't apply to expert opinion.
- B: Hypothetical questions remain permissible under Rule 703. They are no longer required.
- C: Rule 703 allows reliance on inadmissible facts of a kind experts in the field reasonably rely on.
- D: Rule 705 lets an expert state an opinion and reasons without first testifying to the underlying facts, unless the court orders otherwise. They may be required on cross.
- E: Correct. Rule 703's bases include facts made known at the hearing, by observing testimony or through a hypothetical supported by the evidence. Rule 602 doesn't apply to expert opinion. Under Rule 705, she may give the opinion first, and the defense can probe the bases on cross.
Rule: Rule 703 bases: personal observation, facts made known at the hearing (including hypotheticals supported by the evidence), and facts experts reasonably rely on even if inadmissible. Rule 705 allows the opinion first, with bases disclosed on cross.

## Final 14 · Q26 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Silent witness: Luke
A shop owner's motion-activated camera recorded a burglary while he was away. He testifies he installed the system, has used it for years with no problems, and downloaded the file that morning. He has no technical training. The defense objects to the video. What is the most likely ruling?

A. Admitted, because a regular user can support a finding of accuracy.
B. Excluded, because no one who saw the burglary can vouch for the video.
C. Admitted, because any video is self-authenticating under Rule 902.
D. Excluded, because he didn't personally see the events on the video.
E. Excluded, because an engineer must explain how the system works.

Answer: A
- A: Correct. Under State v. Luke, a regular user can lay the Rule 901(b)(9) foundation, showing the process produces an accurate result. Lack of technical knowledge goes to weight.
- B: That is the pictorial-testimony route. The silent-witness route doesn't require an eyewitness.
- C: Video isn't on the Rule 902 list. It needs a foundation under Rule 901.
- D: Firsthand knowledge of the events isn't required under the silent-witness approach.
- E: State v. Luke didn't require technical knowledge, which goes to weight.
Rule: With no eyewitness, the proponent shows the recording process is accurate (Rule 901(b)(9)). A regular user can usually lay that foundation (State v. Luke).

## Final 14 · Q27 · Authentication and Best Evidence — Rule 1006 vs. Rule 107
An accountant offers a chart summarizing 40,000 admissible invoices, made available to the opponent, to prove total sales. Separately, a lawyer wants to use a timeline during closing argument to help the jury follow the evidence. Which is correct?

A. Both are evidence, and both go to the jury room.
B. Neither is allowed, because the originals must be read to the jury.
C. The chart is substantive under Rule 1006; the timeline is a Rule 107 aid.
D. The chart is an illustrative aid; the timeline is a Rule 1006 summary.
E. The chart is allowed only if all the invoices have been introduced.

Answer: C
- A: A Rule 107 aid isn't evidence and generally stays out of the jury room.
- B: Rule 1006 exists because voluminous records can't be conveniently examined in court.
- C: Correct. Rule 1006 (2024) admits summaries of voluminous admissible records as substantive evidence. Rule 107 governs aids that only help the jury follow other evidence.
- D: This reverses the two rules.
- E: Since 2024, Rule 1006 applies whether or not the records have been introduced.
Rule: Rule 1006 summaries are substantive evidence. Rule 107 illustrative aids aren't evidence and stay out of the jury room unless all parties consent or the court for good cause orders otherwise.

## Final 14 · Q28 · Privileges — Client identity: the narrow Baird exception
Attorney Nora Fisk sends the IRS a cashier's check for $90,000 in unpaid taxes on behalf of several unnamed clients. The clients had consulted her for advice after realizing they had underreported their income, and the payment carried out her advice. The IRS summons Fisk to name the clients. She invokes the lawyer-client privilege.

Must she disclose their identities?

A. Yes, because the crime-fraud exception applies to communications about a past tax violation.
B. Yes, because a client's identity is a preexisting fact, never a confidential communication.
C. Yes, because the payment was a business transaction, not a request for legal advice.
D. No, because naming them would reveal what they confided about their unpaid taxes.
E. No, because the lawyer-client privilege always protects the identity of a lawyer's clients.

Answer: D
- A: The crime-fraud exception reaches advice sought to further a future crime or fraud. Seeking advice about past underreporting, and then paying the tax, is the core of what the privilege protects.
- B: That is the general rule, but there is a narrow exception: identity is privileged when disclosing it would itself reveal a confidential communication (In re Osterhoudt).
- C: The clients consulted Fisk for legal advice about their tax exposure, and the payment carried out that advice. That is professional legal service, not a mere business errand.
- D: Correct. Identity and fees are normally not privileged, but they are when revealing them would disclose the confidential communication itself. Naming the clients who paid back taxes through a lawyer would reveal what they told her: that they had underreported income. That is the classic Baird v. Koerner exception.
- E: Identity is usually not privileged. The exception applies only when disclosure would reveal a confidential communication.
Rule: Under the federal common law that Rule 501 applies, client identity and fee arrangements are generally not privileged. They are protected only when disclosing them would reveal the substance of a confidential communication (In re Osterhoudt; Baird v. Koerner).

## Final 14 · Q29 · Privileges — Fisher and Doe: preexisting documents in the lawyer's hands
Facing an IRS investigation, a business owner gave his lawyer the company's general ledgers and invoices from 2022, prepared in the ordinary course of business, so the lawyer could advise him. The government subpoenas the lawyer for the documents. The owner argues that they are protected by the lawyer-client privilege because he gave them to his lawyer in confidence, and by his Fifth Amendment privilege.

What is the most accurate statement?

A. They are privileged, because he gave them to his lawyer in confidence.
B. They are protected by his Fifth Amendment privilege wherever they're kept.
C. They are protected only if they'd have been protected in his own hands.
D. They are protected, because the subpoena is directed at a lawyer.
E. They are protected, because the lawyer has a duty of confidentiality.

Answer: C
- A: Giving documents to a lawyer in confidence protects them only if they would have been privileged in the client's hands (Fisher). Preexisting records aren't communications.
- B: The client isn't compelled when a subpoena goes to his lawyer, so his Fifth Amendment privilege isn't implicated (Fisher).
- C: Correct, though the owner gave the documents to his lawyer to get advice. Under the Fisher case, the lawyer-client privilege protects communications, not preexisting documents. The Fifth Amendment doesn't protect documents in the lawyer's hands, since the client isn't compelled. Transfer to a lawyer protects them only if they would have been protected in the client's hands. Ordinary business records generally wouldn't be.
- D: Lawyers can be subpoenaed for unprivileged materials.
- E: The ethical duty of confidentiality isn't an evidentiary privilege.
Rule: Fisher and Doe: (1) the privilege covers communications, not preexisting documents; (2) the client's Fifth Amendment privilege doesn't reach documents held by the lawyer; (3) documents transferred for advice are protected only if they would have been protected in the client's hands; (4) the Fifth protects only compelled testimonial self-incrimination. Rule 501 leaves these questions to federal common law.

## Final 14 · Q30 · Privileges — Privilege: a lawyer's permitted disclosure doesn't waive it
During a bitter custody fight, Elena Voight told her lawyer, “Sometimes I think I should just kill him and be done with it.” The lawyer told her he couldn't be part of anything like that. Later, believing she might be dangerous, he reported the remark to the family-court judge, as the state's ethics rules permitted. Months later Voight's husband is shot, and Voight is charged with conspiracy to murder him. The prosecution calls the lawyer to repeat her remark.

What is the strongest basis for keeping the remark out?

A. Statements made during divorce or custody proceedings are absolutely privileged.
B. The remark is inadmissible hearsay, because Voight made it out of court.
C. The privilege is hers, so the lawyer's disclosure didn't waive it, and she didn't seek his help in a crime.
D. A lawyer who discloses a client's confidence under an ethics rule commits malpractice, so his testimony is barred.
E. The lawyer-client privilege has no exceptions in criminal cases.

Answer: C
- A: There's no such blanket rule. Communications are privileged because of the lawyer-client relationship, not because of the kind of case.
- B: Offered by the prosecution against Voight, her own statement isn't hearsay under Rule 801(d)(2)(A). Privilege is the real issue.
- C: Correct. Only the client can waive the privilege, so a lawyer's ethically permitted disclosure to prevent harm doesn't waive her evidentiary privilege. And the crime-fraud exception applies only if the client sought the lawyer's advice or help to further a crime or fraud; a remark the lawyer rebuffed isn't an attempt to enlist him.
- D: Here the ethics rules permitted the disclosure. In any event, rules about when a lawyer may disclose don't decide whether the evidentiary privilege survives.
- E: The privilege does have exceptions in criminal cases, most importantly the crime-fraud exception. The argument is that it doesn't apply here.
Rule: The lawyer-client privilege belongs to the client, and only the client can waive it. A lawyer's permitted disclosure under an ethics rule doesn't waive the evidentiary privilege. The crime-fraud exception requires that the client sought the lawyer's services to further a crime or fraud.
